===== page 3 =====
1. How to judge a tax system, and Hawaiʻi’s
today
This report examines a series of tax reforms, some of which would raise revenue and others
reduce it. A tax cut has to be paid for somehow, and a tax increase pays for something, so a
proposal that is silent about either side of the ledger is incomplete. Chapter 9 first estimates,
for each major tax, what taxpayers give up for every dollar the state collects after a small
increase (Table 9.1). It then estimates seven revenue-neutral combinations of changes to the
general excise tax (GET), income tax, food/excise tax credit, transient accommodations tax
and property tax (section 9.7), among them an exemption of groceries from the GET. Two
more, which change how capital gains are taxed, are in Appendix I. We call the second change
in each pair the financing change, or offset.
Except for user or benefit charges (a park entrance fee, say, that pays for the park), the source of
tax revenue does not ordinarily determine how it is spent. If the aim is to do the most good for
residents, taking account of the cost, there is no reason the money raised by taxing groceries
has to be spent on grocery shoppers, or the money raised from hotel rooms on visitors. Thus,
we consider each tax together with what it pays for. In each case we ask who would gain, who
would lose, and how people’s responses would change those gains and losses, as well as the
revenue available to the state. Since the answers often depend on assumptions, we explain
what we have assumed and why it matters wherever they do. UHERO did the analysis and
estimates in this report; what to recommend is for the Commission to decide. Unless a table
or figure gives an earlier date, the law described is the law as of November 20, 2026.
Hawaiʻi gets much more of its state and local tax revenue from sales taxes, broadly conceived,
and much less from property taxes than the United States as a whole (Figure 1.1; section 1.1).
Take a grocery exemption from the GET, Hawaiʻi’s broad tax on sales. Before allowing for
changes in spending, we estimate that exempting groceries would lower state GET collections
by about $263 million and county surcharge collections by about $33 million.1 If stores pass on
the full cut, as most of the evidence for groceries suggests, residents save about $219 million
of the state tax and visitors, who buy groceries here too, about $44 million. If they pass on 70
percent, as some evidence suggests for supermarket goods, residents save about $153 million
and visitors about $31 million, and the other $79 million stays with the owners of stores and
their suppliers (section 3.4). Suppose that the state then raised residents’ income taxes by
enough to make up residents’ savings. As a group, residents would pay exactly as much in
additional income tax as they save on groceries. However, individual households could still
gain or lose, since a household that spends a lot on groceries relative to the income tax it pays
would save more than it pays, and vice versa. And visitors keep their savings while paying no
more income tax, which means that the state and counties are still short by the visitors’ share.
1 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model. After households respond, the state loss is
about $255 million (section 9.7, Figures 9.11–9.12).

An offset through income-tax rates, in either direction, also misses residents whose income
Hawaiʻi does not tax, such as Social Security and most pension income (section 2.6), and those
who live off their savings. In the grocery example, these residents would save on groceries but
pay no more income tax. In the reverse case, if the increases in GET are offset by reductions
in income tax rates, residents would be worse off since they pay more GET and receive little
or no reduction in income tax. Even if full compensation can be provided for the group of
residents in general, this may not be the case for individual households. Refundable credit or
a transfer may be required in order to ensure that retirees and others in this situation are fully
compensated.
Chapter 9 pairs a grocery GET exemption with two financing changes: a rise in all income-tax
rates by the same proportion, or a higher TAT. In both cases, the financing change replaces
the state revenue loss. If residents fund that offset through the income tax, they will also be
paying for the visitors’ share of the loss. Income-tax rates must rise by about 12 percent, and
taxpayers as a group pay about $107 million more for the same state revenue (section 9.7).2
And while the pairs are set to hold state revenue constant in 2027, the counties are not held
harmless: in the grocery pairs, they would lose about $30 million of surcharge revenue that
the offset does not replace.

plied by 1.124.

===== page 5 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 1. How to judge a tax system
It matters which tax raises the revenue, because different taxes fall on different people and
behavior changes when taxes change. When the state taxes people, their value to the tax
system can fall: they might work less, earn less or turn down a raise, either by changing how
much they actually earn or by relabeling their income. They might even leave the tax system
entirely, by moving away or leaving the labor force.3
By contrast, the supply of land is fixed.4 Taxing land can lower its market value because buyers
account for the taxes they will pay, but it does not reduce the amount of land. Buildings are
different: taxing new construction and improvements can discourage investment. In chapter
9, a 1 percent increase in all county property tax rates loses about 2 percent of its revenue to
changed behavior, and it lowers owners’ property values by about $759 million.5 The model
holds land fixed and lets owners build and maintain a little less when the tax rises, which
accounts for the lost revenue.
This report pays attention to two features of a tax base (whatever the tax is levied on). The
first is how responsive it is: how easily it can shrink, move or hide. The second is its spillovers,
the effects of a tax on people other than the taxpayer, from the workers whose pay depends on
a business to the collections of other taxes. The cost of raising a dollar depends on both. It is
higher when the base can move or hide, and higher again when the response spills over onto
other people.6 In chapter 9’s estimates for small tax increases, taxpayers give up about $1.03
for each net dollar raised through the GET, $1.02 through the property tax, $1.42 through all
income-tax rates and $2.02 through the top rate (Table 9.1). If no household left the state in
response, the two income-tax figures would be $1.28 and $1.42.7
When people ask whether a state’s tax system is competitive, they are mostly asking how eas-
ily its taxpayers can move to a different place. Residents can move to another state and take
their future earnings with them. Businesses can make new investments in another state, or
report their profits there even if the work stays here, and visitors can choose another destina-
tion, or buy at home what they would otherwise have bought here. Wealth owners can move
their wealth, or themselves, to a state with lower taxation. Section 1.3 discusses individuals,
business capital (assets such as buildings and equipment used by businesses to produce goods
and services) and wealth, and section 1.4 discusses visitors. Section 1.6 asks to what extent
3 Saez et al. (2012) review the evidence on reported income; Kleven et al. (2020) review the evidence
on migration. Our household model lets the response of taxable income rise with income, from about
0.3 near $26,000 to about 1.3 above $450,000 (Kumar and Liang 2020), and models separately
whether people work at all (chapter 9 and Appendix C).
4 Mirrlees et al. (2011), chapter 16, pp. 368, 371.
5 UHERO calculation using our Hawaiʻi household model. The $759 million is the loss in present value
to all owners when the increase is announced. It assumes that the tax on land and part of the tax
on buildings are reflected in property values and that occupants bear the rest. If the tax were fully
reflected in property values, the upper bound, the loss would be about $1.08 billion.
6 Economists call this extra cost the excess burden of a tax, and the full cost of raising one more dollar,
including the excess burden, the marginal cost of public funds. We use neither term in the main text.
Spillovers do not always raise the cost. Some lower it, as the budget payback in section 1.5 does. In
chapter 9 we report the cost per net dollar raised, which is a related measure: we divide what the
tax increase would collect if no one responded by the revenue the government actually collects.
7 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model. For the GET we assume that businesses
pass the tax on their own purchases on to customers in prices. We do not count the cost of that tax
distorting what businesses buy, and so the true cost is probably higher.

tax considerations affect these decisions compared with other factors that make a location
attractive.
People can also respond to taxes without going anywhere. Some change when they do things,
for instance by waiting to sell an asset and realize a gain, and some change how they label
their income by reporting wages as business profits. These responses shrink the base too. But
they usually do less economic harm than when people work less, businesses invest less because
taxes leave them with less of the return, or people leave the state, since in those cases the taxed
activity itself is lost.
We draw on evidence from other places to assess how Hawaiʻi’s tax bases respond. We give
the population, place and period for each of these estimates because Hawaiʻi could be more or
less responsive than the place it comes from (Appendix B gives the standards, and Appendix
D the estimates). We report gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents, and separately the gains
and losses when we count everyone, including visitors and nonresidents (section 1.2).
1.1 Hawaiʻi’s tax mix
The GET and the individual income tax account for most of the tax revenue the state collects.
Including taxes collected by counties, we find that Hawaiʻi gets much more of its revenue from
sales taxes (broadly conceived) and much less from property taxes than the United States as
a whole. This mix reflects in part the fact that only the counties are allowed to tax property
but fiscal responsibility for education lies with the state, limiting counties’ need for high rates
to finance education spending. It also reflects the taxation of the spending of a large volume
of nonresidents, who pay the GET on almost all purchases of goods and services.
In fiscal year 2025 (FY2025), total tax collections tracked by the Department of Taxation
(DOTAX) were $11.46 billion, up from $11.08 billion in FY2024.8 Fiscal years (FY) are gov-
ernment budget years; Hawaiʻi’s fiscal year ends on June 30. Tax years (TY) are the years for
which income is taxed. Of that revenue, $4.63 billion came from the GET and use tax (40.4
percent of the total). The individual income tax brought in $3.29 billion, net of refunds (28.7
percent). Together, they account for 69.1 percent of the total.
The next largest source of revenue is the transient accommodations tax (TAT) on hotel rooms
and vacation rentals at $816.2 million. The corporate income tax accounts for a relatively
small share: $400.4 million, also net of refunds, or 3.5 percent of DOTAX’s FY2025 total. That
was down from $484.4 million in FY2024, so shares based on FY2024 data are higher. Table
1.1 gives the figures for these taxes and the other taxes discussed in the chapters that follow.
Table 1.1. Hawaiʻi tax collections, FY2025
Tax, FY2025 Collections Share of DOTAX’s total
General excise and use taxes $4.63 billion 40.4%
Individual income tax (net) $3.29 billion 28.7%
Transient accommodations tax (state) $816.2 million 7.1%

Under the state constitution, the power to tax real property belongs to the counties, so property
taxes are not included in these totals.9 Each county also has a 0.5 percent surcharge on the
GET, collected by the state, and a TAT of 3 percent, which is the highest rate that state law
allows.10 A comparison of state governments leaves out Hawaiʻi’s property tax, which appears
only in a comparison of state and local taxes.
Property taxes are reserved for financing the counties. Each county sets its own rates and
exemptions and values the property. We include them here because property taxes come up
throughout this report, especially in chapter 5. For FY2025, the four counties levied $3.06
billion in real property taxes, $1.73 billion of it in Honolulu.11 Changes to the property tax
are decided by the county councils and show up in county budgets.

same section reserves all other taxing power to the state, except where the Legislature gives that
power to others.

2020, Maui 2024, each through December 31, 2030; the state keeps a share for administration).
Hawaiʻi Revised Statutes (HRS) §237D-2.5(a) caps county TATs at 3 percent and gives county fi-

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 1. How to judge a tax system
On a state-and-local basis, Hawaiʻi collects a smaller share of its taxes from property than the
US does, and a larger share from sales and excise taxes. In FY2024, property taxes made up
18.9 percent of Hawaiʻi’s state and local taxes, against 29.4 percent nationally. General and
selective sales taxes together made up 49.6 percent, against 35.2 percent, while the individual
income tax was close to the national share, at 22.4 percent against 21.4 percent (Figure 1.1).12
Among state governments alone, in FY2025 general sales taxes were 44.9 percent of Hawaiʻi’s
state tax collections, against 30.9 percent for the 50 states together, and the corporate income
tax 3.7 percent, against 10.7 percent.13
By the measure in Tax Foundation (2026b), the GET has the broadest base among U.S. general
sales taxes. The base has been this broad since before statehood, when the GET already cov-
ered services and transactions between businesses. It taxes most services and many business
purchases, and in FY2024 its implied base equaled 116 percent of the state’s personal income
(the total income of its residents), against a median of 36 percent among the 45 states with a
general sales tax. This ratio overstates how broadly the tax falls on residents, though, because
nonresidents’ spending and business purchases are in the base, while personal income counts
only what residents earn. In our household model, the GET is charged on about 60 percent of
what Hawaiʻi residents spend on consumption in 2027 (chapter 3).14 Because the GET taxes
business-to-business sales as well as final sales, it can cascade through a chain of production,
which is known as tax pyramiding, and it gives businesses an incentive to bring more stages of
production into the same business. Chapter 3 discusses the effects on prices and production.
Comparisons like these need care, because collections, tax burdens and rankings of tax codes
measure different things. DOTAX and the Census Bureau report what governments collect. The
Tax Foundation and the Tax Policy Center explain and compare tax structures and revenue
sources. To estimate who bears a tax, one has to work out how much prices, wages and the
returns to owners change because of it. The person legally responsible for a tax need not be
the person who ultimately bears its cost (section 1.2).15
Even with the same tax rates as everyone else, a state that has a lot of visitors and little heavy
industry would get more of its revenue from consumption. A state government will also appear

Hawaiʻi’s state and county governments together collect more tax per person than those of
almost any other state. In FY2024 they collected $10,210 per person, against $7,246 for the
US as a whole. Among the 50 states and DC, only DC and New York collected more, and
California, at $10,177, collected about the same (Table 1.2). As a share of residents’ personal
income, which is a different measure, they collected 14.3 percent, the third highest of the 50
states and DC, against a median of 9.4 percent (Figure 1.2).16
Both figures include taxes paid by visitors and by owners who live somewhere else (section
1.2). The share of personal income divides those taxes by what residents alone earn, so it does
not measure what residents themselves pay. Neither figure allows for the cost of living here.17
The state government collects most of the revenue, 72.17 percent of Hawaiʻi’s state and local
taxes in FY2024, against 59.75 percent for state governments in the US as a whole. This is in
part because the state pays for the public schools and there are no school districts with taxes
of their own, while only the counties tax property.18
The other states in the table show how differently states raise their revenue. California col-
lects almost as much per person as Hawaiʻi but gets 30.73 percent of it from the individual
income tax and 18.85 percent from general sales taxes. Washington and Nevada get almost
nothing from individual income taxes and 49.03 and 44.77 percent from general sales and
gross receipts taxes. Oregon has no general retail sales tax and gets 34.25 percent from the
income tax. Hawaiʻi falls in between on both taxes, and it gets a smaller share from property

1.2 Residents, nonresidents and who bears a tax
Who hands over the revenue tells us little about who pays the tax in practice. The public studies
summarized by PFM Group Consulting (2017) estimated that nonresidents paid roughly a fifth
to nearly a third of Hawaiʻi’s state and county taxes.20 Just because the law says a business
is legally responsible for a tax, or has to remit it (send in the payment), does not mean the
business actually pays. The business might respond to the tax by raising its prices, or by paying
less in wages, rent or returns to its owners. In each case someone loses purchasing power
(customers, workers, landlords or owners), and it is these people who bear the tax.
Take the GET. The law requires the business to pay it, but allows the business to add up to
20 PFM Group Consulting (2017), p. 58; DOTAX (2006b), p. 12, an analysis by staff of DOTAX’s Tax
Research and Planning Office, not an official departmental position. The estimates cover different
historical periods.
9

===== page 12 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 1. How to judge a tax system
4.712 percent to the price on the receipt in counties with a surcharge (every county since
2024).21
But a line on the receipt does not tell us who bears the tax. Prices may be higher because of it,
or the owners, workers and landlord of the business may earn less. Which of these happens,
and to what degree, depends on how easily the customers, and each of the others, can change
what they do. If customers can easily buy less, or buy from sellers that the tax does not reach,
then the business cannot raise its prices by much and customers bear little of the tax. The GET
is different: because it applies to nearly every business selling in the state, going to the shop
next door is no escape from it, and so customers bear more of a broad tax like the GET than
they would of a tax on a single shop. On the other hand, a landlord whose building cannot
be used for anything else has little choice but to accept a lower rent, and may end up bearing
a lot of the tax. The general rule is that a tax is borne to a greater extent by those who find
it harder to change what they do. Thus, if the state taxes something that cannot move, the
owner tends to pay.
Nonresidents’ share differs a great deal from one tax to another. An analysis by staff of DOTAX’s
Tax Research and Planning Office (DOTAX 2006b), not an official departmental position, es-
timated that nonresidents paid about 64 percent of the TAT.22 Past estimates put their share
of the GET at around 30 percent,23 and Colby’s (2021) estimates for each tax, from a pre-
sentation by DOTAX staff, are shown in Figure 1.3.24 In chapter 9’s estimate of a GET rate
increase, visitors pay about 23 percent of the added tax and resident households about 52
percent, counting the tax on sales between businesses that is passed on in prices; the rest falls
on governments, health payers, nonprofits and out-of-state buyers (section 9.3).25 Chapter 9
treats all of the state TAT as paid by visitors, because there are no data on residents’ share of
the lodging base.26
Some nonresidents own property or businesses in Hawaiʻi, which helps explain why they pay
such a large share of the corporate income tax and the tax on non-residential property. They
are not all visitors. Visitors alone have been estimated to pay about 19 to 25 percent of GET
collections, less than nonresidents as a whole.27

Figure 1.3. Share of each tax paid by residents and nonresidents, FY2018
Note: The shares are for FY2018. The presentation labels them incidence but does not say how each tax was
divided between residents and nonresidents, so they are shown as the shares paid, not the shares ultimately
borne. Source: Colby (2021), slide 4.
That nonresidents bear a sizable portion of these taxes does not mean raising them will benefit
residents. Responses to changes in these taxes may have net negative effects. These issues are
covered in chapters 3 and 4 of this report.
When part of a Hawaiʻi tax is borne outside the state, economists refer to this as tax exporting.
Visitors who pay higher prices bear part of the tax, as do owners who live elsewhere and receive
lower returns. Some tax burden is also exported to the federal government through federal
deductibility. Residents who itemize deductions on their federal return can deduct state and
local income or sales taxes and property taxes, up to a certain limit. A higher Hawaiʻi tax lowers
their federal income tax to the extent that it raises their deduction, and the federal budget
absorbs part of the increase the resident would have shouldered. Businesses can similarly
deduct some of the state taxes they pay, including the GET and the corporate income tax,
when calculating their federal taxable income. However, the deduction makes no difference to
residents who take the standard deduction, or to taxes above the cap.28
The benefit of a reform depends in part on whose gains and losses are counted. Residents
gain when a nonresident pays a dollar of tax that they would otherwise have paid, but the
nonresident loses that dollar. For this reason, we report both the gains and losses to Hawaiʻi
28 Internal Revenue Code (IRC) §164(a) and (b)(6)–(7), as amended by Pub. L. 119-21, §70120
(2025). The cap on individuals’ deduction is $40,804 in 2027 for most filers, lower at high incomes,
and returns to $10,000 ($5,000 for married people filing separately) from 2030 (chapter 9). Prop-
erty taxes and other non-income taxes paid in carrying on a trade or business are not subject to the
cap; state income tax that an individual pays on business profits is.

residents and the totals for everyone, visitors and nonresidents included.29
1.3 Responsiveness: whether a tax base can shrink, move or hide
Other things equal, taxes do the least damage when the base cannot easily shrink, move or
hide. Hawaiʻi has land, which cannot move, and at the other end of the range, wealth that
can be hard even to see. People can reduce the amount of taxed activity by working less,
selling fewer homes or investing less. They can move the activity to another state, move there
themselves, or report their income there. Or they can avoid reporting it, report a value below
what it is worth, or describe it as something taxed at a lower rate. The more ways people have
to respond, the less revenue a tax increase brings in, and the greater the cost per dollar raised
to people who change what they do to avoid the tax.
Enforcement matters because a tax base is easiest to hide when the government cannot mea-
sure it. Property is difficult to hide, making property taxes difficult to evade, and every taxable
parcel of land has to be valued by the county for the property tax.30 When income is reported
by someone else to the tax authority and tax is withheld, there is rarely much error in how
much income is reported. In U.S. estimates of income omitted or misreported on tax returns
for tax years 2014-2016, the net misreporting of wages and salaries subject to withholding
and third-party reports was about 1 percent; for income with little or no third-party reporting,
such as income from self-employed businesses outside farming, it was about 55 percent.31
In FY2025 DOTAX issued $214 million in assessments, conducted 27,709 audits and collected
$284 million in delinquent taxes.32 DOTAX’s Special Enforcement Section brought in $36.3
million in impact revenue, including $5.0 million in TAT.33 Each tax chapter discusses how
enforcement shapes its own base.
Figure 1.4 gives a rough ordering of Hawaiʻi’s tax bases from the least responsive to the most:
land, property sales, people and their paychecks, capital gains, business capital, and wealth.
The ordering can change with a tax’s design and enforcement. A property tax where values
are assessed frequently and accurately would stay near the top, while an income tax with
preferences that encourage people to change how they classify income would move toward

come “subject to substantial information reporting and withholding”, 6 percent for income with
substantial information reporting but no withholding, and 55 percent for income with little or no in-
formation reporting. The percentages measure the net amount of income reported in error; they do
not measure the share of taxpayers who misreport. Slemrod (2018), p. 22, calls this variation “stark
and compelling evidence for the primary importance of deterrence”. A randomized audit study in

the bottom. Chapter 9 estimates what taxpayers give up for each net dollar raised by a small

it would be about 1.4 to 1.8 at a rate of 2 percent and roughly 1.7 to 3 at 3 percent.35 These

whose reported income responds most, and so it costs more per dollar than the capital-gains
rate.
At the top is land. Land does not move. A tax can reduce its value but cannot make the land
disappear, so the burden falls on whoever owns it when the tax is announced. Hawaiʻi’s county
property taxes are levied on buildings as well as land. Buildings can respond: tax them more
heavily and there will be less building (chapter 5).36
Property sales come next. Property transactions can be moved in time, so Hawaiʻi’s conveyance
tax can have a lock-in effect: people may respond by delaying the sale of their home. The tax is
0.10 to 1.25 percent of the full price depending on the price band and the type of sale.37 And
because the rate applies to the whole price, the tax jumps at every band boundary. Transactions

Capital is more mobile than people and capital owners can choose when to pay tax on capital
gains. A capital gain is the profit an owner realizes when she sells an asset for more than she
paid for it. Because the tax is not due until she sells, she can wait for a year when her income
is low or the tax rate is cut.
With some exceptions, she can avoid the tax entirely by leaving the asset to her heirs: inherited
assets are valued as of the date of death (the “step-up in basis”), so there is no income tax on
the gain up to that point.42 Hawaiʻi’s top tax rate on qualifying long-term gains is 7.25 percent.
In chapter 9, a small increase in this rate costs taxpayers about $1.35 per net dollar raised,
assuming that realized gains fall 0.4 percent for each 1 percent rise in the combined federal
and state tax rate on gains.43

Moving out of the state is another way to avoid the tax, at least for gains on stocks and other
securities. Stock gains are not Hawaiʻi-source income just because the seller once lived here.
Thus, if an owner makes a genuine move and then sells, the whole gain leaves the Hawaiʻi
base, including the part that built up while she was a resident. The same is not true of gains
on Hawaiʻi real estate, which remain taxable here (section 2.5).44
Business capital is easier still to move. New investment can be made in another state, and
a business can report its profits in a different state even if the work stays here. Hawaiʻi’s
corporate income tax rates are 4.4, 5.4 and 6.4 percent.45 In US states, a percentage point
increase in the corporate tax rate reduces employment by about 0.4 percent in the short run
at existing establishments of corporations that operate in more than one state. Roughly half
of that response comes from moving activity to establishments of the same corporations in
other states.46 That is harder for a hotel in Waikīkī or for the electric utility, whose customers
are here, and how much businesses respond depends on which ones are taxed (chapter 6).
Chapter 9 does not estimate business responses (section 9.8).
Wealth offers many ways to respond. Hawaiʻi already taxes the part of wealth that is easiest to
tax, real estate, through the counties’ property taxes. Other kinds of wealth are harder to tax,
in part because it is not always clear what they are worth or whose they are: it can be hard to
put a value on shares in a private company or on a work of art, and hard to attribute wealth
that is held in a trust.47 People would also have an incentive to move in response to a tax on
this kind of wealth, or to change how they report what they own. In Switzerland, where no
third party reports financial wealth to the tax authority, reported taxable wealth rose by at
least 43 percent after six years for each percentage point by which cantons cut their annual
wealth tax rates (chapter 7).48
Twelve countries in the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD)
taxed individuals’ net wealth every year in 1990, and four still did in 2017.49 An estate
tax avoids valuing wealth every year, because it generally measures wealth once, at death.
Hawaiʻi’s estate tax exclusion is $5.49 million, with rates of 10 to 20 percent above that.50
Chapter 7 discusses a wealth tax and the estate tax.

1.4 Spillovers to workers, customers and other taxes
The effects of a tax or spending policy are not limited to the people and businesses it directly
impacts, or whose behavior it changes. If a tax policy change induces a business to close its
doors, the people who work for it or sell to it can lose income as a result and the state then
collects less from its other taxes. This matters for the cost of raising revenue: other things
equal, the process of raising revenue can cost residents more than the revenue is worth when
the response to the tax means lower wages, less spending on local goods or less revenue from
other taxes. A business may cut back on workers within a few years of a tax change, but some
of these effects can take a generation to appear (section 1.5).
Spillovers of this kind may be large in the case of the corporate income tax, although it is hard
to determine with confidence exactly how large. Consider a business deciding whether to put
up a new building or buy a piece of equipment. The return on that investment to the business
is affected by the tax. Higher taxes may lead to businesses investing less in Hawaiʻi or not at all.
This may lead to fewer jobs or less productive equipment/capital, and landowners may have
fewer businesses looking to rent their property.51 S corporations and partnerships are treated
differently. They generally do not pay federal income tax themselves. Their profits pass through
to their owners’ returns instead, which is why they are called pass-through businesses (chapter
6).52
Exactly who bears the burden of the corporate tax is uncertain. One influential estimate for
US states puts about 38 percent of the burden of state corporate taxes on business owners, 35
percent on workers and 27 percent on landowners, but these estimates are imprecise: the data
are consistent with owners bearing much less, or much more, than 38 percent.53 In Germany,
about half of the burden of a local business tax fell on the workers of the firms that paid it, in
the form of lower wages.54
If business investment also makes other workers more productive and better paid, then taxing
it can impose costs beyond those borne by the firm itself. And businesses can generally move
between states more easily than between countries. In fact, one model calculation implies
that a lower corporate rate could raise total revenue in Hawaiʻi once the effects on other taxes
are counted.55 The calculation, by Suárez Serrato and Zidar (2016), combines 2010 Hawaiʻi
data with national estimates of how businesses respond. It is, however, a single calibration,
which uses national average responses and comes with no range of uncertainty. Moreover, a
later study by the same authors, which pools all states, implies that in a typical state a cut
51 Gravelle (2026); Chodorow-Reich et al. (2024). Taxes on investment can raise its cost and reduce
investment, while capital mobility can shift part of the burden to workers and landowners.
52 IRC §§701 and 1363(a); Cooper et al. (2016).
53 Suárez Serrato and Zidar (2023), pp. 3401–3403, Table 1, replying to Malgouyres et al. (2023):
38.1 percent for business owners, 35.0 percent for workers and 26.8 percent for landowners, with a
standard error of 18.1 percentage points on the business-owner share. The earlier estimates are in
Suárez Serrato and Zidar (2016). This is an estimate for US states, not a Hawaiʻi figure.

burden.

would not pay for itself.56 The effect of a rate cut on revenue in Hawaiʻi is therefore highly
uncertain. Sales-only apportionment and expensing are more direct ways to make the tax less
of a deterrent to investment. The two also complement each other. Sales-only apportionment
mainly lowers the cost of capital for multistate firms, while expensing also reaches firms that
operate only in Hawaiʻi. Chapter 6 discusses the uncertainty (section 6.2) and both measures
(sections 6.3 and 6.4).
Who gains from a cut is a separate question. After the 2017 federal corporate tax cut, 87 per-
cent of the short-run private income gains went to the top tenth of the income distribution.57
Visitor taxes involve both responsiveness and spillovers. Once visitors are here, they have to
sleep somewhere, and most of the evidence, including two Hawaiʻi studies, suggests that lodg-
ing demand is fairly insensitive to hotel taxes and that visitors bear most of them (section
4.2). A designer handbag, on the other hand, can be bought at home.58 If that is right, taxing
hotels more heavily than handbags raises the same revenue with less lost visitor spending, as
long as visitors do not shorten their stays. Our estimates from past TAT increases indicate that
visitors do cut back. Raising the state TAT rate from 11 to 12 percent would collect about $53
million of the $88.5 million it would raise if visitors did not respond, a cost per net dollar
raised of 1.66. About two-thirds of the lost revenue is GET on visitors’ other spending rather
than TAT (section 9.4).59 On our estimates, a dollar raised through the TAT costs more than
a dollar raised through the GET, the property tax or the income tax as a whole (Table 9.1).
The estimate is uncertain: the cost is 1.31 with the response to the 2009–10 increases alone
and 2.55 with the response to the 1999 increase alone. On the other hand, a visitor tax heavy
enough to discourage trips to Hawaiʻi reduces jobs and wages in the tourism industry. It costs
residents income as well as revenue (chapter 4).
A change in one tax can also affect revenue from other taxes. If the state raises the TAT and
there is no change in the rooms people book or the prices they pay, it collects the full increase
in revenue. But if the higher TAT starts discouraging people from coming or from staying as
long, the state also loses the GET on what they would have spent outside the hotel and loses
income tax revenue as tourism jobs and wages fall. Chapter 9’s estimate does not include this
income-tax loss or the counties’ TATs. Responses can also cost a tax some of the revenue it
would otherwise raise: in chapter 9, raising the GET from 4 to 4.5 percent loses about $18
million of state revenue because households that pay more tax spend less, work less or leave
the state.60

One caveat is separate from these spillovers, because it comes from what the revenue pays
for. Public spending can also change tax bases and the need for other spending. For example,
spending on schools may increase what students earn later in life and what nearby homes are
worth. The state pays for Hawaiʻi’s public schools, but any additional property tax revenue
goes to the counties. Higher property values expand the potential tax base, and how much
revenue they bring depends on assessments and the rates counties set.62 Section 1.5 takes up

1.5 Budget payback across a generation
Changes caused by either taxes or public spending can return some of a policy’s cost to the
budget, or add to it. A credit might lead a parent to enter the labor force, in which case that
parent would contribute something to income tax revenue. That can offset some of the cost of
the credit in the first place, an effect we call budget payback. It can be partial or slow, and it
can even be negative, as when a tax increase drives taxpayers away and reduces revenue from
other taxes too. In the other direction, cutting the food/excise tax credit by 1 percent raises
slightly more than the amount cut, because a smaller credit leaves people more to gain from
working (section 9.2).63
Payback through children takes the longest. When the benefits of a policy go to families with
children, it is possible that much of the return comes from the children themselves, who are
more likely as adults to work steady jobs and less likely to rely on public assistance. The budget
sees that return only once those children are adults.
Children who grew up after their counties gained access to food stamps, which spread across
the US between 1961 and 1975, were less likely to have metabolic syndrome as adults, and
the women among them became more economically self-sufficient.64 Children whose families
gained more from the earned income tax credit (EITC), a credit that adds to the earnings of
low-income working families, during their teenage years were more likely to finish high school
and college, more likely to work as young adults, and earned more.65
Across a wide range of US policies, direct spending on low-income children’s health and edu-
cation has had some of the highest benefits per dollar of net cost (the cost after subtracting
the revenue the policy brings back), more than $5 on average, and many such policies have
paid for themselves, for governments as a whole, through the taxes their beneficiaries later
paid and the assistance they no longer needed (Figure 1.7).66
There is evidence from the federal EITC that credits for working families can return much
of their cost to governments as a whole through additional tax revenue. When the EITC was
expanded, mothers who received more from the credit worked more, paid more in payroll
and sales taxes, and needed less public assistance. Bastian and Jones (2021), who treat the
federal and state governments as one budget, estimate that this offset about 83 percent of the
government’s EITC expansion cost, so the expansions nearly paid for themselves. Counting
longer-run effects, the expansion could pay for itself in full. Much of the additional tax revenue
goes to the federal government, however, so only part of the state’s cost is recouped. Childhood
Medicaid is similar: greater eligibility for Medicaid as a child raised the federal income and
63 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model. The cost per net dollar raised is 0.99.
64 Hoynes et al. (2016), p. 903. The outcomes include metabolic syndrome; the authors describe “ef-
fects decades after initial exposure”.
65 Bastian and Michelmore (2018), p. 1127: an additional $1,000 of EITC exposure at ages 13-18 raises
high school completion by 1.3 percent, college completion by 4.2 percent, employment as a young
adult by 1.0 percent and earnings by 2.2 percent.
66 Hendren and Sprung-Keyser (2020), pp. 1209–1210: “Many such policies have paid for themselves
as governments recouped the cost of their initial expenditures through additional taxes collected
and reduced transfers.” Their measure of benefits per dollar of net cost is the “marginal value of
public funds”; for direct investments in low-income children’s health and education it has averaged
more than 5.

Of Hawaiʻi’s credits, the state EITC is the one this evidence says most about. The state EITC is
a share of the federal credit, so we would expect it to encourage work in the same way, though
not necessarily by as much. But the state pays the whole cost of its own credit and gets back

A policy can have a high benefit per dollar of net cost without bringing that much revenue
back to the budget. Many of the benefits stay with the families who receive them, and a child
who grows up in Hawaiʻi may be living and paying taxes somewhere else as an adult. Since
we do not know how large these returns will be or when they will arrive, revenue neutrality
in this report refers only to state revenue in 2027.68

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 1. How to judge a tax system
1.6 Competitiveness and fairness
We cannot tell from the tax rules alone whether a tax system is competitive or fair, and what
counts as fair also depends on what we value. As for competitiveness, rankings of states by
their top tax rates and the breadth of their tax bases leave out much of the cost of living and
investing in Hawaiʻi. Many things besides taxes make a place attractive to live and invest in,
and Hawaiʻi is an ocean away from other states. For a family in Honolulu, paying less tax by
leaving would mean moving across that ocean, to new jobs and away from family and friends.
A business deciding where to expand cares where its customers are, and for many Hawaiʻi
businesses they are here.
How much people move in response to state taxes is probably the biggest open question in
Hawaiʻi tax policy. Evidence from elsewhere suggests that people do respond, but mostly in a
few groups.
Millionaires, for instance, move between states slightly less often than other Americans (2.4
percent a year, against 2.9 percent). Taxes do influence where they go, but there are too few
moves to change the top of a state’s tax base very much.69 When California raised its top tax
rate in 2012, Rauh and Shyu (2024) estimate, about 45 percent of the additional revenue
was lost to behavioral responses within the first year, mostly because people who stayed in
California reported less income.70 For comparison, in chapter 9 raising Hawaiʻi’s top rate from
13 to 14 percent loses about 39 cents of each dollar it would raise before responses when no
household leaves the state, and about 61 cents once migration is included (section 9.2).71
Star scientists move between states in response to tax differences, and foreign superstar in-
ventors respond strongly to countries’ top tax rates, though the number of inventors working
in their home country barely changes.72 Over the twentieth century, higher state taxes in the
United States also meant fewer patents in a state, partly because inventors moved to other
states.73 When US states introduced an income tax after World War II, middle- and high-
earning households moved out to states without one, although introducing a new tax is a
much larger change than adjusting an existing rate.74 The response depends on the group

very small share of top income-earners, the observed patterns of migration have little impact on the
millionaire population tax base even over 13 years.” The data are all US tax filers with incomes of
$1 million or more, 1999-2011.

section B.1 discusses how Hawaiʻi could measure moves.
Many things besides taxes affect growth, and the evidence on taxes is mixed. In one study of
US states, the relationship of tax revenue and top income tax rates to growth and employment
was not stable across states or over time.76
Housing constraints can be a large drag on growth. In one model-based estimate, limits on
building in the most productive US cities reduced growth for the country as a whole because
workers could not move to where they would be most productive.77 There is also what the
tax revenue pays for and the natural amenities that attract people to Hawaiʻi in the first place.
With all of these things to consider, a tax change could improve Hawaiʻi’s ranking and still do
little to help residents if housing remains scarce.
Whether a tax is regressive depends on what we measure (annual income, spending or lifetime
income) and the period we use. A tax is regressive if lower-income households pay a greater
share of their resources than higher-income households. Measured against annual income,
Hawaiʻi’s state and local taxes take about the same share from the bottom four-fifths of non-
elderly families and a smaller share from those at the top, a pattern the Institute on Taxation
and Economic Policy (ITEP 2024) labels regressive. Under 2024 law at 2023 incomes, ITEP
estimates that the poorest fifth paid 14.1 percent of their income in state and local taxes, the
middle fifth 14.2 percent, and the top 1 percent 10.1 percent. ITEP’s estimates predate the
2024 income tax cut (Act 46), so the income-tax part of its figures is higher at low and middle
incomes than under current law; section 2.1 shows how Acts 46 and 24 change the income
tax by income.78
Lower-income households spend more of their annual income, which makes a sales tax regres-
sive when measured against income. Measured against spending, a better guide to lifetime
resources, it is less regressive (chapter 3). And while a tax viewed by itself might be regres-
sive, no part of the tax system can be judged in isolation, because the revenue from it can fund
a tax credit or a program for lower-income households that more than offsets it. In chapter
9, raising the GET from 4 to 4.5 percent and cutting all income-tax rates costs households in
the bottom tenth about 1.8 percent of their income, while raising the top rate to 14 percent

Chapter 9 gives estimates of these combinations and of single tax changes. All of them include

A tax system can also be judged by how much its revenue rises and falls from one year to
the next. Hawaiʻi’s total state tax revenue is about as volatile as the average state’s. The Pew
Charitable Trusts scores each state by how far revenue growth in a typical year is from its
average. Over the 15 years ending in FY2024 Hawaiʻi scored 8.0 percentage points, the 15th
most volatile of the 50 states, against 6.4 for the 50 states as a whole (Robyn et al. 2026).
The GET is among the most volatile state sales taxes, the 6th of 45 on the same measure,
and the difference is concentrated in the pandemic years. Visitors pay about one-fifth of the
GET (chapter 4), and GET collections fell 10.4 percent in FY2021. In our own calculation
from Census data for FY2001 to FY2025 the GET ranks 9th of 45, and 24th when FY2020 to
FY2022 are left out, close to the median state. In those other years the individual income tax
is the larger source of swings. It accounts for 49 percent of the year-to-year variation in the
growth of total state taxes while raising about 30 percent of the revenue.80
Single large payments move revenue as well, and the state holds a reserve against swings of
both kinds. The estate tax brought in $405.8 million in FY2025, including a one-off receipt of
$315 million, against an average of $33.9 million a year from FY2013 to FY2024 (chapter 7).
The Emergency and Budget Reserve Fund held $1.57 billion at the end of FY2025, equal to
14.5 percent of general fund spending. The median state held 13.1 percent (NASBO 2025).
The balance was $325.8 million at the end of FY2022, and the increase since then includes a
one-off appropriation of $500 million from the general fund in FY2024.81

2. Individual income tax
Per resident, Hawaiʻi collected more individual income tax in FY2025 than all but seven states:
$2,294, about half again the national figure of $1,517. The individual income tax was 29.8
percent of state-government taxes, against 33.7 percent nationally.1 Collections per person
depend on incomes as well as on tax rates, and they include tax paid by nonresidents.
Most of the tax is paid by residents, though: Colby (2021) estimates that they pay 92.9 percent
of it.2 And among residents, average rates rise steeply with income. In TY2022, before Act 46,
residents with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income of $0 to under $10,000 paid on average −2.6
percent of their income in income tax, because refundable credits paid them more than they
owed, and residents with $500,000 or more paid about 8 percent. There were only 1,824
returns with $1 million or more, and they paid about a fifth of the $2.80 billion total (Figure
2.1).3
Hawaiʻi’s schedule has twelve brackets, which is more than any other state has, and in 2026
only California, at 13.3 percent, has a top rate above Hawaiʻi’s 11 percent (section 2.2). A
top rate says little about what a particular household pays, though. In UHERO calculations,
Hawaiʻi’s income tax is among the lowest in the country for a low-income family with two
children, because its refundable credits pay the family more than it owes, while in 2027 a
single person earning $1 million pays more state income tax in Hawaiʻi than in any other state
(Figure 2.4). The schedule is changing under laws that the legislature has already enacted. Act
46 of 2024 widens the brackets in steps and raises the single filer’s standard deduction, which
had been $2,200 since TY2013, to $12,000 by TY2031. The Council on Revenues estimates
that Act 46 reduces General Fund revenue by $596.6 million in FY2026 and by $1.45 billion in
FY2032.4 Act 24 of 2026 cuts the second and third rates and adds a new rate of 13 percent on
taxable income above $500,000 for single filers, so that at the top of the schedule Act 24 raises
the tax that Act 46 lowered. The 13 percent rate starts in tax year 2027 (TY2027), so no one

tax schedule such as Hawaiʻi’s and the federal income tax, the tax rate on marginal earnings
rises with income. Earnings tend to change from year to year more than spending does, since
households tend to absorb most temporary swings in their income by saving less in order to

sumption smoothing because the income tax bill falls with the household’s earnings in a bad
year, which leaves the household with a smaller income loss to absorb.8 The state also uses
the graduated rates to redistribute income. The GET has no such schedule: the GET rate on
a purchase does not depend on the buyer’s income, although exemptions and the refundable
food/excise credit change how its burden is shared. A resident earning $20,000 and a resident
earning $1 million pay the same GET rate at the register, but under the income tax the resi-
dent earning $20,000 can pay a negative rate, when refundable credits are larger than the tax
owed, and the resident earning $1 million pays 11 percent on their last dollars earned. Because
there exist negative rates for low income individuals, the tax brackets also determine the cost
of redistribution through the tax schedule (section 2.3). How much the state can redistribute
through the tax schedule depends on (1) how high the rates are and (2) how people’s income
responds to them.9 In our estimates, a small increase in all income-tax rates costs taxpayers
$1.42 for each dollar the state collects, of which $1.25 falls on residents and the rest on the
federal government and on nonresident and part-year filers, against about $1.03 for the GET,
which leaves out the cost of distorting what businesses buy (section 2.3 and Table 9.1).
The literature finds that low income individuals tend to respond differently to changes in the
income tax schedule than higher income individuals.10 Estimates of the size of these responses
differ from one study to the next (section 2.3).11 In our household model, when the share of
an extra dollar that a person keeps after tax rises by 1 percent, the income she reports rises
by about 0.3 percent at incomes near $26,000 and by about 1.3 percent at $450,000 and
above, in 2027 dollars. At high incomes a large part of what people earn is capital gains,
business income or retirement income, and these are either taxed under different rules or
easier to shift (sections 2.5 and 2.6). In TY2022, capital gains taxed at no more than 7.25
percent were 20.9 percent of taxable income for residents with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income
of $400,000 or more. At low incomes refundable credits return money to residents, but in
UHERO calculations a family of four with one earner and $40,000 in wages still loses about
three quarters of the next dollar it earns (section 2.4).
The baseline for all of our estimates in this chapter is the law as currently scheduled. That is,
we assume that Acts 46 and 24 take effect on schedule, and that the 2023 credit expansions
expire after TY2027. For each policy change we report the gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents
and then the totals that count everyone, including visitors and nonresidents (chapter 1). On
values we try to remain agnostic, and so for each policy change we say which value judgments
are embedded in it.
Our revenue estimates include changes in the income people report, using responses estimated
in other places (section 2.3 and Appendix D). Chapter 9 gives estimates for the income-tax
options we discuss.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 2. Individual income tax
2.1 The rate schedule under Acts 46 and 24
The income tax will have the same structure in 2031 that it has now. It will still have many
brackets, a personal exemption and a standard deduction, but most of the numbers in it will
have changed. The tables change almost every year under two laws, Act 46 of 2024 and Act
24 of 2026.12
The tax brackets apply to one’s taxable income. A filer first calculates their federal adjusted
gross income (AGI): income included under federal tax rules, less specified deductions, before
subtracting the standard deduction or itemized deductions. Hawaiʻi AGI begins with federal
AGI and then further adjusts according to state allowed exclusions. Among the largest differ-
ences are the exclusion of Social Security benefits and qualifying employer-funded pension
income that the federal government taxes. Hawaiʻi also excludes interest on US federal gov-
ernment obligations, but adds interest on other states’ municipal bonds.13 The standard de-
duction, or itemized deductions, and personal exemptions are then subtracted from Hawaiʻi
AGI to reach taxable income.
Suppose a single filer under age 65 earns $50,000 in wages in 2026 and has no other income
and no adjustments. Her federal AGI and her Hawaiʻi AGI are both $50,000. She subtracts
the $8,000 standard deduction and one $1,144 personal exemption, which leaves her with
$40,856 of taxable income, and it is this $40,856 that the bracket thresholds apply to. Of
course, neither AGI nor taxable income tells us everything about how much a household has
to spend. A retiree with a large exempt pension, for example, can spend that money even
though none of it appears in her AGI or in her taxable income.
Under Act 46, the bracket thresholds rise and the standard deduction grows. A filer who was
in the second bracket may now be in the first, and one who was in the third may be in the
second. Wider brackets mean a lower marginal rate at most incomes, and the larger deduction
($8,000 in TY2026, against $2,200 in TY2023) takes more off the taxable income of filers who
use it.
To understand these changes, we need to distinguish between two rates. The marginal rate
is the tax that a filer pays on her next dollar of taxable income, and it goes up each time
her taxable income crosses a threshold. The average rate is her whole tax bill divided by
her income. A change in the rate in one bracket changes the marginal rate for people in that
bracket and the average rate for everyone above it, since they also have income in that bracket.
So in some cases the state is changing taxes for everyone, and in others only for people above
a particular value of income.

(SLH 2026), approved May 21, 2026, §2 (rate schedules from TY2027: single filers pp. 29–36, joint
filers pp. 12–21, heads of household pp. 22–28) and §9 (start date). The thresholds for joint filers
are twice the thresholds for single filers, and the thresholds for heads of household are one and a
half times as high. There are twelve brackets in TY2027–28 and eleven from TY2029. We use the
signed act, because the online version of §235-51 has not been changed for Act 24 and still shows
the future schedules as they were before it.

$2,400 before, and the 8.25 percent rate begins at $175,000 instead of $48,000.17 From

reach 6.8 percent until $48,000.18
Act 24, signed in May 2026, changes the rates, starting in TY2027. It also keeps the 8.25, 9,
10 and 11 percent brackets at their TY2026 thresholds instead of the higher thresholds Act
46 scheduled for TY2027 and TY2029.19 It lowers the second rate from 3.2 to 2.5 percent
and the third from 5.5 to 5 percent, and it adds a 13 percent rate on taxable income above
$500,000 for single filers, $750,000 for heads of household and $1 million for joint filers.20
The 13 percent rate applies to the part of taxable income that falls into the top bracket, so a
single filer with $600,000 of taxable income pays it on $100,000.
Act 24 is an example of both kinds of change. In TY2027 the second rate applies to a single
filer’s taxable income between $14,400 and $19,200. Every single filer with more than $14,400
of taxable income has some income in that range, so lowering the second rate cuts tax for all
of them.21 The 13 percent rate, on the other hand, only changes the tax of single filers whose
taxable income is above $500,000. Compared with Act 46 alone, Act 24 also raises tax for
single filers with taxable income above about $191,000 in TY2027, and about $189,000 from

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 2. Individual income tax
starts at $325,000 and the 13 percent rate at $500,000.24
Figures 2.2 and 2.3 show the marginal and average rates that these laws produce, by Hawaiʻi
AGI, for a single filer and for a married couple filing jointly, at four points: before Act 46
(TY2023), in TY2026, in TY2027 with Act 24, and fully phased in (TY2031). For lower-middle
and middle incomes, the marginal rate at a given income never rises from one of these four
points to the next, and by TY2031 it is lower than it was before Act 46.
Hawaiʻi’s brackets and standard deduction are not indexed to inflation, and the lack of index-
ing has been a recurring reason for changing them. Unlike the federal standard deduction,
which is adjusted for inflation every year, Hawaiʻi’s is a fixed dollar amount that changes only
when a new law changes it. It was raised in 1987, 1989, 2007 and 2013, and Act 46 dou-
bled it in TY2024, eleven years after the last change.25 The 2020–22 Tax Review Commission,
reporting that nearly every earlier commission had recommended modernizing the standard
deduction and the personal exemption, recommended indexing the deduction, the exemption
and the brackets.26
A threshold that stays at the same dollar amount for years is worth less in each year that prices
rise. Income that has only kept up with prices is then taxed in higher brackets than before. The
long periods without adjustment are part of the reason Hawaiʻi had such high marginal rates
on low incomes before Act 46, when a single filer paid 5.5 percent from $4,800 of taxable
income.27
Inflation indexing is merely a decision to hold the tax system constant. If the thresholds are
updated every year to reflect inflation, a household whose income has only risen by the same
amount as the prices it pays for goods and services will not end up paying a higher share of
its income in taxes.28 By contrast, a decision not to index is a decision to increase taxes every
year in a somewhat arbitrary, and sometimes even unpredictable, fashion, since the size of the
increase depends on inflation.
Under current law the brackets stop moving after TY2029 and the standard deduction after

Different types of income are taxed differently, and someone with a very high income is not
necessarily taxed at the top rate. Long-term capital gains, for example, are taxed at no more
than 7.25 percent. A high-income filer who receives part of her income as gains pays less than
the top rate on that part.31
On the other hand, there are people who have substantial cash income but very little tax-
able income. Gains on assets they have not sold are not taxed until they sell, and qualifying
employer-funded pensions and Social Security are excluded from Hawaiʻi AGI, which took
$7.1 billion out of residents’ federally taxed income in TY2022.32 Section 2.5 takes up capital
gains, and section 2.6 pensions.
2.2 How Hawaiʻi’s income tax compares with other states
Whether Hawaiʻi has one of the highest income taxes in the country depends on the house-
hold, its income and the year, and the claim means little without saying which. In UHERO
calculations using PolicyEngine-US, Hawaiʻi’s income tax is among the lowest for a low-income
family with two children, because its refundable credits pay the family more than it owes. For
a single person, it is above or near the median state at every income shown from $40,000 up.
In 2027, a single person earning $1 million pays more state income tax in Hawaiʻi than in any
other state.

2.3 Progressivity: who pays, what it buys, and how people respond
About half of resident returns, 49 percent, had Hawaiʻi AGI below $40,000, and together they
paid 4.3 percent of the tax after credits.38 The 1,824 returns with $1 million or more paid
about a fifth of it, so even a modest change in the income this group reports would show up
in the state’s total. In our model, 78 percent of the revenue lost to lower reported income
after a small increase in all rates comes from the top tenth of tax units. At the top, people
might move, they might try to recharacterize some of their income as capital gains, or they
might leave income in the business and use business expenses to buy things that are basically
consumption, and we do not know if the responses estimated in other places will generalize
to Hawaiʻi.
What progressivity buys
Consider a filer whose income is well above a given bracket. She pays that bracket’s rate on all
of the income she has in it, as does everyone whose income reaches the bracket, but the rate has
no effect on what she keeps from the next dollar she earns. Her next dollar is taxed at the rate
of the bracket where her income ends (section 2.1). The lower brackets thus collect revenue
from her without discouraging her from earning more. A seller who price discriminates does
something similar. By charging different buyers different prices, the seller collects more and
loses fewer sales than it would at one price, and graduated rates do the same for the state. It
follows that the choice of rates matters for efficiency as well as for distribution. Up to a point, a
graduated schedule raises the same revenue as a single rate at a lower cost, and it puts more of
the burden on higher incomes as it does so.39 There is still a limit to how far the top rate can go,
because beyond some rate a further increase collects less revenue. On our estimates that rate
is about 22 percent for Hawaiʻi, and about 30 percent if no high-income household leaves the
state in response.40 Whether a dollar of revenue costs less to raise through the income tax than
through the GET depends on the two tax bases, on how people respond to each tax and on
who ultimately bears each tax (Appendix D). In our estimates, a small increase costs taxpayers
about $1.00 per net dollar for the bottom three rates, $1.42 for all rates and $2.02 for the
top rate ($1.28 and $1.42 for all rates and the top rate if no high-income household leaves
the state in response), against about $1.03 for the GET, which leaves out the cost of distorting
what businesses buy (Table 9.1). Raising the GET to 4.5 percent and cutting all income-tax
38 DOTAX (2024b), p. 27, and Table A-8, p. 51. The 49 percent is the 311,198 returns below $40,000
out of all 635,117 resident returns, and both counts include returns with losses. The income share
uses only the income on returns with nonnegative income.
39 Saez (2001), section 4. The top bracket is different because it has no ceiling, so everyone who pays
the top rate also faces it on their next dollar.
40 UHERO calculation using the formula in Appendix D and the responses in our household model at
the current top rate: lower reported income, the response of pass-through businesses, the GET lost
on spending and households that leave the state. The formula comes from Saez (2001), section 3.
The rate depends on how strongly the income reported at the top responds to the rate and on how
far the incomes in the top bracket lie above the threshold. The rate is only an outer limit on the top
rate: it is the rate at which revenue is highest and tells us nothing about efficiency, and a state would
want to set its rate further below that limit the more it values a dollar in the hands of people at the
top.

rates by about 27 percent would reduce what taxpayers pay by about $234 million at about
the same state revenue, but as a share of income it would take the most from the households
with the lowest incomes (Table 9.2).
A single rate is not obviously preferable to graduated rates. Saez (2001) and Diamond and
Saez (2011) find that the best schedule has rates that fall and then rise with income, although
Mirrlees (1971) and Mankiw et al. (2009) found schedules close to a single rate under some
assumptions. A single rate with an exemption is also still progressive, because a household’s
tax rises as a share of its income as income rises.41
Residents pay 92.9 percent of the income tax, while part of the GET and the TAT is paid by
visitors. In our estimates, a 1 percent increase in all rates would raise about $29.7 million from
all filers, residents and nonresident and part-year filers together, before anyone responds.42
Even so, the income tax is the only one of the three taxes that can depend on a household’s
income, and so it is where the state decides how the burden is shared among residents. It is
also where the state can give money back, since a tax aimed at visitors that ends up falling
partly on residents can be returned to them through refundable income tax credits, though
the credits do this imperfectly (section 2.4).
As a default, we assume throughout this chapter that the tax on a return is borne by the person
who files it.43
41 Fifteen states tax income at a single rate, and seven of them enacted it between 2021 and 2025 (Fritts
and Loughead 2026; Walczak and Loughead 2025). On the theory, see Mirrlees (1971), pp. 206–
207; Mankiw et al. (2009), Lesson 3; Saez (2001), pp. 223 and 225–226; and Diamond and Saez
(2011), introduction. In one case in Saez (2001, pp. 224–225 and Table 2), the income-weighted
average marginal rate is 51 percent under the best nonlinear schedule and 61 percent under the
best single rate. We do not think the reforms that have been studied settle the question. After Russia
replaced rates of 12, 20 and 30 percent with a 13 percent rate in 2001, real revenue from the tax
rose by more than 25 percent in 2001. Gorodnichenko et al. (2009) find that the households whose
rates fell responded more by complying with the tax than by changing their real activity, and Keen
et al. (2008) think the rise in receipts is explained by the broader economic recovery. Keen et al.
(2008, p. 741) also find that in none of the countries they study did a flat-tax rate cut pay for itself.
We know of no study that isolates the effect of a US state’s move to a single rate.
42 The nonresident part, about $1.6 million, applies the 2027 rates to the TY2022 nonresident base
and includes no responses.
43 Part of the tax may in fact be borne by someone other than the filer. Take high earners who can move
easily between states. If a state raises its top rate, they may leave unless their pay before tax goes
up. To the extent that it does, the tax is borne by their employers, by other workers or by customers.
The evidence for US states points both ways. Feldstein and Wrobel (1998, abstract) use the Current
Population Survey for 1983 and 1989 and estimate that gross wages adjust within a few years to
offset differences in states’ tax progressivity. If that is right, states cannot redistribute through the
income tax. Leigh (2008, abstract and pp. 97 and 100–101) builds an index of each state’s tax
redistribution from its tax rules for 1977 to 2002, combines it with the Current Population Survey,
and finds that gross wages do not adjust enough to offset more redistributive taxes, so those taxes
make after-tax hourly wages more equal. The study measures hourly wages, not family incomes.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 2. Individual income tax
Responses at the top
High-income residents who face a higher rate might move, although only 2.4 percent of US
millionaires moved between states each year in 1999–2011, against 2.9 percent of all filers.44
They might recharacterize some of their income as long-term capital gains, because from
TY2027 a single filer pays 13 percent on ordinary taxable income above $500,000 and no
more than 7.25 percent on those gains (section 2.5).
Or they might leave income in a business and have the business pay for things that are basically
personal consumption, which is tax evasion when someone knowingly deducts such purchases
as business expenses.45
In the US, top income taxpayers receive a lot of their income from pass-through businesses,
S corporations and partnerships, whose profits generally pass through to their owners’ tax
returns, so much of the business income Hawaiʻi taxes is taxed through the individual income
tax. The IRS groups partnership and S corporation income with other income that has some
third-party reporting. That group has more misreporting than wages, which also face with-
holding.46 Section 2.6 returns to business income.
For a single person earning $1 million, Act 24’s 13 percent rate adds about $11,600 a year
to the tax cost of remaining a resident (section 2.2). That is the amount a person considers
in deciding whether to stay. In deciding whether to earn or report another dollar, the same
person looks only at the rate on that dollar. Below the top bracket, a higher rate raises the
tax bill of every filer with income above the bracket’s threshold, and so gives all of those filers
a larger reason to leave, but it changes the tax on the next dollar only for the filers whose
income ends inside the bracket. The top bracket has no ceiling, so there these are the same
filers.
There is no threshold rate below which people do not respond. Some people are always close
to indifferent between working a little more and a little less, or between living in Hawaiʻi
and living somewhere else, and for those people even a small tax increase can change the
decision. To estimate how large the responses are, researchers study how the income that
people report changed after past tax reforms. Most estimates come from the United States and
Scandinavia.47 Saez et al. (2012) put the best available estimates of the long-run response
between 0.12 and 0.40: when the share of each extra dollar that a person keeps goes up

by 1 percent, reported income goes up by 0.12 to 0.40 percent.48 In California, most of the
revenue lost to responses after the 2012 increase came from people who stayed and reported
less (section 1.6),49 and Rauh and Shyu (2024) estimate that additional departures removed
0.8 percent of top-bracket income in 2013.50
Responses measured elsewhere may not carry over to Hawaiʻi, because for most residents
leaving Hawaiʻi means moving across an ocean, away from family and friends, and where
leaving is harder, fewer people leave in response to a given tax increase. There is good reason
to expect migration responses to differ from one state to another, since people live where they
do partly for the amenities of the place.51 For California, the estimates from its own 2012
change are a reasonable basis for asking what its top rate should be. Hawaiʻi’s geography and
amenities can lead to different migration responses.52 In our household model the working
assumption for the long-run migration response of the top 1 percent is 1.0, with a response
a quarter as large for other households in the top tenth of income. Neither is estimated from
Hawaiʻi data. In the model a tax increase leads some of these households to leave, and a tax
cut keeps some who would otherwise have left.53
Hawaiʻi’s own changes to its top rates give a rough first look, as a description rather than
an estimate of a response. The 9, 10 and 11 percent brackets applied from TY2009 through
TY2015, lapsed for TY2016 and TY2017, and returned in TY2018. In IRS data on returns
filed from Hawaiʻi, returns with federal AGI of $1 million or more reported 6.5 percent of the
state’s AGI in 2015, 6.7 and 7.6 percent in 2016 and 2017, and 6.4 and 6.5 percent in 2018
and 2019. Across the US the same class reported 14.3, 13.3, 15.1, 15.3 and 14.5 percent.
Hawaiʻi’s top share rose from about 45 to about 50 percent of the national share while the
brackets were gone, and fell back to 42 percent in 2018, when they returned, and 45 percent
in 2019. We do not see the same pattern in moves out of the state. Among filers with federal

AGI of $200,000 or more who had filed from Hawaiʻi the year before, 3.3 percent filed from
another state between TY2014 and TY2015. The share was 4.5 percent between TY2015 and
TY2016, 3.4 percent between TY2016 and TY2017, and 3.3 and 3.2 percent in the two years
after the brackets returned. Neither set of numbers should be read as the effect of the brackets,
since both times other things were changing for high-income filers as well. When the brackets
were introduced in 2009, the economy was in a recession. When they returned in 2018, the
federal cap on the deduction for state and local taxes took effect in the same year. In between,
federal tax changes in 2012 and 2013 led people to shift income from one year to another,
and the top share jumps in 2012 both in Hawaiʻi and nationally. There is also a problem with
any income class that has fixed dollar boundaries: more returns fall into it each year as prices
and asset values rise. It is for this reason that we compare Hawaiʻi’s shares with the national
ones.54
Land cannot leave the state when its tax goes up, but people can leave when the income tax
does. Some of those who stay may also earn less, because they choose to work less or turn
down a raise. When a resident does leave, Hawaiʻi can still tax the income that person receives
from Hawaiʻi sources but nothing else, and the GET on what the person would have spent here
is lost too. If the person who leaves owns a local business, then some of the investment in that
business may leave as well.55
An estimate of the revenue from a higher top rate that assumes no one changes their behavior
will thus be too high. Some of the income that would be taxed at the higher rate will no longer
be reported in Hawaiʻi.
To get a sense of how large these responses might be, we use our household model to estimate
what would happen if the top rate were raised from 13 to 14 percent in 2027, for every filing
status. If no one changed their behavior, this would raise about $53.8 million. But in the model,
the filers who pay the higher rate report less income, some high-income households leave the
state, and because households have less take-home pay they spend less, so GET revenue falls
as well. The net gain is then about $21.0 million, about 61 percent less than the estimate with
no change in behavior, and each dollar of net revenue costs filers about $2.56, of which $2.45
falls on residents and $0.11 on nonresident and part-year filers. Departures account for about
$11 million of the lost revenue. If no household left the state, the net gain would be about

The size of the net gain depends on the elasticity we assume. In the model, the elasticity of
reported taxable income rises with income, following the estimates of Kumar and Liang (2020).
It is about 0.3 at incomes near $26,000 and rises to about 1.3 at $450,000 and above, in 2027
dollars.58 An elasticity of 1.3 means that the income a person reports rises by 1.3 percent
when the share of an extra dollar she keeps after tax rises by 1 percent. If the elasticity at the
top is higher, then filers in the top bracket cut the income they report by more when the rate
goes up, and the state gains less. To see how much this matters, we also run the model with a
constant elasticity of 0.3 at every income. A small increase in the top rate then loses about 30
percent of its revenue to responses of all kinds, where our main estimate loses 50 percent (9
percent and 29 percent if no household leaves the state). People in the model can also start
or stop working in response to the tax, but at these incomes that makes little difference to the
result. We do not include in either estimate the spillovers from taxing business income onto
investment and employment.

2.4 Refundable credits, effective marginal rates and claiming
A refundable credit is paid in full even when it is larger than the income tax a household
owes, and thus is available to households that owe no tax at all, unlike a cut in tax rates,
which lowers the tax of households that owe some but gives nothing to a household that owes
none.60 Refundable credits are one way for the state to use the income tax to compensate
residents, or to direct more assistance to people with low incomes, though at present the
income tax system does both imperfectly, for the reasons discussed in this section.
One reason is what happens to a family’s credits as it earns more. The credits shrink, and so
do its federal benefits, and between them a family of four with one earner can lose about four
fifths of its next dollar. Some of the credits do not shrink gradually at all. They are withdrawn
in steps, so a family whose income passes a cutoff loses a fixed amount of credit all at once.
A family of four, for example, loses $280 of food/excise credit at $60,000, where a phase-out
would have taken the credit away a little at a time. Many people who are eligible for the
credits never claim them, and most of these are people who do not file a tax return at all.
Those who do claim the credits are paid only once a year. In our estimates, raising the top
rate to 14 percent would pay for food/excise credits about 40 percent larger. Taxpayers as
a group would pay about $33 million more, and households in the lowest tenth by income
would gain about $50 a year on average (Table 9.2).
The credits
Hawaiʻi has four refundable credits aimed at lower-income residents. The earned income tax
credit (EITC) is 40 percent of the federal credit from TY2023 through TY2027. The food/excise
credit pays a fixed amount per person, which is larger for households with lower federal ad-
justed gross income: up to $220 per person below $15,000, with the credit ending at $40,000
for single filers and at $60,000 for others. A household with no income at all can still claim
it. The dependent care credit pays 25 to 15 percent of child care and other dependent care
expenses, depending on Hawaiʻi AGI. The renters’ credit is for households with Hawaiʻi ad-
