to residents and $0.11 to nonresident and part-year filers.
60 HRS §§235-55.6, 235-55.75 and 235-55.85 (refundable credits, paid even when no tax is owed). A

food/excise credit returns to its smaller pre-2023 schedule with lower income limits, and the
dependent care credit’s expense limits fall from $10,000 and $20,000 to $2,400 and $4,800.63
A smaller credit slightly raises the reward to working and reporting income, so a cut brings
in a little more than its amount before responses. In our estimates, a 1 percent cut in the
food/excise credit costs taxpayers $1.00 for each dollar the state collects (Table 9.1).
If the three credits were already at their post-2027 levels in 2027, filers would receive $75.4
million less and the state would collect $74.4 million more.64
Effective marginal rates and cliffs
When a family earns an additional dollar, it does not get to keep all of it. Part of the dollar
is paid in taxes, and the family’s credits and benefits are also reduced because its income
is higher. The share of the additional dollar that the family loses in these two ways is the
effective marginal rate. Whether the family earns more depends on the effective marginal
rate as a whole. The income tax is only one part of it. The phase-out of the EITC and the
phase-out of SNAP benefits are not so different from each other, and we put them in the same
calculation.
Figure 2.5 shows these rates for a family of four with one earner and two children, averaged
over the next $1,000 of wages at eight levels of wages. In 2026, at $40,000, federal and state
taxes together with the reduction in the federal EITC and SNAP food assistance take about
76 cents of the next dollar. Most of that comes from federal programs: the gradual reduction
in the federal EITC takes 21 cents and SNAP 36 cents, while Hawaiʻi’s income tax, including
the reduction in its own EITC, takes about 12 cents and federal income and payroll taxes the

rise, so the lower rate does not mean the family is better off.
Some of the credits end in steps instead of phasing out, and each step is a cliff. A cliff occurs
when a large benefit is suddenly withdrawn when income exceeds some threshold, even if
by only $1. A family of four filing jointly loses $280 of food/excise credit when its federal
adjusted gross income reaches $60,000, and smaller amounts at each lower step.67 For the
family in Figure 2.5 that loss comes exactly at $60,000, so it is not in the bar for the next
$1,000. A different family, with two earners, two children in care and $20,000 of child care
expenses, can lose up to $1,000 of dependent care credit just above $50,000 of Hawaiʻi AGI.
The renters’ credit ends entirely at $30,000 of Hawaiʻi AGI.
The problem with these credits is not that they fall with income. This ensures that the credits
provide cost-effective assistance to the families they are aimed at: if the state paid the full

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 2. Individual income tax
food/excise credit to every family of four, whatever its income, its cost would be much greater.
Rather, the problem is the way in which the credits are reduced: via cliffs.
For a family whose only income is wages, with no adjustments to AGI, the food/excise credit
cliff leaves it with less to live on at $60,000 of wages than at $59,999, and it is unlikely that
most people think the family with one dollar more of income is $280 less deserving. That is a
cost in fairness, which a cliff carries even if no one changes what they do because of it.
Furthermore, cliffs do change what families do: researchers interviewing low-income families
in Colorado and California who faced a cliff in a child care subsidy found that many took
steps to keep their income below it, and some turned down extra hours or even turned down
raises.68 A self-employed parent can do it on paper, for example by rescheduling some business
expenses so they occur this year instead of next year, and a study of the federal EITC finds
that the reported incomes of people who report their own income to the IRS pile up at the
point where the credit reaches its maximum, while those of wage earners do not.69
A cliff can be removed without giving up targeting, by reducing the credit by a fraction of
each additional dollar earned. To keep the cost the same, the state has to start the reduction
below the old cutoff, which takes money from families there; any extension above the cutoff,
which gives money to families above it, must be paid for by starting earlier still. In either case,
families whose income is in the range where the credit is now being reduced lose part of the
credit with every additional dollar they earn, so they keep less of an additional dollar than
before. The loss from any one credit may be small, but when several credits are being reduced
over the same range of income, a family may be left with very little of an additional dollar
that it earns, and this is what produces the rates in Figure 2.5.
The EITC and the dependent care credit can pull a family’s second earner in opposite direc-
tions, which affects the labor force participation of second earners. The EITC depends on the
couple’s combined earnings, so when a second job puts those earnings in the credit’s phase-out
range, the credit shrinks. After federal EITC expansions from 1984 to 1996, participation in
the labor force fell by an estimated 1.1 percentage points among married women in couples
with less than twelve years of schooling.70 This is reversed for the dependent care credit: a
couple’s eligible expenses are limited by the lower earner’s earnings, so the credit rises when
the second earner works. US evidence finds that the federal dependent care credit increases
the use of paid child care, and that it can bring more married mothers into work.71
For single mothers, earlier studies found that EITC expansions brought more of them into
work, and a more recent reanalysis of EITC reforms questions how general those findings

Eligible and claimed
A credit that is not claimed does not help. In TY2021, Moore and Karacaovali (2024) estimated
that about 158,000 eligible people did not receive the food/excise credit, either by claiming it
themselves or by being claimed by someone else, and that they missed about $15.3 million of
the roughly $42 million the credit was worth that year.74 About 17,800 of them were on tax
returns that did not claim the credit, and they missed about $1.3 million. The other 140,000
or so were people who did not file a state tax return at all, most of whom were likely in very
low-income households, since otherwise they would have to file.
Existing filers can claim the food/excise credit on their N-11 return, yet some do not claim the
benefit.75 For the federal EITC, which the state EITC builds on, the IRS estimates that 83.3
percent of eligible Hawaiʻi individuals and families, including those who did not file, claimed
it in TY2022. We estimate that about 4 percent of federal EITC claimants do not claim the
state credit.76

not claimed the EITC raised the share who responded from about 14 to 23 percent.78 DOTAX’s
simplified N-11SF form is aimed at the people who do not file. The form can only be used by
filers whose federal and Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income are both exactly zero. A household
that receives just $1 of income under either measure has to use a different form, even though
it is eligible for the same credit amount and owes no income tax.79 The credits are also paid
once a year, while households pay for food and rent every month, a problem that chapter 3
discusses for the grocery exemption. A household that receives a credit once a year has to
save some of it, or borrow, if the credit is to help with expenses in the other months, and not
every household can. In one US study, EITC recipients spent about 30 cents of each dollar of
their refund at retailers within two weeks of receiving it.80 The GET is paid at the register
all year. A family living paycheck to paycheck has less to spend in every month before the
household receives its refund, even if the credit eventually gives back what the family paid
in GET. Refunds claimed on Form N-11SF are also issued only as paper checks, without the
option of direct deposit.81
In California, outreach alone did little to increase claiming. Six large experiments sent letters
and text messages about the EITC to low-income households, many of whom had not filed
a return the year before. The messages did not increase filing or EITC claims. Experiments
that combined outreach with a simpler way to file did a little better, raising take-up of other
tax-based payments by 0.14 to 2 percentage points.82 It may also be necessary to send a
reminder every year. In one study, an IRS reminder raised filing in the year it was sent but
not in the following year, and filing went up again when a new reminder was sent.83 However,
the effects can last for years once people are drawn into filing. Low-income people who filed
to get a federal stimulus payment went on to claim more EITC and earn more, and were less
likely to be poor in later years.84

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 2. Individual income tax
2.5 Capital gains
A person who buys a stock or another asset and sells it for more has a capital gain. In Hawaiʻi,
the tax on a long-term gain is the person’s marginal income tax rate or 7.25 percent, whichever
is lower,85 and federal tax on capital gains comes on top of that. Other states treat gains
differently.86 Capital gains are generally taxed only when the asset is sold.
Not all income is tied to a person’s location in the same way. Tax on capital gains is much
easier to avoid than tax on wages, for two reasons. Gains held until death escape income tax
through the step-up in basis. And a resident can move, and once the move is genuine, sell in
a state that does not tax capital gains.
Capital gains are heavily concentrated at the top of the income distribution, and the higher a
filer’s income, the greater the share of capital gains in taxable income. In TY2022 residents
reported $3.0 billion of gains eligible for the cap on 43,443 returns, 7.4 percent of resident
taxable income, and the 4,188 returns with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income of $400,000 or
more reported $2.21 billion of it, about 74 percent. For those residents, gains eligible for the
cap were 20.9 percent of taxable income, and from TY2027 the gap between the top rate and
the cap is 5.75 points.87
These numbers count only the gains on which filers compute the alternative tax under the
cap. Filers whose top rate is below 7.25 percent gain nothing from that calculation and have
no reason to make it.88 So the Hawaiʻi data do not show how much in capital gains residents
realize in total.
Income that can be characterized as a capital gain is taxed much more lightly at the top. An
owner’s work building up a business can be reflected in a capital gain when the business is
sold. Carried interest, the share of an investment fund’s profits paid to the people who manage
it, is another example, since it can be taxed as a capital gain under partnership rules.89 Gains
are part of the reason the average effective tax rates for this income group stayed two to three
percentage points below the 11 percent marginal rate.90 For a single filer in TY2027 with
$700,000 of taxable income, the $200,000 above $500,000 is taxed at 13 percent ($26,000)
if it is wages, and at 7.25 percent ($14,500) if it is a long-term gain (total tax about $70,400
85 HRS §235-51(f). Strictly, the tax is limited by an alternative calculation: ordinary tax on the larger
of taxable income less net capital gain and the income taxed below 7.25 percent, plus 7.25 percent
of the rest. It applies to net capital gain (net long-term gain less net short-term loss), not to sale
proceeds.
86 California Franchise Tax Board (n.d.); N.Y. Tax Law §612 (New York income starts from federal

Minnesota adds 1 percent on net investment income above $1 million. Massachusetts taxes most
long-term gains at its 5 percent rate and short-term gains at 8.5 percent, plus a 4 percent surtax on
very high incomes.

against $58,900, or 10.1 against 8.4 percent of taxable income).91
Because gains are taxed only when they are realized, people can choose when to pay. A
prospective capital-gains tax increase can bring asset sales forward and raise revenue before
it takes effect.92 That is important for a tax authority trying to forecast revenue, because it
also means revenue in the year after the reform might be substantially lower than expected,
and not just because the higher rate discourages people from having capital gains.
Before the 1986 federal increase, people sold earlier: a person can sell an asset a year early,
pay tax at the old rate and even buy the same asset back. There is also a lock-in effect: people
respond to a higher rate by delaying the sale of assets they would otherwise sell. The short-run
response in timing is much larger than the lasting one,93 and the lasting response is smaller
but real. Six to ten years after a state changes its tax rate, realized gains per resident fall by
about 0.4 percent for each 1 percent increase in the combined federal and state tax rate on
gains, leaving aside moves, and by about 1 percent once moves to other states are counted.94
These studies differ in how much of the response they attribute to a change in the timing of
sales, and how much to a change in the amount sold over longer periods.95 Our estimates use
−0.4, and a small increase in the 7.25 percent rate then costs taxpayers $1.35 for each dollar
the state collects, of which $1.08 falls on residents and $0.27 on nonresident and part-year
filers, or $1.20 if no high-income household leaves the state in response.96

$500,000. The tax on the first $500,000 is about $44,400; totals are rounded and ignore the small
rounding in the tables in the statute. This is an example.

Inflation, the incentive to hold on to assets, and corporate income tax already paid all provide
reasons to tax gains at a lower rate. Arguments against include who benefits (three quarters
of residents’ gains eligible for Hawaiʻi’s cap went to residents with Hawaiʻi AGI of $400,000
or more) and the reward for relabeling income and using arrangements that reduce or avoid
tax.97
Gains on Hawaiʻi real estate are different from gains on stocks. Nonresidents owe Hawaiʻi
income tax on them, and the buyer holds back 7.25 percent of the sale price toward that
tax.98 Nonresident and part-year returns reported $1.43 billion of gains eligible for the cap in
TY2022, about a third of all such gains, so a change in the rate on gains reaches nonresident
filers more than the rest of the income tax does. How much of that is Hawaiʻi real estate has
not been counted.
Opportunity zones are a further way to defer tax on a gain and to avoid tax on later appreci-
ation. Federal law lets an investor defer tax on a capital gain by reinvesting it in a qualified
opportunity fund, which must hold most of its assets in property in census tracts designated
as opportunity zones. If she holds the new investment for at least ten years, she can elect to
exclude its appreciation from tax. The zones are tracts nominated by each governor, almost
all of them low-income tracts. Hawaiʻi follows these rules only for zones designated by its own
governor, so a gain reinvested in a zone in another state is deferred for federal tax but not
for Hawaiʻi tax.99 Twenty-five tracts were designated in 2018. The 2025 federal act made the
program permanent, and a new set of 25 tracts was designated for 2027 through 2036, 12
of which were also in the first set.100 Treasury tax data show $310 million of property held
through these funds in Hawaiʻi zones through TY2024, with $260 million of it in rural zones
and 77 percent in the fifth of Hawaiʻi zones with the highest incomes (Coyne and Johnson
2026).101 The investors have high incomes. Nationally, the typical individual investor had
adjusted gross income of $738,000 in 2024, against $54,000 for individual filers as a whole.

Evidence on the zones designated in 2018 shows little measured gain for the people living
in them. Chen et al. (2023b) rule out an effect on house-price growth above 0.5 percentage
points, and Freedman et al. (2023) find little or no effect on the employment, earnings or
poverty of zone residents in the first year and a half. Zone businesses added jobs (Arefeva et
al. 2025), but Freedman et al. (2025) estimate that fewer than one in eight of the new jobs
went to zone residents, that about 84 percent were offset by job losses in adjacent low-income
tracts, and that later gains in residents’ employment likely reflect people who moved in. No
estimate exists of what following the federal rules costs Hawaiʻi.102
Under the step-up in basis, gains not realized before death are never taxed as income. If a
person built up a large gain on publicly traded shares over her life and her estate sells some
of them after her death, no one pays capital gains tax on the gain accrued during her life.103
Chapter 7 takes this up from the estate tax side (section 7.4, “Gains at death”).
Two revenue-neutral combinations in Appendix I change how gains are taxed. One taxes long-
term gains as ordinary income and lowers the top income-tax rate, and the other exempts
them and raises the top rate. In our estimates, taxing gains as ordinary income would allow
the top rate to fall to about 10.1 percent at the same state revenue, and taxpayers as a group
would pay about $65 million less. The changes would fall almost entirely on the top tenth.
Exempting gains would require a top rate of about 25.7 percent, and taxpayers would pay
about $402 million more. Both estimates are more uncertain than the combinations in section
9.7, because each is balanced by extrapolating beyond the model runs. A 1 percent increase
in the 7.25 percent rate on gains would raise $3.1 million before responses and $2.3 million
after.
2.6 Business income and retirement income
Business profits that pass through to their owners are taxed on the owners’ returns, and chapter
6 takes up most of how to tax them. Pension income and Social Security are mostly untaxed.
Business income on owners’ returns
Self-employed people report business profits on their own tax returns, and S corporations
and partnerships pass their profits through to their owners, who pay the individual income
tax on them. In the US much of that income goes to people at the top (section 2.3). Since
TY2023, partnerships and S corporations can instead elect to have the business pay Hawaiʻi
tax itself, now at 9 percent, with a credit for the owners.104 Business income is mixed in with
other forms of income on these returns. Linking pass-through income to owners’ total incomes
would help show who a higher top rate would affect. In our household model, pass-through
income responds to tax rates as other reported income does, and part of the tax on owners falls

Pensions and Social Security
Hawaiʻi excludes qualifying public and employer-funded pension income, as well as Social Se-
curity, from Hawaiʻi AGI. In TY2022 residents subtracted $4.74 billion of pension income and
$2.33 billion of Social Security from the income they reported on their federal returns.106 With-
drawals from individual retirement accounts (IRAs) and employer retirement savings plans
known as 401(k) plans are generally taxed when they come from employees’ own contribu-
tions made before tax and the earnings on them.107 Most states do not tax Social Security
either. Pensions are a different matter: California, Oregon and Massachusetts generally tax
pensions paid for by private employers.108
Commissions recommended taxing pension income in 1995–97, 2001–03, 2005–07, 2015–17
and 2020–22.109 The most recent one kept Social Security exempt. For contributory pensions,
such as those of the Employees’ Retirement System, whose members had already paid tax on
their contributions, it would have excluded the first $25,000 a year per person for 25 years,
and its report put the revenue from that design at $50 million in the first year.110
A retiree with a large exempt employer pension can have low Hawaiʻi AGI despite substantial
income to spend, while a worker receiving the same amount in wages pays tax on most of
it, and the exclusion is worth more to people with larger pensions. On the other hand, many
retirees decided how much to save and where to retire with the current rules in place. Retirees
also have no job tying them to Hawaiʻi, and so one might expect some to leave if their pensions

2.7 Changes to the income tax
A change to the income tax affects Hawaiʻi residents, and it affects the totals that count every-
one, visitors and nonresidents included. In both cases the person who files a return need not
be the person who bears the change in the tax (chapter 1). We take no position in this chapter
on how much weight the state should give to residents and how much to everyone else, since
that is a policy choice.
Suppose the state makes a tax cut at the top that is worth $10,000 to the people who receive
it, and that these taxpayers earn and report more income as a result. The tax cut will then cost
the state less than $10,000 in revenue. In our estimates, when the top rate is cut from 13 to
about 10.1 percent and long-term gains are taxed as ordinary income, the first of the capital-
gains pair in section 2.5, taxpayers as a group pay about $65 million less at the same state
revenue, because people report more income at the lower rate (Appendix I). This estimate
counts fewer departures of high-income households at the lower rate, but no households that
would move to Hawaiʻi. What the state gets back in this way is the budget payback of chapter
1. We give figures of this kind for taxpayers across the income distribution. Budget payback
constrains the state whatever its views about equity, because people change their behavior
when tax policy changes regardless of those views. Evidence like this helps the state work
out how to reach its objectives, whatever they are, but what the objectives should be is not
a question it can answer. Several of the revenue-neutral combinations in chapter 9 (section
9.7) involve the income tax. In some of them a higher top rate is paired with lower rates at
the bottom, or with a larger food/excise credit. Table 9.2 gives estimates of what taxpayers
as a group would pay at the same state revenue: about $31 million more for a 14 percent top
rate with lower bottom rates and $33 million more for a 14 percent top rate with a larger
food/excise credit. The capital-gains pairs discussed in section 2.5 are in Appendix I: about
$65 million less when gains are taxed as ordinary income with a lower top rate, and $402
million more when gains are exempt and the top rate is higher. The options discussed in this
chapter are listed in Table 2.2.
Table 2.2. Options for the income tax

the two-year retirement hazard by 2.0 percentage points, from a base of 15 percent. There is no
comparable estimate for Hawaiʻi’s pension exclusion.

3. General excise tax
The GET is Hawaiʻi’s largest tax. Together with use taxes, it raised $4.63 billion in FY2025, or
40.4 percent of the tax collections reported by DOTAX.1
The GET raises this revenue at a low rate: 4 percent of revenue on most sales to final customers,
plus a county surcharge of 0.5 percent. As a share of the price before tax—what consumers
usually see on their receipts—the combined rate is 4.712 percent, compared with 7.68 to 9.57
percent in California, Washington, Nevada and New Mexico. In FY2024, however, state and
local governments in Hawaiʻi collected $3,670 per person from general sales and gross receipts
taxes, more than twice the US average. Only Washington collected more (Table 3.1 and Figure
3.1).2
Much of the reason is the breadth of the tax base. The GET applies to services as well as
goods, and to sales between businesses as well as sales to households. It features few of the
exemptions for necessities that are common in other states, taxing transactions such as grocery
purchases and residential rents. The Tax Foundation has calculated that the base implied by
revenue is 116 percent of residents’ personal income, the broadest of any state with a general
sales tax; in the median state it is 36 percent.3
The ratio can exceed 100 percent because much of what the GET taxes is not residents’ con-
sumption. In our household model, the GET is charged on about 60 percent of what Hawaiʻi
residents spend on consumption in 2027. The share is similar at every income level, between

taxes administered by other agencies.
2 Tax Foundation (2026c), table of rates as of July 1, 2026; US Census Bureau (2026a), as compiled
in Lincoln Institute of Land Policy (n.d.), table 241, for collections per resident; US Census Bureau

ranking is our calculation for the 50 states and DC. Census figures include the county surcharges
in Hawaiʻi and some taxes on business gross receipts in other states. These figures do not estimate
the GET paid by the average household. The table’s 35.9 percent is the share of state and county
tax collections in FY2024; the 40.4 percent above covers different collections reported by DOTAX in
FY2025.

5 A separate calculation from Bureau of Economic Analysis (2026) data on consumer spending by
state, table SAPCE4, gives a similar share. In 2024 the GET was charged at the 4 percent retail rate
on about 62 percent of Hawaiʻi residents’ market consumption, with a range of 55 to 69 percent.
Market consumption is what residents pay for. It excludes the items that the accounts impute with
no payment, chiefly the rent that homeowners are counted as paying to themselves. The accounts
assign spending to the state where the consumer lives, so purchases by visitors from other states
are already excluded (Bureau of Economic Analysis 2024, p. 4), and we remove the spending of
foreign visitors. We classify each spending category by how the GET treats it, with low and high
values where the taxable share is a matter of judgment, as for physician services and rent; these
values give the range. The shares for 2023 and 2025 are within 0.3 percentage points of those for
2024. The excluded items are the imputed rent of owner-occupied housing ($11.2 billion), financial
services furnished without payment, the spending of nonprofit institutions serving households, and
food furnished to employees; together they are 19.8 percent of total consumption. With these items
left in, the GET applied to about 49 percent of total consumption in 2024, with a range of 44 to
55 percent. Most hospitals are nonprofit or run by the state and are exempt (HRS §237-23(a)(6)).
Insurers and utilities pay separate taxes on their receipts in place of the GET (HRS §§237-23(a)(1),
237-29.7 and 239-2), and counting those taxes raises the share to about 67 percent of market
consumption and 54 percent of total consumption. Federal law bars states from taxing air fares (49
U.S.C. §40116(b)). Purchases with Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program benefits (benefits
issued equaled about a tenth of grocery spending in 2024; US Department of Agriculture n.d.) and
prescription drugs are also exempt (HRS §237-24.3(5) and (6)). We also subtract from the taxed
purchases an assumed $1 billion that residents spend outside Hawaiʻi in taxable categories ($0.5
billion to $2 billion for the range). This spending still counts in both market and total consumption.
The exemption for care paid for by Medicare, Medicaid and TRICARE, which took effect in January
2026, lowers the share to about 59 percent of market consumption, with a range of 53 to 65 percent,
and to about 47 percent of total consumption, with a range of 42 to 52 percent. The exemption is in
Act 47 (SLH 2024), §4, and HRS §237-24.3(12); we size it with the Medicare and Medicaid shares
of health spending in Hawaiʻi in 2020 (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2022) and an
assumed addition for TRICARE. Airi and Sammartino (2021), p. 7, put the share at 88 percent for
2018, on a base close to our market consumption. Their method classifies categories of goods and
services and does not apply exemptions that depend on who the seller is, such as those for nonprofit
hospitals and insurers.

To understand the size of GET revenues it is also critical to keep in mind that because the
GET taxes what businesses buy, it can be paid several times at different points in the supply
chain before a good or service reaches its final buyer. Accounting for this phenomenon, which
is known as tax pyramiding, Colby (2020) estimated that the effective GET rate was 4.72
6 UHERO calculations. The base is from DOTAX (2025j) for calendar 2024, and personal income is
$102.9 billion (Bureau of Economic Analysis n.d.). Visitor spending is the 2024 figure of about
$20.7 billion derived in section 4.1, net of the transient accommodations tax; its share of the base
matches our estimate that visitors paid about 18 percent of state GET collections in 2025 (section
4.5). Construction is the contracting base of $14.0 billion. The remainder is measured as a residual,
so it also includes any error in our classification, and it ranges from 27 to 37 points. The base taxed at
lower rates, which is mainly wholesaling, adds about 4 points when it is converted to its equivalent
at 4 percent. The Tax Foundation’s 116 percent is higher still because it is computed from fiscal year
revenue that includes the county surcharges. Note that these shares count only the tax charged on
the final sale. Prices also include the GET paid at earlier stages (section 3.2), and this is true even of
exempt purchases, since hospitals, insurers and utilities buy taxed inputs. As an illustration, if taxed
purchases are scaled up by the 18 percent pyramiding in Colby (2020), slide 25, and taxed inputs
are assumed to make up 24 percent of the value of exempt purchases, the GET collected on residents’
consumption equals the 4 percent rate applied to about 81 percent of market consumption, or about
65 percent of total consumption. With other assumptions the results run from 73 to 87 percent and
from 58 to 70 percent.

===== page 63 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 3. General excise tax
percent, with even higher effective rates in food processing and agriculture and relatively low
rates in retail (section 3.2). Our own calculation for a carton of eggs sold on Oʻahu points
the same way: the GET likely amounts to 6.50 percent of the shelf price for eggs produced in
Hawaiʻi and 5.51 percent for eggs imported from the mainland, against 4.71 percent charged
at the register (Figures 3.2 and 3.3).
GET revenues are also higher than might be expected because residents are not the only con-
sumers in the tax base. Past estimates suggest that nonresidents’ purchases account for around
30 percent of the GET, and visitors alone between about a fifth and a quarter (section 3.3).7
Despite the breadth of the GET’s base, in some cases its structure may inefficiently distort eco-
nomic decisions. A notable case is that of residential rents. Because the GET is levied on rents
but not on owner-occupied housing, the tax discourages the use of properties for residential
rental housing. Absent an ethical preference for owner-occupied over rental housing, it is dif-
ficult to rationalize such a policy, and it likely makes the GET somewhat more regressive, as
renters are on average lower income than owner-occupiers.
Concerns are often raised about the regressivity of the GET more generally. Lower-income
households spend a larger share of their income. Because the GET also taxes groceries and
rent, which many states exempt, a household with a low income can pay a larger share of that
income in GET than a household with a high income. The current system offsets this to some
extent, since food purchased with SNAP or WIC benefits is exempt from the GET. In addition,
the refundable food/excise tax credit pays up to $220 per eligible person to households with
low incomes, including those that owe no income tax (section 3.4). The GET also appears
more regressive when the tax is measured against income than when it is measured against
spending, because in a single year a household with a low income can pay a much larger share
of its income in GET than of its spending (section 3.3).
The GET could be reformed in several ways. Lowering the tax on what businesses buy would
reduce pyramiding (section 3.2). An exemption for groceries would lower prices for everyone
who buys groceries in Hawaiʻi, including visitors. We estimate that such an exemption would
cost the state about $263 million and the counties about $33 million before residents spend
their savings, and about $44 million of the state amount would go to visitors if stores pass
on the full cut. A refundable credit can instead be limited to residents with lower incomes.
It reaches only the households that claim it, however, and in TY2021 about 158,000 people
did not receive $15.3 million of the food/excise credit (section 3.4). The state could also set
higher rates on goods bought mostly by visitors, but what this achieves depends on how visitors
respond, and we consider it separately (section 3.5). Any reform that reduces revenue must
be paid for, so to compare reforms we also need to account for the effects of the policy that
replaces the lost revenue (section 3.6).
7 Residents likely bear most of the cost of the GET, as in most cases businesses likely raise after-tax
prices to reflect the full tax, and we take full incidence on consumers as our default assumption.
In some industries, particularly less competitive ones, the tax may instead reduce wages, profits or
rents, shifting part of the burden to workers, business owners and landlords (section 3.3).

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 3. General excise tax
3.1 The GET base
The GET applies to the revenue of any business in the state, including revenue derived from
sales to other businesses. Unlike business income taxes, the GET does not generally allow
deductions for the costs of the business.8 Even firms experiencing losses must still remit their
GET tax liability.
Most activities are subject to a state tax rate of 4 percent. For manufacturing, producing and
qualifying wholesale transactions, the rate is 0.5 percent, because these are mostly sales be-
tween businesses and a lower rate limits the tax that builds up along the supply chain.9 In
all four counties, a surcharge of 0.5 percent also applies to covered activities subject to the 4
percent state tax.10 An exemption reduces both state and county revenue if the activity is also
subject to the county surcharge.11
A business is not necessarily eligible for the wholesale rate on all of its purchases. The lower
rate is available for purchases for resale within the scope of the law, and for services only under
certain conditions. A contribution to the general costs of the business is not sufficient.12
Goods brought into Hawaiʻi are subject to use tax, which complements the GET on business ac-
tivity within the state. Imports for qualifying wholesale, manufacturing and resale are treated
differently from property used for other purposes.13
Businesses can charge more than the combined state and county tax rate. At a rate of 4.5
percent, the maximum charge is 4.712 percent, because the charge is itself subject to GET.14
The calculation applies to one sale; it does not account for taxes at earlier stages of production.
In Table 3.1, the base implied by revenue in Hawaiʻi is larger than residents’ personal income.
This is because the GET applies to sales between businesses and to purchases by visitors, while
the comparison is based on the income of residents. Delaware, Nevada, Ohio, Oregon, Ten-

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 3. General excise tax
nessee and Washington also tax the gross receipts of businesses, at much lower rates.15
A comparison of the GET rate with retail sales tax rates in other states is therefore incomplete.
The GET also taxes goods and services bought by businesses, and the tax paid on them can
become part of the cost of the final good or service. The tax in the final price can therefore be
higher than the rate on that sale, as we discuss in section 3.2.
3.2 Business purchases and pyramiding
The GET applies to sales between businesses as well as sales to households. This has two
consequences. First, a tax on inputs creates an incentive for a business to do work itself that it
would otherwise buy from another firm. Second, the tax paid at earlier stages of production
can be passed on in final prices. To see the first, suppose there are two firms, only one of
which is vertically integrated and handles its own distribution. The firm that is not integrated
must buy distribution services from another business, and the price it pays may include the
distributor’s GET, either as a separate charge or as part of the price. The integrated firm does
not pay this tax, since it makes no taxable sale to itself. Thus, the tax can favor doing the work
within the firm even if the other business could do it at lower cost. A restaurant faces the same
choice when it decides whether to employ people to do work or buy the same service from a
business. The structure therefore favors businesses that can vertically integrate.
Figure 3.2 gives an example of this effect: a carton of eggs on an Oʻahu shelf, produced either
in Hawaiʻi or on the mainland. Each step of production that takes place in the state incurs
GET or use tax, because the inputs a firm buys to produce the eggs or bring them to market
are taxed, and every sale between firms in the chain adds more. If these costs are passed on
to the consumer, they show up in the shelf price. The price of a carton of eggs produced and
sold on Oʻahu includes anywhere from 11 to 15 cents of excise tax paid along the production
chain.
This cascading effect is known as tax pyramiding. Because the tax is collected at each transac-
tion, the total tax paid in producing a good can exceed the tax implied by the rate on the final
15 Wash. Laws 2025, ch. 420, §109: service and other activities rates of 1.5 to 2.1 percent from October

Washington taxable gross income above $250 million a year, subject to exemptions, from January
1, 2026 through December 31, 2029 (RCW 82.04.288; Washington Department of Revenue n.d.-a).

revenue over $4 million at rates of 0.051 to 0.331 percent, depending on the industry, and Ohio
taxes gross receipts over $6 million at 0.26 percent (Nevada Department of Taxation n.d.; Ohio
Information Release CAT 2023-01). Delaware’s gross receipts tax rates range from 0.0945 to 1.9914

products (Delaware Division of Revenue n.d.), and Tennessee’s business tax ranges from about 0.02
to 0.1875 percent of gross receipts, depending on the class of business (Tennessee Department of
Revenue 2025, p. 66). Nevada, Ohio and Tennessee also have retail sales taxes (Tax Foundation
2026c); Delaware and Oregon do not. Texas’s franchise tax is levied on a measure of margin rather
than gross receipts (Tex. Tax Code §171.101) and is left out. A low rate of tax on gross receipts does
not imply a low rate of tax on profits.

sale.16 Nor does the tax reach final prices evenly. Two goods sold for the same price may carry
different amounts of tax, depending on the taxes paid on their inputs along the way. A lower
tax rate on goods bought by businesses reduces the difference, but some difference remains.
Pyramiding gives goods and services effective tax rates that policymakers do not set directly;
they depend partly on how each good is produced. Figure 3.3 shows our estimates of the
effective tax rates on eggs bought on Oʻahu by production path, which differ with where the
steps of production take place and whether they are done within one firm. Eggs produced in
Hawaiʻi likely face an effective tax rate almost a percentage point higher than eggs shipped
in from the mainland, and eggs from a local producer that is vertically integrated likely face
a rate close to half a percentage point lower than eggs from one that is not.
The size of the tax advantage from vertical integration depends on what the business would
otherwise buy. Consider a hotel on Oʻahu that can run its own kitchen or buy finished meals
from a caterer. In both cases the meals that the hotel sells to guests are taxed at 4.5 percent.
The TAT does not apply to these meals, because it is levied only on room charges (section 4.1).
If the hotel cooks, it buys its ingredients at the 0.5 percent wholesale rate. If it buys finished
meals and resells them, that purchase also appears to be a sale for resale, taxed at 0.5 percent.
16 Mikesell (2007), p. 2, reviews the history of state gross receipts taxes and concludes that they pyra-
mid, change how businesses organize and hide their burden; he reports that Washington’s B&O tax
pyramided an average of 2.5 times along the chain of production (p. 10). Chamberlain and Fleenor
(2006), pp. 1 and 4, describe the same effects, with effective rates on value added that differ widely
across industries.

The layer of tax that the hotel avoids by cooking is therefore small, at 0.5 percent of what
it would pay the caterer.17 The advantage is larger for services that the hotel uses and does
not resell. Services that count as overhead, such as security or accounting, are taxed at 4.5
percent when the hotel buys them from another firm. If the hotel’s own employees do this
work instead, no GET is paid at all, since wages are not subject to the GET.18
To the extent that businesses respond to these incentives, supply chains in Hawaiʻi are partly
a result of the GET, though we cannot tell how much from tax collections alone.
Evidence from other places suggests that businesses do respond in this way. Washington used
to tax cannabis at each stage (the farm, the processor and the store), and in 2015 replaced
these taxes with a single tax at the store. Relative to the trend, it then became less common
for one firm to both grow and process cannabis. In China, a new law let manufacturing firms
claim a credit for the tax on services bought from other firms. Those that used more of these
services started to outsource more.19 When inputs were taxed less, firms in both places started

to buy more from other firms.
Production done outside the state has a similar advantage. An input made in Hawaiʻi in several
steps can carry GET from each of them, while the same input made elsewhere pays Hawaiʻi
use tax at most once, when it is brought in.20 The use tax narrows the tax advantage of buying
from outside the state (section 3.1), but it does not reach the earlier stages of production, so
the more of those stages that move out of state, the less pyramiding there is.21
These incentives arise because the GET is levied on sales between businesses. A tax levied only
on final purchases would remove them, but only if the state is able to accurately distinguish
purchases of inputs from purchases for final use. A value-added tax (VAT), which is used in
37 of the 38 OECD countries, achieves much the same result through a different mechanism
(OECD 2024).22 A VAT is charged on sales at each stage of production, as the GET is. Unlike
the GET, however, a VAT allows a registered business to subtract the tax it has already paid on
eligible purchases. The reform in China described above replaced a tax on the gross receipts
of service businesses, which was much like the GET, with a VAT.
Hawaiʻi already reduces some pyramiding through a lower rate on sales for resale (the whole-
sale rate), through exemptions, and through the capital-goods excise tax credit. Each of these
provisions lowers the tax on business purchases, and so moves the GET part of the way toward
a tax on final consumption such as a value-added tax. For eggs produced in Hawaiʻi, the use-
tax exemption for animal feed lowers our estimate of the effective tax rate by 0.1 percentage
point, or 0.76 cents a carton.23 The capital-goods credit is a refundable income tax credit equal
to 4 percent of the qualifying cost of eligible equipment and other physical business property
used in the state, apart from real estate, that tax rules treat as depreciable, allowing deduc-
tions for its cost over time.24 Businesses claimed $34.6 million of it on 2023 tax returns.25 For
eggs produced in Hawaiʻi, we estimate that the credit lowers the GET passed on in the price
by 0.4 cents a carton. The credit ends for taxable years that begin after December 31, 2027,
20 HRS §238-2. The use tax on imported goods is 4 percent of their value when the importer uses
them, 0.5 percent when a retailer imports them for resale, and nothing when a licensed wholesaler

under a 2026 law (Act 24).26
A self-service laundry on Oʻahu shows what the credit does. The laundry buys its washers and
dryers to use and not to resell, so it pays tax on them at 4.5 percent. This tax is the GET
included in the price if the laundry buys the machines from a local dealer, and the use tax if
it brings them in from the mainland. The credit returns 4 percent of the cost of the machines,
so the net tax is 0.5 percent, the same as the wholesale rate. The laundry’s own receipts are
then taxed at 4.5 percent. If the laundry passes its costs on, the price of a wash thus includes
both taxes. Its electricity and county water bills add no GET, because utilities pay a separate
tax and county water systems are exempt. Once the credit ends, the tax on a new machine
will be the full 4.5 percent.27
Because goods bought for resale already bear only the 0.5 percent wholesale rate, most pyra-
miding in Hawaiʻi comes from what businesses buy at the full 4 percent rate for their own
use: business and professional services, commercial rent, construction, and equipment that
the capital-goods credit does not cover. Ending the credit will raise the tax on equipment
bought by businesses, by about $35 million a year at the level claimed on 2023 returns, and
move the GET further from a tax on final consumption. The increase will be largest in the
industries that rely most on qualifying equipment.
Colby (2020) estimated that the GET, after the capital-goods credit, amounted to 4.72 percent
of final taxable consumption statewide. That is 18 percent more than the 4 percent state rate.
This does not mean that 18 percent of the final price is tax. Colby (2020) estimated higher
rates for food processing (6.28 percent) and agriculture (5.79 percent) than for retail trade
(4.32 percent).28 Pyramiding varies considerably across industries.
A switch from the GET to a VAT would largely eliminate pyramiding, which the wholesale
rate, exemptions and the capital-goods credit only serve to imperfectly reduce at present. A
VAT system allows businesses to subtract purchases they make from other businesses from
the revenue that is subject to taxation. Thus, two goods sold for the same price would carry
the same amount of tax regardless of how many firms took part in making them, and vertical
integration would not be beneficial on tax avoidance grounds alone. Moreover, this would
eliminate the disadvantage tax pyramiding imposes on goods made largely within Hawaiʻi
relative to those produced using supply chains that exist largely outside the state.
The main cost of such a switch is administrative. Under a VAT, businesses would have to keep
invoices for their purchases and claim credits for the tax on them, which the GET does not
require in the general case. The GET already imposes similar burdens in some cases, however.

To sell at the wholesale rate, a business must in practice obtain its buyer’s GET license number
on a resale certificate, and the capital-goods credit must be claimed and documented on the
business’s income tax return.29
The two taxes also differ in who has a reason to document a sale. Under a VAT, a business
can generally claim credit for the tax on a purchase only if it holds an invoice from the seller,
so buyers ask for invoices and the paper trail is created in the ordinary course of business.
Evidence from elsewhere suggests that this incentive matters. In the United Kingdom between
2004 and 2014, 43 percent of firms below the VAT registration threshold registered voluntarily,
and firms that bought more of their inputs from other businesses and sold less to consumers
were more likely to do so (Liu et al. 2021). In Chile, Pomeranz (2015) finds that announcing
additional monitoring has less effect on transactions that are subject to a paper trail. Under the
GET, the seller keeps the resale certificate and does not send it to DOTAX, so the department
sees it only on audit.30 Because the rate depends on who the buyer is, a retail sale recorded as a
sale for resale is taxed at 0.5 percent rather than 4 percent. Keen and Smith (2006) note that a
VAT offers no such opportunity. In a 2021 review of GET exemptions, the Office of the Auditor
described the same arrangement for a parallel certificate, which supports the deduction for
subleases of real property, and reported that DOTAX does not automatically verify eligibility
for two other exemptions. Washington State, whose sales tax relied on resale certificates that
buyers issued themselves, replaced them in 2010 with permits issued by its Department of

so is taxed at 4 percent, unless the business sells only at wholesale. HRS §237-13(2)(F)(ii).

Revenue.31
A switch would also require a higher statutory rate, since sales between businesses would no
longer be taxed. This increase is largely illusory, however, because pyramiding already makes
the effective rate higher than the statutory one. If the GET amounts to 4.72 percent of final
taxable consumption, a VAT at a state rate of about 4.7 percent would raise the same revenue
and leave the average effective rate roughly unchanged.32 The rate would rise for goods that
now carry little pyramided tax and fall for goods that carry a lot. Colby’s estimates for retail
trade (4.32 percent) and for food processing (6.28 percent) and agriculture (5.79 percent)
suggest how large these shifts could be.
Pyramiding can still be reduced under the status quo GET structure by lowering the tax on
the business purchases where most of it arises. One option is to extend the wholesale rate to
more business services, which today qualify only when they are resold under the conditions
described in section 3.1. Another is to keep the capital-goods credit rather than let it end
after 2027, as it will under current legislation. Businesses claimed $34.6 million of the credit,
which is worth 4 percent of the cost of qualifying equipment, on 2023 returns. Keeping the
credit would cost about $35 million a year at that level of claims. Like any reform that reduces
revenue, either change must be paid for (section 3.6).
Estimating pyramiding is difficult both because the excise system is complex once differential
rates and exemptions are accounted for, and because we lack high-quality information about
supply chains. Each dollar of a company’s sales can be taxed at one of four GET and use tax
rates, depending on where the seller and buyer are located, what the buyer does with the
good or service, and which of the 56 exemption and deduction categories applies. To estimate
pyramiding for an industry, every intermediate input of that industry has to be assigned to
one of these rate categories. Appendix E gives an estimate of the excise tax costs each industry
incurs before its final sale. That estimate draws on the state input-output table, in which
purchases between industries are largely estimated, not surveyed; Appendix E describes the
limits of the table.
Our household model assigns the tax on business purchases to the final buyers, assuming it
is passed on in prices. It does not count the way that tax distorts what businesses buy, so its
estimate of what a GET increase costs per net dollar of state revenue, 1.03 (Table 9.1), is
probably too low (section 9.3). Of the 1.03, about 0.57 falls on resident households, 0.25 on
visitors, 0.14 on other nonresidents and 0.07 on health payers and nonprofits. These shares

3.3 Who bears the GET
A household with low annual income may pay a larger share of its income in GET than a
household with higher income. This can be true even if they pay the same share of expenditure.
To interpret this result, we need to distinguish low income in a particular year from a persistent
lack of resources. These differences do not make it any less important that some households
have difficulty paying their current bills.
We need to measure the household’s tax burden before comparing it with income or expendi-
ture. This burden may be larger than the tax reported on a bill, since taxes on inputs can be
reflected in higher prices even when they do not appear as a separate charge. The measure
has to include these taxes on inputs without counting the same tax twice, as both a cost to
consumers and a loss of income to business owners.
For example, suppose that three households each spend $40,000 this year, including $1,600
in consumption tax. Their annual cash incomes before tax are $20,000, $40,000 and $80,000.
The tax is 8 percent, 4 percent and 2 percent of income, respectively, but 4 percent of expen-
diture for each household (Figure 3.4). The household with the lowest income must finance
the difference between income and expenditure, while the household with the highest income
can save.
Figure 3.4. The same tax and spending can produce different annual-income burdens
Note: Illustrative households, not Hawaiʻi estimates. Each spends $40,000, including an assumed $1,600 tax
burden, which is 4 percent of spending and not a statutory GET rate. Other taxes and transfers are omitted.
Source: UHERO arithmetic.
A household that saves pays the tax when it spends the money, not when it earns it. A com-
parison using one year of income can therefore make the tax look more regressive. Poterba
(1989) compares the burden of federal excise taxes as a share of income and as a share of ex-
penditure. If households base their spending on the resources they have over time, spending
70

===== page 73 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 3. General excise tax
tells us more than income in one year.33 A retired household living on its savings, for example,
has low income, and it will not earn more later.
However, spending in a single year is only a proxy for the resources a household has over
its lifetime. Savings may be spent outside Hawaiʻi, or never spent by the person who saved
them. Moreover, some purchases are made only occasionally. A household that buys a car this
year will use it for many years, so its spending in a single year can be quite different from its
consumption, although this is less of a problem in data that cover more than one year. Nor
can we ignore annual income. If business income falls for a year, a household with savings can
draw on them, while a household with the same income and no savings may have to reduce
its spending.
The GET treats renters and owners differently. People who rent their homes pay a business
for the use of the property, and the business pays GET on the rent. People who own their
homes receive housing services without making an equivalent payment, so they pay no GET
on those services, although construction and other inputs may still be taxed. This favors owner
occupation in the tax code.34 What it means for the distribution of the tax burden depends
on who bears the GET on rent. If renters bear it, exempting owner-occupied housing tends
to favor higher-income households, who are more likely to own. If landlords bear it through
lower rental income, we need to know the incomes of those owners and whether they live in
Hawaiʻi.
Residential rent is taxed at the general rate of 4 percent, or 4.5 percent with the county
surcharge, whatever the length of the lease.35 A landlord may list the tax separately on the
lease, at up to 4.712 percent of the rent, or charge a single amount that includes it. The tax
is owed in either case. The exemptions are narrow. They cover certified affordable housing
projects and the first $2,000 a year of rental income received by a person with a disability,
whose remaining rental income is taxed at 0.5 percent, and a deduction lowers the rate on
subleases to 0.5 percent.
Whether renters or landlords bear the GET on rent depends on which side can adjust more
easily. Colby (2020) assumed that property owners bear it, and showed that the GET becomes
slightly regressive for housing if part of it is passed on to renters.36 Measures of land-use
regulation rank Hawaiʻi among the most restrictive states, which could make housing supply
slow to respond to prices.37 However, a limit on the number of homes does not determine

tax is passed on to consumers in prices. We use this approach to measuring resources, but not the

over a lifetime than in a single year, though still regressive because their rates differ across goods.
Caspersen and Metcalf (1994), p. 731, find that a broad US value-added tax looks quite regressive
against annual income but roughly proportional to slightly progressive against lifetime income.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 3. General excise tax
how they are used. An owner may rent a unit to long-term tenants or to visitors, or sell it to
someone who will live in it. Thus, even with a fixed number of homes, a tax on rent may leave
fewer units for long-term tenants, in which case renters bear more of the tax. It is difficult for
landlords to reduce the number of units they rent out in the short run, so much of the tax is
likely to come out of the rent they receive, but in the long run they can respond by building less
and spending less on maintenance, which shifts more of the tax to renters. This is consistent
with the evidence on property taxes. In Germany, about a third of an increase was passed on
to rents at first and about four-fifths after three years, but hardly any where housing supply
was most restricted.38
No study measures how much of the GET is passed on to rents in Hawaiʻi, and we found no
published estimate of how the supply of long-term rentals here responds to rents. We therefore
rely on evidence from elsewhere, which comes mainly from property taxes on rental housing.
Carroll and Yinger (1994) estimate that renters in the Boston area in 1980 bore at most about
15 percent of a property tax increase, net of the value of the public services it paid for, and
nothing if tenants could move freely, and Schwegman and Yinger (2020) find about 14 percent
in New York City, Buffalo and Rochester. By comparison, the German estimate is about four-
fifths after three years. Two recent studies follow individual buildings. After a reassessment in
Philadelphia in 2023, about 73 percent of the tax increase on large, professionally managed
apartment buildings was passed on to tenants within about two years, although rents in the
wider market did not rise by an amount that could be detected (Ding et al. 2026). In Berkeley,
California, landlords whose buildings were reassessed passed on $0.50 to $0.89 of each dollar
of additional tax to renters (Baker 2025). Studies of rent subsidies provide additional evidence
that landlords can bear much of a change in what tenants are able to pay. In France in the
early 1990s, each additional euro of housing benefit raised the rent of new claimants by 78
cents (Fack 2006). Fack attributes this to a supply of rental housing that responded very little.
In the United Kingdom in 1996 and 1997, landlords bore between 60 percent and two-thirds
of a reduction in housing benefit (Gibbons and Manning 2006). These two studies imply that
landlords would bear most of a tax on rent if supply responds as little to the tax as it did to
these subsidies.
The studies above imply that tenants bear from about 15 percent or less to nearly 90 percent
of a tax on rental housing, and so we cannot infer the share for Hawaiʻi from this evidence.
The share is higher once landlords have had time to adjust, and lower where housing supply
is most restricted. Appendix C describes the assumption used in our household model.
The same question determines how much of the tax can fall on nonresidents. Most long-term
tenants are residents, so the tax can fall on nonresidents mainly through the part that land-
lords bear. DBEDT (2017) reports that 10.8 percent of residential parcels in FY2016 had an
owner or manager on the US mainland, and 1.1 percent had one abroad.39 These shares cover
38 Löffler and Siegloch (2024), abstract and p. 16, which find that “83 percent of the tax burden is
passed through to rental prices” after about three years, and around one-third in the first two years.

all residential parcels, including owner-occupied homes. The share for rented homes is not

Not all GET revenue represents a burden on residents. Past estimates suggest that nonresidents
pay around 30 percent of the GET.40 Visitors are only part of this group. In our household
model, visitors pay about 23 percent of an increase in the GET rate from 4 to 4.5 percent,
and resident households about 52 percent, counting the tax on sales between businesses that
is passed on in prices. The rest falls on governments, health payers, nonprofits and out-of-
state buyers (section 9.3). Since the 23 percent covers visitors only, it compares with the past
estimates for visitors in the note, not with the 30 percent for all nonresidents.41 Because we
evaluate the GET from the perspective of Hawaiʻi residents, the relevant question in what
follows is how the residents’ share of the tax is distributed across households. An analysis that
instead gave weight to everyone who pays the tax would also have to count the losses of visitors
and other nonresidents. Even the portion paid by visitors may not be costless to residents, since
visitor spending may also affect the wages and profits that residents earn (chapter 4).
The amount of GET a household pays also needs to be distinguished from its burden after
credits. What matters for a household’s resources is the food/excise tax credit it actually re-
ceives, not the one it is eligible for. We do not estimate GET burdens after credits. The closest
evidence is in section 9.7: a higher GET rate, even when paired with lower income-tax rates,
costs households in the bottom tenth about $210 a year on average.
3.4 Exemptions and refundable credits
Unlike an exemption, a refundable tax credit can be targeted. The amount of the credit can
depend on a household’s income and size, and it is paid even if the household has no income
tax liability. An exemption, by contrast, benefits anyone who buys the exempt goods, what-
ever their income, and this includes visitors. It is therefore possible to use a credit to provide
relief to households with little or no taxable income while continuing to tax the purchases of
other households. However, a household only benefits from a credit if it receives it. Thus, in
evaluating a credit, we need to know which households are eligible, whether they claim it,
and when it is paid.
The grocery exemption illustrates the difference. We estimate that exempting groceries would
reduce state and county revenue by about $295 million in 2027 before allowing for additional
spending out of the savings: $263 million of state GET and $33 million of county surcharge. If
stores reduce prices by the full amount of the tax removed, residents would save about $219

are already exempt.44 Households receiving these benefits would save only on the groceries

Evidence from elsewhere suggests that stores pass on all or nearly all of a cut in the tax on
groceries. In the United States, where sales taxes are added at the register, grocery prices
before tax rise when a county raises its grocery tax but hardly change when it lowers the tax.
The price including tax therefore falls by about the whole cut.45 Several countries temporarily
cut the VAT on food between 2020 and 2023. In Portugal the whole cut reached shoppers,
and in Poland all or nearly all of it did.46 At large retailers in Spain, more than 90 percent
of the 2023 cut was passed on in its first two months.47 The share was lower in Germany in
2020, at about 70 percent for all supermarket goods and up to about 80 percent for food, and
in Argentina in 2019 it was 84 percent at supermarket chains but 35 percent at independent
stores.48 The lowest estimates of the share of a tax cut that is passed on come mainly from
services, not from groceries. In Finland, prices rose twice as much after a VAT increase on
hairdressing as they fell after a VAT cut, and the same asymmetry appears across VAT changes
in the European Union.49 Estimates for the recent cuts in state grocery taxes in the United
States are not yet available, and there is no estimate for Hawaiʻi. Our main case is that stores
pass on the full cut, since the US evidence on taxes added at the register and most studies of
recent cuts in taxes on food sold in supermarkets find that all or nearly all of a cut reaches
shoppers. We also show a lower case in which stores pass on 70 percent of the cut, the estimate

for all supermarket goods in Germany. In Europe the VAT is included in the shelf price, so a cut

If stores pass on the full cut, residents save about $219 million of state GET at the register and
visitors about $44 million. If they pass on 70 percent, residents save about $153 million and
visitors about $31 million, and the other $79 million stays with the owners of stores and their
suppliers, some of whom live outside Hawaiʻi. In our model, 73 percent of that amount goes
to households in the top tenth of income.51
We rank households by their annual cash income before tax, which includes wages, business
and investment income, pensions, Social Security and other cash benefits, including income
that is excluded from AGI. Benefits paid in kind, such as food assistance from SNAP and WIC,
are not included. Annual cash income does not reflect a household’s savings or its future
earnings, and so the ranking captures only part of the resources a household has over its
lifetime.
Households with higher cash incomes receive more dollars from the exemption on average
because they spend more on groceries subject to GET. With households ranked by income
and the full cut passed on, households in the top tenth save about $750 a year on average,
compared with about $250 in the second tenth and $180 in the lowest.52 Relative to cash
income, the benefit is larger for households with lower incomes: with full pass-through, about
1.2 percent of income in the lowest tenth and 0.11 percent in the top. Relative to spending, it
is much more even. With full pass-through, households save between 0.29 and 0.39 percent
of their total spending in each of the bottom nine tenths and 0.21 percent in the top tenth. At
70 percent pass-through, they save between 0.20 and 0.27 percent in the bottom nine tenths

For the same total amount of relief to households, a credit can provide more to residents
with low incomes and less or nothing to those with high incomes, while keeping purchases by
visitors subject to tax. In our model, cutting the food/excise credit by 1 percent costs taxpayers
about $1.00 for each net dollar the state gains (Table 9.1), so the credit works almost entirely
as a transfer, with little change in behavior. Households with the same income still differ in
size, grocery spending and use of exempt benefits, so no credit matches each household’s
saving from an exemption (section 3.6).
Some households do not claim credits for which they are eligible. A study by Moore and
Karacaovali (2024) of TY2021 estimated that food/excise tax credits worth about $1.3 mil-
lion were not claimed for roughly 17,800 people on tax returns, including filers, spouses and
dependents.54 Including people who did not file, about 158,000 people did not receive about
$15.3 million, more than a third of the credit’s value that year (section 2.4).55 The results show
that being eligible is not the same as receiving the credit. Nor do they tell us what take-up of
a new credit would be today.
Under the expanded schedule, the food/excise credit is worth up to $220 per eligible person
and falls as income increases. Single filers are no longer eligible at federal adjusted gross
income of $40,000; for other filers, the threshold is $60,000. The earlier schedule is due to
return after 2027, but the credit does not end (section 2.4).56 For these reasons, how a credit
compares with an exemption depends on the year in which the reform is assumed to take effect,
since the expanded schedule is temporary. The comparison is also different if we assume that
every eligible household receives the credit than if we count only the households expected to
claim it.
Even if a household receives a tax credit every year, it needs to pay for food throughout the
year. The state could pay more frequently, but it would need to know who is eligible and be
able to pay them, accounting for changes in income and household circumstances. A simpler
tax form is not enough. Hawaiʻi’s TY2025 N-11SF, for example, is available only to specified
filers with zero federal and Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income who cannot be claimed as anyone’s

Removing the GET on rent does not necessarily reduce the amount renters pay by the amount
of tax removed. If the burden falls partly on the income of property owners, an exemption
may increase that income instead. A credit for renters provides relief to them, if they are
eligible and claim it. The exemption can also affect the supply of rental housing. To compare
the policies, we need to consider both who bears the tax in the short run and the response of
housing supply over time.
If the full exemption is paid for by residents, they must also replace the tax revenue from
visitors. Other ways of paying for it have different effects. Chapter 9 pairs the exemption with
higher income-tax rates. Raising every ordinary income-tax rate by about 12 percent leaves
state revenue unchanged but costs taxpayers about $107 million more than current law, and
counties lose about $30 million that is not replaced even after residents spend their savings. If
stores pass on the full cut, households in the bottom seven tenths gain on average, about $175
a year in the bottom tenth and about $260 in the fifth, and the top tenth loses about $3,230.
If they pass on 70 percent, the lower case, the state loses about $248 million instead of about
$255 million, and income-tax rates rise by 12.1 percent instead of 12.4 (section 9.7). Paying
for the exemption with the TAT instead is discussed in section 9.7 (see also section 3.6).
3.5 Different rates on final purchases
The presence of visitors provides a reason for taxing some goods at higher rates than others.
However, the share of demand accounted for by visitors does not tell us which goods should
face higher taxes. Visitors can substitute spending at home for some of their purchases in
Hawaiʻi, or choose not to buy a particular good at all. Purchases outside the state do not
generate GET revenue. Thus, an increase in the tax rate on goods that visitors can easily buy
elsewhere may reduce demand more than a tax on other goods.
A familiar benchmark in public finance holds that when the income tax can be set freely, and
preferences meet a separability condition, taxing all goods at the same rate is best.58 Visitors,
most of whom pay no Hawaiʻi income tax, are the reason to depart from it. Moore (2025)
develops a framework for this problem in which changes in the income tax can compensate
residents. In that framework, the best rates are lower on goods that visitors can more easily
replace with spending outside the destination. The response of residents to changes in relative
prices affects the size of the differences in rates. Other things equal, goods consumed dispro-
portionately by visitors who are more likely to cancel a visit as its cost increases also get lower
rates.59 Hawaiʻi already taxes one such good more heavily: hotel rooms pay the TAT on top of
57 Form N-11SF and Form N-11SF Instructions (2025), p. 1 of each. Qualifying single or joint filers
can use the form if they claim only the food/excise credit. This does not make payment automatic
for all eligible households.
58 Atkinson and Stiglitz (1976), p. 68: if utility “is weakly separable between labor and all consumption
goods (taken together), then no commodity taxation need be employed.”
59 Moore (2025), abstract (p. 1). The result for goods that visitors can buy elsewhere is in §2.2 of
that paper (pp. 10–11), and the result for visitors who cancel a visit as its cost increases is in §2.3
(pp. 12–15).

===== page 81 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 3. General excise tax
the GET, and studies of the TAT find that much of it is borne by visitors.60
These results are relevant to proposals for higher taxes on luxury goods. Goods that appeal to
wealthy visitors may also be goods that they can easily buy in other places. By contrast, some
services can only be consumed in Hawaiʻi. Visitors may have few alternatives to these services,
but they can still choose not to visit if the trip becomes too expensive. Chapter 4 considers
these responses and their effects on residents’ incomes. The fact that a good is a luxury, or that
visitors account for a large share of demand, does not tell us how much demand will change.
A proposal for a higher rate must also specify the goods to which it applies and the way in which
residents would be compensated. Businesses would have to report their sales of these goods
separately, and consumers may respond by purchasing other goods instead. Both of these
affect the revenue raised by the tax and its effects on residents.61 Consequently, we cannot
estimate the revenue without a definition of the tax base and evidence on these responses.
Visitor spending is concentrated in a few places, and this is the basis for proposals to set a
higher GET rate in a visitor district such as Waikīkī. Visitors spent $8.7 billion in Waikīkī in
2024, which is 36.4 percent of visitor spending in the state, and DBEDT attributes $798.6
million of state taxes of all kinds to this spending, counting the taxes paid by firms that sell
to visitors and by the firms that supply them.62 The statutes provide only for a surcharge that
a county adopts for the county as a whole, at a rate of no more than 0.5 percent, so a rate
that applied in one part of a county would likely require a change in state law.63 The taxes
we found in other states that target visitors by place apply to lodging or to a defined set of
visitor businesses, not to all sales in a district. Sacramento and Clark County, Nevada, charge
higher rates on hotel rooms in some zones than in others, and Montana allows small resort
communities to tax sales by lodging, restaurants, bars and recreational facilities.64 Residents
who shop or eat in a district would pay a higher GET rate on all sales there, and both residents
and visitors could move some of their purchases to businesses outside it. Studies of state and
local sales taxes in the United States suggest that buyers move their purchases in response to
differences in tax rates. Baker et al. (2021) find that households respond strongly to changes
in sales-tax rates, in part by shopping more online and across borders, and Agrawal (2015)
finds that local sales-tax rates in towns near a state border vary with the driving time to the
neighboring state. We know of no study of a higher sales-tax rate in one district of a city.

We also know of no data on how sales in Waikīkī are divided between visitors and residents.

3.6 Changes to the GET and their offsets
The effects of a reduction in the GET cannot be separated from the effects of the policy used to
replace its revenue. Consider a reform that reduces taxes on inputs and makes up the revenue
with a higher tax on final sales. The total tax on a good would fall if much of the tax it currently
bears is paid at earlier stages of production, and would rise if most of it is paid on the final sale.
The reform would also change prices, earnings and production, and these changes also affect
who gains from the reform. Thus, the taxes that households pay under the current system are
not sufficient to determine who would benefit from the reform.
The same is true of an exemption that is paid for by an increase in income taxes. For some
households, the benefit of the exemption will exceed the additional income tax they pay, while
for others it will not. This is the case even if the two are equal on average at each level of
income, since households with the same income do not spend the same amounts or claim the
same credits.
The capital-goods credit ends for tax years that begin after 2027, and the food/excise credit
returns to its earlier schedule (section 2.4 sets out this baseline).65 As a result, a pair of policies
that leaves revenue unchanged in 2027 can have different effects in a later year, depending
on whether the expanded credit schedule expires as scheduled or is extended. It is also neces-
sary to consider the state and county governments separately, since a policy that replaces the
revenue lost by the state does not ensure that the counties receive as much as before. In the
grocery exemption paid for by higher income-tax rates, the counties lose about $30 million
that the higher income tax does not replace.
Section 9.7 gives estimates of two such pairs. Raising the GET from 4 to 4.5 percent and cut-
ting all ordinary income-tax rates by about 27 percent leaves state revenue about unchanged
while taxpayers pay about $234 million less, but households in the bottom four tenths lose
on average. The grocery exemption paid for by higher income-tax rates goes the other way:
taxpayers pay about $107 million more (Table 9.2). We do not estimate a grocery exemption
paid for by repealing the food/excise tax credit.
65 Act 24 (SLH 2026), §3 (capital-goods credit); Act 163 (SLH 2023), §5 (food/excise credit).

4. Visitors and tourism
The taxes on a night in a Hawaiʻi hotel are the state and county transient accommodations
taxes (TATs) and the general excise tax (GET). Together they come to 18.7 percent of the room
charge. On a $400 night that is $74.85 in tax, which is $24.85 more than in Orlando, $21.33
more than on the Las Vegas Strip and $10.85 more than in Portland, including Portland’s
separate 3 percent tourism assessment (Table 4.1). Most lodging taxes in the United States
are local, so rankings by state miss much of them.1 A ranking of cities better captures how
local taxes contribute to the tax a visitor faces. Honolulu’s combined rate of 17.75 percent
ranked 11th among the 150 largest US cities at 2025 rates, and at the 2026 rate it would rank
about sixth.2
The state TAT raised $816.2 million in FY2025, which makes it the third-largest state tax, after
the GET and the individual income tax.3 The county TATs raised about $247 million more.4
Colby (2017) describes the TAT as a tax borne largely by nonresident visitors.5 PFM Group
Consulting (2017) estimated that visitors paid about one-fifth of the GET.6 Our household
model gives a similar figure: visitors would pay about $138 million, or 23 percent, of an
increase in the GET from 4 to 4.5 percent, counting the tax embedded in the prices they pay.
Resident households would pay about 52 percent, and governments, health payers, nonprofits
and out-of-state buyers the rest (section 9.3).7 Both figures are for visitors alone. Past estimates
for all nonresidents, who include owners of property and businesses in Hawaiʻi, are around
30 percent of the GET (chapter 3).
