Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 4. Visitors and tourism
idea is predicated on the state being able to make residents whole for what they pay on the
same purchases, on the tax not costing residents their jobs or profits, and on the rate not being
so high that it drives away enough visitor spending to lower revenue.
We know more about which goods to tax more heavily than about how high to set the rates.
Moore (2025) develops a framework for a destination that can compensate residents for their
share of a visitor tax. In it, the rates that raise the most from visitors are highest on purchases
that must be made here, such as the hotel room and the rental car.9
Section 4.2 explains what that compensation involves. Compensating residents for higher
prices is fairly easy. Compensating them for effects on jobs and wages is much harder. A
calculation made before Hawaiʻi’s hotel room tax of 1987 and a study of the tax afterward
both suggest that most of the tax fell on visitors, at a combined rate about half of today’s.10
Section 4.3 considers an alternative to the percentage TAT, a charge for each night, and sec-
tion 4.4 compares the options. What matters most there is how visitor spending responds to
the rate, and section 4.5 sets out the evidence from Hawaiʻi’s own increases.
4.1 The taxes on a visitor’s trip
Visitors pay three main taxes in Hawaiʻi: the TAT, the GET and the rental vehicle surcharge,
and residents pay them too. The state TAT is 11 percent of the room charge. Act 96 of 2025
raised it from 10.25 percent on January 1, 2026, and under current law it falls to 10 percent
after 2030.11 The act also extended the TAT to cruise fares, but a federal appeals court has
blocked that part of the act while the litigation continues, and DOTAX is not collecting it.12
Until 2021 the counties received a share of the state TAT. The law that ended that share let
each county levy its own TAT instead, and each now adds 3 percent, the maximum allowed
by state law.13
Visitors also pay the GET on what they buy, at the same rates as residents, 4 percent for the
state and 0.5 percent for the county surcharge. A hotel may add up to 4.712 percent of the
room charge for the GET, slightly more than 4.5 percent because the GET is also due on the

GET the guest pays.14 The GET and use tax, with the county surcharges, raised $5.20 billion
in FY2025 (Table 1.1). Car rental companies pay a surcharge of $7.50 a day in 2026,15 and
the surcharge on rental, tour and shared vehicles raised $118.2 million in FY2025.16
The hotel operator is required to pay the TAT and the GET, and it legally owes both whether or
not they appear on the bill.17 One question we need to ask, then, is to what extent the burden
of these taxes is paid by the guest and to what extent it is paid by the hotels once room prices
and wages adjust, and section 4.2 takes up that question.
Although it is commonly called the hotel room tax, the TAT applies to any room, house or
condominium rented for less than 180 consecutive days. The state taxes vacation rentals as
well as hotel stays, including any resort fees the guest is required to pay. People staying in
timeshares, even if they own them, pay tax on a statutory “fair market rental value,” calculated
from half the owner’s gross daily maintenance fees.18 Maui County has also enacted a phase-
out of vacation rentals in apartment districts, which is being challenged in court (chapter 5).19
But the government has to be able to see the activity it wants to tax. Many short-term rental
operators were not complying with TAT requirements. Enforcement on vacation rentals has
been strengthened over the past decade. In FY2016 DOTAX made its Special Enforcement
Section responsible for them. In FY2022 the law gave the section the authority to examine
any sector, and it now cites operators whose ads do not show a tax registration number.20
Some TAT receipts are earmarked by law for particular uses.21 The 2026 increase was pre-
sented as a “green fee,” but the law does not earmark it. Act 96 only asks the governor to
request about the same amount of general-fund spending on natural resources, resilience and
hazard mitigation, and visitor experience.22 The TAT is also not a user charge. It is levied on

the room charge regardless of which public services the guest uses during the stay. Thus, the
fact that visitors pay the tax does not by itself give tourism-related spending a claim on the
revenue. An earmark determines how the revenue is used, but an earmark does not tell us
which spending is best for residents. We therefore consider tourism-related spending in the
same way as any other use of public funds, in terms of its benefits, its costs and its effects on
public budgets.
Visitors spent $21.91 billion in Hawaiʻi in 2025. Of this, $10.03 billion, or 46 percent, was
spent on lodging, the largest category. Spending on food and drink was $4.72 billion, on
shopping $2.15 billion and on rental cars $1.48 billion.23 The TAT base is a little more than a
third of that spending. The state collected $816.2 million of TAT in FY2025 at a rate of 10.25
percent, so the stays it taxed came to about $8.0 billion, and that figure includes stays by
residents. Averaging calendar 2024 and 2025, visitors spent about $21.3 billion a year. The
operator of the hotel or rental remits the TAT on a stay, so that all of the tax on that stay is
collected in one place.24
On their other purchases, such as meals and shopping, visitors pay the GET at the same rate
as residents. Visitors pay about one-fifth of the GET (see the start of this chapter), a much
smaller share than they pay of the TAT. That share rises and falls with the number of visitor
days.25 We discuss the GET as a whole in chapter 3.

4.2 Visitor responses and compensation for residents
Residents and visitors pay the same GET, and the same TAT if they stay in hotels, so a tax
on visitors also has a cost to residents. Moore (2025) considers a benchmark in which the
state can fully compensate every resident for that cost. Under the model’s assumptions, the
state should then raise as much revenue from visitors as it can.31 That is to say, the impacts of
the tax on residents drop out, but only because each resident is assumed to be made whole.
Handing residents back what they pay in total is not the same thing, since some households
would still be left worse off. Where the visitors’ part of the revenue goes matters as well: if
the state holds total revenue fixed, it pays for an income-tax cut larger than what residents
pay in the higher tax, whereas if the state spends it (on parks or roads, say), residents get the
spending in place of that larger cut.
Suppose the state raises a broad tax on purchases and cuts the income tax at each income level
30 See also DOTAX Announcement No. 2025-03; DOTAX Announcement No. 2026-01; Florida DOR
Form DR-15DSS (2025), pp. 1–2.
31 Moore (2025), pp. 1, 4–6. The result requires that residents be fully compensated. The rest of this
section explains what that involves.
87

===== page 90 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 4. Visitors and tourism
by just enough to leave every resident’s purchasing power where it was.32 If every purchase is
taxed at the same rate and pre-tax prices do not move, the two changes cancel out for residents:
they face the same budgets as before and pay the same total tax. Whatever additional revenue
the state collects comes from visitors. This result assumes that Hawaiʻi policymakers care only
about the well-being of residents and give no weight to that of visitors.33
Wages and prices can also change in the model. Residents then need compensation for any
loss of earnings as well as the higher prices. The income tax can do this in the model because
wages change in the same way across sectors, and residents also pay tax on imports, as they
do under Hawaiʻi’s use tax on goods purchased outside the state.34 The common wage change
matters here. This is because a tax cut that depends only on income gives the same amount to
any two workers with the same income. Thus, if the loss in earnings is not the same for these
two workers, it is generally not possible for the tax cut to compensate each of them for the
loss.
The model assumes that hotels earn no profits above their costs. If the tax does reduce the
profits of hotels, then in order to fully compensate residents, it would also be necessary to
compensate the resident owners of hotels. Note that lost profits are only a cost to residents
when the hotel is owned by residents. If the owners of the hotel live outside Hawaiʻi, then they
themselves bear that part of the tax, in the same way that visitors bear the part of the tax that
they pay.35 If instead the hotel is owned by a family in Hilo, that part of the tax is borne by
residents.36
In practice, it is easier to compensate residents for higher prices than it is to compensate
them for lost earnings. Since it is possible to predict the effect of a tax increase on prices, a
credit or a rate cut can be put in place, at least in part, before residents pay the higher price.
It is more difficult to predict the effect of the tax increase on earnings. Payroll in Hawaiʻi’s
recreation businesses appears to have fallen after the TAT increases of 1999 and 2009–10,
while restaurant payroll barely moved (section 4.5). Even then, the state can hardly know in
advance which tour guide or hotel worker will lose hours, or how many hours will be lost,
which implies that it is much more difficult to compensate the worker before the loss. The
32 Moore (2025), pp. 4–6 and 8. The result assumes that all resident consumption is taxed at the
same rate, pre-tax prices are fixed and the income tax can be adjusted at every income level. Under
those assumptions, an income tax cut can leave every resident’s purchasing power unchanged after
a higher consumption tax.
33 If they do, the state would want to tax visitors somewhat less, but it would still want to tax them.

34 §2.5 of Moore (2025), pp. 16–18, and p. 20; HRS chapter 238. The wage change has to be the same
in every sector for this to work, though residents are allowed to differ in other ways.
35 Moore (2025), p. 20. If the tax changes wages by more for some groups of workers than for others,
and the income tax cannot be set separately for each group, then we must also account for how
those wage changes affect tax revenue. It also matters who owns the profits, and who pays tax on
them, if the firms that serve visitors do earn profits above their costs.

estimates in chapter 9 leave these payroll effects out, and so also leave out any income tax lost
when earnings in tourism fall (section 9.4).
A refundable credit is also received well after the tax that it is meant to offset. A household
pays the GET throughout the year, for instance each time it purchases groceries, but only those
who file a return receive the credit, and only after the year is over. A tax cut based on income
may also give some households too little compensation, because households with the same
income receive the same cut even if one of them spends more on taxed purchases or includes
a housekeeper at a Kāʻanapali resort who has lost hours of work. Section 2.4 discusses whether
Hawaiʻi’s credits reach the people eligible for them.
We therefore need to compare the tax cut each household receives with what it loses through
higher prices or lower earnings. Equal totals for residents’ tax increases and tax cuts do not
establish that everyone has been compensated. The pairing of a higher GET with lower income-
tax rates in chapter 9 shows the gap. Raising the GET from 4 to 4.5 percent and cutting all
ordinary income-tax rates by about 27 percent leaves state revenue about unchanged, and
taxpayers as a group pay about $234 million less. Yet resident households in the bottom four
tenths lose on average. Those in the bottom tenth lose about $210 a year, or 1.8 percent of
income (section 9.7).37 Lower income-tax rates are also of little use to residents with little
taxable income. In particular, a retiree living on pensions or Social Security may have little
income tax to cut, since Hawaiʻi does not tax Social Security or most employer-funded pen-
sions. The same is true of households that are spending down their savings. Reaching these
residents may take a refundable credit or a transfer (chapter 1).
Changes in the income tax are not the only way to compensate residents. The state could also
exempt or subsidize things that only residents buy. Long-term rent is one example. It is subject
to the GET, and it is paid by residents rather than visitors (chapter 3 discusses how much of
the GET on rent falls on renters). Property tax is another. Maui, for example, taxes a principal
home at lower rates than a second home (chapter 5).38 Whether an owner or tenant lives in
a home is easier to establish than residency at the point of sale.
Chapter 9 estimates one such pairing, an exemption of groceries from the GET paid for by a
higher TAT. Most grocery spending is by residents. With the estimated visitor response, the
TAT rate would have to rise by about 4.6 to 4.8 percentage points, and taxpayers as a group
would pay much more for the same state revenue. Without any visitor response the rate would
rise by about 2.9 points, and taxpayers would pay slightly less (section 9.7).39
When the state sets the overall level of the tax, the revenue it has to worry about losing is mostly
from visitors who choose not to come, or who stay fewer nights (Figure 4.2). In chapter 1’s
terms, the base shrinks. If a family goes to Cancún instead, or stays home, then it pays no
Hawaiʻi tax on anything it would have bought here. The base can also move, in the sense
that some visitors still come but buy at home what they would otherwise have bought at Ala

Note: Illustrative diagram of the mechanism in §§2.3–2.4 of Moore (2025), pp. 7, 12–16. It carries no magnitudes.
Where visitors can buy a good also matters for how rates should differ across goods. In the
model, if there were no visitors, the best thing for residents would be to tax every good at the
same rate. With visitors that is no longer true. Consider a visitor who wants a new handbag.
She can buy it at home just as easily, whereas the only place she can buy a night in a Hawaiʻi
hotel is here, and so the model calls for a higher rate on hotel nights than on handbags.40 That
result is about which of the two rates should be higher, and we cannot read it as saying that
either rate should go up or down from where it is today. Visitors can, after all, still buy fewer
hotel nights when the price goes up.
Residents, on the other hand, determine how far apart the rates should be. Even when an
40 Moore (2025), pp. 4–7, 10–11. Whether equal rates are best depends on the model’s assumptions
about residents’ preferences, and on the state being able to compensate residents through the income
tax. The comparison here is the result in §2.2 of that paper; its §2.3 also allows rates to differ when
goods are bought by visitors with different responses in their decision to visit. The paper’s own
example is a visitor who buys “a luxury handbag from a retailer at home” (p. 7). The rule places
lower taxes on goods that visitors can more easily substitute for spending outside the destination.
Moore notes that it “provides no rationale for taxing goods with relatively high tourist demand more
heavily” (p. 11).

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 4. Visitors and tourism
income tax cut makes up for higher prices, residents change what they buy when goods are
taxed at different rates, and every such change costs the state some revenue. So the larger
the share of a good that residents buy, or the more they respond to its price, the closer its
rate should be kept to the rates on other goods, though residents’ purchases never change
which rate should be higher. Many US places tax hotel rooms more heavily than other goods,
which is consistent with this ranking, though they may do so for other reasons too. Florida
and Nevada also impose separate rental-car charges (section 4.1).41
Section 4.5 estimates how TAT revenue responded to Hawaiʻi’s own increases, which does not
tell us who bore the tax. On that question the Hawaiʻi evidence is very old, but it points toward
visitors bearing most of it. Before the tax existed, Fujii et al. (1985) calculated from their own
estimates using Hawaiʻi data that about two-thirds of a hotel room tax would be borne by
visitors and the rest by hotel operators, through lower receipts.42
Bonham et al. (1992) studied the TAT that took effect in 1987, when the combined tax on a
hotel room, GET included, rose from 4.16 to 9.41 percent. Their results suggested that the
tax was almost fully passed on to visitors in higher prices and that demand for hotel lodging
may change very little when its price rises.43 One reason they offered is that a 5 percent rise
in lodging spending raises the total cost of a typical Hawaiʻi vacation, airfare included, by less
than 1.5 percent.
These studies do not tell us much about who bears the tax today. They concern a lower tax
rate, before vacation rentals were offered through online platforms and before hotel rooms
were booked online, and cover only the first three years after the tax took effect.
Three years is a short time for a hotel to adjust. Given longer, it can change how many rooms
it offers, how many staff it employs to look after guests, and how much it spends on keeping
rooms clean and well maintained. If it does, workers and suppliers bear some of the tax, so the
share that visitors paid in the first three years after 1987 may differ from the share they bear
over a longer period. The rate is also twice as high today, a combined 18.7 percent against 9.4
percent in 1987, and demand may respond differently at the higher rate. Consistent with the
1992 finding, a recent study of hotel taxes in California cities finds negligible effects on hotel
sales and employment, though that evidence comes from a different market. The same study
finds that hotels in cities with higher tax rates had lower credit ratings, a sign of financial
stress that is consistent with owners bearing part of the tax.44

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 4. Visitors and tourism
Estimates from elsewhere give a range for how strongly lodging demand responds to price.
After Georgia began charging $5 per room per night in 2015, the estimates of Collins and
Stephenson (2018) imply a price elasticity of demand for hotel rooms of about −0.7, or −1.25
in an alternative specification, with respect to the price guests paid. That is to say, if that price
rises by 1 percent, then with an elasticity of −0.7 the number of rooms rented falls by about
0.7 percent. Bibler et al. (2021) estimate −0.48 for Airbnb nights booked, with respect to
the price including tax, in 27 US metropolitan areas in 2014–2017. A meta-analysis (Peng et
al. 2015) of studies of international tourism demand from 1961 to 2011 finds an average own-
price elasticity of −1.28, and −0.73 for accommodation. In Hawaiʻi, Bonham and Gangnes
(1996) found no statistically significant effect of the 1987 tax on real hotel room revenues,
though their estimates cannot rule out large effects.45 These estimates measure responses to
different prices, a room night in some studies and a whole trip in others, in different places
and periods, and none comes from Hawaiʻi at today’s rates. They do not settle how far the
TAT could rise before revenue falls. The closest evidence we have is our own, from the TAT
increases of 1999 and 2009–10 (section 4.5).
It might seem that we could simply look at what happened to arrivals each time Hawaiʻi raised
the rate (Figure 4.3). However, arrivals were also rising and falling with recessions, airfares
and exchange rates over the same period. Thus, a comparison of the tax rate with arrivals
over time cannot be used to show how visitor spending responded to the tax.46 Evidence from
other places is limited in a different way, as it is informative only about the rates that those
places have tried. Thus, the effect of a much larger increase here would remain uncertain in
any case (section 4.4).
45 Collins and Stephenson (2018), p. 10; Bibler et al. (2021), pp. 637–638 and 646; Peng et al. (2015);
Bonham and Gangnes (1996), pp. 1281 and 1290, using monthly data for 1978–1990.
46 The state has raised the TAT six times since 1987: 5 percent from 1987, 6 percent from July 1994,
7.25 percent from 1999, 8.25 percent from July 2009, 9.25 percent from July 2010, 10.25 percent
from 2018 and 11 percent from 2026 (HRS §237D-2(a), (e); Act 1 (SLH 2017, First Special Session);
Act 96 (SLH 2025)). The increases of 2009 and 2010 came during the Great Recession and its
aftermath. PFM Group Consulting (2017), PDF p. 26, notes that General Fund TAT revenue was flat
over FY2007–FY2011 and attributes this to the downturn in tourism and the Great Recession. We
would want to know what would have happened to arrivals in the absence of each increase. After
a rate increase arrivals can still go up, because airfares fall, exchange rates change or mainland
incomes rise, and the pandemic mattered too. With only Hawaiʻi’s rate changes it is unclear how to
separate these effects. Section 4.5 therefore compares Hawaiʻi with places that did not change their
lodging taxes at the time, and the estimates in chapter 9 (section 9.4) use the responses it finds for
1999 and 2009–10.

4.3 A per-night charge as an alternative design
The TAT is proportional to the room price, so upgrading the room increases the tax. A fixed
charge per night would work differently: for a visitor staying the same number of nights, an
upgrade would leave the tax unchanged. Visitors would then have less reason to choose a
cheaper room to save on tax. Either tax changes the cost of the trip, and so how much visitors
spend and who chooses to come, and either raises the price of an extra night. A fixed charge
raises that price proportionally more for a visitor in a cheaper room, who may shorten her
stay more. We know of no estimate of that response for Hawaiʻi. A per-night charge can also
reduce the number of room-nights sold.48
A per-night charge also makes sense for two other goals: making sure visitors make some
minimum contribution toward the costs they impose on residents, and managing the number
of visitors. The aim could be fewer visitors to Hawaiʻi in a year, or fewer present on any given
day, and the costs to residents are arguably more closely related to the latter. A visitor who
stays ten nights adds to the crowding on beaches, trails and roads ten times as long as someone
who stays one night, but paying $900 for a room rather than $200 does not add anyone to
47 HRS §237D-2(a), (e); Act 1 (SLH 2017, First Special Session); Act 96 (SLH 2025).
48 Collins and Stephenson (2018) found that after Georgia began charging $5 per room per night,
hotels in the state rented about 2.7 percent fewer rooms in their specification in levels (an implied
price elasticity of demand of −0.7) and about 7 percent in their semi-log specification, while the fall
in the price hotels received was “imprecisely estimated.” The study covers only the first six months
of the fee, so we treat it as suggestive, as we do much of the evidence on hotel taxes.
93

===== page 96 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 4. Visitors and tourism
the crowd on the Diamond Head trail.49 A charge for each guest tracks visitor numbers more
closely than a charge for each room, since several guests can share a room.50 Still, visitors differ
in the costs they impose, so a fee at the crowded beach or trail would be closer still. Hawaiʻi
already charges such fees at Hanauma Bay, Diamond Head, Hāʻena and Waiʻānapanapa, and
residents with Hawaiʻi identification enter all four free.51
A per-night charge has costs too. The charge is the same number of dollars whatever the price
of the trip, and so it is a larger share of the cost of a budget trip than of a luxury one. Suppose
that budget visitors are the more likely to cancel when a trip costs more. Then the charge would
discourage more visits for each dollar of revenue. It would also burden residents staying at
hotels, as the TAT does now, so their share would need to be part of any compensation (section
4.2). We know of no data on how much of the taxed lodging residents account for. In chapter
9, treating all of it as resident rather than visitor lodging changes the revenue from a one-point
TAT increase from $89 million to $86 million before any response (section 9.4).52
A percentage tax has its own advantage for raising revenue: visitors in expensive rooms pay
more.53 The state could use both taxes, choosing how much to charge for another night in-
dependently of how much to charge for a more expensive room. How much revenue the two
taxes would raise, and how much crowding would change, would depend on how visitors
respond to each of them.
The tax paid on a night differs a great deal across the hotel classes in Hawaiʻi. In 2025 a luxury
room cost $862.82 a night on average, whereas rooms in the other four classes cost between
$201.00 and $328.06. At the 10.25 percent rate in force that year, the state TAT was therefore
about $88 on a luxury night and $21 to $34 on a night in the other classes, and at the 11
percent rate in force since January 2026 the same room prices imply about $95 and $22 to
$36. Luxury hotels also filled the smallest share of their rooms, at 66.1 percent compared
with 73.9 percent for all hotels. These figures cannot be used, however, to determine how
each class would respond to a different rate. To our knowledge, the response to a lodging tax
has not been estimated separately for luxury and budget hotels in any prior study, for Hawaiʻi
49 Moore (2025), p. 20, makes the point that crowding and environmental damage depend on where
visitors go and what they do there, which a tax on hotels or restaurants reflects only indirectly.
50 Kyoto, for example, charges its lodging tax per person per night, with the amount set by the price
of the stay per person (Kyoto City 2026).
51 City and County of Honolulu (2026a); children 12 and under, and active-duty military stationed in
Hawaiʻi and their dependents, also enter free. For the state parks, DLNR (n.d.-a, n.d.-b, n.d.-c).
52 A per-night charge on rooms is not a visitor fee, since residents who rent rooms pay it too, and
similar charges already exist elsewhere, such as Georgia’s $5 per room per night (section 4.1). A fee
on each visitor’s arrival is not a workable option, since federal law bars a state from adding a charge
to airfares (49 U.S.C. §40116(b); Kittinger 2021).
53 Concern for inequality among visitors would be another reason to tax luxury stays more, but the
result in section 4.2 is for residents alone. A proportional TAT already collects about four times as
much per night from a luxury room as from a midscale-and-economy room. For the full year, a
luxury room cost $862.82 a night on average and a midscale-and-economy room $215.87 (STR, Inc.
data in DBEDT 2026b, printed p. 5, Figure 3 (year to date)). Different percentage rates by hotel
class would go further. Assessing them would require a definition of each class and estimates of how
visitors respond to lodging taxes in each class.

or elsewhere. The closest evidence is indirect.54
4.4 Comparing visitor tax designs
The choice with the largest consequences for revenue in this chapter is how high to set the
percentage TAT. At some rate, the revenue lost because visitors spend less will exceed the
additional tax collected from those visitors who still come. It is not clear how close Hawaiʻi is
to that rate (section 4.5). For a one-point increase from today’s rate, the estimates in chapter
9 have state revenue still rising under each past episode: about $53 million with the average
response, $35 million with the 1999 response and $68 million with the 2009–10 response, out
of $89 million before any response. These figures apply each episode’s response to the 2027
tax base, in which the TAT is a larger share of the TAT and GET combined than it was in 1999.
The cost per net dollar raised is about $1.66, and between $1.31 and $2.55 depending on the
episode (section 9.4).55 On our estimates, a dollar raised through the TAT costs more than a
dollar raised through the GET, the property tax or the income tax as a whole: the comparable
figures are $1.03 for the GET, $1.02 for the property tax and $1.42 for income-tax rates as a
whole (Table 9.1). The reason is that visitors who spend less also pay less GET. Only the top
income-tax rate costs more, at $2.02. Even if a higher rate would raise more revenue, whether
residents are better off depends on which other taxes the additional revenue allows the state
to cut. The benefit to residents of cutting other taxes must be weighed against the losses that
residents bear through prices, employment, wages and profits. In chapter 9, using a one-point
TAT increase to cut all ordinary income-tax rates by about 3 percent makes taxpayers as a
group pay about $9 million more with the estimated response (section 9.7). This is not the
compensation described in section 4.2: the household results leave out the TAT residents pay
on their own stays and any effect on tourism jobs, and the income-tax cut is not set household
by household. Whether residents come out ahead depends on how the TAT divides between
visitors and residents, which the model does not estimate.
This choice depends critically on how visitor spending responds to the TAT rate at present.
The best evidence comes from Hawaiʻi’s own increases in 1999 and 2009–10, which raised
the state TAT from 6 to 9.25 percent. Counting the TAT alone, each increase kept 81 to 90
percent of what it would have raised if nobody had changed plans. But when visitors spend
54 STR, Inc. data in DBEDT (2026b), printed p. 5, Figure 3 (year to date); statewide, full year 2025.
The room prices are average daily rates before tax, and the TAT figures are those rates times the state
TAT rate, leaving out the county TAT and the GET. Sharma, Perdue and Nicolau (2022) use data on
about 100 urban hotel markets in the United States from 2013 to 2018. They find that higher lodging
taxes lower hotel revenue per available room from group bookings; their estimate for bookings
by individual travelers is also negative but not statistically significant. Hudson et al. (2021) study
lodging tax increases in eight US destinations and conclude that hotels appear to have absorbed
them with little effect on their business. Neither study reports estimates by hotel class. Marsi and
Randon (2021), in an unpublished study of about 300,000 online reviews of 893 hotels in five Italian
cities, find that guests who mention the city lodging tax give lower ratings, and that this penalty
is largest at two-star hotels and smallest at five-star hotels. However, reviews are written by guests
after a stay, so we cannot tell from these results which guests book fewer nights.

and residents.

4.5 Empirical evidence of tax revenue responses to TAT increases
The best evidence on how much room there is to raise the TAT comes from studying Hawaiʻi’s
own TAT increases. The state raised its TAT from 6 to 7.25 percent in 1999, and from 7.25 to
97

===== page 100 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 4. Visitors and tourism
9.25 percent in two steps, in July 2009 and July 2010.56 This section asks how much revenue
those increases raised, how many visitor-days they cost, and what they did to the GET that
visitors pay on everything else they buy.
How we answer the question. TAT revenue changes annually, often for reasons unrelated to
the TAT rate. The number of arrivals and how much visitors spend depends partly on airfare
costs, visitor incomes, business cycles, etc. Therefore, one cannot simply examine tax revenue
collections before vs. after Hawaiʻi raised the TAT to determine how much additional revenue
was raised when the TAT was increased. For example, the 2009 and 2010 TAT increases came
during the Great Recession (section 4.2), which caused visits and spending to fall much more
than they would have if the only factor to consider was the TAT rate. We therefore require
more sophisticated statistical approaches in order to evaluate the elasticity of tax revenue with
respect to the TAT rate (i.e. what was the percentage increase in tax revenue when the TAT
rose by 1 percent?). Specifically, we compare Hawaiʻi with a “synthetic Hawaiʻi”: a weighted
average of places that did not change their lodging taxes at the time, with weights chosen
so that the weighted average’s lodging-tax collections closely match Hawaiʻi’s TAT collections
over the three years before each TAT increase. After the TAT rate increase, the gap between
Hawaiʻi’s TAT collections and synthetic Hawaiʻi’s lodging tax collections is our estimate of
the effect of the tax on TAT collections.57 These comparison places are counties with a large
tourism industry, and Guam. A county qualifies if its share of jobs in accommodation is at
least 1.5× the national share and it has at least 250 such jobs. We leave out any place that
changed its lodging tax rate, or what its lodging tax covers, during the comparison period. In
our main model, synthetic Hawaiʻi for 1999 is built from 12 counties in Florida, Montana and
New Hampshire, and for 2009–10 from 11 places in Florida, Guam, Montana, Oregon and
Washington.58
Any comparison of this kind involves choices: how many years before the increase to match
on, how to remove seasonal patterns, how strict to make the tourism intensity threshold (e.g.
2× the national average is also reasonable), and so on. Rather than pick one combination,
we estimated every combination of choices we considered reasonable, 144 versions for 1999
and 192 for 2009–10, and we report the middle of what they find (the median). We chose the
rules for which specifications to include before computing them (Clemens et al. 2026).
TAT revenue rose almost in step with the rate. We measure the response of TAT revenue as
the percent change in revenue for each 1 percent increase in the rate. A response of 1 means
the taxed room revenue did not change, so TAT revenue rose in step with the rate. A response
between 0 and 1 means tourists responded by staying at cheaper hotels or for less time but
TAT revenue increased, and a response below 0 means the TAT rate increase lowered revenue
due to very strong behavioral responses. The median response was 0.83 for the 1999 increase
56 Act 156 (SLH 1998), §16 (effective January 1, 1999); Act 61 (SLH 2009) (8.25 percent from July 1,
2009, and 9.25 percent from July 1, 2010). We treat the two steps of 2009 and 2010 as one increase.
We also studied the increase to 10.25 percent in 2018, but our estimates for it are too uncertain to
say how large the response was (Appendix F).
57 The method is synthetic difference-in-differences: Arkhangelsky et al. (2021). Appendix F describes
the data and method.
58 Main version, county comparison pool. Florida 3, Montana 5 and New Hampshire 4 in 1999; Florida
3, Guam 1, Montana 1, Oregon 3 and Washington 3 in 2009–10. Arizona, Nebraska, New York, Utah
and Virginia also appear in some versions (Appendix F).
98

===== page 101 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 4. Visitors and tourism
and 0.91 for 2009–10 (Figure 4.1). In round numbers, raising the rate by a tenth, say from 10
to 11 percent, raised TAT revenue by 8 to 9 percent. The response was below 1 in 93 percent
of the 1999 versions and 70 percent of the 2009–10 versions. It was below zero in none of
the 1999 versions and in 1.6 percent of the 2009–10 versions. We therefore have a degree of
confidence that both TAT increases raised TAT revenue overall, with a small amount of revenue
leakage relative to the scenario where tourist behavior is unchanged.
In dollars, the 1999 increase would have raised an additional $51.6 million a year if nobody
had changed plans, and instead it raised $42.0 million (Table 4.3).59 The 2009–10 increase
would have raised $80.9 million and instead raised $72.7 million. So the state actually col-
lected only 81–90 percent of the additional revenue it would have collected if nobody had
changed travel plans. The rest, $9.6 million a year after 1999 and $8.2 million after 2009–
10, was lost because visitors bought fewer or cheaper room-nights. These responses are from
TAT rate increases from 6 to 7.25 percent in 1999 and from 7.25 percent to 9.25 percent in
2009–10. The state rate is now 11 percent, or 18.7 percent with the county TAT and the GET
included, and we cannot say whether visitors respond in the same way at today’s rate until
enough time has passed to collect data on the most recent state TAT increase of 2026.
Room revenue losses can reflect fewer visitor-days, reduced visitor spending on accom-
modations, or lower prices for accommodations. Relative to synthetic Hawaiʻi, the room
revenue on which the TAT is charged fell by 3.2 percent after the 1999 increase and 2.2 per-
cent after 2009–10.60 That can happen because fewer visitors come or they stay fewer nights,
because they choose cheaper rooms, and/or because hotels cut their pre-tax prices in response
to the TAT increase (in an attempt to keep their after-tax room rate down). Each TAT increase
raised the price of a room, tax included, by only about 1 percent, so even if hotels had ab-
sorbed the whole TAT increase, their pre-tax rates would have fallen by only about 1 percent.
We found no consistent drop in Hawaiʻi’s average pre-tax room rate after the TAT increases,
though a change of 1 percent is small relative to natural variation in hotel room rates (Ap-
pendix F.7), which makes assessing how much of the additional tax burden is paid by hotels
vs. tourists difficult. The three cases differ for policy, because they imply very different effects
outside lodging. If visitors spend fewer days in Hawaiʻi, the state also loses what they would
have spent on meals, shopping, tours and transport, along with the GET on that spending and
the jobs it supports. If visitors stay as many days but spend less on their rooms, the loss is
mostly confined to lodging, and their other spending need not fall. If hotels lower their pre-
tax prices, part of the tax is borne by hotel owners and possibly their workers, and visitor
spending elsewhere is largely unaffected. Our estimated GET responses are too imprecise to
show which case applied (Appendix F.4), and the visitor-day figures that follow take the first
case as given (Appendix F.6). If the whole drop in taxable room revenue came from fewer
visitor-days, not from tourists staying in cheaper hotels or from hotels lowering their prices,
then Hawaiʻi lost about 1.8 million visitor-days per year after the 1999 TAT increase (from
about 57 million in total) and about 1.3 million after the 2009–10 increases (from about 60
59 In 2025 dollars, at the size of the economy at the time: each increase is applied to TAT collections

60 Before tax. These declines follow from the responses above: if revenue rises by less than the rate,

million in total).61 Those visitors would also have spent money on meals, tours, shopping and

prices and spending per visitor-day. Changes in room choices, prices or spending by visitors
who still come could change the implied losses.
Counting the GET makes the result less favorable, and less certain. When visitors spend
less, the state also collects less GET. Applying a uniform 4 percent rate to visitor spending
gives an amount about 18 percent of state GET collections in 2025.63 A loss of GET revenue
can offset much of the TAT gain.64 We examine how GET collections changed due to TAT rate
increases in two ways.
First, suppose visitors’ non-hotel spending fell by the same percentage as room revenue, which
is the same assumption we used to count visitor-days. Then the 1999 TAT increase would
have raised an additional $22.7 million per year in total across TAT and GET, rather than the
additional $42.0 million which comes from the TAT alone. Similarly, the 2009–10 increases
raised an additional $59.4 million rather than $72.7 million from the TAT alone (Figure 4.4,
panel A).65
Second, we directly estimated the change in Hawaiʻi’s GET collections due to the TAT increases,
by the same method as with the TAT collections discussed above: by comparing Hawaiʻi with
states that did not change their respective sales taxes at the same time.66 The estimated
changes are small: GET collections fell by about 0.9 percent for each 10 percent increase
in the TAT rate after 1999, and by about 0.3 percent after 2009–10. Because the GET is so
much larger than the TAT, even small changes matter. These point estimates imply GET losses
of $49.4 million and $29.1 million a year, so that the net impact of the TAT rate increases on
total (TAT and GET) tax revenue was a loss of $7.4 million in tax revenue after the 1999 TAT

Recreation businesses appear to have lost more than restaurants. If visitors respond to
TAT increases by buying fewer goods in Hawaiʻi, they are likely to do so differentially across
industries in Hawaiʻi. For example, visitors may be more willing to sacrifice a surfing lesson
than a nice dinner in order to stay within their travel budget. It is very difficult to under-
stand which industries are the biggest “losers” when the TAT is increased because the type of
data necessary to estimate statistical models is challenging to acquire (if it exists at all). In
order to provide some illustrative evidence for this phenomenon of differential visitor spend-
ing responses, we compared payrolls in Hawaiʻi with payrolls in our comparison places for
the restaurant industry vs. the amusement/gambling/recreation industry. Payroll in amuse-
ment/gambling/recreation fell after both TAT increases: by about 5.9 percent for each 10
percent increase in the TAT rate after 1999, and 2.5 percent after 2009–10. The estimate is
below zero in every version. Conversely, restaurant payroll barely moved (about −0.9 and
−0.1 percent).68 These results are only suggestive. Payroll is not sales (we were unable to ac-
quire publicly available industry-level sales data), and the recreation category leaves out most
tour operators. Residents’ spending is also mixed in with visitors’. However, these estimates
point to differential impacts of the TAT across sectors. In practice, raising the TAT may induce
visitor spending responses across all sectors, and it is largely unpredictable which sectors will
require greater compensation. It is worth noting that lower wages implies lower individual in-
come tax collections, which to this point we have not counted when considering TAT impacts
on net tax collections.
We cannot tell how close Hawaiʻi is to the rate that raises the most revenue. As discussed
in section 4.2, a main result of Moore (2025) is that if the state can fully compensate residents
for the higher prices and lower wages that a visitor tax brings, it should set the TAT to raise as
much total tax revenue from visitors as it can. Counting the TAT alone, each increase kept 81

F).

to 90 percent of what it would have raised if nobody had changed plans. That is far from the
point at which a higher rate raises nothing more, where the share kept would be zero (Figure
4.5). Counting the GET, the share kept ranges from 73 percent (2009–10, if other spending fell
in step with room revenue) down to −14 percent (1999, with the estimated GET response).69
A negative share implies that the tax increase was counterproductive: accounting for GET
tax base spillovers, total revenue actually fell. Thus, taken at face value, our 1999 estimate
suggests Hawaiʻi may have been past the revenue maximizing point and a TAT decrease would
be warranted. However, there is considerable uncertainty about this matter, as our 2009–
10 estimates suggest Hawaiʻi is safely under the revenue maximizing TAT rate.70 While our
evidence cannot identify the precise revenue maximizing TAT rate, it shows that the TAT may
be a costlier way to raise revenue (in terms of economic competitiveness) than many of the
alternative tax reforms discussed in this report, and relying on TAT increases for further tax
revenue generation should be done with caution.

5. Property and real estate
Only the counties may tax real property in Hawaiʻi, so the property tax here is a county tax
(section 5.2). Hawaiʻi taxes owner-occupied homes at a low rate, but it collects about as much
property tax per resident as the median state. Property taxes paid by homeowners in Hawaiʻi
in 2024 were 0.29 percent of the value of their homes, the lowest of the 50 states. The rate
for the United States was 0.90 percent (Table 5.1).1 The median tax paid by homeowners in
Hawaiʻi was $2,239 a year in 2020–2024, less than the median for the United States, while
the median home was worth more than twice as much (Figure 5.1).2
However, state and local governments in Hawaiʻi collected $1,928 in property taxes per resi-
dent in FY2024. This is close to the median of the 50 states. Property taxes were 18.88 percent
of their tax revenue, compared with 29.42 percent in the United States (Table 5.1).3
Both facts can be true in large part because the counties tax a property at a rate that depends
on how it is used. In Honolulu in 2025, the rate on commercial property was 3.46 times the
rate on a home with the median value. This was the fifth highest ratio among the 53 cities in
the study by the Lincoln Institute of Land Policy and Minnesota Center for Fiscal Excellence
(2026).4 The owner of a home assessed at $2 million pays $6,580 a year in Honolulu and
$2,835 in Maui if she lives in it, and $15,400 and $15,250 if it is a second home (Figure 5.2).
Visitors and owners who live elsewhere also support part of the tax on rental, business and

one of the county rates in Figure 5.2.

a forecast of what Maui’s law will do, but they do illustrate the tradeoff between a ban and a

Maui could instead have raised the property tax rate on these rentals, and it can still do so for
the rentals the ordinance does not cover and for any that continue after rezoning. Maui already
taxes short-term rentals at higher rates than long-term rentals. A further increase would push
the least profitable short-term rentals out of the market. The most profitable ones would keep
operating and keep paying county taxes. Unless the rate were very high, a tax increase would
not free up as many homes as a ban, but it would consequently do less harm to tourism, and
the county would collect more revenue than under a ban. Compared with the rates in place
today, revenue could rise or fall, depending on how many rentals close. A very high tax on
these rentals or on vacant homes could have many of the costs of a ban.7

sold. In FY2025, the state collected $96.0 million from this tax, compared with $97.4 million

in FY2024.8 Each rate applies to the entire price of the sale, so the tax bill jumps when the
price crosses a threshold (section 5.6).
In chapter 1 we order taxes by how easily people can change their behavior to avoid them.
For example, a worker facing a higher tax on her earnings can work less or leave Hawaiʻi (in
which case the state loses the tax on her earnings). A business that faces a higher tax on its
income has less reason to invest here, since the tax lowers the return on that investment. Land
is unique from other production factors because its supply is fixed. A tax can lower the value of
land, but it cannot make the land disappear. This suggests that raising revenue from property
taxes rather than income taxes induces smaller responses, although the state would first need
to amend the constitution (section 5.2). In our household model, a small increase in county
property tax rates costs taxpayers $1.02 for each dollar the counties collect, against $1.42 for
the income tax as a whole (Table 9.1). Moving revenue from the income tax to the property tax
would therefore save roughly 40 cents for each dollar moved (section 9.7).9 However, property
taxes are not better in every respect. A high tax on business and industrial property makes it
more costly to operate here, and a high tax on homes would make Hawaiʻi a less attractive
place to live, so that in principle some people would leave. In our model this effect is very
small. Most of a lasting increase in the tax is reflected in lower home prices, which an owner
does not escape by leaving, and the home stays on the tax roll. For a 1 percent increase in
residential property tax, the revenue lost when households leave is about $16,000 a year. For
the top income-tax rate, by comparison, allowing high-income households to leave raises the
cost per net dollar from $1.42 to $2.02 (section 9.2).10
Property owners can change what they build, how they maintain it, whether they rent to
visitors or to residents, and when they sell, even when the supply of land is fixed. The supply
of land is an argument for a tax on its value only if what owners pay is independent of these
choices. County property taxes are also levied on buildings, with rates that vary by use, and the
state’s conveyance tax is levied on sales, so neither one gets the full benefit of that argument.
5.1 A statewide property tax as an alternative
A new state property tax could replace part of the income tax or the GET. It would require
a constitutional amendment (section 5.2), and the revenue from the new levy would pay for
cutting the state’s other taxes. The state would have to decide what to tax and what to exempt.
In particular, the tax could be levied on buildings as well as land, or on land only, and it could
exempt homes that are occupied by their owners. The exemption matters for the size of the
base. Of the $29.9 million a year that a 1 percent increase in all county rates would raise,
about $8.0 million, or 27 percent, is levied on homes owned and lived in by residents.11

goes to special funds (see below).
9 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model. The property tax figure holds the amount

10 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model. Both figures use a long-run migration elas-

Who gains and who loses depends on these choices, and on which of the state’s other taxes
is cut. For example, a retiree who has an expensive home but little taxable income would pay
more in property tax and would benefit very little from a reduction in the income tax. Renters,
on the other hand, can benefit from a lower GET, although a swap of property tax for GET
would gain little in efficiency, since a dollar raised through the GET costs taxpayers about
$1.03 (Table 9.1). We simulated a small version of a swap for the income tax, in which the
property tax on owner-occupied homes rises by 1 percent and the proceeds are used, through
lower state transfers to the counties, to cut all income-tax rates. Taxpayers as a group would
pay about $5 million a year less for the same revenue. All of the $8.0 million in added property
tax is paid by residents who own and live in their homes. The income-tax cut is worth less
than $1 a year on average to households in the bottom tenth of the income distribution, about
$14 in the fifth tenth and about $219 in the top tenth (section 9.7).12 A state levy, with or
without the home exemption, has not been simulated.
5.2 The county system, the state’s role and how Hawaiʻi compares
The reason a state property tax would require a constitutional amendment is simple. Hawaiʻi’s
constitution gives the counties the exclusive power to tax real property, except in Kalawao.
That is, the state can tax the conveyance of real property and the income that the property
generates, but not the property itself.13 Thus, the levy discussed in section 5.1 would be a new
tax, which the state would collect in addition to the existing county property taxes and whose
revenue would go to the state rather than to the counties.
The amount of property tax that a household pays is not determined by the county tax rate
alone. The county assesses the property, subtracts any exemption for which the owner qualifies,
and then applies a tax rate to the remaining value, where the rate depends on the tax class
of the property and both the exemption and the tax class can depend on how the property is
used. Note that the tax bill does not depend on the income of the household, and so the bill
alone cannot tell us whether the household can afford to pay it.
As an example, consider a single-family home with an assessed value of $2 million (land
and improvements together), and suppose that the owner is 50 years old, lives in the whole
property and qualifies for the full ordinary home exemption in fiscal year 2027. In Honolulu,
the taxable value after the exemption is $1.88 million. The Residential rate is $3.50 per $1,000,
12 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model. We do not assign the added property tax
to income groups. For the GET, we assume that the tax on purchases that businesses make from one
another is passed forward in prices, and we do not count how that tax changes what businesses buy.
The true cost of the GET is therefore probably higher than our figure.
13 Haw. Const. art. VIII, §3. Only the counties can tax real property, except in Kalawao. The state retains
other taxing powers unless it gives them to another government. The conveyance tax is imposed by
HRS §247-1.

Table 5.1 compares Hawaiʻi with four western states and the United States as a whole. Figure
5.1 uses a different measure for the 50 states and DC: the median tax divided by the median
home value. On this measure Hawaiʻi is also the lowest of the 51, at 0.27 percent. The median
is 0.80 percent.
The measures differ because their bases differ. The rate for homeowners leaves out rental,
business and resort property, which the counties can tax at higher rates, as Figure 5.2 shows
for second homes. Collections per resident include the tax on all of these properties. Part of
that revenue is supported by visitors and by owners who live elsewhere, but we divide it by
the number of residents only.
Hawaiʻi finances its public schools statewide, while the counties levy property taxes. We there-
fore combine state and local governments, which makes the revenue shares more comparable,
although the way schools are financed still affects the mix of taxes. In most states, property
taxes pay for much of the cost of schools. In Hawaiʻi they do not, yet collections per resident
are close to the median, so the property tax revenue that pays for services other than schools
is likely higher per resident here than in the typical state. Even so, these figures do not show
who bears the tax or how large it is relative to household income, as sections 5.3 and 5.4 and

5.3 Land and buildings
When the supply is fixed, a tax on the value of land can affect the price. Buyers decide how
much they are willing to pay in light of the tax payments they expect to make. If they expect
higher taxes, they will be willing to pay less. The loss in value would be borne by the owners
at the time expectations change. Buyers who come later may pay a lower price, so the amount
of tax they pay need not reflect the burden they bear.16 In our household model, a permanent
1 percent increase in all county rates raises $30.6 million a year and lowers property values
by about $759 million when it is announced (section 9.5).17
This does not mean that the tax imposes no burden. A tax on the value of land raises revenue
without giving people the same incentive to work less or to invest less that other taxes give
them. Even if property owners sell, the value of land is still subject to tax. If the tax does not
depend on what the owner does with the land, doing less does not reduce the tax. The owner
still loses the amount of revenue the government gets. What is missing is the extra cost that
arises when people change what they do to avoid a tax, the cost beyond the revenue itself
that chapter 1 describes (economists call it the excess burden of the tax, or deadweight loss).
Our household model comes close to this case. With the amount of property fixed, the cost
per net dollar raised is $1.00, whether the tax is reflected in prices in part or in full. Our main
estimate also lets owners build and maintain a little less when the tax rises, which brings the
cost to $1.02 (Table 9.1). It is a lower bound, because it leaves out moves between tax classes
(section 5.5).
Buildings are different. A building cannot move, but its owner can invest less in maintaining
it. The tax is levied on the value of improvements as well as land, so an owner can pay less
tax without moving, by not improving or keeping up the property. Higher property taxes can
therefore reduce the value of existing buildings and the amount invested in the future, an
effect that is missed if all real estate is treated as fixed. Our main estimate in chapter 9 holds
land fixed and lets owners build and maintain a little less when the tax rises, which is why its
cost per net dollar is $1.02 and not $1.00.
Like most property taxes, Hawaiʻi’s county taxes apply to both land and buildings. So they
combine a base that cannot move with one that can respond. Someone who wants a home in
Hawaiʻi has to build or buy it in Hawaiʻi, while a firm choosing where to put a new plant has
other options. A higher tax on buildings can reduce how much is built and how well it is kept
16 Mirrlees et al. (2011), chapter 16, §16.1.1 and note 4. The price effect depends on expected future
taxes. The chapter considers buildings, valuation and the timing of development. We use these
concepts without adopting its UK recommendations.
17 UHERO calculation using our Hawaiʻi household model. The main case assumes that the tax on
land and part of the tax on buildings are reflected in property values and that occupants bear the
rest. The share reflected in property values is about 70 percent statewide. We have no estimate
of it for Hawaiʻi. Most estimates for other places lie between about 60 percent and the whole tax,
and they are higher where the supply of housing is constrained (see the discussion of capitalization
later in this section). The discount rate is 2.82 percent. If the whole tax were reflected in property
values, the upper bound, the fall would be about $1.08 billion, the present value of the future tax.
If assessments follow prices down after a year, revenue settles at $25.6 million a year and the cost
per net dollar raised is $1.005.

===== page 115 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 5. Property and real estate
up.
A land-value tax has a base consisting only of land. A split-rate system taxes land and buildings
at different rates.18 A higher tax rate for the former and a lower rate for the latter would change
the return to investment. Evidence from Pennsylvania, where a number of cities tax land at a
higher rate than buildings, suggests that this can lead to more construction. Pittsburgh raised
its rate on land to more than five times its rate on structures in 1979–80, and Oates and
Schwab (1997) conclude that the land tax played a supporting role in the building boom
there in the 1980s. Banzhaf and Lavery (2010) compare census tracts in Pennsylvania over
1970–2000 and find that the number of housing units grew faster under split rates. Even so,
the amount of construction depends on its cost and on whether it is allowed. A change in taxes
will not make it possible to build where additional housing is prohibited. Even if it is allowed,
construction must be worthwhile.
This comparison requires a distinction between the value of land and the value of buildings.
That is a difficult task when the two are sold together. A new building is supposed to be
factored into the new valuation of the property, but if it also raises the assessed value of the
land, the owner pays more tax for building, even under a land tax. Undeveloped land that is
taxed at a lower rate creates the same sort of problem, because the owner then has a reason
to put off developing it. Thus, depending on how assessments are made, even a tax that is
levied only on land may affect how much is built and when.
The counties already depart from market value in one large case. An owner can dedicate
agricultural land to agricultural use for a fixed term. The land is then taxed on its value in
that use, or on a small percentage of its market value, rather than on what it would sell for.19
Dedication lowers the tax on the land for as long as it is not developed, which (as noted above)
can affect when the land is developed, or whether it is developed at all.
Economists do not agree on who bears the burden of a local property tax, and there are three
main views. In the traditional view, landowners bear the part of the tax that is levied on land.
The tax on buildings is an excise tax, and it is borne by the people who use the buildings in
the form of higher rents and prices. In the capital-tax view, owners of capital cannot avoid a
tax that applies everywhere, and so they bear the national average rate wherever they invest.
Only the difference between the local rate and the national average rate acts as an excise
tax, which implies that where the local rate is above the national average, housing costs are
higher and wages and land values are lower, and that the reverse holds where the local rate
is below it. Finally, under the benefit view, the tax is the price that households pay for local
services such as schools. If households can choose among many jurisdictions, then households
effectively pay for the services they use, either because zoning ensures that this is the case or
because any gap between taxes and services is capitalized into home values. In this case, the
18 Mirrlees et al. (2011), p. 379.
19 Honolulu, ROH §8-7.3 (land dedicated for 5 years is assessed at 3 percent of its fair market value,
and for 10 years at 1 percent); Maui, MCC §3.48.350 (10 or 20 years, assessed at the value in
agricultural use, and land dedicated for 20 years at 50 percent of that value); Kauaʻi, KCC §5A-
9.1 (5 years, assessed at 5 percent of fair market value); Hawaiʻi County, HCC §§19-60 and 19-61,
under which dedicated land is taxed on its productivity rather than its market value. Each code also
provides for rollback taxes when the land leaves the dedicated use.

===== page 116 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 5. Property and real estate
tax has little effect on the distribution of income.20
The capital-tax view contains the traditional view as a special case. No single jurisdiction can
change the national average rate, which owners of capital bear wherever they invest. It can
only change its own rate, and the effect of doing so is the excise effect of the traditional
view. The capital-tax view and the benefit view are harder to tell apart, because both imply
that taxes are capitalized into property values.21 Evidence from New Hampshire, where a
reform of school finance in 1999 changed property tax burdens, suggests that which of the
two applies depends on how easily housing can be built. Lutz (2015) finds that the tax cuts
raised residential construction in most of the state, as the capital-tax view implies, but that
near Boston, where the supply of housing is less elastic, they were capitalized into prices.22
Oates and Fischel (2016) conclude that the capital-tax view fits less developed areas and that
the benefit view fits developed urban areas, where zoning limits construction. They also argue
that the benefit view does not hold where the state’s financing of schools has broken the
connection between the local property tax and school spending, as in California.23
The conditions for the benefit view are not likely to hold in Hawaiʻi. Only four counties tax real
property, and the public schools, which are the service most closely tied to the benefit view,
are paid for by the state rather than by the counties. In this chapter we therefore rely on the
capital-tax view. The national average part of the tax is borne by owners of capital whatever the
counties do, and so what county tax policy changes is the excise effects. We assume that a tax
on land is borne by whoever owns the land when the tax is introduced. Homeowners in Hawaiʻi
pay 0.29 percent of the value of their homes, compared with 0.90 percent nationally (section
5.2), and so for owner-occupied homes the excise effect works like a subsidy, which implies
somewhat lower housing costs and higher land values than otherwise. Business property is
taxed much more heavily than homes, and in Honolulu the rate on commercial property is 3.46
times the rate on a median home. Note that this ratio compares two rates within Honolulu,
and so it does not show that commercial property there is taxed more heavily than in the rest
of the country. Only the property classes that are taxed above the national average effective
rate, likely hotel and resort property and commercial property, bear a positive excise burden.
Simulations of a small open economy imply that 64 percent of the part of this burden borne by
residents falls on land and on workers in the intermediate run, but that about three-quarters
of it falls instead on consumers of housing and services if workers can also move between
jurisdictions. Part of the burden may also fall on visitors to the extent that it raises hotel
prices.24

How much of a change in the tax is borne by those who own property at the time of the change
depends on how much of it is capitalized into prices. Evidence from elsewhere suggests that
the capitalized share is large, although the estimates vary. Studies that compare homes with
the same public services but different tax rates find that about 62 percent of the tax was
capitalized in subdivisions of Houston (Palmon and Smith 1998) and more than 70 percent
of school district taxes in Dallas County in 2014–2016 (Giertz et al. 2021). Høj et al. (2018)
find that the changes in land taxes that followed a reform of Danish municipalities in 2007
were fully capitalized, which is the effect described at the start of this section.25 Capitalization
also appears to be stronger where regulation and geography constrain the supply of housing
(Hilber 2017).26 Buyers do not always take future taxes into account, however. Bradley (2017)
finds that home buyers in Michigan ignored scheduled increases in their taxable values and
overpaid by nearly $10,000 for the average home, and Elinder and Persson (2017) find that a
large cut in the national property tax in Sweden in 2008 raised prices only for the homes with
very high tax values. Thus, a change in the rate on one class of property may be capitalized
slowly and incompletely.
Part of the burden of a property tax may also fall on renters. Whether rents rise when the
tax rises depends on the other options available to renters and on the supply of housing, and
the supply of housing can itself respond to the tax over time, so that the rent that a tenant
pays in the first year of the tax may differ from the rent that she pays a few years later. For
towns in the Boston area in 1980, Carroll and Yinger (1994) found that tenants paid roughly
$0.09–0.16 of each additional dollar of tax. Recent studies find that much more of the tax is
passed on. Löffler and Siegloch (2024) use about 5,500 changes in municipal property tax
rates in Germany, and they estimate that 83 percent of the tax is passed on to rents after
three years, with about one-third passed on in the first two years. Baker (2025) estimates
that a dollar of additional tax in Berkeley raises the rent charged to new tenants by $0.53,
and Ding et al. (2026) estimate that 73 percent of a tax increase in Philadelphia was passed
on to the tenants of professionally managed apartments, although they find no measurable
effect for the rental market as a whole. Watson and Ziv (2025) use changes in property taxes
on individual buildings in New York City in 2007–2019, and find that a building’s rents rise
at least in proportion to an increase in its own costs, which they interpret as evidence that
25 Palmon and Smith (1998), p. 1106, Table 2. They cannot reject full capitalization at a higher discount
rate (p. 1109), and they report earlier estimates of 15–30 percent for Massachusetts. In an early
study of towns in northern New Jersey, Oates (1969), pp. 965–966, found that about two-thirds of
a difference in tax rates was reflected in lower home values. Livy (2018) finds full capitalization of
differences in tax rates within school districts at a discount rate near 3.5 percent, and Lyu (2024)
estimates that at least 71 percent of expected tax payments was capitalized under the property tax
pilot in Shanghai.

landlords have pricing power.27
The German estimate comes from a market in which the supply of housing is mostly elastic.
In an earlier version of their paper, Löffler and Siegloch (2021) find hardly any pass-through
to rents in the municipalities where supply is constrained, that is, where most of the land
is already developed or cannot be developed.28 This is what we would expect if a tax on
something in fixed supply is borne by its owner. The supply of housing in Hawaiʻi is tightly
constrained by the scarcity of land and by the regulation of land use. The evidence from
elsewhere thus suggests that, in the long run, more of a change in the property tax here
is capitalized into land values and borne by the owners at the time of the change, and less
is borne by renters, than the German average would imply, although landlords with pricing
power may still pass on a large part of an increase in the first few years. We have found no
study that measures capitalization or the pass-through to rents in Hawaiʻi, and so this is an
inference from evidence for other places. The main case of our estimates in chapter 9 rests on
this inference (section 9.5). About 70 percent of an increase is reflected in property values,
which is within the range of the estimates of capitalization above, and renters bear 14–32
percent of the tax on rental housing, which is close to the estimate for the Boston area and
well below the recent estimates for Berkeley and Germany.29
The owners themselves do not all live in Hawaiʻi. Hawaiʻi data for fiscal year 2016 show that
owners with mailing addresses outside the state paid 32.3 percent of county property taxes,
and that owners on the mainland paid 52.0 percent of the tax on hotel and resort property
and 21.1 percent of the tax on residential property.30 To the extent that the tax is capitalized,
these owners bear it, and visitors bear whatever part of the tax on hotels is passed on in room
prices. UHERO calculations imply that resident owners bear 54.6 percent of county property
taxes, resident renters bear 8.0 percent, and nonresidents (owners who live outside the state,

5.4 Assessments and fairness
County property taxes are levied on the assessed value of a home, and assessed values are
only estimates. Unlike stocks, homes are not traded every day, and so the assessor cannot
observe a market price and must instead estimate what each home is worth. These estimates
are imperfect. Suppose that two homes have the same value but different assessed values.
Then, at the same tax rate, the owner of the home with the higher assessed value pays more
tax, even before any difference in exemptions or classification is taken into account. We refer to
this as an error in assessment. Note that which households should receive relief (for example,
low-income households or owners who live in their principal homes) is a different question,
and we treat the two questions separately.
When a home is sold, its sale price can be compared with its assessed value. We say that
assessments are “regressive” if the ratio of assessed value to sale price is, on average, higher
for cheaper homes than for expensive homes, in which case owners of cheaper homes pay
more tax per dollar of sale price even though they face the same tax rate. Cheaper homes may
be assessed too high relative to their prices, expensive homes may be assessed too low, or both.
Annual ratio statistics computed to the standards of the International Association of Assessing
Officers (IAAO) would show whether this happens in each county.32 Of course, these ratios
compare assessed values with prices and say nothing about the income of the household, so
they cannot show what share of its income a household pays in property taxes.
31 The calculation divides county property tax revenue of $3.06 billion (fiscal year 2025 for Honolulu
and Maui, and fiscal year 2024 for Hawaiʻi County and Kauaʻi) among the three groups. We assume
that the share of a tax on housing that is capitalized is the share of land in the value of the property,
plus a part of the remainder that depends on the elasticities of the supply of and the demand for
housing. For owner-occupied homes this share is 83.3 percent in Honolulu and 62.5–68.2 percent
in the other counties. Owners bear this share as a loss in value when the tax is announced and
occupants bear the rest, so that renters bear 14–32 percent of the tax on rental housing. We assume
that half of the tax on commercial property is passed on to its occupiers, half of the tax on hotels
to their guests and one-third of the tax on industrial property, and we divide the part borne by
owners between residents and nonresidents using the mailing addresses in DBEDT (2017), Table
14, p. 33. None of the elasticities is estimated for Hawaiʻi. Full capitalization is the upper bound on
what owners bear. With it, the three shares are 63.2 percent, 0 percent and 36.8 percent.

Our household model links county property tax to household income. For a 1 percent increase
in all county rates, the cost to resident households averages about $24 a year per household in
the bottom tenth of the income distribution and about $68 in the top tenth, 0.012 percent of
income. The averages include owners and renters, and the link between homes and households
combines data from different years (section 9.5).33
Berry (2021) compares assessed values with sale prices for residential sales across the United
States. He finds that, within a jurisdiction, cheaper homes tend to have higher ratios of as-
sessed value to sale price.34 His estimate is not for Hawaiʻi, and it tells us nothing about how
the burden is spread by income or race here. However, since Berry finds the pattern within ju-
risdictions across the United States, it is worth checking whether assessments in Hawaiʻi show
the same pattern. Berry (2024) later looked at sales in Urban Honolulu, where the median
tax bill was 0.37 percent of the sale price for the cheapest fifth of homes and 0.31 percent for
the most expensive fifth. A tax bill depends on exemptions and classification as well as on the
assessed value, so this difference need not be due to errors in assessment.35 An owner who
believes that her assessment is wrong can appeal, but an appeal changes only the assessment
of the owner who files it, and so appeals do not remove errors in assessment if some owners
are less likely to file than others. In Cook County, Illinois, for example, Black and Hispanic
homeowners were less likely to file an appeal, and those who filed were less likely to win.36
Infrequent reassessment is one possible source of assessment regressivity. Berry (2021) notes
that out-of-date values raise regressivity if cheaper homes lose value faster than expensive
ones and lower it if they gain value faster. Studies of other jurisdictions find that infrequent
reassessment accounts for part of the regressivity, although how large a part depends on what
is counted as infrequent reassessment. Amornsiripanitch (2022) attributes about 40 percent of
the regressivity that remains in his data to infrequent reappraisal and about 60 percent to the
valuation methods of assessors. Hou et al. (2025) report that in Philadelphia the price-related
differential, for which the IAAO range is 0.98 to 1.03, fell from about 1.4 in 2010–2013 to

1.14 in 2019, after citywide reassessments in 2014 and 2019.37 County documents show that
Honolulu and Kauaʻi value property every year as of October 1, and the Lincoln Institute of
Land Policy (2023) reports that all four counties value and assess property annually.38 With
annual valuation, the length of the reassessment cycle is less likely to produce regressivity in
Hawaiʻi than in jurisdictions that reassess every two to four years. The evidence suggests two
more likely channels, which are the 3 percent caps in Hawaiʻi County and Kauaʻi discussed

ratio statistics for any county in Hawaiʻi, so neither channel can be measured from public
documents.
A household can have trouble paying its property tax even when the assessed value is right.
Tax bills go up as home values rise. A retired owner on a fixed income has no additional money
to pay a higher bill, and the bill does not fall when her income does. Relief for a household
like hers can take two forms. The first reduces the tax, and the second delays its payment.
The counties already have several programs of the first kind. Honolulu, Maui and Kauaʻi give
homeowners a credit for tax above a share of household income (3 percent in Honolulu and
Kauaʻi, 2 percent in Maui), subject to income limits. Hawaiʻi County and Kauaʻi limit the
annual increase in the assessed value of a home with a home exemption to 3 percent.39 The
cap applies to assessed value, not to the tax bill, which still changes with the rate. However,
such a cap does not make an assessment more accurate. When it binds, the assessed value of
the home no longer rises with its market value. The gap between the two can keep growing for
as long as the owner keeps the home, so that of two homes with the same market value, the
one that has been held longer has the lower tax bill. The cap therefore produces the same kind
of assessment error described at the start of this section, although here the county creates the
error on purpose in order to give relief.
Each of these programs reduces the tax bill, but none of them delays payment. One way to
