delay payment is to let the owner defer the tax until the home is sold or the owner dies, with the
deferred tax and interest secured by a lien on the home. Oregon does this for homeowners aged
62 or older with household income of $70,000 or less. The state pays the county, charges 6
percent interest a year, and generally requires repayment after the owner dies, sells or moves.40
Texas lets owners aged 65 or older defer the tax on their home, with interest at 5 percent a
year, until they no longer own and live in it.41 British Columbia runs a similar loan program
for owners aged 55 or older.42 In effect, the owner borrows against the value of the home
to pay the tax. The deferred tax accrues interest, so the owner repays more than the tax she
deferred, but she pays later, usually when she sells, moves out or dies, and she can remain in
the home until then. A county that adopted one of these programs would be giving relief to
the owner, and any error in her assessed value would remain.
5.5 Rates by use and owner-occupancy
When tax rates differ across uses of a property, taxes can affect how that property is used.
This is different from a tax that applies equally to land regardless of its use. For example, an
increase in property taxes on short-term rentals reduces their profitability relative to other
uses. Some property owners may respond by providing long-term rentals instead. Others may
continue to rent to visitors and pay the higher tax. The effects on housing and revenue depend
on the extent of each response.
A decline in short-term rentals need not translate into an equal increase in long-term rentals.
Property owners can also choose to live in a home themselves, use it as a vacation home, keep
it empty, or put it up for sale. In the last case, its use depends on the decision of the buyer.
Counting the properties that disappear from a booking platform would therefore be insuffi-
cient to measure the increase in housing for residents. What matters is how the properties
are used after the change. Ellis et al. (2025) found, for Florida counties, slower growth in
residential rents after Airbnb began collecting the county tourist development tax from hosts,
although the study did not establish how many vacation rentals became long-term homes.43
40 Oregon Department of Revenue (2026a), pp. 1–4; ORS §§311.666–311.701. An applicant must be
62 or older or disabled, with 2025 household income of no more than $70,000 and net worth,
excluding the home, below $500,000. The interest rate of 6 percent a year is set in ORS §311.674.
ORS §§311.684–311.688 generally require repayment when the claimant dies, when the home is
sold or otherwise changes ownership, when it is no longer the claimant’s homestead (unless the
absence is required by health), or when a manufactured structure or floating home is removed from
the state. These rules are subject to statutory provisions for continuing the deferral, including for an

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 5. Property and real estate
All four counties set their rates by class, and most of the classes are defined by how a property
is used. In fiscal year 2027, Maui taxes the first tier of value at $1.65 per $1,000 for a home
occupied by its owner, $2.90 for a long-term rental, $6.25 for a non-owner-occupied home
and $13.00 for a transient vacation rental, so that the rate on a vacation rental is about eight
times the rate on an owner-occupied home. The same comparison gives a ratio of 4.4 in Kauaʻi
and 2.6 in Honolulu.44 Other cities in the United States also tax business property at a higher
rate than homes, but on average by less. In the 53 cities studied by the Lincoln Institute of
Land Policy and Minnesota Center for Fiscal Excellence (2026), the ratio of the rate on com-
mercial property to the rate on a median home averaged 1.82 in 2025, compared with 3.46
in Honolulu.45
In Maui, several dwelling classes depend on actual use and exemptions. Owner-occupied prop-
erty must be the owner’s principal residence, with the home exemption. For the long-term
rental class, the property must have a rental exemption and be rented to the same person for
at least 12 consecutive months. For transient vacation rentals, the stay is less than six con-
secutive months. Homes that do not qualify for the other dwelling classes are classified as
non-owner-occupied.46 These rules make the tax depend on the use of a property, even when
there is no change in ownership.
Many homes in Hawaiʻi are owned by people who live outside the state, most of all on the
Neighbor Islands. Tyndall, Bond-Smith, and Inafuku (2024) report that 13 percent of property
owners on Oʻahu list an out-of-state mailing address for property tax purposes. The share is
23 percent on Hawaiʻi Island, 29 percent on Kauaʻi and 32 percent in Maui County.47 Buyers
from outside the state also take a large share of the homes that are sold. In 2025, 16.6 percent
of the single-family homes and 24.3 percent of the condominiums sold in the state went to a
buyer who listed an out-of-state home address. In each Neighbor Island county such buyers

first tier of its class: up to $1.5 million for owner-occupied homes and long-term rentals in Maui, $1
million for non-owner-occupied homes and $900,000 for vacation rentals there. The rates on higher
tiers reach $17.00. In Kauaʻi the rates are $11.30 for vacation rentals (up to $1 million) and $2.59

and $3.50 for the Residential class. Hawaiʻi County has no separate class for vacation rentals. It
taxes them with other homes that are not the principal home of the owner, at $11.10 on the first $2
million, compared with $5.75 for homeowners. The ratios are our calculations. They compare rates
only and ignore the home exemptions, which lower the tax on owner-occupied homes further.

took between 44 and 55 percent of condominium sales (Tyndall et al. 2026).48
The counties do not tax these owners by where they live. Each county keeps its home ex-
emption or its owner-occupied class for an owner’s principal residence, and an owner who
lives outside Hawaiʻi cannot claim one here. Homes owned from out of state are therefore
taxed almost entirely in the other residential classes, together with residents’ second homes
and rentals. Those classes carry most of the residential property tax on the Neighbor Islands.
About half of the residential tax records in each county are owner-occupied. Yet in FY2027
the other residential classes pay 91.4 percent of the residential property tax in Maui County,
85.9 percent on Kauaʻi and 73.9 percent in Hawaiʻi County. In Maui County the tax on those
classes averages 1.03 percent of gross assessed value, against 0.13 percent for owner-occupied
homes. The same two rates are 0.80 and 0.21 percent on Kauaʻi, and 1.21 and 0.39 percent
in Hawaiʻi County.49 The property tax is thus already strongly differentiated, by use and by
exemption but not by where the owner lives.
An owner who lives on the mainland may rent her home to a local family, and a resident may
own a vacation home that is empty for most of the year. Where the owner lives therefore does
not tell us whether residents live in the home. Where the owner lives also does not tell us
who bears the tax, which depends on how prices, rents and investment respond. To evaluate
a change in policy we would need evidence on how homes are used and on who bears the tax.
A county may set higher rates on business and resort property because part of the tax on these
classes is borne by people who do not live in the county. In fiscal year 2016, owners on the
mainland paid 52.0 percent of the tax on hotel and resort property and 21.1 percent of the
tax on residential property (section 5.3). The share borne by nonresidents also differs by class.
The Minnesota Department of Revenue (2026) estimates that in 2023 residents bore all of
the local property tax on owner-occupied homes, about 80 percent of the tax on recreational
property and second homes, about 57 percent of the tax on commercial property and about
16 percent of the tax on industrial property, with the rest borne by owners and customers
outside the state.50 However, a higher rate on business property also raises the cost of doing
business in the county. In the Chicago metropolitan area, Cook County assesses commercial
and industrial property at a higher share of its value than homes and the surrounding counties

do not. Dye et al. (2001) find that higher property tax rates there led to significantly slower
growth in employment and in commercial and industrial property.51 Thus, a class rate that is
partly paid by nonresidents can still be costly for residents if businesses invest less and employ
fewer workers because of it.
Rates that depend on class also give owners an incentive to move a property from one class
to another. A county whose goal is more homes for residents wants owners to respond in this
way. However, owners may move a property to a class other than the one the county intended,
and each property that leaves the class reduces the revenue from the higher rate. Gwynedd, in
Wales, is an example. The council there has charged a premium on the ordinary tax for second
homes since 2018, and it raised the premium to 100 percent in 2021 and to 150 percent in
2023. The council’s own analysis finds that after the 2023 increase more second homes became
main residences than the reverse, and that the number of second homes fell from about 5,000
to about 4,200. However, in 2023 Wales also made it harder for a holiday rental to be taxed
as a business rather than as a home, and when about 550 holiday rentals moved back into the
second-home class, the number of second homes increased again to about 4,850.52
UHERO calculations imply that the same response is important for revenue in Hawaiʻi. Sup-
pose that Honolulu raised only its Residential A rate, by $1 per $1,000 of value. This would
raise $46.2 million a year if no property changed class, but only $38.7 million by the fifth
year, because some owners would move their property into other classes, and the cost would
be about $1.19 for each dollar of net revenue, against $1.02 for a uniform increase in all county
rates (Table 9.1). For the same increase in the rate on Maui’s non-owner-occupied class, about
24 percent of the revenue is lost by the fifth year. For an increase of $2 per $1,000 in the
vacation rental classes, the loss ranges from about 6 percent in Maui to 36 percent in Hon-
olulu. The Honolulu figure is the least certain: across the range of our estimate of how owners
substitute between classes, it runs from 23 to 48 percent.53 Thus, a higher rate on one class

likely raises less revenue than the value of the property in that class would suggest, and at a
higher cost for each dollar than a uniform increase.
The figures in this section for single classes are a rough approximation. Our estimate of
how owners move property between classes rests largely on what happened after Honolulu
changed its rates. We extrapolate from those patterns to the other counties, to other classes
and to rate changes that no county has tried, and what would actually happen is uncertain.
The figures are better read as showing the direction and rough size of the effects than as
forecasts. They do show correctly how taxing classes at different rates differs from raising all
rates. An owner can avoid a higher rate on one class by changing how the property is used or
classified, which is not possible when every rate rises. The revenue raised, the cost for each
dollar and the effect on how property is used can therefore be quite different from those of a
uniform increase, and they differ from one county and class to another.
The same calculations show where the property goes. Suppose that each county raised the
rate on its class for homes that are not owner-occupied by $1 per $1,000 of value. By the
fifth year the cost for each dollar of net revenue would be about $1.13 in Kauaʻi, $1.19 in
Hawaiʻi County and $1.32 in Maui, compared with $1.19 for Residential A in Honolulu. Most
of the property that leaves the class on the Neighbor Islands would go to a class for homes
that residents live in. We estimate that about 560 properties would move to the homeowner
class in Hawaiʻi County and about 280 to the owner-occupied class in Kauaʻi, and in Maui
about 510 would move to the owner-occupied and long-term rental classes. For Honolulu the
calculation does not show how the property would be used. About 1,070 properties would
leave Residential A for the Residential class, but this class taxes owner-occupied homes and
other homes at one rate.54
The share of the revenue that is lost rises with the size of the increase on vacation rentals.
For an increase of $5 per $1,000, the loss by the fifth year would be about 8 percent in Maui,
15 percent in Kauaʻi and 48 percent in Honolulu, or 53 percent if Honolulu taxed transient
vacation rentals at its hotel rate. In Maui about 380 properties would leave the vacation rental
class, and about 320 of them would become long-term rentals or owner-occupied homes. In
Kauaʻi about 270 would leave, about 200 of them for the non-owner-occupied class. These
losses count the property tax alone. A vacation rental that leaves the class would also stop
collecting the TAT and GET on its stays, though some of its visitors would stay in hotels instead.
Once these taxes are counted, the Maui increase would lose 9 to 13 percent of the revenue
that it would raise if no property changed class, and the Kauaʻi increase 17 to 26 percent. The
Honolulu increase would lose 63 percent under the lower of our two assumptions about the
TAT and GET and 110 percent under the higher one, in which case the increase would reduce

total revenue.55
Only Maui taxes all four uses in separate classes, so only Maui can change the rate on all
long-term rentals without changing other rates. Honolulu has no class for long-term rentals,
and below $1 million its Residential class taxes owner-occupied homes and other homes at
one rate, apart from the home exemption. Kauaʻi and Hawaiʻi County have classes for some
long-term rentals, but the calculations do not include these classes and tax long-term rentals
there with other homes that are not owner-occupied. For Hawaiʻi County, whose long-term
rental class is new in fiscal year 2027, the calculations therefore probably understate how
much property would leave the Residential class when its rate rises. We therefore calculated
two changes for Maui that favor resident use. In the first, Maui cuts its long-term rental rates to
its owner-occupied rates. By the fifth year there would be about 400 more long-term rentals,
but only about 235 more properties in the two resident classes together, because some of
the new long-term rentals would otherwise have become owner-occupied homes. The county
would collect about $9.8 million a year less.56 The second change is designed to leave revenue
about unchanged before owners respond: the rates on non-owner-occupied homes and vaca-
tion rentals rise by $0.50 per $1,000 and the rates on owner-occupied homes and long-term
rentals fall by $0.77. By the fifth year about 570 more properties would be in the two resident
classes, and the county would collect about $9.2 million a year less. Thus, in these calcula-
tions lower rates on resident uses do move property into those uses, at a cost to the county
of about $42,000 a year for each property added to the resident classes in the first case and
about $16,000 in the second.

apartment districts, where 85 percent of the owners who use their units in this way have an
out-of-state mailing address.57 Evidence from elsewhere suggests that demand from outside
buyers raises house prices and brings little new building. For US zip codes from 2011 onward,

Gorback and Keys (2020) estimate that a 1 percent increase in foreign capital raises house
prices by 0.27 percent and the housing supply by 0.004 percent. For England and Wales,
Sá (2025) finds that purchases by overseas companies raised house prices and had no effect
on construction. They also reduced the number of vacant dwellings, which is not what we
would expect if such homes were left empty. Two policies aimed at outside buyers have been
studied. British Columbia introduced a 15 percent tax on foreign buyers in August 2016. Pavlov,
Somerville, and Wetzel (2024) find that house prices in the Metro Vancouver neighborhoods
with above-median concentrations of foreign buyers then fell by 6 percent relative to the other
neighborhoods. Switzerland banned new second homes in 2012 in municipalities where they
were 20 percent or more of homes. Hilber and Schöni (2020) find that the ban lowered the
price growth of primary homes and raised that of second homes, and that it raised the growth
of unemployment in the affected areas.58 How far these results carry over to Hawaiʻi is not
known. Demand from outside the state is present here: 13 to 32 percent of property owners in
each county list an out-of-state mailing address, and out-of-state buyers took 44 to 55 percent
of condominium sales in each Neighbor Island county in 2025.
A tax on vacant homes creates one more class defined by use, in this case by whether anyone
lives in the home. Vancouver has taxed empty homes since 2017, at 3 percent of assessed
value since 2021, and a home counts as vacant there if the owner declares it vacant, if a
city audit finds it vacant, or if the owner does not file a declaration. The number of homes
declared vacant fell by 67 percent between 2017 and 2024. However, this count would have
changed with reporting and audits, and with the supply of and demand for housing, even in
the absence of the tax. Thus, the decline alone does not tell us how much housing the tax made
available to residents, or even how much of the decline is a response to the tax.59 Caracciolo
and Miglino (2024) compare census areas on either side of the boundary between Vancouver
and the neighboring city of Burnaby, where Vancouver’s tax does not apply. They estimate
that between the 2016 and 2021 censuses the tax lowered the share of dwellings that were
unoccupied by 1.5 percentage points, or about one fifth, and they find no significant effect
on average rents or on new construction.60 In France, Segú (2020) estimates that a tax on
vacant homes reduced recorded vacancy rates by about 13 percent, with most of the reduction
among homes that had been vacant for a long time. The share of homes recorded as primary
residences also increased, although the study does not show whether each previously vacant

home became occupied.61 This is still the strongest estimate of the effect of a tax on vacancy.
Thus, the estimates for Vancouver and France suggest that a tax on vacant homes leads some
of these homes to be occupied, although neither study finds that the tax lowered rents.
The reports on two other taxes on vacancy show who pays the tax, but they do not estimate
its effect on housing. British Columbia has its own speculation and vacancy tax, which from
2019 charged 0.5 percent of value to Canadian owners and 2 percent to foreign owners and
to what the province calls satellite families. A review by the province found that these two
groups paid 68 percent of the revenue in 2018–2020, so most of the tax was paid by owners
from outside the province. The review also reports that about 20,000 units were added to the
rental market. This figure is a count of condominiums and includes newly built units, so the
figure is not an estimate of the effect of the tax.62 Since 2018, homes in Melbourne, Australia
that are left vacant for more than six months of the year have been subject to a tax of 1 percent,
and it is the owner who must report that the home is vacant. The state’s Parliamentary Budget
Office estimated in 2023 that about 5,000 properties might be liable, while only about 900
were paying the tax, which suggests that a tax of this kind is paid mostly by the owners who
choose to report.63 In the United States, we have found no evaluation of the tax on vacant
property in Washington, DC, and the tax that San Francisco voters approved in 2022 was held
invalid on appeal in September 2026 under a California law that protects the right of an owner
to take units off the rental market.64

Census Bureau counts a home as vacant if no one usually lives there, and so the count includes
vacation homes and many vacation rentals. Homes held for seasonal, recreational or occasional
use were 13.2 percent of all housing units in Maui County, 13.1 percent in Kauaʻi, 8.3 percent

The ruling depends on a California statute, and so it tells us little about how a similar tax would be
treated in Hawaiʻi. With some exemptions, the tax applied to units vacant for more than 182 days a
year in buildings with more than two units, at $2,500 to $5,000 a unit in the first year.

States.65 This implies that in Maui and Kauaʻi more than half of the homes that the Census
Bureau counts as vacant are held for these uses, and those counties already tax second homes
and vacation rentals at higher rates through the classes described above.
Honolulu has considered a tax on vacant homes. A 2024 bill would have created a new tax
class for homes that are neither lived in by their owners nor rented to a tenant for at least six
months of the year, with some exceptions. The City Council would have set the rate for this
class each year. An earlier draft of the bill had set the tax at 3 percent of assessed value.66
The bill was filed without a final vote on August 1, 2026, under the Council’s rule that ends
consideration of bills pending for two years, and in July 2026 a proposal to put such a tax
before the voters as a charter amendment fell one vote short at the Charter Commission.67
The bill cited the 2020 Census, which counted 34,253 vacant units in Honolulu, and Tyndall
(2024) starts from the Census counts and estimates that about 26,000 homes would be subject
to the tax. Ernst & Young (2025), in a draft feasibility study prepared for the city, arrived at
a much smaller number. They estimated vacancy for single-family homes from water use and
for condominiums from the rates in other jurisdictions, and found that only about 1,500 to
2,100 properties would be liable for the tax.68 The two estimates differ by more than a factor
of ten, so how many homes the tax would return to residents, and how much revenue it would
raise, depends on which of them is closer to the number of homes that the tax would in fact
reach.
Some homes are empty because nobody can live in them. Many people in Lahaina had their
homes destroyed by the wildfires, and Maui temporarily exempts unoccupied properties in the
Lahaina reentry zone from property taxes.69 The owner of a home that cannot be occupied
has no way to respond to a tax on vacancy, so a higher tax would be a burden on her without
changing how the home is used. The effect of a tax on vacancy therefore depends on which

A reduction in short-term rentals could also lead to fewer visitors, resulting in less income for
residents who work in tourism or sell to visitors, and less revenue for both the county and
the state. Some visitors may stay in hotels instead, which would change this calculation. Thus,
the effect on revenue is more than the taxes paid by the rentals that continue to operate. The
calculations earlier in this section count the properties that change tax class, which is not the
same as the number of homes added for residents. Their figures for the TAT and GET give
two bounds: one assumes that the visitors who would have stayed in these rentals do not stay
elsewhere, and the other that some of them move to hotels. The first is an assumption, and the
second rests on the UHERO study of Maui’s phase-out. The class estimates earlier in this section
are not part of our household model, which does not include these moves between classes
(section 9.8) or the TAT and GET that change with them. Bonham et al. (2025) estimated the
effects on housing and visitors for Maui’s phase-out. A higher tax rate would require estimates
of its own.
5.6 The conveyance tax
The conveyance tax is collected when real estate is transferred. It therefore creates an incentive
to delay a sale: the supply of land is unchanged, but there is no transaction to tax. It is a
separate source of revenue from property taxes collected by counties and from income tax on
gains from the sale of real estate.
The conveyance tax applies to the full amount paid for the property, legally called considera-
tion, rather than the seller’s profit. This amount includes applicable liabilities and payments
other than cash. Under the ordinary schedule, the rate is 0.10 percent below $600,000 and
reaches 1 percent at $10 million or more. A higher schedule applies to a single-family res-
idence or condominium if the buyer does not qualify for a county homeowner exemption.
Rates in this schedule run from 0.15 to 1.25 percent.70 The relevant distinction is eligibility
for the exemption, rather than simply whether the buyer is a resident.
A higher rate applies to the full consideration at the threshold. For example, a taxable cash
sale just below $1 million faces the ordinary rate of 0.20 percent, giving a tax of about $2,000.
If the price is $1 million, the rate is 0.30 percent and the tax becomes $3,000. For the higher
schedule, the corresponding tax goes from $2,500 to $4,000. Figure 5.3 illustrates the result:
the increase in liability can exceed the increase in price.
70 HRS §247-2(1)–(2), and Form P-64A Instructions (2025), pp. 1–2. Exemptions (§247-3) are applied
before the rates. Generally the person making the transfer owes the tax. It is rounded to the nearest
ten cents, and the minimum is one dollar.

A buyer and a seller can avoid this jump by agreeing on a price below the threshold, and the
seller may be willing to accept the lower price if the reduction in tax exceeds the loss in sale
price. Some sales will not happen at all. A sale is worthwhile only if the buyer is willing to pay
more than the seller is willing to accept, and when the tax is larger than this difference no
price leaves both of them better off. A sale lost in this way is not among the sales we see just
below the threshold.
Kopczuk and Munroe (2015) study the million-dollar mansion-tax threshold in New York and
New Jersey. They find sales clustered just below the threshold and fewer sales just above it
than would otherwise be expected. The extra sales below the threshold do not account for all
the missing sales above it. Bargaining helps explain these responses; their evidence does not
support evasion as the explanation in the setting they study.71
Best and Kleven (2018) study a temporary reduction in the tax. During a 16-month period
in 2008–2009, the UK removed a 1 percent tax for a particular range of housing prices. They
estimate that the number of transactions in the affected price range increased by about 20
percent in the short run, once sales clustered around the tax thresholds are accounted for, and
71 Kopczuk and Munroe (2015), pp. 214–215, 242–243, 250–251.

===== page 133 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 5. Property and real estate
that less than half of this increase was reversed after the tax was reintroduced.72
Sales have also been found to respond to transfer taxes in other countries. Dachis et al. (2012)
study a land transfer tax of about 1.1 percent that the City of Toronto introduced in 2008. Com-
paring homes on either side of the city border, they find that sales of single-family homes fell
by about 15 percent. In Germany, where states have set their own rates since 2006, Fritzsche
and Vandrei (2019) find about 7 percent fewer sales of single-family homes for each percent-
age point of tax. For Australia, Davidoff and Leigh (2013) find that a 10 percent increase in
the amount of stamp duty lowers the number of sales by about 3 percent in the first year and
6 percent over three years.73
Transfer taxes also reduce how often households move. In the UK the rate rises from 1 to 3
percent at a price of £250,000, and Hilber and Lyytikäinen (2017) find that the higher rate
lowers the share of households that move in a year by 2.6 percentage points, or about 37
percent. In Finland a 2013 reform raised the rate on apartments from 1.6 to 2 percent and
added housing-company loans to the tax base. Eerola et al. (2021) find that mobility among
apartment owners then fell by about 5.6 percent relative to owners of detached houses.74
Much of the tax appears to fall on sellers in these studies, including where the law makes the
buyer pay it. For Toronto, Dachis et al. (2012) find that prices fell by about as much as the tax.
For Germany, Dolls et al. (2025) find that prices are about 3 percent lower for each percentage
point of tax a year after a change in the rate.75
These responses make a transfer tax a more costly way to raise revenue than an annual prop-
erty tax. Dachis et al. (2012) estimate a welfare loss in Toronto of about $1 for every $8 of
revenue, relative to a property tax that raised the same amount. The cost also increases with
the rate. In the model of Määttänen and Terviö (2022), which is calibrated to the Helsinki
region, raising one more unit of revenue costs households about 1.3 units when the rate is 2
percent and about 3 units when it is 7 percent.76 Mirrlees et al. (2011) recommend that the
UK tax be abolished, and the OECD (2022) recommends that countries lower their taxes on
transactions and make more use of recurrent taxes on property.77
72 Best and Kleven (2018), pp. 180–183, Figures 8–9. The estimate for the broader price range is about

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 5. Property and real estate
The most recent evidence from the US is the study of Measure ULA in Los Angeles by Green
et al. (2026). Since April 2023 this measure has taxed sales above $5 million at 4 percent
and sales above $10 million at 5.5 percent. The authors find that the rate at which affected
properties sold fell by about half, and that receipts were about half of the roughly $672 million
that the city had projected for the first year. They also estimate that about 80 percent of the
revenue is offset by lower property tax collections in the future. We would not expect an
offset of this kind in Hawaiʻi, because it depends on California’s Proposition 13, under which
a property is reassessed when it is sold, whereas the counties in Hawaiʻi assess property at
market value every year.78
Hawaiʻi’s rates are low compared with most of the taxes in these studies. The top rates of 1
and 1.25 percent apply only at $10 million or more, and the ordinary rate on a $1 million
sale is 0.30 percent. Toronto’s tax was about 1.1 percent, Finland’s 2 percent on apartments
and the UK’s 1 to 3 percent. In the Helsinki model, one more unit of revenue costs about 1.3
units at a rate of 2 percent, so the cost of the tax itself is likely modest at Hawaiʻi’s rates. Our
rough calculation from the responses estimated in these studies puts it at about $1.10 for each
net dollar raised at the average rate and $1.20 to $1.40 in the top brackets (chapter 1). The
cost rises quickly with the rate, to about $1.40 to $1.80 at 2 percent and roughly $1.70 to
$3 at 3 percent. All of these studies come from other places, and Best and Kleven’s from a
recession, so none of them establishes how sales in Hawaiʻi would respond to a similar change.
The jumps at the thresholds are a separate cost, and they could be removed without giving
up the tax. If the rates on high-value sales were raised to levels like the 4 and 5.5 percent in
Los Angeles, the evidence suggests that the number of sales would fall, and that the increase
would raise less revenue than a calculation at the current number of sales implies.
A higher rate could instead apply only to the part of consideration above a threshold, as with
the brackets of the income tax. The jump at the threshold would then be removed, but the sale
would still be taxed. Washington State has taxed sales in this way since 2020. Its rates run
from 1.1 to 3 percent, and each rate applies only to the portion of the price that falls within its
band.79 Under such a schedule there would still be an incentive to delay a sale or not to sell at
all. Revenue would also fall if the rates were kept the same, because the higher rates would no
longer apply to the part of the price below each threshold. The state would then need higher
rates or another tax to raise the same revenue.80 The conveyance tax return reports the price
78 Green et al. (2026), manuscript of May 15, 2026, pp. 3, 7–8. They give a range for the property tax

5.7 Design choices and tradeoffs
The appropriate form of relief depends on the problem that it is meant to solve. An assessment
that is wrong should be corrected, whereas a household that cannot pay its bill may be helped
by a change in when it pays, through deferral of the kind used in Oregon, Texas and British
Columbia. A change in rates or exemptions, or in other taxes, is different from both of these,
because it changes who bears the burden of the tax and is therefore a choice about who
receives relief and who pays for it. The revenue and the losses to residents, including the
losses that come from changes in behavior, cannot be counted until the problem has been
identified. Chapter 9 counts both for a uniform increase in county rates (section 9.5) and
for a small swap of property tax for income tax (section 9.7). Table 5.2 sets out the options
discussed in this chapter.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
In TY2023 the 7,564 C corporations that apportion their income among states reported $2.7
billion of net income, about eight times the $334.5 million reported by the 9,743 that operate
only in Hawaiʻi (section 6.3). Partnerships and S corporations are generally taxed on the re-
turns of their owners instead (section 6.5), and corporations claimed credits worth about 40
percent of their liability before credits.
However, the effects of the tax can reach far beyond the corporations that pay it. A reduction in
investment can affect wages and employment, as well as the value of land used by businesses,
and with them the collections from the GET and the income tax.3 For a business that remains in
the state, the burden can also fall on consumers through higher prices. These effects matter for
Hawaiʻi because the owners, workers and consumers need not live in the same place. Whether
they live here determines how much of the gain or loss falls on Hawaiʻi residents. It is uncertain
how large these effects are. Estimates put the owners’ share at about 40 to 50 percent (section
6.2), but they are imprecise, and there is only one calculation of how a rate change would affect
total revenue in Hawaiʻi. It uses 2010 data and responses estimated across the US (section 6.2).
For C corporations, the uncertainty is greatest for a change in the rate. A multistate business
pays Hawaiʻi tax on a share of its total profit. The formula that determines this share includes
the property and payroll of the business in the state, so that the business owes more tax here
when it adds a building or hires a worker, even if its total profit is unchanged. The deductions
for an investment matter as well. A business generally cannot deduct the full cost in the year
it invests. It deducts the cost over the life of the asset, and a deduction that it must wait for
is worth less than one it receives at once. Thus, under the current structure, part of the tax is
levied on new investment and jobs in Hawaiʻi. However, it is hard to predict what a change in
the rate would do to investment, employment and revenue.
We are more certain about two changes to the structure of the tax. For an integrated multistate
business, apportionment assigns a share of firmwide taxable profits rather than measuring prof-
its separately in Hawaiʻi. Sales-only apportionment would remove property and payroll from
the formula for multistate firms, reducing the tax disincentive to locate jobs and investment
in Hawaiʻi (section 6.3). Hawaiʻi has not adopted federal bonus depreciation, which allows
larger deductions in the year an investment is made. The state retains a $25,000 limit on
investment eligible for immediate deductions, reduced when qualifying investment exceeds
$200,000. Expensing would allow a business to deduct the cost of qualifying new investment
immediately, lowering the cost of marginal investment relative to deductions spread over time
(section 6.4). Both changes would lower the tax on a new investment in Hawaiʻi. How many
jobs this would bring is uncertain, because the evidence on employment is unsettled. They
also apply to different firms. A business that operates only in Hawaiʻi already has all of its in-
come assigned to the state, so sales-only apportionment would not change its tax. Its tax on a
new investment would fall only under expensing. Thus, they appear to be changes that would
make the tax more efficient, particularly in combination. Whether the state should adopt them
depends on how much revenue they would cost and how that revenue would be replaced. We
do not estimate either in this report (section 6.7).
3 Business property is a large part of the property tax base. In our Hawaiʻi household model, commer-
cial, hotel, industrial and agricultural property accounts for about $10.3 million, or a third, of the
$30.6 million a year that a 1 percent increase in all county property tax rates would raise (section
9.5). This is a property tax result, not a corporate one.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
Section 6.1 describes the tax and its revenue. Section 6.2 reviews the evidence on investment,
profits and who bears the tax. Sections 6.3 and 6.4 consider apportionment and the deductions
for investment and losses, and section 6.5 considers businesses taxed on the returns of their
owners. Section 6.6 considers real estate investment trusts, and section 6.7 compares the
options.
6.1 The corporate income tax
Not all business income in Hawaiʻi is subject to corporate income tax. C corporations pay the
tax on their profits. That describes who pays in a legal sense, the statutory incidence of the
tax. It is not the same as who bears the cost. A corporation can pass the tax on to its workers
through lower wages, to landowners through lower rents, or to its customers through higher
prices, and section 6.2 shows that much of the burden may fall on them. For partnerships
and S corporations, income is generally taxed as part of the income of their owners. Hawaiʻi
also allows these businesses to pay at the business level and provides a credit to the owners
(section 6.5).4 Thus, a change in the corporate tax rate has different effects on different types
of businesses. Changes in individual income taxes can affect businesses as well as workers,
because they affect the tax paid on income from businesses owned in these other forms.
The ordinary corporate tax rates are 4.4 percent on taxable income up to $25,000, 5.4 percent
on the next $75,000, and 6.4 percent above $100,000. They apply to profits, not to the amount
of sales. A business can have high revenue but pay little tax if it also has high costs. Net
capital gains are subject to an alternative calculation, and banks and certain other financial
institutions pay a separate tax on their income, called the franchise tax.5 By contrast, the GET
applies to receipts. It can therefore impose a tax on a business even if there is no taxable profit.
Chapter 3 considers its effect on production. In our household model, about 37 percent of
state GET liability is collected on sales between businesses. The model assigns this tax to the
final buyers, on the assumption that it is passed forward in prices (section 9.3).6
In Figure 6.3, net collections fall by 17.3 percent from FY2024 to FY2025, while gross pay-
ments increase by 5.4 percent. The difference is due to refunds, which more than doubled.
Refunds adjust the amounts previously paid to reflect the tax liability after credits. Conse-
quently, the decline in revenue is not sufficient to establish whether there was a reduction in
investment or in the profits reported by businesses. That would require information from their
returns, including the years to which the payments apply. Payments and refunds in a given
year need not reflect only that year’s business activity.
4 HRS §235-51.5(a), (b) and (e); DOTAX (n.d.-c); see section 6.5.

6.2 Investment, profits, and who bears the tax
Business taxes can affect the level of investment and employment, and the distribution of
income between owners, workers and consumers. They can also affect how much income is
reported in a state without an equivalent change in production. For example, a hotel company
deciding where to build next could choose a site on the mainland instead of one here. A busi-
ness could also expand its existing operations in Hawaiʻi more slowly. It could report more of
its profits in another state without changing where it produces anything. All of these responses
can reduce the tax base with different implications for the incomes of Hawaiʻi residents.
To see how the tax can affect workers, consider a business deciding whether to make an
investment in Hawaiʻi. The business compares the income it would receive after tax with the
cost, taking into account the return it could earn elsewhere. If the tax makes this investment
less attractive, the business may decide to wait or invest somewhere else. If the investment
would have increased workers’ productivity, they may receive lower wages as a result. Thus,
the tax can affect the income of workers even if the business continues its existing operations
in the state.
Some businesses may be able to earn more than is needed to keep them in a given location.
The business may have a valuable location, or customers who are willing to pay more for its
product. In either case, it can earn income above the return needed to remain there. A tax on
this additional income can therefore reduce the income of the owners with a relatively small
effect on that decision. However, the state must be able to tax that income. A business with a
valuable brand could earn additional profits while also having opportunities to report income
elsewhere. An inability to move production does not necessarily imply an inability to move
reported income.
Giroud and Rauh (2019) examine the effects of state taxes on business activity in the US. An
increase of one percentage point in the corporate tax rate leads to a reduction of about 0.4
percent in employment at existing C-corporation establishments. Approximately half of the
effect is offset by a change in activity within the same firms in other states.14 For Hawaiʻi,
attracting activity can be valuable even if there is no increase in the number of jobs in the US
as a whole. However, the firms in this study are large businesses with operations in several
states.15 A business serving local consumers from a single location in Hawaiʻi may have fewer
opportunities to respond in this way.
The effects on workers are not limited to employment. Fuest et al. (2018) examine changes
14 Giroud and Rauh (2019), pp. 1265–1266 and 1287–1289, Table 3; for the offset in other states,
p. 1265 and p. 1290, Table 4. The sample includes firms with at least 100 employees and operations
in at least two states over 1977–2011 (p. 1265). A one-percentage-point increase in the rate reduces
employment at existing establishments by approximately 0.4 percent for C corporations and 0.2
percent for pass-throughs. This is different from a 1 percent proportional increase in the tax rate.
15 Sapollnik and Swonder (2025) study a different response: the entry of new employer businesses.
Five years after substantial corporate tax cuts, they find that entry is roughly 10 percent higher,
although the new businesses tend to have lower predicted growth potential. Their sample covers the
contiguous states, so it does not include Hawaiʻi.

in municipal business taxes in Germany. Among firms subject to the tax, they estimate that
about half of the burden falls on workers in the form of lower wages.16 The estimated share
of the burden is smaller when the calculation includes firms not subject to the tax. Thus,
workers as well as the owners of a business can bear the tax. Applying the estimate to Hawaiʻi
would require assumptions about the businesses and workers in the state and how wages are
determined.
Using US data on wages, rents and business location, Suárez Serrato and Zidar (2023) esti-
mate that roughly 40 percent of the burden of state corporate taxes falls on business own-
ers, with the rest on workers and landowners.17 Further work by Suárez Serrato and Zidar
(2024), using how the labor demand of existing firms and local productivity respond to tax
changes, finds that business owners bear about half of the burden, workers 25 to 40 percent
and landowners 10 to 25 percent.18 Productivity matters for these estimates because a tax cut
can raise average productivity in two ways. The firms already there can become more produc-
tive, or the state can become more attractive to highly productive firms. The average can be
the same in both cases, but the implications for the income of workers and landowners are
different. To interpret these estimates for Hawaiʻi, we would need to know both the response
to the tax and the types of businesses responsible for it.
Suárez Serrato and Zidar (2016) used a model estimated across US local labor markets to
calculate the corporate tax rate that would raise the most revenue in each state. The Hawaiʻi
calculation combines national estimates of how businesses and workers respond with 2010
data on the state’s revenue mix, share of US business establishments and apportionment for-
mula. Counting only corporate revenue, they put the rate for Hawaiʻi at 28.6 percent, far above
the actual 6.4 percent. The answer changes when the calculation includes income and sales
taxes lost when wages fall and workers leave. In those data, Hawaiʻi raised 57 times as much
from these taxes as from the corporate tax, against 17 times in the median state. Including
revenue from these other taxes, the 2010 analysis put the rate that maximizes total revenue
in Hawaiʻi at 0.7 percent, or 1.0 percent after allowing for the weight on sales in the appor-
tionment formula.19 The calculation shows how the rate affects total revenue once other taxes
are counted. However, the figure of 0.7 percent is not estimated from Hawaiʻi’s experience
with its own corporate tax, since the calculation is a single calibration of the model that com-
bines national averages for the responses of businesses and workers with Hawaiʻi data from

the tax, the estimated share of the burden on workers is 50.5 percent, with a standard error of 17.0
percentage points. The share is about 22 percent with firms not subject to the tax in the sample.
17 Suárez Serrato and Zidar (2023), pp. 3401–3403, Table 1. They estimate shares of 38.1 percent

2010, a recession year. The authors do not report a range of uncertainty for the result, and
the uncertainty is likely to be large. In addition, the GET and individual income tax now raise
about 20 times as much as the corporate tax, compared with 57 times in the data used for the
calculation. Thus, if the other inputs of the model were unchanged, the rate that maximizes
total revenue would be higher than 0.7 percent.
In a later study, the same authors pool all states and put the rate that maximizes total state tax
revenue close to 10 percent (Suárez Serrato and Zidar 2018). Most states charge less than this,
and on these estimates a cut in the rate would not pay for itself.20 Hawaiʻi is not a typical state
in this respect. Its other taxes raise much more, relative to the corporate tax, than those of
most states, and so the rate that maximizes total revenue is lower here. It is therefore possible
that a substantial cut in Hawaiʻi’s corporate rate would raise total revenue, once we count the
effects on the bases of the other taxes. However, the only calculation for Hawaiʻi is the one
from 2010, and this is not sufficient to establish that it would.21
A corporate tax cut that raises wages need not benefit low-income households if the higher
wages go to workers who are already highly paid. Kennedy et al. (2026) study the federal
corporate tax cut and find that the gains went mainly to owners and highly paid workers.
Within firms, they find that earnings rose for the highest-paid tenth of workers, and for ex-
ecutives most of all, while earnings did not rise for the other nine-tenths. Since the workers
who gained pay individual income tax on their higher earnings, part of the corporate revenue
loss can be recovered. The authors also argue that it could cost the economy less to tax labor
income than to tax corporate income, although they do not estimate the difference. Suppose
that they are right about these costs, and that the goal is to raise revenue from high incomes.
Then a tax on those incomes directly could raise the same amount at a lower cost than a tax
on corporations. However, the federal result may not apply to a state tax, because it is easier
for firms and workers to move between states than to leave the country, and so a state tax
can shift more of its burden onto the workers and landowners who stay.22 In our household
model, raising the top income-tax rate costs taxpayers $2.02 for each dollar the state collects,
or $1.42 if no high-income household leaves the state in response (Table 9.1 and section 9.2).
Of the $2.02, $1.93 falls on residents and $0.09 on nonresident and part-year filers. We have
no comparable estimate for the corporate tax.

are imprecise.

===== page 148 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
If the corporate tax lowers wages and the value of land, the state and the counties also collect
less revenue from their other taxes. Lower wages reduce the revenue from the income tax and
the GET, and lower land values reduce the revenue from the property tax. Thus, the corporate
tax may raise less revenue, on net, than it collects. Most of the federal evidence finds that
the revenue recovered through other taxes pays for only a small part of a corporate tax cut.
Chodorow-Reich et al. (2024), for example, find that the federal cut increased investment, but
estimate that additional individual income-tax receipts would offset only 1.5 percent of the
corporate revenue loss in the first year and 6.3 percent in the tenth.23 In Hawaiʻi, the other
taxes are large relative to the corporate tax. They raise about 20 times as much, so a change
of 1 percent in their bases is worth about a fifth of corporate tax collections.
However, the state would gain back less from a cut in the corporate tax than this ratio suggests.
Not all of the revenue from the other taxes moves with the wages of residents. Part of the GET
is paid by visitors. In the estimates reported in chapter 3, nonresidents as a whole pay about 35
percent of the tax, and visitors alone about 23 percent (section 3.3). Another part of the GET
base is purchases by businesses (section 3.2). Neither of these parts of the GET base moves
one for one with residents’ wages. However, neither is fixed when the corporate tax changes.
Purchases by businesses depend on the level of business activity, and the number of visitors
the state can serve depends in part on corporate investment in hotels and other facilities.
A cut in the corporate tax would give up the part of the tax that is exported, but tax exporting
applies to only part of the burden. The studies above put the owners’ share of the tax at about
40 to 50 percent, with workers and landowners here bearing the rest, and Hawaiʻi exports the
owners’ share only to the extent that the owners live elsewhere. The tax exported after a cut
also depends on which tax would replace the revenue, since the individual income tax is levied
mainly on residents, but visitors pay part of the GET. The federal deduction for state taxes also
changes each of these shares (section 1.2). Rousslang and Chow (2017), in a study for the
2015–2017 Tax Review Commission, concluded that residents would lose from eliminating
the corporate income tax. They assumed that most corporate investment in Hawaiʻi is owned
by nonresidents, so that a repeal would transfer income to shareholders elsewhere and give up
tax now exported. They counted the gains in residents’ wages and rents. However, they did not
count the additional income tax and GET that the state would collect if wages, employment
and land values were higher. This omission is the main reason why their conclusion differs
from the one above.24
A lower rate increases the income of the owners of assets already in place. It may also af-
fect whether a business chooses to make an investment in the first place. The two structural
changes described at the start of the chapter lower the tax on new investment in different

and report an increase of 8 to 14 percent in tangible corporate investment. The offset includes tax
on higher wages, distributions from C corporations and profits of S corporations. It is a projection
of the revenue response to the corporate provisions, not a measure of income-tax receipts collected
after the reform. Kennedy et al. (2026), p. 3398, explain why firms and workers are less responsive
to federal taxes than to state and local taxes.

the revenue with the GET or the individual income tax, about 35 and 32 percent of which they
estimate is exported (pp. 22–23).

ways. Sales-only apportionment (section 6.3) mainly affects multistate firms. Their property
and payroll in Hawaiʻi would no longer add to the income assigned to the state. Thus, a new
building or an additional employee here would no longer increase their Hawaiʻi tax. Expens-
ing, a state deduction for the full cost of an investment when it is made (section 6.4), reaches
all firms, but for multistate corporations it is not tied to investment in Hawaiʻi, and these cor-
porations report about eight times the net income of corporations operating only in Hawaiʻi
(section 6.3). For a business that operates only in Hawaiʻi, only expensing matters, because
all of its income is already assigned to the state.25 These options must be compared together
with the taxes used to finance them.
The corporate income tax is not the only thing that discourages business investment in Hawaiʻi.
The costs of doing business here and the rules businesses must follow matter too, and it would
be a mistake to think that changes in business taxes alone could solve the state’s competi-
tiveness problems. The wider business climate is beyond the scope of this report. Still, the tax
deserves attention. Capital moves more easily than almost anything else the state taxes, which
is why business capital is among the most responsive bases in Figure 1.4. If the tax leads a
business to put a new building or machine in another state, part of the cost is borne by work-
ers and landowners here. It is uncertain how much Hawaiʻi’s corporate tax does this. For a
multistate business, it works mainly through the apportionment formula (section 6.3).
Our household model in chapter 9 does not include the corporate income tax, so Table 9.1
has no row for it, and we have no estimate of how a cut in the corporate rate would change
the revenue from the income tax and the GET.
6.3 Apportionment
For an integrated business operating across state lines, there is no uniquely measurable amount
of profit earned in each state. Formulary apportionment applies the state’s statutory tax to a
fraction of the business’s total taxable profits. Holding total profits and the firmwide totals of
the formula factors fixed, the tax works like a set of taxes on the business’s property, payroll
and sales in the state. The implied rates on these factors scale with the firm’s overall profitabil-
ity.26 So the choice of formula affects where a business puts its jobs and buildings. Hawaiʻi
uses an apportionment formula to determine the share of a business’s income taxed by the
state. Under the general formula, this share depends on the business’s property and payroll as
well as its sales, even when its total profit is fixed. For a business in several states, the share is
the average of three fractions: its property in Hawaiʻi divided by its total property, its payroll
25 The Commission discussed pairing accelerated depreciation with sales-only apportionment at its
meeting of November 9, 2026 (Tax Review Commission 2026a, p. 6). On November 18 it returned to
sales-only apportionment and discussed accelerated depreciation for housing (Tax Review Commis-
sion 2026b, pp. 2–3, draft minutes). For a multistate firm, the Hawaiʻi tax affects where investment
is made mainly through the apportionment factors, and so expensing matters mainly for firms that
operate only in Hawaiʻi. The ratio is from DOTAX (2025d): $2.7 billion of net income for apportioned
C corporations against $334.5 million for those operating only in Hawaiʻi.
26 McLure (1981), who describes the state tax as “a composite of taxes levied on whatever factors enter
the state’s apportionment formula,” and McLure (1980). Gordon and Wilson (1986) add that the
implied rates on these factors differ among firms in the same state, which creates further distortions.
147

===== page 150 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
here divided by its total payroll, and its sales assigned here divided by its total sales.27 If each
fraction is one-tenth, then one-tenth of the business’s income is generally assigned to Hawaiʻi.
Because the three fractions determine how much income is assigned to Hawaiʻi, the state does
not need to estimate the profit earned by the business at each of its offices.
With a flat tax rate, no credits and a formula based only on sales, the mechanical tax on
each dollar of Hawaiʻi sales is the statutory rate times the firm’s total taxable profit per dollar
of firmwide sales, holding total profits and firmwide sales fixed. The implied rate on sales
therefore differs with the firm’s profitability.
Consider a business with activity in several states, and suppose that it hires one more person
in Hawaiʻi while nothing else changes. Its share of payroll or property in the state goes up, and
more of its income is taxed here even though the tax rate has not changed. The three-factor
formula thus works in part as a tax on the jobs and buildings that the state wants to attract.28
With a formula based on sales alone, that part of the tax goes away. Customers are harder to
move than workers or buildings. A firm can serve customers in Hawaiʻi from another state, but
the customers themselves remain here. This is the main argument for a formula based only on
sales, now the main formula in 36 of the 44 states with a corporate income tax, as described
in Mazerov (2005), ITEP (2012) and Tax Foundation (2026a); Mazerov and ITEP are critical
of the change, citing its cost in revenue and the weak evidence that it creates jobs.29 If the
goal is to stop taxing the jobs and buildings the state wants to attract, a formula based only
on sales is one of the two more certain ways to do it that section 6.2 describes.
Figure 6.5 illustrates why some firms would gain while others lose. Both firms are hypothetical.
For Firm A, Hawaiʻi accounts for half of property and payroll, but one-tenth of sales. Under a
formula based only on sales, the income share assigned here falls from about 37 percent to 10
percent. For Firm B, Hawaiʻi accounts for one-tenth of property and payroll, but half of sales.
The income share rises from about 23 percent to 50 percent. Each firm has the same profit,
locations and jobs under both formulas. The change in the income share assigned to Hawaiʻi
is due to the formula alone.

Even with the same rates, sales only would raise the tax on some businesses and lower it
on others. It goes up where a business’s share of sales in Hawaiʻi is bigger than its share of
property and payroll here, and down where it is smaller. The first group includes businesses
based elsewhere that sell here without many workers or buildings in the state. Businesses
based in Hawaiʻi that sell mostly elsewhere would pay less, and the throwback rule limits
the cut on goods sent to places where the seller is not taxable.31 Some sellers would not pay
more at all: federal law bars a state from taxing the income of a business whose only activity
there is asking for orders for goods that are approved and shipped from outside the state.32 In

among states report most of the net income of C corporations in Hawaiʻi.33 An analysis of other
states draws the same distinction (Mazerov 2005). A switch tends to lose revenue where the

would raise the effective tax rate on businesses based outside Hawaiʻi whose share of sales
here exceeds their share of property and payroll, even with the statutory rate unchanged. A
reform could pair it with a lower rate.
Businesses that sell into Hawaiʻi from elsewhere, including online sellers, are already counted
in the current formula. A sale of goods is assigned to Hawaiʻi when the goods are delivered
here, and since TY2020 a sale of a service is assigned here to the extent that the service is used
here. State law also presumes that a business with no physical presence is doing business in
Hawaiʻi once it has 200 transactions or $100,000 of gross income from Hawaiʻi sources in the
current or previous calendar year.35 A sales-only formula would change neither rule. It would
raise the weight on these sales from one-third of the formula to all of it.

sales-only apportionment would raise corporate income tax revenue (Tax Review Commission
2026a, 2026d).36
6.4 Investment deductions and losses
Tax deductions affect investment through their timing as well as their amount. The cost of an
investment is paid when it is made, but the associated deductions may be spread over several
years. The deductions have a higher value if they are received earlier, because the tax savings
can be used in the meantime. This depends on the return available during that time and
whether there is sufficient taxable income to use them. Changing their timing can thus reduce
the cost of additional investment without reducing the tax on income from all investments
already in place.
Federal rules for deducting investment costs over time generally apply in Hawaiʻi, but there
are important exceptions. For tax years beginning after December 31, 2025, Act 35 of 2026
updated the reference to federal law without adopting bonus depreciation, which allows larger
deductions in the year an investment is made, or the new allowance for certain buildings used
in production. The state retains a $25,000 limit on investment eligible for immediate deduc-
34 Mazerov (2005), p. 29. Mazerov adds that a gain is unlikely in states without a throwback rule. When
California made a sales-only formula mandatory for most multistate firms from 2013, after allowing
them to choose it, the Legislative Analyst’s Office projected about $1 billion a year of additional
revenue from 2013–14 (LAO 2012). That is a different change from the one Hawaiʻi would make.

that a business that interacts with a customer through its website or app is doing business in the
customer’s state (Art. IV.C).

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
tions, reduced when qualifying investment exceeds $200,000.37 Nor does Act 35 adopt the
new federal provision allowing immediate deductions for domestic research expenses.38 The
state and federal taxes can therefore treat the same investment differently, and a comparison
of statutory rates leaves out these differences in the base.
An argument in favor of expensing follows an investment whose return is exactly equal to its
cost. With expensing, the cost is deducted from taxable income immediately. Suppose that
future revenue is taxed at the same rate, and that the deduction is worth the same amount
even if the business makes a loss. The investment still breaks even after tax. Thus, under these
assumptions, the tax does not affect the decision to undertake this investment.39 This result
holds only if the deduction is taken in the year the investment is made and is worth its full
amount even to a business with a loss, and if the tax rate is the same when the receipts are
later taxed.40
There are also arguments against expensing. The result is only true for an investment that
just breaks even. Where a business expects to earn more than it needs to, its choice of location
depends on the tax on all of its profits, and expensing leaves that tax in place. If interest is also
deductible, an investment paid for with debt can face a negative tax rate.41 A state deduction
is also not tied to investment in the state. Hawaiʻi starts from business income and uses the
apportionment formula to assign a share to the state. So faster deductions for a machine
bought in another state lower Hawaiʻi tax just as much as for one bought here, holding fixed
the share assigned to Hawaiʻi.42 Finally, the state would lose revenue at first. Businesses would
still write off what they bought before the change one year at a time, and write off new things
up front, so for a while the state would be paying for both. Denying the remaining deductions
on past purchases to avoid this cost would raise the tax on investments already made.

pp. 2, 24–26); DOTAX Announcement No. 2026-06, pp. 2–3. Sections 168(j), (k), (m) and (n) are
inoperative for Hawaiʻi purposes. HRS §235-2.4(o), as set out in Act 35 (SLH 2026), p. 24, retains the
section 179 cap of $25,000 and the $200,000 phaseout threshold, with additional state exceptions.

40 This example is meant to illustrate the economic argument, and it is not a statement of Hawaiʻi law.
Suppose that an investment costs 𝐼, that its future receipts are worth 𝑉 in today’s dollars, and that
the tax rate 𝑡 is constant. The business keeps (1 − 𝑡)𝑉 of the receipts after tax. With an immediate
deduction of the full cost, it also pays 𝑡𝐼 less in tax, and so the investment costs it only (1 − 𝑡)𝐼.
Thus, the investment is worth (1 − 𝑡)(𝑉 − 𝐼) after tax. Notice that this is zero when 𝑉 = 𝐼, since
the tax takes the same share of the cost as it does of the receipts. We have assumed that the rate
used to convert future receipts into today’s dollars takes risk into account. We have also assumed
that there is no separate deduction for financing. With such a deduction, the calculation would be
different and full expensing need not give this result.

===== page 154 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
A business also might not have the income to use a deduction right away. Then the deduction
is worth less, and if the business never makes any money, the deduction may be worth nothing.
In Hawaiʻi, losses after 2017 can generally reduce taxable income in later years, with no time
limit, but the deduction cannot exceed 80 percent of that year’s taxable income. They generally
cannot reduce tax on earlier years, except that farming losses can be applied to the preceding
two years.43 The state takes a share of the profits right away, but it only shares in the losses
once the business is profitable. So a business losing money at first can pay more tax than one
that earns the same amount in a steady way. Expensing has to be considered together with
how losses are treated.
6.5 Pass-through businesses
Businesses can respond to individual income taxes as well as corporate taxes. For partnerships
and S corporations, business income is generally taxed as income of the owners. The rate on
an additional dollar therefore depends on their other income as well as profits. This is why
a change in the individual tax can affect the incentive to undertake investment in a business.
However, not every response to an individual tax change represents an effect on investment.
Owners can also change their work, move to another jurisdiction, or change the amount of
income that they report.44
Giroud and Rauh (2019) find different effects for different types of businesses. Employment
at existing pass-through establishments falls by about 0.2 percent following an increase of one
percentage point in personal income-tax rates. It responds to these rates rather than corporate
tax rates.45 The businesses are large and have operations in several states, providing opportu-
nities to respond by moving activity. Businesses that depend more heavily on a local market
may have fewer such opportunities. The study shows which tax matters for each legal form,
but an estimate of the employment effect for Hawaiʻi requires assumptions about the types of
businesses affected.
Studies give different answers on how much of a tax on business owners passes to their workers,
typically dependent on the context and setting. In particular, the answer depends on which
workers are compared: the employees of the affected firms, or other workers in the same local
43 Act 35 (SLH 2026), pp. 23–24, retains IRC §172 as of December 31, 2019, subject to HRS §235-
7(d). Act 27 (SLH 2018); Form N-30 Instructions (2025), p. 2. The instructions separate losses for
tax years ending after December 31, 2017 from the limit that applies to tax years beginning after
that date. In general, a loss after 2017 may be deducted in any number of later years, up to 80
percent of taxable income in each of those years, and it cannot be used for earlier years, except that
farming losses may also be applied to income in the preceding two years. Other special provisions,
including the transition rules, can also affect the treatment of a loss.
44 Goodman et al. (2025) study the federal deduction for pass-through business income introduced in
2018. They find an increase in reported business income eligible for the deduction, and the share
of partnership income paid to partners as guaranteed payments, which do not qualify, fell by about
15 percent in 2018. They find little evidence of changes in real economic activity, as measured by
investment, employment or wages paid to non-owners. In our Hawaiʻi household model, allowing
the highest-income households to leave the state raises the cost of a higher top income-tax rate from
$1.42 to $2.02 per net dollar. Migration accounts for about 21 of the 50 cents lost on each dollar
(section 9.2). The model applies this response to all high-income households, whether or not they
own a business.
45 Giroud and Rauh (2019), p. 1287 and Table 3; see section 6.2.

===== page 155 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
economy. Risch (2024) studies the 2013 increase in the top federal income tax rate, which
raised the tax on the owners of many S corporations. Risch finds that for each additional dollar
of tax on the owners, the earnings of their employees grew by 11 to 18 cents less. Nearly
all of this reduction was for the highest-paid 30 percent of workers in the affected firms.46
While owners bore most of the burden of the tax change, their higher-paid employees bore
part of it, similar to the 2017 corporate tax cut (section 6.2). However, this evidence comes
from a federal tax change. For state and local business taxes, the evidence is on corporate
taxes, which also fall partly on workers through lower wages, and on employment. No study
measures how much of a state tax on pass-through owners falls on their workers. Mobility
between states gives no reason to expect a smaller share to fall on workers at the state level.47
Kindsgrab (2022) looks at the same 2013 increase by comparing local economies where top
earners had larger or smaller shares of income. Kindsgrab finds no detectable effect on the
wages of other workers, and Risch’s estimates are small enough to be consistent with that.48
None of this implies that a change in Hawaiʻi’s top income tax rates would have much effect on
revenue through the wages that these businesses pay. Many taxpayers with high incomes own
businesses, but the top rates also apply to those who do not. Thus, only part of the response to
a higher top rate works through businesses. The top-rate estimates in chapter 9 treat business
income like other taxable income. They also let 14.5 cents of each dollar of tax on the owners
of partnerships and S corporations fall on the wages of highly paid private-sector employees,
the midpoint of Risch’s range of 11 to 18 cents, and this changes the estimated revenue very
little (section 9.2).
In Hawaiʻi, partnerships and S corporations can choose to pay the elective pass-through entity
tax. This is an annual choice. The tax is 9 percent of the Hawaiʻi taxable income allocated to
eligible owners, down from 11 percent in TY2023. This income includes payments to partners
that do not depend on the profits of the partnership. Each member can then claim a credit for
their share of the tax paid.49 Thus, the payment by the business is not an additional tax of the
same amount. To determine its effect, we must consider the return of the business together
with the returns of its owners, including the credit and the timing of each payment.
Many other states have an elective tax of this kind. Hawaiʻi’s 9 percent is close to California’s

Hawaiʻi’s credit is nonrefundable. From TY2024, though, it can be carried forward when it
is more than the member’s tax for the year, which was not possible for TY2023 credits. That
matters for a taxpayer whose credit is greater than the individual income tax they would
otherwise pay. From TY2025, members who claim the credit must also add their share of the
entity tax back into their Hawaiʻi taxable income. Before then, the income passed through
to members for Hawaiʻi purposes was already reduced by the entity tax, and they claimed
a credit for the tax as well. Thus, in effect, the state allowed a deduction for its own tax in
TY2023 and TY2024. The add-back closes that. For TY2023, the cost of the deduction was at
most the entity tax paid times the top individual rate, 11 percent of $185.4 million, or about
$20 million.51

qualifying state income tax, the owners have less income passed through to them for federal
purposes.52 The federal deduction for state and local taxes is capped, but these payments are
not subject to the cap, so the election helps any owner whose state and local taxes exceed the
cap, as well as owners who do not itemize.53 How much the owners save depends on federal
rules and on the owner. For the state, the election can move some of the tax from the owners’
returns to the business. The state gets more from the business and less from the owners, who
claim the credit. To find out whether it comes out ahead, we need to compare the two with
what the owners would have paid without the election.

6.6 Real estate investment trusts
A real estate investment trust (REIT) is a corporation that holds mainly real estate, pays out
at least 90 percent of its taxable income as dividends and deducts the dividends it pays. Its
income is therefore taxed on the returns of its shareholders and not on its own.55 Hawaiʻi
follows the federal rules.56 Bills to disallow or limit the deduction have been introduced in
nearly every session since 2017, and one passed in 2019 and was vetoed.57
DBEDT (2016) counted 42 REITs operating in Hawaiʻi in 2014, with $7.8 billion of Hawaiʻi
assets at cost. The 33 with Hawaiʻi property reported $720.6 million of positive net income
apportioned to the state in 2014, compared with $208.8 million in 2013. DBEDT put the
corporate tax forgone at no more than $36 million in 2014, at an assumed rate of 5 percent,
and at about $9.6 million a year on average over 2009–2014. A 2026 bill cites a DOTAX
estimate that the deduction “may be as much as $26.8 million” for TY2026, without describing
the method.58 That is 6.7 percent of net corporate collections in FY2025 (section 6.1). Each
of these figures is calculated as if REITs would own the same property, and finance it in the
same way, without the deduction.
The deduction does not make this income free of tax. It determines which state taxes it. To
see this, consider a building in Hawaiʻi whose owner lives in another state. If this person owns
the building directly or through a partnership, then the rents and gains are Hawaiʻi-source
income, and Hawaiʻi taxes them (sections 1.3 and 9.6). If instead a REIT owns the building,
the income is paid out to its owners as dividends. Dividends are taxed by the state where the
shareholder lives, and they are not taxed at all when the shareholder is a retirement fund or
another exempt investor. Hawaiʻi therefore collects only from the shareholders who live here.
On DBEDT’s estimate, they paid about $955,000 on $19.1 million of these REITs’ dividends in

with the database accessed on April 7, 2025. These are TY2023 amounts, when the rate was 11
percent. Their sum is 146.8 + 38.6 = $185.4 million. To estimate the fiscal effect, we must match
these amounts with member credits and the corresponding cash collections.
