===== page 158 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
2014. Suppose finally that a C corporation owned the building. It would pay the corporate tax,
but Hawaiʻi would not tax its nonresident shareholders either. A REIT also pays the GET on its
rents and the county property tax, like any other owner.59 Thus, the REIT form differs from
the others in one respect, which is that Hawaiʻi collects no income tax on the income from
Hawaiʻi real estate that goes to nonresident shareholders. If the deduction were disallowed,
the income of all shareholders would be taxed at the corporate rate. Resident shareholders
would then be taxed twice unless they were given a credit, and exempt investors would be
taxed for the first time.
Who would bear this tax? The answer depends on what can respond to it. REIT shares trade
in a national market, and investors will not hold Hawaiʻi property if it pays a lower return
than they can earn elsewhere. Thus, a small jurisdiction that taxes capital at source cannot
lower the return that investors receive, and the tax falls on whatever cannot leave (Gordon
1986). Land cannot leave, and neither can a building that is already in place. For these the
tax is capitalized, as with a tax on land (section 5.3): the value of the property falls when the
change is announced, and whoever holds the shares at that date bears the loss. Since most of
these shareholders live elsewhere, this part of the tax would be exported. New construction
and renovation are different, because the REIT must earn the same return after tax as it did
before. Less is then built, unless another owner builds it, and the burden moves to land values,
to tenants and, in the case of hotels, to workers and visitors (sections 5.3 and 6.2). These
conclusions follow from how the tax works, and we have not been able to check them against
experience. New Hampshire taxes publicly traded REITs without the deduction, but we have
found no estimate of the effect there.60
How much the tax would raise depends on the legal form of the owner and on its taxable
income, and the owner can change both. First, a REIT can sell the property to a partnership, a
private fund or a pension fund. We know that the form in which businesses are held responds to
how each form is taxed (Gordon and Slemrod 2000; Goolsbee 2004). If these buyers value the
property nearly as highly as the REIT does, then the price falls little and the state collects little,
and all that is lost is whatever advantage the REIT form had in raising capital. Second, a REIT
can borrow more, since interest is deductible. Firms do borrow more when state corporate
taxes rise (Heider and Ljungqvist 2015). Third, a multistate REIT is not taxed on the profit of
its Hawaiʻi buildings. Its Hawaiʻi income is a share of its total income, and the apportionment
formula sets that share (section 6.3). Each of these responses would bring revenue below the
figures above. In addition, the base is volatile, as it was about three and a half times as large in
2014 as in 2013. A temporary tax, such as the four-year tax in the 2019 bill, would give owners
less reason to restructure, and it would fall mostly on existing property. However, if investors
expected the tax to be repeated, they would require a higher return on new investment here.
The same property is already taxed by the counties. The property tax is owed whatever the
owner’s legal form, borrowing or tax status, and it costs taxpayers $1.00 to $1.02 per net dollar
raised (Table 9.1). Owners on the mainland paid 52.0 percent of the tax on hotel and resort
property in fiscal year 2016 (section 5.3). A 1 percent increase in the rates on commercial,
hotel, industrial and agricultural property would raise about $10.3 million (section 9.5), so
$26.8 million corresponds to an increase of about 2.6 percent in those rates. However, this
59 HRS §237-13(9).
60 N.H. Code Admin. R. Rev 302.05(d); DBEDT (2016), p. 12.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 6. Business taxes
revenue belongs to the counties. For the state to use it, it would have to reduce its transfers to
the counties, as in the swap in section 9.7, or impose a levy of its own, which would require
a constitutional amendment (section 5.1). We have no estimate of the cost per net dollar of
disallowing the deduction. It would be close to 1.0 only if owners could not change their legal
form or financing and little new building were deterred.
Narrower designs follow from narrower goals. If the goal is to tax nonresidents on income
from Hawaiʻi real estate as they would be taxed if they owned it directly, a tax on the REIT
with a credit for resident shareholders would do so, as the elective pass-through entity tax
does for partnerships (section 6.5), though it would be open to the same responses. If the
goal is to keep a corporation from moving its own income into a REIT that it controls, the
deduction can be limited for REITs owned mainly by one corporation, or that corporation can
be denied a deduction for what it pays such a REIT, as in the model statute of the Multistate
Tax Commission (2011). If the goal is revenue from the owners of commercial and resort
property at the lowest cost, the property tax reaches all of them. Since TY2022 a REIT must
identify itself to DOTAX and attach its federal return.61 We found no published tabulation of
these returns. One showing Hawaiʻi income, debt and ownership would allow the base and
these responses to be estimated.
6.7 Design choices and tradeoffs
It is not settled who bears the tax on C corporations, and there is only one calculation of how
a rate cut would change total revenue in Hawaiʻi (section 6.2). For pass-through businesses, a
study of large multistate firms finds a modest employment response to personal income taxes,
and a study of a federal deduction finds little evidence of effects on employment or investment
(section 6.5).62
Expensing is appealing because an immediate deduction lowers the cost of a qualifying
marginal investment relative to deductions spread over time. With sales-only apportionment,
payroll and property would no longer be in the formula, and so a multistate firm that adds
a building or hires a worker here would not owe more Hawaiʻi tax for that reason alone. We
do not know how many jobs either change would bring. Expensing could be limited to invest-
ment in Hawaiʻi or allowed for all investment, because a state deduction is not otherwise tied
to investment in the state. Whether the state should adopt either change depends on how
much revenue it would cost and how that revenue would be replaced. Table 6.2 sets out these
and the other options in this chapter.

also does not mean that employment and investment never respond. Neither of these estimates tells
us how much the income reported by owners changes, or how much of the tax on the owners is
borne by their employees through lower earnings.

7. Wealth and estates
Hawaiʻi taxes estates worth more than $5.49 million after debts and deductions. This $5.49
million exclusion is the federal amount for a death on December 31, 2017, and because the
law fixes the exclusion at that date it does not rise with inflation.1 The tax rate is 10 percent
on the first $1 million above the exclusion and goes up in steps to 20 percent on anything
more than $10 million above the exclusion.2
The estate tax brings in little revenue in most years and a great deal in a few. From FY2013
to FY2024 collections averaged $33.9 million a year and ranged from $12.1 million to $58.1
million, and they could more than double or fall by more than half from one year to the next
(Figure 7.1).3 In FY2025 they were $405.8 million, $315 million of it (78 percent) from a one-
off receipt in September 2024.4 That year Hawaiʻi raised more death and gift tax per resident
than any other state, about $283 against $22 for the country as a whole.5 Swings like these
suggest that a few large estates pay much of the tax.6
Hawaiʻi is one of twelve states with an estate tax in 2026, along with the District of Columbia
(Table 7.2). Its exclusion is the fourth highest of the 13, and its top rate of 20 percent, shared
with Washington, is the highest (Figure 7.2). The federal exclusion is much higher, $15 million
in 2026, so a taxable estate between $5.49 million and $15 million owes tax to Hawaiʻi and
nothing to the federal government (Table 7.1).
1 The exclusion is reduced by adjusted taxable gifts and can be increased by a spouse’s unused ex-
clusion if the first spouse’s estate elects (HRS §§236E-2, 236E-6(a); Form M-6 (2025), Schedule A,
lines 5–9, and Part 2; Form M-6 Instructions (2025), p. 1; IRS (n.d.-c), basic exclusion amount table,
2017 row). For a nonresident, the exclusion is apportioned by the share of the gross estate that is
Hawaiʻi property subject to the tax (HRS §236E-6(b)). Hawaiʻi’s conformity date is now December
31, 2025, but the exclusion is still the 2017 amount and the rates are the same (Act 35 (SLH 2026),
§§5 and 7).
2 HRS §236E-8(b); Form M-6 Instructions (2025), p. 6. The marginal rate is 10 percent on the first $1
million of the estate above the exclusion, and it goes up by one point on each of the next four (11, 12,
13 and 14 percent). It is then 15.7 percent from $5 million to $10 million above the exclusion, and
20 percent after that. Act 3 (SLH 2019), §§1 and 3, created the 20 percent bracket for deaths after
December 31, 2019. Hawaiʻi also has a 2.25 percent tax on generation-skipping transfers (DOTAX
2025a, p. 30).
3 DOTAX (2007–2025), Table 1.8, “Inheritance and Estate” row, nominal dollars; our calculations. The
median year brought in $30.3 million, the average of FY2018 ($29.4 million) and FY2021 ($31.3
million). Collections are what was received in each fiscal year, not the tax owed by the estates of
people who died in that year.
4 DOTAX (2025a), section 1.15.2, p. 30.

An annual wealth tax has many advocates, but Hawaiʻi already taxes wealth in several ways,
and the estate tax is the most direct of them.7 Its critics call it a death tax, or double taxation
(section 7.1).
Take a family whose wealth is a home in Honolulu, some publicly traded stock and a family
business that has never been sold. The county already taxes the home every year, based on
what its assessor says the home is worth. The estate tax applies to the home, the stock and the
business alike, but only when an owner dies, and then only if the estate is worth more than
the exclusion after debts and deductions, which is $5.49 million for a single person with no
earlier taxable gifts. An annual wealth tax would need a value for the home, the stock and the
business every year. The stock has a market price and the assessor already values the home,
but the business has never been sold, so nobody can look up a price for it.
7 The 2001–2003 Tax Review Commission treated the estate tax as a question of conformity with
federal law, and no earlier commission recommended raising it (Karacaovali 2021, pp. 9–10). Most
of the evidence on mobility and on wealth taxes discussed in this chapter has appeared since.

===== page 163 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 7. Wealth and estates
A wealth tax is hard to avoid if the state can put a value on what it taxes and the owner cannot
move it out of the state’s reach. Hawaiʻi already taxes the kinds of wealth that pass both of
these tests. The property tax is a tax on one kind of wealth, land and buildings. The county
can put a value on them, and the owner has to pay the tax where the property is. The GET
also reaches the wealth of people who earned their income somewhere else and moved here,
when they spend it here (section 7.2). The estate tax only has to measure the value once, at
one point in time, and Hawaiʻi does it with federal rules and the federal return form. But the
owners of large estates can move or plan around it.
Whether the state comes out ahead with an estate tax therefore depends on how readily the
wealthy move. A published study with estimates for Hawaiʻi finds that the income tax the state
loses when billionaires leave exceeds the estate tax it gains. That result rests on an income tax
rate for Hawaiʻi well above the state’s 2017 rates. Recomputed at those rates, the study’s own
model gives the opposite answer, both for billionaires and for a broader tax with an exclusion
like Hawaiʻi’s (section 7.1). An annual tax on all other wealth would have to pass both tests
every year, which makes it unlikely to raise much revenue for Hawaiʻi alone (section 7.3).
7.1 The estate tax
Hawaiʻi’s estate tax largely follows federal definitions, with its own exclusion and rates, set
out at the start of this chapter. The 20 percent bracket has applied to deaths since 2020.
Like other states, Hawaiʻi relies heavily on the way the federal government has calculated the
estate in order to levy its own tax. An estate that owes Hawaiʻi tax but no federal tax still fills
out a federal return, just for Hawaiʻi, and does not send it to the IRS.8
Above $15 million the federal tax changes what the Hawaiʻi tax costs, because the estate can
deduct state estate taxes from its federal taxable estate, so each dollar of Hawaiʻi tax lowers
the federal tax by up to 40 cents (the top federal rate).9 On a taxable estate of $30 million,
the Hawaiʻi tax in Table 7.1 is about $4.3 million, but it adds only about $2.6 million to what
the estate pays in total. There is no federal tax to lower below $15 million, so the estate pays
the whole Hawaiʻi tax.
8 Rev. Proc. 2025-32, §2.14; HRS §§236E-6(a), 236E-8(b); 26 U.S.C. §2010(c). The federal exclusion
will be indexed for inflation starting in 2027. An estate has to file a Hawaiʻi return if it has to file a
federal one, or if it owes any Hawaiʻi tax. If it owes Hawaiʻi tax but does not have to file a federal
return, it still fills out a federal Form 706 for Hawaiʻi, which is then used only to compute the tax
(HRS §236E-9(a); Form M-6 Instructions (2025), pp. 1 and 3). The estates in Table 7.1 are the
simplest case. Which estates actually owe tax under either system also depends on deductions (such
as for property left to a spouse or to charity), on earlier taxable gifts and on a spouse’s unused
exclusion (HRS §§236E-2, 236E-7(1); 26 U.S.C. §§2055–2056).
9 26 U.S.C. §2058. Hawaiʻi computes its own tax on the federal taxable estate before this deduction
(HRS §236E-7). An estate gets the full 40 cents per dollar only if it is far enough above the federal
exclusion that all of the Hawaiʻi tax it deducts would otherwise have been taxed at 40 percent.

Swings like Hawaiʻi’s also occur in other states’ estate taxes, where a single death can bring in
more than many ordinary years together. When Bud Walton, a co-founder of Walmart, died in
1995, Arkansas’s estate tax revenue rose the next year from $34.8 million to $183.2 million, in
2017 dollars.11 The typical year therefore understates what the tax raises over time, since the
rare large estates count toward the average too, and a single large year overstates it. We would
need many more than twelve years of data to see large estates about as often as they really
occur, so we cannot say what the long-run average is. The Council on Revenues attributed part
of the decline it forecast for FY2026 to the unusually high FY2025 base.12
An estate tax applies to the estate before it is distributed; an inheritance tax applies to what
an heir receives. Four other states only have an inheritance tax, and Maryland has both, so 34
states have no tax of either kind (Table 7.2).13 In the West, Washington and Oregon are the
only other states with an estate tax.

A common objection to the tax is that it taxes savings that were already taxed. Gale and
Slemrod (2001) argue that what matters is the combined tax burden and the cost of collecting
it, rather than the number of times wealth is taxed. It is also not true that everything in an
estate has been taxed before. Savings out of income were taxed when the income was earned.
But if someone buys an asset and holds it until they die, the increase in its value is generally
never taxed as income, because of a rule called the step-up in basis at death. Gains that were
not realized before death escape income tax under that rule, and Hawaiʻi’s income tax follows
it.17
Suppose, for example, someone bought stock in a company like Google many years ago, before
it became big, and never sold any of it. She is now very rich on paper, and none of the gain
has been taxed as income, since she has not sold. She can also spend without selling, since
she can borrow against the stock. Most people cannot borrow this way, since it takes a lot of
assets that have gone up in value. When she dies, her estate sells some of the stock and pays
16 Wash. Laws 2026, ch. 209.
17 Gale and Slemrod (2001), Claim 10. 26 U.S.C. §1014(a); HRS §235-2.3. The rule can also step the
basis down, and there are exceptions, including income in respect of a decedent (§1014(c)), such
as balances in traditional retirement accounts. Gains that accrue after the heir inherits are taxed
when the heir sells. Chapter 2 discusses capital gains. When the tax on gains goes up, people tend
to realize fewer gains. Our household model assumes that gains realized fall by 0.4 percent for each
1 percent rise in the combined federal and state marginal tax rate on long-term gains (chapter 9,
section 9.2). The model has no step-up at death and no estate tax.
164

===== page 167 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 7. Wealth and estates
off the loans, and because of the step-up there is generally no income tax on the gain that
built up during her life, only on any gain after the date of her death. The estate tax is the only
tax that reaches what those gains built, and it only does so above the exclusion.
The most direct evidence on whether the rich move because of the tax is a paper by Moretti and
Wilson (2023) on where the Forbes 400, the richest Americans, choose to live in response to
state estate taxes. Before 2001, a federal credit meant their estate tax was the same wherever
they lived. After the federal tax reform of 2001 phased out the credit, their estate tax was
higher if they lived in a state that had one. The number of Forbes 400 members living in
estate-tax states then fell by about 35 percent relative to other states, and the response was
stronger among older billionaires.18 This is evidence from elsewhere, and from a single study
of a few hundred people (the Forbes 400 after 2001). Still, the 2001 change raised the tax only
for people living in states with an estate tax of their own, so comparing them with billionaires
in the other states separates the tax from other reasons to move. The study shows that the
very richest do move in response to the tax. The same study estimates that the death of the
average billionaire brought the state $165 million in estate tax over the next five years, in 2017
dollars. In the years shown in Figure 7.1, Hawaiʻi has had one receipt of that size. The $315
million paid in September 2024 was about one and a half times the study’s average, which is
about $211 million in 2024 dollars. It was also about nine times the $33.9 million that the
tax brought in during an average year from FY2013 to FY2024.19 But this receipt is only the
benefit of the tax. When a wealthy resident moves away because of the estate tax, Hawaiʻi
loses the income tax she would have paid over the rest of her life, and this is the cost of the
tax. Moretti and Wilson estimate both for each state, the estate tax it collects when wealthy
residents die and the income tax it loses from those who move away. In their model of a 2017
tax on billionaires alone, the cost for Hawaiʻi is 1.12 times the benefit, a net loss of about $20
million in present value, that is, with future amounts discounted to today. This calculation rests
on one person, the single Forbes billionaire living in Hawaiʻi in 2017. Our household model
makes a similar assumption for the income tax: some of the highest-income households leave
when taxes rise. Under that assumption, a 1 percent increase in the top income-tax rate loses
50 cents of each dollar instead of 30, and 21 of those cents are tax that households who leave
no longer pay (chapter 9, section 9.2).20
Of the eleven states with an estate tax and at least one billionaire in 2017, Hawaiʻi was the
only one where the cost was larger than the benefit. The authors attribute this mainly to its
high income tax rate, which makes it more costly to lose a resident’s income tax. That rate is
an error in the study’s input data. It uses 11.2 percent for Hawaiʻi in 2017, when the state’s
top rate on ordinary income was 8.25 percent and its rate on long-term capital gains 7.25

percent. In the model the cost is proportional to the income tax rate and the benefit to the

tax loses in both cases.22

wealth and income held fixed, so the ratio scales exactly. The published ratio and net present value
imply a benefit of about $166.7 million and a cost of about $186.7 million. At (8.25 + 7.25)/2 =
7.75 percent, the ratio is 1.12 × 7.75/11.20 = 0.775 and the net present value is 166.7 − 186.7 ×
7.75/11.20 = about $37 million; with Hawaiʻi’s 2017 top estate rate of 15.7 percent in place of 16
(p. 432), the ratio is 0.79. At current law, 1.12 × (9.125/11.20) × (16/20) = 0.73. This keeps the
authors’ equal weights on ordinary income and capital gains. If the income were all capital gains,
taxed at 7.25 percent, the ratio would be 0.58, and if it were all ordinary income, taxed at 11 percent,
it would be 0.88. These keep the authors’ 2017 population, wealth and dollars.
22 Moretti and Wilson (2023), Table 7, p. 463, and pp. 462–463. Their broad tax follows the 2017
federal exclusion of $5.5 million for individuals and $11 million for couples, and it assumes an
average estate tax rate of 10 percent, 16 percent times the ratio of the average to the top federal
rate (p. 462, n. 57). This is not Hawaiʻi’s current schedule. The authors had no estimate of how
readily people who are wealthy but not billionaires move, so in one case they assume that everyone
above the exclusion moves as readily as billionaires, and in the other half as readily. Their tables cover
fifteen states. Fourteen have an estate tax, and Pennsylvania has only an inheritance tax (the text on
p. 432 counts 13). Hawaiʻi, Minnesota and Oregon are the only three where costs exceed benefits in
the baseline case. The dollar figures add up taxes over people’s remaining lives, in present value, and
are not annual amounts. Our recomputation scales each ratio by 7.75/11.20: 1.18 × 0.692 = 0.82,
a gain of about $481 million, and 0.52 × 0.692 = 0.36, a gain of about $1.93 billion. At current

None of these studies tells us whether people who are wealthy but not billionaires move as
readily as billionaires. Per point of tax, the two main studies are not far apart. Moretti and
Wilson’s 35 percent fall came with an estate tax rate of 16 percent, roughly 2 percent per point.
Bakija and Slemrod find 1.4 to 2.7 percent fewer estate tax returns per point of a state’s average
estate tax rate, and nearly 4 percent for estates above $5 million. The older study still finds
only a modest loss of revenue, and the reason is that this average rate is low. The separate state
taxes it simulates take about 4.5 percent of net worth on average, so a response of even several
percent per point removes a small share of the base.25 An estate above the federal exclusion
also saves less by moving than the Hawaiʻi tax it avoids, because of the federal deduction. If an
estate of $30 million leaves Hawaiʻi, it avoids $4.3 million of Hawaiʻi tax but pays about $1.7
million more in federal tax, so it saves about $2.6 million (Table 7.1). Moretti and Wilson’s

investment that may or may not leave with wealthy people who move, such as a firm that
moves with its owner.26 Our household model has to assume how readily households below

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 7. Wealth and estates
9.2). This is a working assumption, not a Hawaiʻi estimate.27
For a US citizen or resident who becomes domiciled outside Hawaiʻi, ordinary financial assets
generally fall outside the Hawaiʻi estate tax. Hawaiʻi real estate and tangible property located
here are taxed, with the taxable estate and the exclusion apportioned for nonresidents. The
statute looks through only a single-member LLC that the decedent owned and that is not taxed
as a corporation. The statute says nothing about a corporation or a partnership, so Hawaiʻi real
estate that a nonresident holds through one of them may not be taxed. Hawaiʻi real estate that
the owner holds in her own name is still taxed after she moves away, unless she sells it before
she dies. If she stays a resident, selling does not lower the estate tax, since the tax applies to
the money from the sale just as it did to the property. She would also have to pay income tax
on a gain that would otherwise have escaped income tax at death.
For the estate tax, a person is a Hawaiʻi resident if she is domiciled here. Her domicile is
roughly the permanent home she intends to return to, so spending time in another place
does not change it (chapter 1, section 1.3). If someone dies with property in Hawaiʻi, the law
presumes she was a resident, and the estate has to prove she was not.28 Owners who do not
move can still lower the tax, for example with gifts to their children, with trusts, and through
the way a family business or property is owned and valued.29 Hawaiʻi does not have a gift tax.
What a person gave away during life is not added back into the estate, but gifts made during
life count against the exclusion at death. Gifts beyond the exclusion are generally not taxed by
Hawaiʻi, and any value that property gains after it is given away stays out of the estate. A gift
does not get the step-up in basis that property held until death gets, so stock that has gone
up in value keeps the giver’s basis when it is given to a child. A child who sells the stock pays
capital gains tax on the gain from before the gift as well, and the family pays back in income
tax part of what it saved in estate tax.30 Gifts and trusts are harder to study than moves, since
people make gifts and set up trusts years before they die and any effect on estates only appears

in the data much later. The estate tax may also change how much wealth people accumulate
and how much of that wealth they leave to charity. In US estate tax returns from 1916 to 1996,
higher estate taxes go with smaller reported estates, and estate tax incentives raise charitable
bequests.31
The tax may also change what heirs do. Older workers in the United States who receive an
inheritance tend to retire sooner than they expected, especially if the inheritance was unex-
pected.32 A study using federal tax returns found that single people who inherited more than
$150,000 were about four times as likely to leave the labor force as those who inherited less
than $25,000.33 If a smaller inheritance keeps an heir working, and if the heir lives in Hawaiʻi,
some revenue comes back through the Hawaiʻi income tax, the budget payback discussed in
chapter 1 (section 1.5).
Another concern is that the heirs of closely held businesses might have to sell the business
to pay the tax.34 For the family in our example, there are two problems, one about what the
business is worth and one about where the money to pay the tax comes from. The business
has never been sold, so there is no market price to use, and its value has to be worked out
under the federal rules (though only once). And even if the business turns out to be worth a
lot, it may not have enough cash to pay the tax, and it may be difficult to sell a minority share
of a small family company. For farms, at least, the evidence suggests that cash is a problem for
some estates but not most. In a study of federal returns filed in 2000 (CBO 2005), at most 138
of the 1,659 farm estates that owed estate tax did not have enough cash and assets that could
readily be sold to pay it. Among estates that claimed the federal deduction for family-owned
businesses, at most 164 of the 485 that owed estate tax lacked enough cash and assets that
could readily be sold to pay it.35
Hawaiʻi follows the federal rules on valuation and how long people have to pay. If a closely
held business makes up more than 35 percent of the estate after debts and expenses, the tax
on it can be paid in up to ten annual installments, with the first payment of principal delayed

7.2 The property tax and the GET as taxes on wealth
The property tax is the part of a wealth tax that Hawaiʻi can collect every year. What makes it
attractive is that it is easy to measure and hard to avoid. The assessor can estimate the value of
land and buildings from sales of similar properties, and neither can move, although taxes on
buildings can reduce construction and upkeep (chapter 5).40 This is why chapter 1 puts land
at the least responsive end of its ordering, and wealth at the most responsive end. People who
live somewhere else pay the tax on property they own here, too. Nor can an owner escape the
36 HRS §§236E-9(e), 236E-2; Form M-6 Instructions (2025), pp. 2–3; 26 U.S.C. §6166, which uses 35
percent of the adjusted gross estate. Under the federal tax, part of the deferred amount is charged
interest at only 2 percent, but this low rate does not apply to the Hawaiʻi tax. The rule on valuing
property at its current use is known as special-use valuation (26 U.S.C. §2032A), and it applies in
Hawaiʻi because the state conforms to the federal definitions. There is a limit on how much it can
reduce the value of a property, which is $1.46 million in 2026 (Rev. Proc. 2025-32, §4.41). It is also
only available if the property is a large enough share of the estate and the decedent or family took
part in using it (§2032A(b), and §2032A(c) on recapture).
37 IRS Statistics of Income (n.d.-a), Table 1, all taxable returns: $23.9 billion, or 18.6 percent of gross
estates. The 18.6 percent is a share of gross estates before deductions. It is not the share of the tax
that these assets account for. The returns are for the whole country, and most of them are for people
who died in 2023. An estate that owes tax only to Hawaiʻi owes nothing to the federal government,
so it is not one of these taxable returns, and such estates may hold a different mix of assets.

tax by selling, because the buyer will have to pay it from then on and will offer less for the
property for that reason. To the extent that the price falls, it is whoever owns the land when
the tax goes up who bears an increase in the tax on land, and later buyers do not.
Our household model makes similar assumptions. It holds the amount of property fixed, and
it lowers the price of a property by the value today of the part of the added tax that the owner
cannot pass on to occupants. In the model, raising every county rate by 1 percent brings the
counties $30.6 million a year. Owners and occupants give up $1.00 for each dollar collected,
or $1.02 once owners build and maintain a little less. Property values fall by about $759
million. Residents who own property bear $492 million of that loss and nonresident owners
$267 million. Renters and other occupants bear the rest of the tax as it is paid (chapter 9,
section 9.5).41
Chapter 9 (section 9.7) works through an illustrative swap. The counties raise the property
tax on owner-occupied homes by 1 percent, or about $8.0 million a year in total, and the state
cuts its transfers to the counties by the same amount and uses the saving to cut income tax
rates. Taxpayers then pay about $5 million a year less for the same state and county revenue.42
As a wealth tax, the property tax has limits, since it misses financial wealth and most of the
value of businesses. It is based on the market value of the property, so a mortgage does not
reduce it.43 Values are estimated by the assessor, and chapter 5 (section 5.4) discusses how
accurate they are. The counties also give owner-occupants a home exemption, and some tax
the more valuable part of a home or the more valuable classes of homes at higher rates, so the
tax depends on more than value (chapter 5, section 5.2).
One complaint about the property tax is that the value of a home has gone up, and with it the
property tax, but the owner does not have more money. This is the same difference between
value and cash that arises for family businesses at death. The owner is richer and could sell the
house or borrow against it, but the tax has to be paid in cash every year, whatever the owner’s
income.44 Averaged over all households in each tenth of the income distribution, owners and
renters together, a 1 percent rise in all county rates costs resident households about $24 a
year in the bottom tenth and $68 a year in the top tenth (0.012 percent of income) (chapter
9, section 9.5).45
The GET is another tax on wealth that is not called a wealth tax. People who move to Hawaiʻi
with savings they earned somewhere else while nonresident generally have not paid Hawaiʻi
income tax on that income, but they pay the GET when they spend the savings here, and so
do visitors. In our household model, visitors would pay about 23 percent of a half-point rise in

A permanent increase in the GET is also like a one-time tax on wealth. An increase in a tax
on spending that people did not expect lowers what their savings can buy, so in this sense it
taxes wealth people already have as well as income.48
7.3 An annual wealth tax
An annual tax on net wealth has many advocates. Economists such as Saez and Zucman (2019)
have made the case for one in the United States.49 The evidence makes it unlikely that such
a tax, levied by Hawaiʻi alone, would raise much revenue at all, partly because wealth is so
difficult to measure and partly because people respond strongly to the tax, by reporting less
wealth or by moving and taking their income tax with them (for the income tax, see chapter
9, section 9.2).
Wealth is very hard to measure. Publicly traded stock is the easy case, since anyone can look
up its market price. A painting might be sold once in twenty years, and a closely held family
business that has never been sold has no price at all. Kopczuk (2019) points out that a wealth
tax has to rely on valuations that cannot always be checked against an actual sale between
independent buyers and sellers.50 The wealth of the very wealthy is the hardest to measure,
since as we go up the wealth distribution, more of what people own is privately held businesses
and other assets that are hard to value.51
Most OECD countries that had an annual wealth tax have given up on it. Twelve OECD coun-
tries had one in 1990 and four still did in 2017, and apart from Switzerland, those that kept it

of savings brought from elsewhere apart from their other spending.
47 HRS §235-4(a)–(b).
48 Kaplow (2008) discusses why a move to a tax on consumption works like a levy on existing capital,
and why it matters whether the change was expected and whether it might be repeated. The increase
works like a levy on existing wealth only to the extent people did not expect it, so they could not
spend before it took effect, and only if it is permanent, so they cannot wait it out; either way, it
reaches only wealth that is spent in Hawaiʻi. Chapter 3 discusses who pays the GET.
49 Saez and Zucman (2019).
50 Kopczuk (2019), p. 518. Advani et al. (2020), pp. 52 and 56–57, describe how the share of hard-
to-value assets rises with wealth; pp. 98–99 discuss annual valuation. For an annual UK wealth tax
with a £500,000 threshold, they project annual administrative costs to the government of about

projections for the United Kingdom, not observed costs. The authors prefer a one-time wealth tax to
an annual one (p. 106). A one-time tax faces the same problem of valuing assets, though only once.

raised little revenue from it.52 Reported wealth also responds strongly to the tax. Across Swiss
cantons, a one percentage point cut in the wealth tax rate raised reported taxable wealth by at
least 43 percent after six years.53 Estimates from other countries span a wide range and do not
all measure the same outcome. A one percentage point cut in the rate raised wealth reported
near a kink in the tax schedule by less than 1 percent in Sweden, and raised accumulated or
taxable wealth by 14 to 32 percent in the Netherlands, Denmark and Catalonia.54 A state may
face more mobility because taxpayers can move within the same country. Unlike the property
tax, a tax on net worth is about the person instead of the asset, and a person can move. When
Spain brought back its wealth tax in 2011, every region except Madrid levied it, and wealthy
taxpayers moved their reported residence to Madrid, at least on paper. The regions they left
lost six times as much in income tax as in wealth tax.55
7.4 Design choices and tradeoffs
The exclusion matters most for estates between $5.49 million and $15 million, which can
owe tax to Hawaiʻi and nothing to the federal government (Table 7.1). The largest estates are
affected more by the top rate, and by the rules on residence, gifts and entities, which decide
how much of an estate can be moved out of Hawaiʻi’s reach before death. What any of these
changes would raise or cost depends heavily on whether a very large estate is among those
taxed. The tax brought in $33.9 million in an average year from FY2013 to FY2024, and then
a single receipt in September 2024 was $315 million. Table 7.3 at the end of this section lists
the options.
The exclusion and indexing
Because the exclusion is fixed at $5.49 million, each year more estates will owe the tax as
prices and asset values rise. The state could index the exclusion to inflation. It could also go

further and follow the federal exclusion, which is $15 million in 2026 and will be indexed
after that. None of the estates in Table 7.1 between $5.49 and $15 million would then owe
any tax, and larger estates would owe less too, because the exclusion is subtracted before the
rates are applied. Hawaiʻi’s tax on the $30 million estate would fall by about $1.9 million.
Either change would cost the state revenue, from estates both below and above $15 million.
With the federal exclusion, though, every estate that owes Hawaiʻi tax would have to file a
federal return as well. DOTAX could then rely more on the federal government’s review of
those returns and do less of its own.
Rates
A few large estates seem to pay much of the tax, so for revenue it is the top rate that matters
most. Raising it would bring in more from these estates, unless their owners move away or
change their estate plans (section 7.1). Above $15 million the federal Treasury in effect pays
part of any increase, because with the federal deduction each added dollar of Hawaiʻi tax
costs the largest estates as little as 60 cents (Table 7.1). The deduction also lowers what an
owner with an estate of this size saves by moving away, which is only the Hawaiʻi tax net of
the deduction.
Relief for family businesses
If a closely held business is more than 35 percent of the estate after debts and expenses, current
law lets the estate pay the tax on it in up to ten annual installments. The value of some farm
and business real property can also be set by its current use, which can lower it by up to $1.46
million in 2026 (section 7.1). Beyond this, Hawaiʻi could let estates deduct a family business,
with a deduction of the kind discussed in section 7.1. The deduction could be capped, as in
Washington and Minnesota, or it could cover the full value of the business.56 The latter would
cost the state more revenue and DOTAX would have to check whether each estate qualifies
under either version. Business owners could avoid the tax by holding more of their wealth in
a form that qualifies.
Gifts and other ways around the tax
Moving away and giving assets away before death both lower the tax (section 7.1). How much
an owner saves depends on Hawaiʻi’s rules for residence and gifts, and also on what other
states tax.57 Not every gift lowers the tax, because Hawaiʻi follows federal law, and federal
56 Washington allows up to $3.076 million in 2026 and Minnesota up to $2 million beyond its $3
million exclusion, and Minnesota puts the cost of its own deduction at about $19 million to $20
million a year. Closely held stock, farm assets and other business assets were about 19 percent of
gross estates on taxable federal returns filed in 2024. Sources are given in section 7.1.
57 Another option, which we do not evaluate, is to tax heirs on what they receive instead of taxing the
estate. Five states have such an inheritance tax for deaths in 2026 (Kentucky, Maryland, Nebraska,
New Jersey and Pennsylvania), and Maryland has both kinds of tax (Tax Foundation 2026b, Table
37, p. 52). Iowa’s inheritance tax does not apply to deaths on or after January 1, 2025 (Iowa Code
§450.98).

===== page 177 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 7. Wealth and estates
law treats certain transfers made before death as still part of the estate.58 If a resident’s estate
pays death tax to another state, it can also take a credit for that tax against the Hawaiʻi tax,
but only up to the share of the Hawaiʻi tax that the property taxed there accounts for.59
Hawaiʻi generally does not tax what an owner gives away after lifetime gifts have used up the
exclusion (section 7.1). New York and Minnesota add gifts made within three years of death
back into the estate. Hawaiʻi could do the same, or it could levy a gift tax of its own.60 Either
would raise revenue from the largest estates. DOTAX would then have gifts to keep track of
as well as estates. A nonresident may also avoid the tax on Hawaiʻi real estate by holding it
through a corporation or partnership, since the statute only looks through single-member LLCs
(section 7.1). The state could look through these other entities too, but it would then need
rules for valuing an interest in an entity that owns property in Hawaiʻi and also somewhere
else.
Gains at death
The estate tax is the only tax that reaches wealth built from gains never realized before death,
and it applies only above the exclusion (section 7.1). A state could tax the gains themselves.
Hawaiʻi could treat the gain on the Google stock from section 7.1 as income on the owner’s
final return. Taxing the gain on the final return requires a value for everything she owns
at death, which large estates already report for the estate tax, and it would reach only the
estates of residents. Or Hawaiʻi could end the step-up, so that her heirs pay income tax on
the whole gain since she first bought the stock, whenever they sell it. Someone would then
have to keep her purchase records for decades, and Hawaiʻi could tax only heirs who live here
when they sell, or who are selling Hawaiʻi real estate. The step-up is a federal rule that the
Hawaiʻi income tax follows, so either way the state would have to keep its own basis records,
and wealthy residents would have one more reason to move away before they die or before
they sell. We found no state that taxes gains at death or ends the step-up for its own income
tax. Bills to do this have been introduced, but none has become law.61 Acting alone, Hawaiʻi
would have to keep its own basis records, and it could reach only the estates of residents and

8. Tax credits
This chapter draws heavily on analyses first prepared by Branden Hing, a former
UHERO research assistant.
In TY2023, Hawaiʻi taxpayers claimed $621.3 million in tax credits.1 DOTAX sorts them into
three groups by purpose (Figure 8.1). About $243 million went to credits intended to avoid
taxing the same income twice or taxing business inputs again as goods pass from one business
to the next (pyramiding; chapter 3). About $218 million went to credits that give relief to
targeted groups, like the earned income tax credit and the food/excise tax credit. Another
$152 million went to credits intended to change what certain households and businesses do.
The two largest of these are the renewable energy technologies credit ($100.1 million) and
the film credit ($43.5 million).
The total overstates what the state spends through credits, because a third of it is credit for
tax already paid. The pass-through entity credit ($133.8 million) and the credit for income
tax paid to another state ($74.4 million) keep the same income from being taxed twice. We
treat them as part of the definition of the income tax base and do not count them as spending
(section 8.1).
Other states have credits for many of the same purposes (Table 8.1). Hawaiʻi’s earned income
credit is 40 percent of the federal credit, which ties it for 5th among 48 jurisdictions (Figure
8.2). The law that raised it from 20 percent is set to be repealed at the end of 2027. Hawaiʻi’s
35 percent rate for rooftop solar is the highest in the table, but its $5,000 cap is the same as
New York’s. The film credit pays 22 to 32 percent of qualified costs, and the state allows $60
million a year. California allows $750 million.
By our estimates, each dollar of net state expenditure on the solar credit produces about
71 cents of benefits for Hawaiʻi residents, less than the dollar a cash payment would give
them. Counting everyone, including the global benefit of lower emissions, the figure is $1.95
(section 8.3). For film, Colby (2025) estimates that the state collects between 30 and 50 cents
in tax for each dollar of credit. We find no effect of the credit on film jobs in Hawaiʻi, but film
employment here varies so much from year to year that our estimates could miss a large one
(section 8.4).
Tax credits are sometimes called tax expenditures, because they are spending that happens
through the tax code by providing tax relief to people. To a tax economist, there is not that
much daylight between a credit and just spending money on a program. When a household
on Oʻahu claims $5,000 for the solar panels on its roof, the state collects $5,000 less in tax (or
provides a refund). The state’s budget is then in the same position as if the state had collected
the tax and paid the household $5,000 through a program, and the questions to ask are the
1 DOTAX (2025f), Table 4, PDF p.14 (printed p.10). Amounts claimed on all returns.
177

===== page 180 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
Figure 8.1. Hawaiʻi tax credits by DOTAX purpose group, TY2023
Note: Credits claimed on all returns in TY2023, grouped as DOTAX groups them in its Table 4. Group totals are

percent once the capital goods credit is added. Source: DOTAX (2025f), Table 4, PDF p.14.
ones we would ask of any program: What is the net cost of the credit to the state? What
outcomes does it cause that would not have happened anyway?
A relief credit works if the funds reach the households it was meant for, even if nobody does
anything differently. An incentive credit is meant for people who would not have done some-
thing without the credit. If it is given to people who would have done it anyway, it is very
similar to giving people money, and the new outcomes it causes must be worth the cost. Sec-
tion 8.1 describes each group and compares Hawaiʻi’s credits with those of other states. Section
8.2 sets out seven questions to ask of any credit, and sections 8.3 to 8.5 take up the solar credit,
the film credit and the other business credits. Section 8.6 brings the answers for the solar and
film credits together.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
8.1 Credits as spending
The three groups of credits
DOTAX sorts the credits claimed in TY2023 into three groups: credits that avoid double tax-
ation or pyramiding ($242.8 million), credits that promote social welfare ($218.4 million)
and credits that encourage certain industries or economic activities ($152.4 million). Expired
credits and amounts not shown separately make up the remaining amount of about $8 mil-
lion.2 We use these groups, except for two credits (the capital goods credit and the low-income
housing credit), for reasons given below.
The pass-through entity tax credit ($133.8 million) and the credit for income tax paid to
another state ($74.4 million) prevent the same income from being taxed twice. Since 2023, a
partnership or S corporation can elect to pay Hawaiʻi income tax itself. The owners are taxed
on that income as well. The first credit gives them credit for the tax their business has already
paid on their share of its income, and the second does the same for a resident who also earned
income on the mainland and paid tax on it to another state. Both are part of the definition of
the income tax base, so we do not count them as spending.3
The earned income tax credit ($77.1 million in TY2023) and the food/excise tax credit ($64.0
million) are cash support paid through the tax return. Both are refundable. A family is paid
the full credit even if the credit exceeds the income tax the family owes. The state earned
income credit has been 40 percent of the federal credit since tax year 2023.4 A family benefits
from both credits whether or not it changes how much it works or what it buys. Our household
model supports this for the food/excise credit. A 1 percent cut in the credit raises about as
much state revenue as households lose (a cost of 1.00 per net dollar raised; chapter 9, section
9.2 and Table 9.1), because recipients change their work and reported income very little, and

state $74.4 million, capital goods excise $34.6 million); “Tax Credits to Promote Social Welfare”
$218.4 million; “Tax Credits to Encourage Certain Industries or Economic Activities” $152.4 million
among active credits. Amounts DOTAX does not publish separately total $3.5 million.
3 DOTAX (2025f), PDF p.38. The two income tax credits together are $208.2 million, 33.5 percent

residents bear all of it.5 Chapter 2 discusses which families receive the credits, why some
eligible families do not claim them and how a loss of eligibility affects a family whose income
rises. Chapter 9 (section 9.7, Table 9.2) works through one way to pay for a larger food/excise
credit. Raising the top income-tax rate from 13 to 14 percent would pay for credit amounts 38
percent larger. Taxpayers would pay about $31 million a year more for the same state revenue,
and households in the bottom three tenths of the income distribution would gain $47 to $73
a year on average.6
The child and dependent care credit ($14.8 million) serves both purposes.7 By lowering what
it costs parents to go to work, it is intended to change their behavior, but it also provides
targeted tax relief to families. The earned income credit has both purposes as well, since it
requires earned income.8
The capital goods excise tax credit ($34.6 million) is intended to reduce tax pyramiding. The
credit returns 4 percent of what a business spends on qualifying machinery or equipment,
approximately covering the GET on the purchase. Chapter 3 discusses why taxing these pur-
chases can cause pyramiding and how credits compare with exemptions. Under Act 24 of 2026,
the credit ends for tax years beginning after December 31, 2027.9
We treat the low-income housing tax credit ($60.9 million) as an incentive, although DOTAX
includes it among the social welfare credits. The credit pays developers and investors to build
or preserve affordable rental units, so its value depends on how many units exist because of
the credit. We ask the same of every incentive credit.10
Hawaiʻi’s credits and those of other states
Other states have credits for many of the same purposes (Table 8.1). Hawaiʻi is one of 32 states
with an earned income credit and one of 28 where the credit is refundable.11 At 40 percent
of the federal credit, Hawaiʻi’s is the same as New Jersey’s and larger than New York’s (30
percent, less an offset for its household credit) or New Mexico’s (25 percent). Hawaiʻi’s rate is
tied for 5th among the 48 jurisdictions in Figure 8.2, and if we count the states with no credit
as zero, the median is 10 percent.

8.2 Seven questions for any credit
Seven questions give a rough-and-ready way of assessing any credit (Figure 8.3), one that can
be applied quickly to a specific case even without a lot of evidence. A full evaluation would
answer them with data.
Figure 8.3. Seven questions for any tax credit
Note: Conceptual figure. Source: UHERO.
What is the goal?
What counts as a benefit depends on what we are trying to accomplish, and for whom. Relief for
working families, cleaner electricity, jobs and cultural goals each call for different, measurable
evidence. If the credit is meant to reduce emissions, for example, we would want to know how
many tons of carbon dioxide it avoids.
184

===== page 187 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
Who benefits?
Who actually benefits from a credit is a question of fact, and the answer often reaches be-
yond the people the credit is meant for. Part of each dollar of solar credit goes to installers
through higher prices, for example, and most of its climate benefit goes to people who live
outside Hawaiʻi (section 8.3). It is not for economists to say whether the gains of nonresidents
should count as much as those of residents. That is for the Commissioners, policymakers and
ultimately the people of Hawaiʻi to decide, so we report the results separately for Hawaiʻi
residents and for everyone.
Who actually responds?
For a credit meant to incentivize or change behavior, we need to know who would have done
the same thing regardless. Consider two households claiming the solar credit. One would
have installed panels without it. For this household, the credit is like free money, and the state
gets no extra installation or reduction in emissions. The other household would not choose to
install without the credit, so its installation is what the state is paying for. The gains and losses
can also reach other people, including other electricity customers and everyone affected by a
changing climate.
Whether a credit changes behavior, and by how much, is a critical part of the analysis. Bartik
(2018) concludes that a typical incentive changes the location decision of between 2 and 25
percent of firms receiving it.18 Even at the top of that range, for each firm an incentive brings
in, three others receive it for doing what they would have done anyway. Households buying
panels and productions choosing where to film make different decisions, so sections 8.3 and
8.4 look at the evidence on those.
What is the net gain?
For a household that would not install without the credit, the gain is less than the amount it
receives. For this household, the credit is not like receiving cash. It would not have bought the
panels at full price, so they are worth less to it than they cost. Thus, the household gains only
the amount by which the credit exceeds this difference.
The same is true of a resident hired onto a film crew who would have earned almost as much
in some other job. All of her pay counts as production spending, but the production adds little
to her income. Treating all of the spending or payroll as a benefit therefore overstates the
benefits of the credit.
What is the cost to the state?
When we ask what a credit costs the state, we should ask what it costs on net. We need to
include the credit itself, administration and any other public costs, and subtract the extra
revenue and savings in spending that it causes. An installation or production that would have
happened anyway would already have paid GET and income tax, so we can count only the
taxes on activity caused by the credit against its cost. This budget payback need not cover the
whole credit. In the film example in section 8.4, it falls well short: Colby (2025) puts it at 30
to 50 cents per dollar of credit. For the food/excise credit, our household model finds that this
18 Bartik (2018), PDF pp.3, 5 and 12–13. The 2–25 percent range is his conclusion from 34 estimates,
not their full range; he judges a majority of those estimates likely to be too high.
185

===== page 188 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
feedback is very small. A 1 percent cut ($0.53 million) changes other state revenue by less
than $0.01 million, with slightly more income tax and slightly less GET (chapter 9, section
9.2).19
Is there a better alternative?
A natural benchmark for a credit is for the government simply to send people checks (in
practice, direct deposits). A dollar spent this way gives a dollar of benefits to whoever receives
it. We can then ask whether, for each dollar of net cost, the credit gives the people we are
counting more or less than they would get from the checks. A credit usually gives them more
only if it does something that the checks cannot do, such as reducing pollution that markets
leave alone. A credit with $1.10 of benefits per dollar of net cost is one where someone receives
$1.10 in benefits. The government does not get $1.10 back in revenue.20
What could change?
The last question is which of our answers, had it been different, would change the conclusion.
For most incentive credits it is the answer about how much of the activity the credit causes.
For film, Colby (2025) estimated that 60 percent of production spending was due to the credit,
and section 8.4 compares this estimate to evidence from other states.21
For the film credit, the goal and whose benefits we count can matter just as much. Pay to
nonresident cast and crew qualifies for the credit, and nonresidents received nearly half of the
wages that qualified for the credit in 2023 (section 8.4). Paying part of nonresidents’ wages
still lowers the cost of filming in Hawaiʻi, and a production that comes hires residents too.
However, if the goal is to raise residents’ earnings, the state could spend the same amount on
a higher rate for residents’ wages and Hawaiʻi purchases only. A higher rate on those costs also
lowers the cost of filming in Hawaiʻi, so the state can attract productions in that way as well.
Act 185 (SLH 2026) already adds 5 percentage points for productions whose workforce is at
least 80 percent Hawaiʻi residents (section 8.4). Whether residents would gain more depends
on how many productions would still come. If that is the goal, the part of the credit paid on
nonresidents’ wages is difficult to justify, except to the extent that it brings productions to
Hawaiʻi that would not come under a credit weighted toward residents’ wages and Hawaiʻi
purchases, and that then hire residents.

The solar credit’s benefits depend heavily on whose benefits we count. By our estimates, each

Taxpayers claimed $100.1 million of the credit in TY2023 ($58.3 million on individual returns,
$38.6 million on corporate returns and $3.2 million on other returns).24 We analyze the solar
panel component for households, because it is the part that is easiest to analyze. The corporate
claims and wind systems are not included.
A household can claim 35 percent of what a solar system costs, up to $5,000 on a single-
family home, so the cap limits the credit on any system costing more than $14,285.71, and
on a typical $20,000 system the credit covers 25 percent of the cost.25 The credit used to exist
alongside a 30 percent federal credit, which ended for expenditures made after December 31,
2025. On a system below the cap, the household’s out-of-pocket cost used to be 35 cents on
the dollar, and moving forward it is 65 cents on the dollar.26
Act 24 of 2026 changed the credit. Claims must now be certified by the Hawaiʻi State Energy
Office, and the total credit is limited to $40 million a year, with each claimant receiving a share
in proportion to the amount claimed. Beginning in TY2027, the credit is no longer available to
individuals with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income above $175,000, or joint filers above $350,000,
and it ends after TY2029.27 The estimates in this section are for the law before these changes.
Act 24 also changes how a household claims the credit. Before the Act, a household computed
the credit on a form attached to its tax return, and no agency approved the claim in advance.
A claimant must now send the Energy Office a certified statement of its adjusted gross income,
the cost of the system and the credits claimed, before March 1 of the year after the system is
installed. The Energy Office certifies that the claimant is eligible and notifies each applicant
of the certified amount by May 31. The claimant then files the certificate with the tax return,
which DOTAX may still audit. The Act does not say how the Energy Office will check the
income a claimant reports. As of November 20, 2026, neither the Energy Office nor DOTAX
had published the certification procedure. We found no source that quantifies what the credit
costs the state to administer or what it costs households to comply, before or after Act 24.

What is the goal?
Assumption: The purpose of this credit is to create cleaner energy with lower carbon emissions.
We assume this is what residents and policymakers would consider to be the purpose of the
credit.
Who benefits?
Most of the credit goes to higher-income households. DOTAX (2025f, Table A-5) shows that,
in TY2023, of the $58.3 million claimed on individual returns, 72 percent went to filers with
income of $100,000 or more, and 44.6 percent to those with $200,000 or more. The income
measure is Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income for residents, and worldwide income under Hawaiʻi
tax rules for nonresidents and part-year residents.32 This is perhaps to be expected: higher-
income households are more likely to have a home suited to panels and to be able to finance
the installation. The national figures look similar. Among the four federal clean energy credits
for households from 2006 to 2012, including rooftop solar, the top fifth of earners received
about 60 percent of the money and the bottom three-fifths about 10 percent.33
Hawaiʻi households with Hawaiʻi adjusted gross income of $20,000 or less ($40,000 for joint
filers) can receive the state credit as a refund. Other households can also take a refund, but
must accept a credit 30 percent smaller. In TY2023, individuals claimed $13.4 million of
the credit as refundable and $44.5 million as nonrefundable.34 The income limits starting in
TY2027 will exclude the households at the top.
Who actually responds?
The result depends on how many households install panels only because of the credit. We
assume about 46 additional installations for every 100 that would have happened without it,
based on research on California.35 Another California study finds that subsidies do cause instal-
lations: rebates there account for an estimated 53 percent of the installations made between
2007 and 2012.36
What is the cost to the state?
The denominator includes credits for installations that would have happened anyway and for
those caused by the credit. We assume no administrative cost or change in other tax revenue,

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
so net state expenditure in this calculation equals total credits paid. These are benefits per
dollar of that expenditure; the dollar itself has not been subtracted from them.
What is the net gain?
Figure 8.4 shows how the benefits divide among the people affected. Households that would
have installed panels anyway receive 55 cents per dollar of net state expenditure. For these
households, the credit is like free money. However, households that would not have put in
solar PV without the credit gain only 11 cents. This is because part of the credit covers the
difference between the cost of the system and the benefit to the household from installing
solar PV. Installers benefit by 16 cents. Part of the credit is passed on to installers in increased
prices.37
Utility shareholders and other ratepayers together lose 11 cents. As more people install solar
PV systems, and thus buy less electricity, the utility incurs lower generation costs. However, it
still incurs infrastructure costs (poles, wires, power plants). It can pass on these costs to other
customers in increased prices, or see reduced profits. Adding these gains and losses gives 71
cents of benefits to residents per dollar of net state expenditure, counting installers and utility
shareholders as Hawaiʻi residents.38 Avoided carbon emissions add $1.24, for $1.95 in all.
Our estimate of gains and losses to Hawaiʻi residents counts none of the climate benefit, be-
cause the benefits of reduced carbon emissions go primarily to people who do not live in the
state. This follows from the nature of the policy and is not a judgment on whether the credit
is worth having. The estimate that counts everyone values the global benefit at the Environ-
mental Protection Agency’s estimate of the damage done by a ton of carbon dioxide.39 How
much we value the benefits to each group is a value judgment. Depending on that choice, the
benefit is below or above a dollar per dollar of net state expenditure. The resident comparison
is therefore 71 cents of benefits against a dollar available for another use; it is not a 71-cent
gain after paying for the credit.
Is there a better alternative?
What could the state do with the revenue if it ended the credit? One possibility is to have
the utility buy large solar installations supplying the electricity grid, which might be a more
cost-effective way to do the same thing. Studies of climate policies in the United States and
Europe put the cost of avoiding a ton of carbon dioxide through solar subsidies at about $140
to $2,100. Some of the difference comes from economies of scale. Large arrays cost much

What could change?
A stronger response raises climate benefits and lowers residents’ benefits, because more of
each dollar goes to households whose own gain is small (Figure 8.5). With a response to
price a little over a third or about three-quarters as strong as the one assumed (about 15 or
33 extra installations per 100), residents’ benefits are 88 or 78 cents per dollar of net state
expenditure, and benefits counting everyone are $1.39 or $1.74.42 In each case the benefit
to residents stays below a dollar per dollar of net state expenditure, and the benefit counting
everyone stays above it.
The cost to other ratepayers is the most Hawaiʻi-specific and least certain number in the anal-
ysis. It shows up as a cost either through reduced profits to the utility and its shareholders or
as higher rates for other ratepayers. To approximate the loss, we subtract avoided generation
costs from the revenue lost on each kilowatt-hour no longer sold, then apply the utility’s al-
lowed return on equity, the regulator-approved rate of return to shareholders. This gives the
11-cent cost to shareholders and other ratepayers together. We do not have an estimate of the
share of lost revenue borne by shareholders and other customers. Using the allowed return for
this purpose is an approximation. If the utility recovered the entire loss from other customers,
they would bear a cost many times larger. A Hawaiʻi estimate would require evidence on the
fixed costs recovered through electricity rates and the costs the utility avoids when households
generate their own power.
The climate benefit depends on which plants produce less power when households install solar.
To keep it simple, we use one emissions rate for the whole state and the life of the system, from
the Oʻahu plants that go up and down with demand. To the extent the utility can decarbonize
the grid, a panel installed today would replace cleaner power as it ages, and the climate benefit
would be smaller.43
8.4 The film production credit
The film credit refunds part of the money spent by film productions in Hawaiʻi. This section
describes how the credit functions, what questions its design raises, and what we know so far.
Key findings: In New Mexico, Louisiana and Georgia, there was a sharp increase in film jobs
shortly after those states introduced large credits. This suggests that credits may bring film
production to a state, but does not prove it. In Hawaiʻi, we detect no impact of the credit on film
jobs, but our estimates may be missing a large impact. The patterns in the data are consistent

The credit refunds 22 percent of qualified production costs incurred on Oʻahu and 27 percent
of those incurred on the neighbor islands. Act 185 of 2026 added an additional 5 percentage

Act 185 also raised the total credit allowed each year from $50 million to $60 million, raised
the limit per production from $17 million to $20 million (but with no limit for productions
spending at least $60 million), and extended the program from January 1, 2033, to January
1, 2038. The Council on Revenues estimates that these changes to the generosity of the credit
will cost the General Fund an additional $10 million a year.46
The following sub-sections consider the seven questions in section 8.2. The cost to the state is
considered before the net gain, as the net gain is given per dollar spent by the state.
What is the goal?
Assumption: The purpose of this credit is to create employment in Hawaiʻi, with additional
benefits to local business through local production purchasing and to tourism via cultural
exposure. We assume this is what residents and policymakers would consider to be the purpose
of the credit.
Who benefits?
The film credit can finance nonresident salaries and purchases from businesses outside Hawaiʻi.
Pay to nonresident cast and crew for work in Hawaiʻi qualifies for the credit.47 These wages
are taxable here, as other wages earned in Hawaiʻi are, so some of the credit comes back
through income tax. But the credit still subsidizes 22–32 cents of every dollar a nonresident
earns for their work. To qualify for the new local-hire bonus, at least 80 percent of the people
in a production’s workforce must be Hawaiʻi residents. Notably, this does not require that 80
percent of the total payroll go to Hawaiʻi residents, merely that the share of workers be 80
percent.48

Purchases from businesses outside Hawaiʻi qualify only if the production shows that it tried
to find comparable goods or services here and could not.49 These purchases are small, about
1 to 3 percent of qualified spending each year from 2019 to 2024 (Appendix G). Credit paid
on nonresidents’ wages and out-of-state purchases does not go to Hawaiʻi residents. It helps
them only indirectly, when nonresident cast and crew spend money here while they work,
supporting local jobs and tax collections.
DBEDT’s data show Hawaiʻi residents received about 53 percent of the wages that qualified
for the credit in 2023. The gap is largest at the top of the payroll: of the 2023 wages paid
to principal cast, directors, producers and writers, residents received about 15 percent ($5.3
million) and nonresidents $30.8 million.50,51
From 2019 to 2024, each dollar of credit corresponded to $4.76 of eligible spending in Hawaiʻi:
$1.48 of wages to residents, $1.53 of wages to nonresidents, $1.65 to Hawaiʻi businesses, and
10 cents to businesses outside the state. Over the six years of the credit, residents received (on
average) 49 percent of the wages that qualified for the credit, slightly less than nonresidents,
though their share in more recent years rose to 53 percent in 2023 and 62 percent in 2024.52
Who actually responds?
What earlier studies find
Several studies of film incentives find little or no lasting gain in film jobs or wages, even though
credits affect where some productions shoot. Button (2019) studies state film incentives across
the United States and finds that adopting an incentive raised the number of television series
filmed in a state on average, but with no meaningful effect on feature films or on employ-
ment, wages, or business establishments in the film industry or related industries.53 California
allocated its first film credit partly by lottery, and of the projects that applied for the credit,
lost, and were eventually made, about a third were filmed in California anyway.54 Some states
have evaluated their own tax credits by comparing the estimated tax revenue the credit gener-
ated with estimates of the total credit cost. Each state conducted this analysis with their own
method. Florida estimated that each dollar of film credit generated 7 cents in additional tax
revenue. Georgia estimated a 15–19 cent return per dollar of credit, Virginia estimated a 20

tion the tourism benefits included in such estimates.55 Georgia’s figure is likely high, because
its evaluation assumes that 92.1 percent of film activity in the state is due to the credit.56
Credits move productions between states, so part of one state’s gain is another state’s loss.
What a credit attracts is also not necessarily what would leave if the credit ended. Some
crews, suppliers, and studios that have grown up around the credit may remain in place (or
they may leave). Studies of states that repealed their credits find suggestive evidence that film
employment declined, but these estimates are not generally statistically significant, meaning
there may have been no change in employment.57
Regarding Hawaiʻi, Colby (2025) notes that “there is little evidence that the film credit has
generated a local film industry that can continue at the same scale without the ongoing support
of credits.”58
Film jobs after the credit
As discussed above, we assume a primary purpose of the film tax credit is to create employment
in Hawaiʻi. It is therefore important to evaluate how effectively the credit does this, and how
much the state spends per job it creates through the credit.
Public discussion of film credits often confuses three different measures of film industry jobs.
The first is the number of hires on productions that received the credit, which DBEDT reports;
it counts each person hired by each production, however briefly, and includes extras. The
second is the change in film-industry employment, from federal data, which includes growth
that would have happened without the credit. The third, and the one that matters for judging
the credit, is the number of film jobs that exist because of the credit. No data source reports
that number, and we do not claim a clean estimate of it. We use the federal data to ask what
size of response the patterns are consistent with.59
As a baseline, we start from each state’s share of US film jobs in 1992–1996, before Hawaiʻi

FY2028; Virginia JLARC (2017), PDF p.26: 20 cents per dollar of credit awarded in FY2012–FY2016.
The analysts cited in Florida’s review describe film-tourism benefits as “largely unsubstantiated and

or the states we will use as comparison (New Mexico, Georgia, and Louisiana)60 had a large
credit. Hawaiʻi made up 0.53 percent of all US film jobs at that time. For each later year, we
multiply US film jobs by that share to approximate the number of jobs Hawaiʻi would have had
in absence of any state tax credits, and we count the film jobs above that number. We do the
same for every state. This count is not an estimate of the jobs the credit caused. A state’s share
can also change with investment in studios, incentives in other states, a location’s popularity
and, in the 2010s, the rise of streaming, and without its credit Hawaiʻi’s share could have
fallen while other states subsidized production. We use the count to compare Hawaiʻi with
other credit states.
We compare the share of US film industry employment coming from Hawaiʻi, New Mexico,
Louisiana, and Georgia with the film industry employment share of the median state among
the other 36 jurisdictions that have had a film credit of some kind since 1990, leaving out
California and New York, which form a group of their own.61 The figures label this median
the “typical credit state.” In 2024, Hawaiʻi’s share of US film jobs was 1.16 times its 1992–
96 share, and the median credit state’s was 1.17 times (Figure 8.6). Averaged over 2022–
2024, the figures were 1.19 for Hawaiʻi and 1.12 in the typical state. Hawaiʻi’s share of US
film jobs rose and fell a good deal from year to year, but none of the changes to its credit
before 2026 were followed by a lasting rise above the typical credit state. Conversely, New
Mexico, Louisiana and Georgia, which we call the “boom states,” had much larger employment
increases over this same time period. Their shares of US film jobs rose sharply soon after they
adopted large credits, and by 2024 their shares were 3.9, 2.3 and 4.6 times their 1992–96
shares, respectively.

16 percent of its film jobs, were above that share. Across the other early-1990s baselines, the
number of film jobs above the baseline share is between about 175 and 420.62 Whatever the
baseline, jobs above the early-1990s share were a much smaller share of film jobs in Hawaiʻi
(12 to 29 percent) than in the boom states (64 to 79 percent), and Hawaiʻi was much closer
to the median credit state (9 percent on the 1992 to 1996 baseline).

the comparison states would predict. However, the estimate is too imprecise to rule out no
effect at all.63 We also consider Hawaiʻi and the three boom states individually. New Mexico’s
rise in film jobs was larger than chance would produce. The estimates for film payroll responses
in Louisiana and Georgia were fairly large, although they were not statistically significant. For
