===== page 201 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
Figure 8.7. Film jobs above and below the early-1990s share of US film jobs, Hawaiʻi, New Mexico, Louisiana
and Georgia, 2022–24
year that an effect would have to be about 160 percent, or greater than two and a half times
its baseline film jobs, to be detectable with a high degree of reliability.
These patterns are consistent with a relatively limited employment response to the credit in
Hawaiʻi, even though credits appear to have drawn much more production to New Mexico,
Louisiana and Georgia. We do not have a clean causal estimate for Hawaiʻi. But nothing in the
data is consistent with a large share of Hawaiʻi’s film jobs being newly created by the credit,
especially relative to those states.
Why did film jobs grow so much in New Mexico, Louisiana, and Georgia but not in Hawaiʻi?
Our data cannot say for certain, but four explanations seem to be likely contributors. First, the
boom states’ credits were more generous. When their film industries took off in the mid-2000s,
they paid 25 to 30 percent of qualified costs, against 15 percent on Oʻahu and 20 percent on the
neighbor islands, and Georgia’s credit has no annual limit. Second, New Mexico and Louisiana
offered large credits early. Both raised their credits to 25 percent in 2006, when only eight
states offered that much. By the time Hawaiʻi raised its rates to 20 and 25 percent in 2013,
about 20 other states already offered 25 percent or more. A state that attracts productions
early can build up crews, suppliers and studios that keep productions coming after other states
match its credit. Third, these states may have supported the film industry in other ways at the
same time, such as investment in studios or crew training, and we cannot separate the effects
of those policies from the effect of the credit. Fourth, Hawaiʻi competes for a different kind of
production. Georgia can stand in for almost any American setting, so a production choosing
between Georgia and another mainland state can be swayed by a difference in cost, and a
credit can tip that choice. Hawaiʻi is far from the mainland and generally expensive to film
in. A production that films here usually does so because the story needs Hawaiʻi’s landscapes
or setting, and many such productions would probably have come without the credit. To win
productions that could film anywhere, Hawaiʻi would need a credit large enough to offset its
higher costs. That credit would be very expensive, and much of it would go to productions
that would have come anyway. The boom states show how large a response to a credit can be
when productions can easily switch locations. They do not show what a larger credit would
do in Hawaiʻi.
199

===== page 202 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
The design of Hawaiʻi’s credit may also limit what it does for local employment. The state
limits the total credit each year and the credit for most productions, which can restrict how
much production the credit draws at any rate; Georgia has no annual limit. The credit also
pays the same rate on nonresidents’ wages as on residents’, and nonresidents received about
half of the wages that qualified in 2019–24. The bonus for hiring residents began only in 2026,
after the years in these data, and it counts people, not payroll.
Louisiana provides one example of what can happen to film jobs when a state cuts back its
credit. The state buys unused credits back from productions for cash, and claims reached
$243 million in fiscal year 2014. In 2015 Louisiana capped the credits that could be claimed
on returns or transferred to the state at $180 million a year for fiscal years 2016 to 2018 and
suspended buybacks for fiscal year 2016. Its share of US motion picture employment fell from
2.7 percent in 2015 to 1.6 percent in 2016, and had recovered to 2.2 percent by 2022. The
state’s legislative auditor calls this an informal analysis, but it suggests that some productions
may leave quickly when a credit becomes less generous or less certain. Louisiana cut its credit
again in 2017, lowering the base rate from 30 to 25 percent and making permanent both
the $180 million limit on claims and a $150 million annual limit on credits granted in final
certification letters, and in 2024 it lowered both yearly limits to $125 million from July 2025.64
Jobs and pay
Film payroll can rise because film businesses employ more people or because they pay the same
people more. In the ten years after New Mexico, Louisiana and Georgia started or expanded
their credits, real film payroll rose to 7.9–10.5 times its baseline level (Figure 8.8). After each
of Hawaiʻi’s three credit changes, payroll was only 0.9–1.8 times its baseline level. In the
boom states, more jobs account for 71–98 percent of the rise in payroll.65 In Hawaiʻi, film
employment was 0.94–1.23 times its earlier level. In the cases where Hawaiʻi’s film payroll
did rise, it appears to be mainly because each job paid more rather than because there were
more jobs.
Film employment also grew nationally over these years, to 1.05–1.36 times its starting level,
but the boom states grew much faster: relative to national film employment, their film employ-
ment grew 4.7–6.5 times. Hawaiʻi’s film employment kept pace with national film employment
in the ten years after its 1997 and 2013 changes, and fell about 30 percent relative to it in the
ten years after its 2006 increase. The boom states gained both more film businesses and big-
ger ones. Between 1994–96 and 2022–24, the number of film establishments doubled in New
Mexico and Louisiana and more than tripled in Georgia, and jobs per establishment roughly
doubled in New Mexico and Georgia and rose about two and a half times in Louisiana. Hawaiʻi

What is the cost to the state?
Colby (2025) estimates that the state collects between 30 and 50 cents in tax revenue for each
dollar of film credit, a net fiscal loss of $19–$27 million.69
How much Hawaiʻi spends per film job
To estimate how many resident jobs exist on productions which claimed tax credits, we divide
residents’ pay on those productions in each year by that year’s average annual pay per film job
in the state, and add up over each year. With this estimate of the number of jobs, we then divide
the credit, in 2024 prices, by that number of jobs in order to report the cost per resident film
job. We report this in the first column of Table 8.2.70 More importantly, we report the credit per
film job above Hawaiʻi’s early-1990s share in column 2 of Table 8.2. We discussed above that
the patterns in film employment are consistent with a relatively limited response to the credit
in Hawaiʻi. Divided by the film jobs above the early-1990s share, the credit is high: between
$107,000 and $304,000 per job per year, well above a year’s average film pay (though notably,
it is also high in the boom states). The range reflects the choice of baseline years and is not a
margin of error. The figure is a ratio that describes the program, not an estimate of what the
69 Colby (2025), PDF pp.4, 13 and 21–22. The range corresponds to Colby’s estimate that 60 percent
of spending is caused by the credit (30 cents) and to all spending being caused by it (50 cents).

What is the net gain?
For the employment baselines in Table 8.2 over 2019–24, gross credit cost per film job-year
above the early-1990s share is about 2.0–4.7 times average annual film pay, compared with
1.2–1.5 times in the boom states. This comparison does not apply to the DBEDT attribution
scenarios in Table 8.3.
For film, as for solar in section 8.3, we report benefits to residents per dollar of net state
cost, the measure set out in section 8.2. Because we do not know how much of the credited
activity the credit caused, we compute the benefit-cost ratio for several assumed shares, as an
illustration rather than an estimate.
𝑠 × (0.2 × resident wages + 0.1 × local purchases)
Ratio =
credit + Film Office and public-service costs − 𝑠× state tax revenue
In the numerator, only the share the credit caused counts (𝑠), and within it only what residents
gain. For workers, that is the difference between their pay on the production and what they
would have earned in other work, which we assume is a fifth of their wages. For Hawaiʻi
businesses, it is their profit on sales to productions, which we assume is 10 percent. Pay to
nonresidents and gains to studios count as zero. The net cost to the state is the credit plus
the costs of the Film Office and other public services, minus the state taxes that the caused
activity brings in. Unlike the solar calculation, which assumes the credit does not change
other tax revenue, this subtracts those taxes from the cost.73 Table 8.4 displays the ratio for
five assumed shares, next to an income tax cut and a general excise tax cut that cost the state
the same amount. For the tax cuts, the table allows for changes in behavior and leaves out the
part of each cut that goes to nonresidents.

fees, with amounts above the cap counted in the following year. State tax revenue is from the model
in the DBEDT film reports (Bai and Akamine 2021, 2022; Kamita and Akamine 2023, 2024a, 2024b;
Colby 2025), multiplied by the assumed share. All amounts are pooled over 2019–24.

Depending on the proportion of credited activities assumed to be new, residents benefit by
between 7 and 94 cents for each $1 of net cost to the state. At 60 percent, as Colby (2025)
assumes, residents benefit by 40 cents. If the state gives money directly to residents, the ratio
is 1 (see section 8.2). With an income tax cut of the same net cost, residents benefit by about
98 cents ($0.95–$1.05). With a general excise tax cut, residents benefit by about 73 cents
($0.54–$0.86), as visitors pay part of this tax. If we consider everyone, including the studios
and non-resident workers who receive most of the credit, then for every $1 of net cost to the
state, the benefit is between $1.03 and $1.56 ($1.23 at 60 percent).74 If we consider half
of the wages paid to residents on new productions a benefit to residents, and 71 percent of
credited activities are new, the ratio of benefits for residents is approximately 1, even without
tourism. If we randomly sample the assumed values 10,000 times, 3.9 percent of samples give
a ratio of 1 or greater.75
Is there a better alternative?
One option for the film credit is to limit eligible spending to wages for Hawaiʻi residents and
purchases from Hawaiʻi businesses, raising the credit rate to keep total state expenditure ap-
proximately unchanged. This would create greater incentives for productions to employ resi-

dents and purchase from local businesses. However, how much work residents gain depends
on whether local workers and suppliers can replace those brought in from out of state. It would
also change what types of productions get most support. Even if the overall cost for the state
remains constant, the number of productions may not remain constant. To determine whether
this would be a better use of the credit, we would need to estimate how many additional jobs
and how much additional earnings residents would gain. This is discussed in section 8.2.
Not every film credit pays for work done by nonresidents. Ontario’s film credits count only
spending on Ontario residents and businesses. Its credit for Canadian productions pays 35
percent of wages paid to Ontario residents, and its credit for other productions pays 21.5
percent of spending paid to Ontario businesses and residents.76 Canada’s federal credit for
productions filmed there pays 16 percent of labor costs paid to Canadian residents for work
done in Canada.77 A credit built this way pays for what the province wants from it, which is
work for its own residents and sales by its own businesses.
If part of the purpose of the credit is cultural, then we would want culturally specific criteria
applied in allocating it, favoring productions that are about Hawaiʻi or that tell stories that
are important to Hawaiʻi, which need not include every production filmed here. The credit’s
rate currently depends on the county where the cost is incurred and, since 2026, the share of
residents hired, but neither the production’s subject nor its creators’ cultural ties to Hawaiʻi
affects the rate.
A further increase in the rate would be a bet on tourism rather than on jobs, since we detect
no effect of the credit on film jobs in Hawaiʻi, and it would also pay more to productions that
would have come anyway. The local-hire bonus counts people, so a test based on residents’
share of the wages would target their earnings more directly, a smaller step toward the first
option above. Tourism effects take years to appear, so measuring them should start well before
the credit’s 2038 end date.
Effects of the film credit on tourism
One possible benefit of the film credit is that it may promote tourism. This is, however, an
indirect effect that we have not studied. To the extent that it does benefit tourism-based in-
dustries, we would want to count the extra wages of residents working in tourism which stem
from additional spending caused by the film tax credit. One may also consider any social costs
which stem from additional tourism, such as overcrowding or pollution.
Kamita (2024) attributes $1.05 billion of 2022 visitor spending (5.3 percent of the total) to
film and television made in Hawaiʻi, a figure that reflects “the cumulative effect of years of
television program and movie filming,” yet Kamita and Akamine (2024b) omit tourism from
their cost-benefit analysis of the credit.78

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 8. Tax credits
8.5 Renewable fuels and other business credits
The remaining business credits raise the same questions, and 2026 law moved them in both
directions: Act 24 ends the renewable fuels, research and high technology credits, while other
acts extend the enterprise zone and low-income housing credits.79
The renewable fuels production tax credit pays per unit of qualifying fuel sold in Hawaiʻi, for
fuels whose emissions over their production and use are below those of fossil fuels.80 Claims
were $69,000 in TY2023, and the credit ends after TY2028.
The research activities credit is refundable and equals the federal research credit, counting
only research expenses incurred in Hawaiʻi. DBEDT certifies claims in the order it receives
them, up to $5.0 million a year.81 From TY2020 to TY2023 the credit was paid on all qualifying
research and not only on increases over a firm’s earlier spending. In TY2023, 32 firms applied
for $12.7 million of credit, and DBEDT certified $5.0 million to 11 of them. Most applicants
received nothing. A 2024 law went back to the federal rule of paying only on increases in
research, and it limited the credit to firms with no more than 500 employees that do more
than half of their activity in qualified research in the state. For TY2024, DBEDT certified $2.6
million to 18 firms.82 DOTAX reports $7.0 million of research credit claimed on 27 returns for
TY2023, which is more than the $5.0 million DBEDT certified for that year. The two figures
are measured differently, and we have not reconciled them.83
Hawaiʻi data do not show how much research the credit causes. The State Auditor reviewed
the credit in 2022 and could not determine whether it was achieving what the Legislature
intended, because the information on results is unverified answers that applicants give on
a DBEDT questionnaire. The Auditor recommended that the Legislature state the outputs it
wants from the credit and direct DBEDT and DOTAX to carry out a cost-benefit analysis.84 On
that questionnaire, 9 of the 18 firms certified for TY2024 said they would have significantly
reduced or stopped their research without the credit, and 6 said they would have spent nearly
the same amount.85 Evidence from elsewhere suggests that research credits raise research
spending where they are offered. Bloom, Griffith, and Van Reenen (2002) study nine OECD
countries from 1979 to 1997 and find that a 10 percent fall in the cost of research raises
research by just over 1 percent in the short run and just under 10 percent in the long run. For
a state credit, much of that increase appears to come from other states. Wilson (2009) studies
US states from 1981 to 2004. He estimates a long-run elasticity of research in a state of about
−2.5 with respect to the cost of research in that state, and of about +2.5 with respect to the
cost in other states. His estimates suggest that a credit raising research in one state by 3 to
4 percent lowers research elsewhere by about as much, so research nationwide is essentially

The research activities credit and the high technology business investment credit are both
repealed as of January 1, 2029. The high technology credit was closed to new investment in
2010, yet Pew reports that the State Auditor found in 2015 that the state still faced obligations
of almost $2 billion from credits that could be carried forward to later years without limit.87
The enterprise zone credit ($639,000 in TY2023) targets businesses in designated areas, and
Act 182 of 2026 extends the period over which it can be claimed from seven to nine years.88
Opportunity zones, a federal capital gains incentive that Hawaiʻi follows for 25 designated
tracts, are a second incentive tied to place (section 2.5).
The low-income housing tax credit is the largest of these ($60.9 million in TY2023). Act 205
of 2026 lets a partner or member that is a partnership or limited liability company (LLC) sell
an allocated credit once to another taxpayer and keeps the 2016 expansion of the credit in
force through December 31, 2032. The Council on Revenues estimates that the act will cost
the General Fund $57.1 million in FY2028, rising to $72.9 million in FY2033.89
The closest thing we have found to an estimate of what the credit causes is Georgia’s evaluation
of its state credit.90 It attributes about 21 percent of the low-income rental units made available
for occupancy from 2000 to 2009 to the credit. Excluding units that would have been built
anyway, the evaluation finds the credit’s net effect on state output and revenue is negative.91
Slattery and Zidar (2020) study 543 subsidies that US states and localities promised to individ-
ual firms from 2002 to 2017. The average subsidy was $160 million for about 1,500 promised
jobs, or $108,000 per job. They compare counties that won a firm with the runner-up counties.
After the deal, the winning counties have about 1,500 more jobs in the firm’s own industry, but
the authors find no strong evidence of gains in other industries or in total county employment.

9. Joint tax reforms: revenue and household
effects
Some of Hawaiʻi’s taxes lose much more revenue than others when people respond to them.
We simulate a small increase in each tax and ask what taxpayers give up for every dollar the
state ends up collecting (Table 9.1; sections 9.2 to 9.6 take the taxes one at a time). For the
nonresident income tax, the general excise tax (GET) and the property tax, the answer is close
to $1.00 ($1.00, $1.03 and $1.02). It is $1.42 for the income tax as a whole and $2.02 for the
top income-tax rate. Both figures count the high-income households that would leave the state
over time, and without them they are $1.28 and $1.42. The transient accommodations tax
(TAT) costs $1.66. On our estimates, a dollar raised through the TAT therefore costs more than
a dollar raised through the GET, the property tax or the income tax as a whole, mainly because
visitors who cut back also pay less GET. The visitor response behind the estimate is measured
from only two past rate increases, which give $1.31 and $2.55 when taken separately (section
9.4).
These differences matter when one tax replaces another, and section 9.7 reports seven com-
binations (two more are in Appendix I), each set so that state revenue in 2027 is unchanged.
Suppose the GET rose from 4 to 4.5 percent and all income-tax rates were cut by about 27 per-
cent. Taxpayers would then pay about $234 million less. If instead groceries were exempted
from the GET and the TAT were raised to pay for it, the TAT would have to rise by about 4.6
to 4.8 points, and taxpayers as a group would pay much more for the same state revenue. We
do not recommend any of these reforms.
The estimates come from our Hawaiʻi household model, built on PolicyEngine.1 The model
starts from a public Census survey of Hawaiʻi households, pooling three years of responses.
This survey does not ask about capital gains, among other things, so we impute these with
a statistical model fitted to a national sample of IRS tax records; spending is filled in the
same way from a national survey and adjusted to match what Honolulu households spend.
We then reweight the data to match what we know about Hawaiʻi as a whole (for example the
population, total income at each income level, and how many people claim each credit), and
UHERO forecasts of income and population (Bonham et al. 2026) carry the data forward to
2027.2 For each household, the model works out state and federal taxes and benefits under
current law, and again under the reform. The TAT, the property tax and the nonresident income
tax are not paid mainly by the households in the survey, so we estimate them from aggregate
data and add them. Appendix C describes the household model in more detail, and section
1 PolicyEngine is an open-source model of federal and state tax and benefit rules in the United States,
maintained by the organization of the same name. Our Hawaiʻi household model is built on it, with
Hawaiʻi data and adjustments. The tax cut and the tax increase in each combination use the same
assumptions.
2 All amounts are for 2027. Each is an average over three versions of the household data that differ
in how missing income items are filled in.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
9.1 explains how revenue is compared.
9.1 How to read these estimates
A tax increase has two effects on revenue. The mechanical effect is what taxpayers would pay,
and the state would collect, if nobody changed what they did. However, people do respond
(they report less income, sell fewer assets, work less, book fewer hotel nights or move away),
and so the state collects less than the mechanical amount. These responses can also change
what other taxes bring in. A household that pays more income tax has less to spend, and it
therefore pays less GET.
To put the two together, we divide the mechanical amount by what the state actually collects
after people respond, and refer to this as the cost per net dollar raised. For example, when every
ordinary income-tax rate rises by 1 percent, taxpayers would pay $1.42 before they respond
for each $1.00 the state ends up collecting (section 9.2). The cost per net dollar raised is then
1.42: taxpayers give up $1.42, and 42 cents of it never reaches the state because the tax base
has shrunk. A tax at 1.00 loses no revenue to changed behavior, and below 1.00 behavior adds
revenue. Thus, the higher the number, the more expensive the tax is as a way to raise a given
amount.3
Two conventions apply to every estimate in this chapter. First, revenue is counted in the short
run, the first year of the change. A household that gains or loses a dollar changes its spending
only partly in that year, and so the GET on its spending keeps moving afterward. Where this
matters we report what the long run adds, as saved income is spent, and then the total. Second,
the income-tax estimates count the high-income households that leave the state when their
taxes rise, and we give the cost without them beside it.
There are three things the measure does not tell us. First, it does not say who pays: a tax with
a low cost can be paid mostly by households with low incomes, and so the sections below
report this separately. Second, it does not count what the revenue pays for. Finally, it describes
a small change from current law, and a large increase in the same tax would usually cost more
per dollar than a small one.
The nine small increases we simulated are presented in Table 9.1, with what each would raise
in 2027. The steps differ in size, so these revenue amounts say how large each simulated
change is and should not be compared across rows. The costs can be compared.

raised is the mechanical tax increase divided by the net change in state revenue (county revenue for the property
tax). Revenue raised is that net change, counted in the short run. The two columns that follow the cost divide it
between the part that residents bear and the part that others bear, each per net dollar raised; they sum to the cost,

tax by itemizers, and nonresident and part-year filers, who are assumed to respond in proportion to residents: for
all ordinary rates the 0.17 is 0.09 and 0.08, and for the top rate and the capital-gains rate it is all nonresident
and part-year filers. For the GET, others are visitors (0.25), other nonresidents (0.14), and health payers and

household model and aggregate tax data.
The taxes that cost the least per dollar are on land and buildings and on everyday spending,
bases which cannot easily move or shrink. The income tax costs more, and its cost rises with
income, from about 1.0 for the bottom three rates to 2.02 for the top rate. The two highest
costs in the table are also the least certain. They depend on how many high-income households
would leave and on how much visitors would cut back, the two responses that are least well
measured.

===== page 218 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
9.2 The income tax
Each of the five estimates in this section changes one part of the income tax. Beside each cost
per net dollar raised we give the part that residents bear and the part that others bear (the
federal government and nonresident and part-year filers); the two add up to the cost, and the
resident part shows what people who live in Hawaiʻi give up for each dollar the state collects.
People can respond in several ways: they can report more or less taxable income, sell assets
sooner or later, decide whether to work, or leave the state, and pass-through businesses such
as partnerships and S corporations can respond as well. Figures 9.1 to 9.5 chart, for each
estimate, how much revenue the state loses (or, for the credit, gains) through each of these
responses. They chart revenue and do not show who bears the increase; we have no estimates
of the burden of these single changes by tenth.
Two assumptions matter most. The first is how strongly reported income responds. Our main
case takes this response from national evidence, in which it grows with income, and we give
two alternatives with the same response at every income.4 The second is how many high-
income households leave the state when they keep less of their income. We describe that
assumption with the top rate below, where it matters most, and we give each cost without
migration as well.
All ordinary rates
We raised every ordinary rate by 1 percent of itself, so that the 13 percent rate scheduled for
2027 becomes 13.13 percent. Taxpayers would pay $29.7 million more if nobody responded.
Once they respond, the state collects $21.0 million, and the cost per net dollar raised is 1.42
(Figure 9.1). Residents bear 1.25 of this, the federal government 0.09 through the deduction
of state tax by itemizers, and nonresident and part-year filers 0.08. Without migration the
state collects $23.1 million and the cost is 1.28.
Most of the lost revenue comes from one response: residents report less ordinary income, and
the state loses $5.1 million of tax on it, about 18.2 cents of each mechanical dollar. Households
that leave the state cost another $2.07 million, about 7.4 cents. The other responses are small.
Fewer capital-gains sales cost $0.16 million, pass-through businesses $0.06 million and the
decision to work $0.05 million. The state also loses GET, since households have less to spend:
about $0.8 million in the short run, or 2.8 cents of each mechanical dollar.5
4 The main case follows the estimates of Kumar and Liang (2020), in which the response of taxable
income to the share of an extra dollar that a taxpayer keeps is larger at higher incomes. The alter-
natives set this elasticity at 0.3 and at 0.6 for everyone. They give costs of 1.18 and 1.25 for all
ordinary rates, 1.02 and 1.01 for the bottom three rates, and 1.38 and 1.37 for the capital-gains
rate. Without migration these are 1.09 and 1.14, 1.01 and 1.00, and 1.22 and 1.21. A 1 percent step
is a numerical approximation to a small change.
5 Households do not change their spending by a full dollar in the year that they gain or lose a dollar.
Survey evidence indicates that they spend about 57 cents of it within the year, on average. The short-
run figure counts the GET on that part. The rest is saved and spent later, and the long-run figure is
the additional GET on it. We report the two separately wherever a tax change affects GET revenue
through what households have to spend. The split does not apply to the direct effect of a change in
the GET rate, or to the direct cost of exempting groceries, which are the same in the short run and
the long run. The revenue totals, costs per net dollar raised and balancing settings in this chapter
are all for the short run, and the long-run addition is reported beside them.

who itemize deduct state income tax on their federal returns, and so $1.9 million of the $28.1
million that residents pay is passed to the federal government.6
6 Counting only the burden that residents bear after this federal offset, the cost per net dollar raised

The top rate
From 2027 the top rate is 13 percent, on taxable income above $500,000 for single filers and
$1 million for joint filers (chapter 2). We raised it to 13.13 percent. The mechanical increase is
$7.0 million and the state collects $3.5 million, so the cost per net dollar raised is 2.02 (Figure
9.2), of which residents bear 1.93 and nonresidents 0.09. The state loses about 50 cents of
each mechanical dollar. Households that stay report less ordinary income, which costs $1.7
million, or 26.0 cents. Households that leave cost $1.4 million, or 20.8 cents. Most of the rest
is GET on what these households would have spent: about $0.19 million in the short run, or
2.8 cents.
No other change in Table 9.1 costs as much, for two reasons. In our main case the response of
reported income is strongest at the top, and the households that may leave are the ones that
pay this rate. With the same response of reported income at every income, the cost would be
1.43 or 1.57.7
Migration is the less certain of the two. A household that leaves Hawaiʻi is assumed to stop
paying all of its Hawaiʻi income tax and GET, so the state loses more than the increase on that
household. If no household left, the state would collect $4.9 million and the cost would be
1.42. We have no estimate of the migration response from Hawaiʻi data (the assumption is
carried over from studies of other states), and it is therefore the least certain number in this
section.8
A top rate of 14 percent raises $21.0 million at a cost of 2.56 (residents 2.45, nonresidents
0.11). The model scales up the response to a small change in the rate, so we use it only for
changes of a point or two. For the rate at which revenue is highest we apply the formula in
Appendix D to the model’s responses at the current rate, including the households that leave
the state and the response of pass-through businesses. It gives about 22 percent, or about 30
percent if no household leaves. In the other direction, removing the 13 percent rate altogether,
so that the top rate is 11 percent, would lower state revenue by $42.0 million, and taxpayers
would save $2.56 for each dollar the state gave up (residents $2.45, nonresidents $0.11).
Raising the 11 percent rate by one percentage point would raise $4.7 million at a cost of 2.61
(residents 2.18, the federal offset 0.18, nonresident and part-year filers 0.25).
Only households with income in the top bracket pay, and these are almost all in the top tenth
of households by income.
7 The cost is 1.43 with an elasticity of taxable income of 0.3 at every income and 1.57 with 0.6. Without
migration these are 1.10 and 1.18.
8 We assume that the number of high-income households living in Hawaiʻi falls by 1 percent when the
share of income they keep after taxes falls by 1 percent. This applies to households with adjusted
gross income of at least about $645,000 in 2027, and at a quarter of that strength to households
above about $208,000, a ratio taken from Bakija and Slemrod (2004). The value is transported from
evidence on other states through a model of interstate migration, and it describes the long run. A
literal reading of that model gives a larger response of 2.3 and a cost of 4.46. A one-year response
of 0.12, based on California’s experience after 2012, gives 1.47. A lower rate works the other way
and reduces departures. A household that leaves is assumed to owe no Hawaiʻi tax afterward.

The bottom three rates
We raised the three lowest rates by 1 percent of themselves and left the others alone. The
mechanical increase is $3.2 million and the state collects about the same amount. The cost
per net dollar raised is about 1.0, the lowest among the four rate increases (Figure 9.3).
To see why the cost is low, take anyone whose income reaches past the third bracket. They
pay a fixed amount more, but they face the same rate as before on their last dollar, and so
they have no new reason to report less income. This holds for most taxpayers: a change in
the bottom rates changes their tax bill without changing what they keep from an additional
dollar of income. The response comes from taxpayers whose income ends inside the first three
brackets and, in our main case, their reported income responds less than at higher incomes.
Little revenue is lost as a result.9
Nearly every resident who pays income tax pays more. Since the increase is capped at a small
dollar amount, it is a larger share of income for households in the middle of the distribution
than at the top.
9 With an elasticity of taxable income of 0.3 or 0.6 at every income, the cost is 1.02 or 1.01.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
The capital-gains rate
Hawaiʻi taxes long-term capital gains at no more than 7.25 percent, below its upper ordinary
rates (chapter 2). We raised this rate to 7.3225 percent. The mechanical increase is $3.1 mil-
lion and the state collects $2.3 million. The cost per net dollar raised is 1.35 (Figure 9.4), or
1.20 without migration. Residents bear 1.08 of the 1.35 and nonresidents 0.27, since nonres-
idents hold about 20 percent of Hawaiʻi-source gains.
Two responses account for most of the loss. First, people can choose when to sell an asset, and
a higher rate leads them to sell less: in the model, a 1 percent increase in the combined federal
and state tax rate on a gain reduces long-term gains realized by 0.4 percent, and the state loses
$0.36 million. Second, some of the households that pay this tax leave the state, which costs
$0.24 million. The tax on ordinary income moves the other way and rises by $0.04 million.10
Long-term gains are concentrated in a small number of high-income households, and so is this
tax increase. The concentration also makes the result harder to estimate: in the survey data
behind the model, five households hold about 60 percent of long-term gains, so that the result
depends heavily on a handful of households.
10 The realization response is an elasticity of −0.40 with respect to the combined federal and state
marginal tax rate on gains, informed by the estimates of state capital-gains tax changes in Agersnap
and Zidar (2021). The migration response is the one described in the previous subsection.

The food/excise credit
The food/excise credit pays up to $220 per person and falls in steps as income rises, ending
at $40,000 for single filers and $60,000 for others (chapter 2). Cutting it raises revenue, so
we treat a cut as the tax increase. We reduced every credit amount by 1 percent. Households
would receive $0.53 million less, and state revenue would rise by $0.54 million. The cost per
net dollar raised is 1.00 (Figure 9.5), all of it borne by residents.
Before rounding, the cost is slightly below 1.00. The credit is withdrawn as income rises, which
reduces what a household gains from earning more. With a smaller credit there is less to lose
at each step, and in the model a few households report or work slightly more, adding about
$0.007 million of income tax on reported income and $0.005 million from work. However,
households that receive less credit also spend less, and the state loses GET: $0.009 million in
the short run.11
The credit goes to households with low incomes, including those with no income at all, and
they would bear the whole cut (Figure 9.5 shows the small revenue gain from each response,
not who bears the cut).
11 The simulation cuts the credit by fractions of a dollar, which the actual credit schedule would not
do, in order to measure a small change. With an elasticity of taxable income of 0.6 at every income,
the cost is 0.99.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
9.3 The general excise tax
Consider an increase in the state GET rate from 4 percent to 4.5 percent, leaving the county
surcharges alone. Before any change in behavior, taxpayers would pay $608.2 million more
in 2027. The State and counties pay $44.7 million of this on their own purchases, which is
not net revenue to government. About $17.7 million more is lost when people respond, and
the state would collect $545.8 million in the short run. The cost per net dollar raised is 1.03
(Figure 9.6). Resident households bear 0.57 of it, visitors 0.25, other nonresidents 0.14, and
health payers and nonprofits 0.07. A much smaller increase, to 4.04 percent, gives the same
value.12
The cost is close to 1.00 because the model allows few responses to the GET. Households
that pay more tax have less to spend, and the state loses GET on the purchases they give up.
The higher rate also lowers what high-income households keep of their income, and under
the migration assumption of section 9.2 some of them leave. People also work and earn less
when prices rise, which costs about $6.1 million. This is the part of the response of income
that reflects real changes in work and earnings and not changes in how income is reported.
Together the three responses cost the state about $17.7 million. We assume that visitors and
businesses buy the same amounts as before.
Even so, the true cost is probably higher than 1.03. The GET is also paid on sales from one
business to another. The estimate assumes that this tax is passed forward in full to final buyers,
and it does not count how this pyramiding changes the way goods are produced and which
goods are bought (section 3.2). A higher rate makes those distortions larger. Colby (2020)
estimated that pyramiding raises the GET to about 4.72 percent of final taxable consump-
tion (section 3.2), but how much a higher rate adds to the distortions it causes has not been
estimated. We therefore cannot state by how much the true cost exceeds 1.03.
Residents do not pay all of the $608.2 million. Resident households pay $313.8 million (about
52 percent), and about 38 percent of that is tax embedded in prices through business pur-
chases. Visitors pay $137.5 million (about 23 percent). Other nonresidents (the federal gov-
ernment and Medicare, out-of-state buyers and nonresident owners) pay $75.0 million (about
12 percent). The State and counties pay $44.7 million on their own purchases (about 7 per-
cent), and Medicaid, other health payers and nonprofits pay $37.2 million (about 6 percent).13
12 If the rate rises by 0.04 percentage points, taxpayers pay $48.7 million more and state revenue rises
by $43.8 million, a cost per net dollar raised of 1.03. Under the half-point increase, county GET
revenue falls since residents spend less, by about $0.42 million in the short run and about $0.30
million more in the long run ($0.72 million in all).
13 These amounts assign the tax collected on sales between businesses to the final buyers, on the
assumption that it is passed forward in prices (Appendix C).

9.4 The transient accommodations tax
Next we raise the state TAT rate by one percentage point, from 11 percent to 12 percent. The
state would collect $88.5 million more in 2027 if visitors booked the same stays at the same
prices. Our main estimate is that it would collect $53.3 million, which makes the cost per net
dollar raised 1.66 (Figure 9.7). Our GET account puts about seven-eighths of the TAT base
on nonresident guests, but the estimate here does not divide the cost between payers. This
estimate rests on our study of Hawaiʻi’s past TAT increases (Appendix F).14
The state loses revenue from two sources. Take the TAT first. In the two past episodes (1999
and 2009–10) the amount of lodging subject to the tax fell after the rate went up, and on
average TAT revenue rose by 0.87 percent for each 1 percent increase in the rate. This takes
$11.9 million from the $88.5 million. The GET loss is the larger of the two. Visitors who stay
fewer nights or spend less on rooms also spend less on everything else, so the state collects
less GET from them, a loss we estimate at $23.3 million, about two-thirds of the $35.2 million
total.15 Had we counted the TAT alone, we would have missed most of the cost.
This answer depends heavily on which episode we use. The cost per net dollar raised is 1.31
with the response after the 2009–10 increases, but 2.55 with the response after the 1999
increase. (It is 1.00 if visitors do not respond at all.) Where in this range the response to an
increase today would fall is unclear, since we have only two episodes and the GET responses
in the study are imprecisely estimated. The 1.66 uses the average response over the two.
We assign the whole TAT to the guests who pay it, and treat those guests as visitors. Residents
who stay in hotels and vacation rentals pay it too, but there are no direct data on how much
of the base they account for.16 The estimate leaves out the county TATs, which would also lose
revenue when stays fall, and any loss of income tax from lower earnings in tourism.
14 We take the visitor response from the estimates for the TAT rate increases of 1999 and 2009–10 in
Appendix F. As chapter 4 explains, it is hard to separate the effect of a rate increase from everything
else that moves visitor arrivals. We assume that the response in those episodes carries over to today’s
larger tax base and that it is proportional for a change of this size.
15 Averaged over the two episodes in the study, state GET revenue fell by 0.06 percent for each 1
percent increase in the TAT rate. We apply that figure to all state GET revenue projected for 2027
($4.9 billion).
16 For comparison, a staff analysis at DOTAX (DOTAX 2006b) estimated that nonresidents paid about
64 percent of the TAT (chapter 1). Treating every guest as a visitor, as we do here, puts that share at
100 percent. In projecting the tax base to 2027, the $88.5 million figure treats all taxed lodging as
visitor lodging. At the other extreme, treating all of it as resident lodging gives $86 million before
behavior and, with the main response, a cost per net dollar raised of 1.76.

9.5 The property tax
We raised every county property tax rate by 1 percent of its current level, for all classes of
property in all four counties (a rate of 0.35 percent would become 0.3535 percent). Property
tax bills would rise by $30.6 million a year. The counties end up collecting $29.9 million of
that, and the cost per net dollar raised is 1.02 (Figure 9.8). The state has no property tax, and
its revenue falls by about $0.09 million.
The $0.6 million that the counties lose comes from building. Land cannot respond to the tax,
but a higher tax discourages construction over time, and our main case counts that long-run
response. If we instead hold the amount of property fixed, nobody can change behavior to
avoid the tax. The counties then collect all $30.6 million and the cost is 1.00.17
Who bears the tax is a separate question. Buyers pay less for a property when the tax on it
is higher, so part of the increase appears at once as a lower price (an effect known as capi-
talization). We do not assume that the price falls by the value of all the future tax increases.
The share that is capitalized depends on how much of a property’s value is land and on how
strongly buyers and builders respond to prices. For owner-occupied homes it is between 0.63
and 0.83, depending on the county.18 The rest of the increase is passed on to the people who
occupy the property, renters among them, as a higher cost each year.
Of the $30.6 million a year, $21.4 million is capitalized. The people who own property on
the day the increase is announced therefore lose about $759 million in property value (the
capitalized stream, valued today).19 Later buyers pay the higher tax but are compensated by
the lower price. The other $9.2 million is paid by occupants year by year. If instead the whole
increase were capitalized, owners would lose $1.08 billion and renters would bear nothing,
with 63 percent of the cost on resident owners and 37 percent on nonresidents. We treat that
as an upper bound.
These numbers change if assessments follow market values down after a year. The tax base
then shrinks and the loss in property value is $642 million. With the amount of property
fixed, the counties’ gain settles at $25.6 million a year and the cost per net dollar raised is
1.005, which reflects only the higher collections in the first year. With the response of building
counted, the gain is $25.0 million and the cost 1.03.
Resident owners pay 54.6 percent of the annual cost ($16.7 million), renters pay 8.0 percent
($2.4 million) and nonresidents pay 37.4 percent ($11.4 million, of which $2.0 million reaches

changes on single classes.

9.6 The nonresident income tax
Hawaiʻi also taxes people who live elsewhere on their income from Hawaiʻi sources, such as
wages earned here, rents, and gains on Hawaiʻi real estate (chapter 2). Since the household
model covers residents only, we estimate what nonresidents pay separately, using the incomes
reported on nonresident and part-year returns.
Suppose all ordinary income tax rates rise by 1 percent of their current levels for these filers.
They would pay about $1.6 million more a year (Figure 9.9), compared with the $28 million
that the same increase costs resident households before any change in behavior (section 9.2).
This estimate has no behavioral response in it. We assume that nonresident filers report the
same income after the increase. The state then collects all $1.6 million, so the cost per net
dollar raised is 1.00 by construction, which tells us nothing about how nonresidents respond.
Where this chapter splits a cost between residents and others, we assume instead that non-
resident and part-year filers respond in proportion to residents; on that assumption the state
would collect about $1.1 million of the $1.6 million. They may respond more than residents
do, since someone who owns property in several states can choose where to invest and a seller
can choose when to realize a gain. We have no estimate of those responses for Hawaiʻi and
did not want to borrow one from the resident evidence.
The dollar amount is also rougher than the others in this chapter, since we apply the 2027 rate
schedule to the incomes that nonresident filers reported for 2022 without projecting those
incomes forward.22 We therefore read it as the rough size of what nonresident filers would
add to a change in income tax rates. Little of it is paid by people who live in Hawaiʻi all year.
22 The estimate covers only ordinary income. As chapter 2 notes, nonresident and part-year filers re-
ported about a third of the capital gains eligible for the capped rate in tax year 2022. A change in
the rate on gains would therefore reach them more.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
9.7 Revenue-neutral combinations
So far we have changed one tax at a time. The reforms below raise one tax and cut another, so
that the state collects the same revenue in 2027 as it does under current law. To do so we leave
one setting in each combination free (the multiplier on income tax rates, say, or the size of the
TAT increase) and let the model adjust it until state revenue, counted after people respond,
is back where it started. We find that setting by interpolating between two model runs. Two
further combinations, which change how capital gains are taxed, are reported in Appendix I.
As in the earlier sections, the revenue is counted in the short run and the income-tax changes
include migration. County revenue is not held fixed, and we report it where it moves.
What changes, then, is the amount taxpayers pay. We call this the burden change, by which
we mean the change in what taxpayers pay before they adjust their behavior, at equal state
revenue. If it is negative then the state collects the same revenue while taxpayers pay less.
That is to say, the combination is more efficient than current law. If it is positive, they pay
more. The sign follows from the costs per net dollar raised in Table 9.1. Thus, a combination
is favorable when it raises money from a tax with a low cost and uses that money to cut a tax
with a high one.
Table 9.2 lists the seven combinations, ordered from most to least favorable. None of them is
a recommendation.
The burden change measures efficiency only, and it says nothing about who gains and who
loses. For that we chart, for each combination, the average change for households in each
tenth of the income distribution.23 The charts show the change in what each household has
to spend: the income it earns and reports, less its state and federal income tax and the GET
it pays. They leave out the TAT and the property tax. Counting income matters most in the
top tenth. When a rate falls, people earn and report more income, and the charts count that
income as a gain to the household; when a rate rises, they count the income no longer earned
or reported as a loss. The top tenth therefore gains in the combinations that lower its rates
and loses in those that raise them, often by more than the change in its tax bill.

Combination Balancing setting Burden change
GET rate from 4% to 4.5%, all ordinary Income tax rates ×0.734 −$234.0 million
income tax rates cut

in state transfers to counties
(illustrative)

A higher GET rate with lower income tax rates
This combination raises the state GET rate from 4 percent to 4.5 percent while cutting every
ordinary income tax rate by the same proportion. To keep state revenue unchanged the rates
must be cut by about 27 percent. State GET revenue rises by about $584 million in the short
run, which includes the GET on what households spend out of their income tax cut. In the
long run, as saved income is spent, it rises by a further $14.2 million, to about $598 million.
Income tax revenue falls by about as much as GET revenue rises.
Taxpayers pay about $234 million less in total, which is the largest reduction among the com-
binations. They pay about $564 million more in GET ($608 million with the GET that govern-
ment pays on its own purchases) and about $797 million less in income tax ($754.6 million
for residents and $42.9 million for nonresident filers). The income tax cut can be that large
because people report more income when rates fall and fewer high-income households leave,
which returns about $252 million to the state. The GET increase loses only about $17.7 million
to changed behavior in the short run ($6.4 million of GET on lower spending, $6.1 million
from work, $3.9 million from households that move away and $1.3 million from people who
stop working) and $6.0 million more in the long run. This reflects the gap in Table 9.1, where a
dollar of state revenue costs taxpayers about $1.03 through the GET and about $1.42 through
ordinary income tax rates.24
However, the combination also shifts who pays. Households in the bottom four tenths of the
income distribution lose on average (Figure 9.10), by about $210, $257, $250 and $185 a
year. Because these households spend most of what they earn and owe little income tax, a
higher GET costs them more than lower income tax rates return. From the fifth tenth up the
average household gains, and the gains grow quickly with income: about $90 in the fifth tenth,
$1,169 in the eighth, $2,434 in the ninth and $10,389 in the top tenth, where 87 percent of
households gain. These gains are larger than the tax cut alone, since the chart also counts the
24 The $1.03 for the GET assigns the tax on business purchases to final buyers but does not count the

spending fixed. Counting either would raise the cost of the GET and shrink the $234 million.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
A grocery exemption paid for by the income tax
Chapter 3 discusses the grocery exemption on its own. To pay for it through the income tax,
the state must raise every ordinary income tax rate by the same proportion. In our main case
stores pass the full tax cut through to shoppers in lower prices, since most studies of food tax
cuts find that most or all of the cut reaches shoppers.
The exemption lowers state GET payments by about $263 million and county surcharge pay-
ments by about $33 million, or $295 million in all. About $7 million of the state’s loss comes
back within the year, mostly as GET on what residents buy with their savings. Thus, the state
has about $255 million to replace in the short run, and the income tax rates are set to replace
that amount. In the long run, as saved income is spent, a further $3.3 million comes back from
the exemption. The income tax increase works the other way, however. Households that pay
more income tax spend less, and in the long run this costs the state about $5.3 million of GET.
For the combination as a whole, state GET revenue therefore falls by about $266 million in
the short run and by $2.0 million more in the long run.
This requires multiplying income tax rates by 1.124, an increase of about 12 percent in every
rate. Before they respond, taxpayers pay about $369.4 million more in income tax ($349.5
million from residents and $19.9 million from nonresident filers) and $262.6 million less in
state GET. However, some of them then report less income and some leave the state, and the
state loses about $114 million of the income tax increase (from residents, $108 million: $70.8
million on ordinary income, $25.8 million from households that move away, $1.1 million on
capital gains and $5.4 million in GET; and $6.1 million from nonresident filers). With 70
percent pass-through the state’s loss is about $248 million, not $255 million, and the income-
tax increase needed is slightly smaller (rates multiplied by about 1.121, not 1.124). In total
taxpayers pay about $106.8 million more than under current law for the same state revenue,
since the combination moves revenue from the GET, which is among the lowest-cost taxes in
Table 9.1, to the income tax, which costs more.
With full pass-through, the average household gains about $175 a year in the bottom tenth
of the income distribution and about $261 in the fifth (Figure 9.11); these households buy
groceries every week and owe little income tax. Higher up, the income tax increase outweighs
the grocery savings. The losses begin in the eighth tenth, and the average household in the
top tenth loses about $3,229. The loss at the top is larger than the added tax, since the chart
also counts the income that these households no longer earn or report at the higher rates.26
Since the exemption applies to county surcharges as well, county revenue falls by about $30.0
million once the same recovery is counted. The combination does not replace it.
26 The combination balances state revenue only. County revenue falls by about $30.0 million, and
the model also reports about $261.1 million less in federal income tax, since people who report
less income to Hawaiʻi report less to the federal government as well. Both flows sit outside the
balance. Visitors also receive part of the grocery savings. The split by income tenth follows the
earlier allocation of the GET among households, so it shows the pattern across tenths more reliably
than the dollar amounts.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
A grocery exemption paid for by the TAT
The same exemption can instead be paid for by visitors’ lodging. In this combination groceries
are exempt and the state TAT rate rises until the roughly $255 million is replaced. The size of
the rate increase depends on how visitors respond to it. Here and in the next combination, as
in section 9.4, we treat all of the TAT as paid by visitors.
Suppose that visitors did not respond at all. The TAT rate would then need to rise by about 2.9
percentage points, and taxpayers as a group would pay slightly less than under current law,
since the exemption returns a little revenue through added spending and the TAT loses none.
This case is unlikely, however, since our estimates from Hawaiʻi’s past TAT increases indicate
that a higher TAT reduces visitor spending. This means that the state collects less TAT than
the rate increase alone would bring in, and it also collects less GET on what visitors would
have spent (for a one-point increase, the second loss is about twice the first; see section 9.4).
With that response, the TAT rate has to rise by about 4.6 to 4.8 percentage points to replace
the same revenue, and taxpayers as a group pay much more than under current law. This is
the least favorable of the seven combinations. To see why, note that each point of TAT now
brings in less than it would without a response. The rate must therefore climb further, and
everyone who still pays the tax pays the higher rate.27
Figure 9.12 should be read with care, since it counts only the grocery savings. These savings
do not depend on how the exemption is paid for, and so every household gains. With full pass-
through the average saving is about $177 a year in the bottom tenth, $251 in the second and
$754 in the top tenth. The figure does not include the TAT that residents pay on their own
stays in Hawaiʻi hotels and vacation rentals, which the model does not assign to households,
nor any effect of lower visitor spending on residents’ jobs and earnings.28 County revenue falls
by about $33 million before responses, and the combination does not replace it.
27 The response estimates come from past changes in the TAT rate of about one point. The required
increase is 4.6 points if the response to a one-point increase is carried over at a constant elasticity
and 4.8 if it is carried over in proportion. Without a response the increase is about 2.9 points.
28 Appendix F also reports effects of the TAT on payrolls in recreation and restaurants. These are not
included in any figure in this chapter.

===== page 246 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
A higher TAT rate with lower income tax rates
The last combination in this group raises the state TAT rate by one percentage point and uses
the revenue to cut all ordinary income tax rates by the same proportion. Could Hawaiʻi lower
the cost of its tax system by moving revenue from the income tax to a tax that visitors largely
pay?
Again, the answer depends on the visitor response. Before any response, one more point of
TAT would bring in about $89 million. With the estimated visitor response the state keeps
about $53 million. The loss comes from two sources: about $12 million because the taxed
lodging base shrinks, and about $23 million because visitors spend less on goods and services
that carry the GET. The $53 million that remains pays for a cut of about 2.7 percent in income
tax rates.
Before any response, guests pay $88.5 million more in TAT and income-tax filers pay $79.4
million less (residents $75.2 million, nonresident filers $4.3 million), so taxpayers as a group
pay about $9.1 million more. This is because the combination raises a dollar through the TAT
at a cost of about $1.66 and uses it to cut a tax that costs about $1.42. If instead visitors did
not respond, the TAT would cost $1.00 per dollar, the income tax cut could be about two-
thirds deeper, and taxpayers would pay roughly $43 million less.29 Thus, the visitor response
reverses the result, as it does for the grocery exemption paid for by the TAT.
The burden change does not tell us who pays. Visitors pay much of the added TAT while the
income tax cut goes mostly to residents, so a combination can cost taxpayers as a group more
and still leave residents ahead. We cannot say whether this one does, since the model does
not divide the TAT burden among visitors, residents who stay in local lodging, and the owners
and workers of the visitor industry. As a result, Figure 9.13 shows only the income tax side.
Average gains rise from under $1 in the bottom tenth to about $67 in the fifth, $340 in the
ninth and $1,210 in the top tenth. They count the added income that people report at the
lower rates as well as the tax cut.30
29 The case without a visitor response is scaled from the main case in proportion to the larger amount
of revenue, which is an approximation. It gives a multiplier of about 0.956.
30 The estimated response is the average over the two episodes in Appendix F. The two episodes taken
separately give costs of about $1.31 and $2.55 per net dollar for the TAT. At $1.31 the combination
would be slightly favorable, since $1.31 is below the $1.42 cost of the income tax; at $2.55 it would
be much more unfavorable.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
A top rate of 14 percent, with a larger food/excise credit
The food/excise credit pays up to $220 per person to households with low incomes (chapter 2).
Here the top income-tax rate rises from 13 to 14 percent and all of the revenue goes into the
credit. Each credit amount could be 40 percent larger, which would bring the largest amount
to about $307 per person. Taxpayers as a group would pay about $32.6 million a year more
than they do now for the same state revenue (Figure 9.14).
Before anyone responds, the higher top rate adds $53.8 million to tax bills. But those who
pay it report less income and some of them leave, and it brings in only $21.0 million after
responses in the short run (in the long run, $1.3 million less). That is all that is available
for the credit, and its recipients barely change what they do when it grows. So high-income
taxpayers give up $53.8 million and lower-income households receive $21.1 million, and the
$32.6 million between the two is the loss. Whether that is worth doing depends on how much
the state values a dollar in the hands of each group. The model does not tell us that.
The gains are largest at the bottom. In the bottom three tenths households gain $49 to $76
a year on average. From the fourth to the eighth tenth the average gain is $5 to $41, and the
ninth tenth loses $3.31
The top tenth, the group that pays the higher rate, loses $1,464 a year on average. That is
more than its added state tax, since the chart also counts the income that these households
no longer earn or report at the higher rate. Federal tax paid by residents falls by about $52.7
million in this simulation.32
31 The households in the middle of the distribution that gain are likely to be those that include a lower-
income tax filer, such as an adult child or an older relative, who claims the credit.

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
A top rate of 14 percent, with lower bottom rates
Here the same one-point increase in the top rate pays for cuts in the bottom three income-tax
rates, which are 1.4, 2.5 and 5 percent in 2027. Each could fall by 6.6 percent, to about 1.31,
2.34 and 4.67 percent. Taxpayers as a group would pay about $31.2 million a year more for
the same state revenue (Figure 9.15).
The arithmetic is close to that of the larger credit. The top rate again takes $53.8 million and
brings in $21.0 million after responses. The lower bottom rates hand back $22.6 million, and
about $1.5 million of that returns to the state as people earn and report slightly more.
What differs is who receives the money. Nearly every taxpayer has some income taxed at
the bottom three rates, so a cut in them reaches households at every income level, and the
average household gains in every tenth below the top. These gains rise with income, however:
the average is $1 a year in the bottom tenth, $22 in the third, $45 in the fifth and $84 in the
ninth. Households in the bottom tenth owe little or no income tax to begin with. Under the
larger credit of the previous section, those same households receive about $49 a year. If the
aim is to help the state’s lowest-income residents, the credit gets much more to them at about
the same cost.
The top tenth again loses on average, by $1,351 a year, for the reason given above. Federal
income tax paid by residents falls by about $47.4 million.33

Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
An illustrative swap of property tax for income tax
The property tax belongs to the counties, so the state cannot raise it and spend the proceeds.
We therefore simulate a three-part arrangement, as an illustration only. The counties raise
the property tax on owner-occupied homes by 1 percent, which brings in about $8.0 million
a year.34 The state reduces its transfers to the counties by the same amount, leaving county
budgets where they started. The state then uses the saving to cut all ordinary income-tax rates,
by about 0.4 percent. Taxpayers as a group would pay about $5.3 million a year less for the
same state and county revenue (Figure 9.16).
We kept the swap small, so the gain is small too. The property tax brings in $8.0 million more,
all of it paid by residents who own and live in their homes. Income-tax bills fall by $13.3
million, but the cut costs the state only $8.0 million after responses: people report a little
more income at lower rates and fewer high-income households leave, and $5.3 million comes
back. What produces the saving is the difference in cost between the two taxes. Land cannot
leave and buildings respond slowly, so a dollar of property tax costs taxpayers $1.02, or $1.00
if the amount of property is fixed (section 9.5). A dollar from all income-tax rates costs $1.42
(section 9.2). Thus, if those assumptions hold, a larger swap of this kind would save roughly
40 cents for each dollar moved from the income tax to the property tax.
The household model does not let owners respond to the higher rate on owner-occupied homes
by moving a property into another tax class, for example by renting it out. Section 5.5 esti-
mates such moves with a separate model of individual parcels, and finds that taxing one class
at a different rate leads some owners to move properties between classes, which changes both
revenue and the number of homes. Because this swap raises the rate on one class only, its
result here probably looks somewhat better for the property tax than it would if those moves
were counted.
Figure 9.16 shows the income tax side only. The average household gains in every tenth: under
$1 a year in the bottom tenth of the income distribution, about $14 in the fifth and $219 in
the top tenth. The income tax cut and the added income reported at lower rates go mostly
to households with high incomes.35 The figure does not include the transfer or the value of
county services.
34 Income-tax rates are multiplied by 0.9955.
35 The property side is the change in tax on owner-occupied homes before any response; we do not
assign it to income tenths.

===== page 253 =====


===== page 254 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 9. Joint tax reforms
Two combinations that change how capital gains are taxed
We simulated two further combinations. In the first, long-term capital gains are taxed as or-
dinary income and the top rate falls to about 10.1 percent; taxpayers as a group would pay
about $65 million a year less. In the second, long-term gains are exempt and the top rate rises
to about 25.7 percent; taxpayers would pay about $402 million more. Both are balanced by
extrapolating beyond the model runs, and both involve large changes to the top rate or to the
taxation of capital gains, where the model’s calculation of responses is least accurate. Their
results are therefore more uncertain than the others in this section, and we report them in
Appendix I.
9.8 What the estimates leave out
Three things are left out of the estimates in this chapter. The first is the effect of GET pyramid-
ing, the tax on purchases that businesses make from one another (chapter 3). The estimates
assign this tax to the final buyers, as if it were passed forward in full in prices, but they do not
count the changes in what businesses produce and buy (section 9.3), and so the GET looks
cheaper than it is. Thus, the gain from the GET-for-income-tax swap is probably overstated.
Nor do we allow owners to respond to property tax rates by moving a property from one class
to another (chapter 5). Finally, the model’s population is not built from Hawaiʻi tax returns.
It starts from a Census survey and is adjusted to match published tax statistics (Appendix
C). Beyond these three, the model does not cover the corporate income tax or the estate tax,
so neither appears in Table 9.1, and we have not simulated any change to the state earned
income tax credit.
Two of the responses that the estimates do count are long-run responses and are not well mea-
sured. The first is migration, which is in every income-tax estimate and in the combinations of
section 9.7. Without it the cost of a dollar from the top rate falls from $2.02 to $1.42 (section
9.2). So the combinations that raise the top rate would look less costly without migration, and
the one that lowers it would look less favorable. The second is the response of building to the
property tax. With the amount of property fixed, the cost of the property tax falls from 1.02
to 1.00 (section 9.5).

===== page 255 =====
10. What we cannot know yet, and how
Hawaiʻi could know more
There are things in this report that we cannot know right now, or that are highly uncertain,
but which we think Hawaiʻi could know much better given additional research and better use
of information it already has. Our estimates of revenue and of who gains and loses depend on
how people and businesses respond to taxes. Most of the evidence on these responses comes
from other places.1 This evidence helps show how taxes affect work, investment, prices and
where people live. But a large share of purchases in Hawaiʻi are made by visitors, housing
costs are among the highest in the country, and moving to another state requires crossing an
ocean. Responses here could differ from those on the mainland. The higher cost of moving
suggests smaller migration responses, but no study measures the difference for Hawaiʻi. How
much the assumed response matters varies by tax. For the top income-tax rate, the cost per
net dollar raised is 2.02 in our main case, and 1.43 to 1.57 if every taxpayer responded alike
(section 9.2).2
Our household model (Appendix C) is weighted to match Hawaiʻi totals, but it has not been
checked against Hawaiʻi data other than the totals it is weighted to. Income the household
survey does not collect, such as capital gains, is imputed from national tax data, so estimates
for reforms aimed at top incomes are the least certain.
Take migration. The estimates for the top income-tax rate in chapter 9 allow high-income
households to leave the state. For a small increase in that rate, this raises the cost per net
1 For several of these responses, the evidence cited in this report has no comparable estimate for
Hawaiʻi. On taxes, they are how migration and reported income respond, how much of the GET
businesses pass on through prices, how demand for luxury goods responds to a tax, how lodging-tax
responses differ by hotel class, and how construction responds to property taxes. The cited evidence
also has no Hawaiʻi estimate of how much solar and housing activity is caused by credits, how solar
subsidies are divided between buyers and installers, how inheritances affect work, or how much
future tax revenue and savings on benefits follow as children grow up. In our household model, for
example, the response of reported income follows national estimates (Kumar and Liang 2020), the
response of capital-gains realizations follows a study of state tax changes (an elasticity of −0.40;
Agersnap and Zidar 2021), and the migration response is carried over from studies of other states
(chapter 9). The model assumes that the GET is passed on in full to prices (Appendix C). The TAT
response in chapter 9 comes from Hawaiʻi’s 1999 and 2009–10 increases but does not separate hotel
classes. For the property tax, chapter 9 holds the amount of land fixed but lets owners build and
maintain a little less when the tax rises. The cost is then 1.02 per net dollar raised, and it would be
1.00 if the amount of building were fixed too.
2 The 1.43 and 1.57 assume that reported income responds with an elasticity of 0.3 or 0.6 at all
incomes.
253

===== page 256 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 10. What we cannot know yet
dollar raised from 1.42 to 2.02 (section 9.2).3 The response we assume is carried over from
studies of other states (section 1.6), not measured in Hawaiʻi, and plausible alternatives give
anything from 1.47 to 4.46. It is the least certain number in that chapter.
Hawaiʻi data could sharpen those estimates and show where responses here differ. Where
we have Hawaiʻi estimates, such as our own estimates of how TAT revenue responds to the
TAT rate (Appendix F), we use them. Hawaiʻi’s own tax changes, such as the end of the 2023
credit expansions and the changes in the top rates since 2009, could identify some of these
responses with Hawaiʻi data (Appendix B, section B.1). Other gaps are harder to close. Taxes
change alongside other things in the economy, and it can be hard to tell how much of what
happens is due to the tax. Research often gives us a range of possible responses. Which of
these is most relevant for Hawaiʻi depends on the circumstances. The TAT is an example. Our
study of Hawaiʻi’s past TAT increases gives a cost per net dollar raised of 1.31 from the 2009–
10 increases and 2.55 from the 1999 increase (section 9.4 and Appendix F). Which value is
closer to the truth matters for the combinations in section 9.7 that use the TAT.
Much of the information we would want is already in the records of DOTAX and other state
agencies. DOTAX has tax returns, the Department of Labor and Industrial Relations (DLIR)
has wage records, and the Department of Human Services (DHS) has records of SNAP, TANF
and Medicaid. Researchers in government and outside it have difficulty using these records
together. If the state wants estimates based on how Hawaiʻi households respond, tax returns
would need to be linked with earnings and benefit records for the same households over time,
with DOTAX, DLIR and DHS sharing records under written rules for their use.
Some of our questions can only be answered with tax records linked to other agencies’ records.
For example, researchers in California estimated which families receiving food or cash assis-
tance were likely to receive the expanded federal child tax credit in 2021 by linking tax-filing,
wage and benefit records.4 Linking Hawaiʻi tax returns to wage and benefit records in the
same way would help estimate how many Hawaiʻi families are eligible for credits but do not
receive them because they do not file a return.
Each agency has legal and privacy obligations for the records it holds, so linking them would
require legal authority and agreements among the agencies (tax returns, as discussed below,
are confidential). Before the records are linked, each agency would turn names, Social Security
numbers and other identifiers into scrambled codes. A separate team would then link the
records using the scrambled codes, so that the people who link and analyze the records never
see names or Social Security numbers. Access would be given only to trained researchers, the
3 The 2.02 assumes a long-run migration elasticity of 1.0 for the top 1 percent of households and a

gives 1.47, and a long-run response of 2.3 gives 4.46. The model allows no tax-induced migration
below about the 90th percentile of income and does not model anyone moving to Hawaiʻi, so for
broad reforms these costs leave out both responses. A cut in the top rate is credited with fewer
departures, but not with new arrivals. Under the same assumption, the cost for all ordinary income-
tax rates rises from 1.28 to 1.42, and for the capital-gains rate from 1.20 to 1.35. The revenue-neutral

5 Ramesh et al. (2022), pp. 1, 7 and 13, describe this “hashed” linkage. They recommend that the
team linking the records never see the identifiers, or the “salt,” which is a secret value added to each
identifier before it is scrambled. Once the records are linked, the scrambled identifiers are deleted
or locked away. California Policy Lab (n.d.) describes the review of results for personal information.
6 DOTAX, General Instructions for Filing the General Excise/Use Tax Returns (Rev. 2025), pp. 4–6, lists
the activity classifications. Form BB-1 (Rev. 9/2023), line 15, asks for the NAICS code and the prin-
cipal business activity. DOTAX (2026b), printed p. 5, assigns each business one industry code, taken
from the code the taxpayer reports on its return, and does not disclose tabulations with five or fewer
returns at the state level or 10 or fewer below it.
