===== page 258 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission 10. What we cannot know yet
no published table connects the GET activity lines to industries, the estimates of pyramiding
by industry in section 3.2 and Appendix E start from DBEDT’s input-output table, and we can
only check these estimates against statewide GET totals.
For the rows of Table 10.1 that concern DOTAX data alone, what would help is access for
research to data from individual returns, with names and other identifiers removed and under
rules that keep the data confidential.7 With income tax returns, the top incomes and capital
gains in the household model could be checked against what Hawaiʻi taxpayers report. With
business returns, corporate payments and refunds could be assigned to the years they apply
to, and pass-through entity tax payments could be matched with the credits that members
claim. With estate tax payments by estate, the long-run average revenue of that tax could be
estimated. Other states have given access of this kind, and they are subject to the same federal
rules on tax information as Hawaiʻi. In California, the Franchise Tax Board gave researchers
at the California Policy Lab data from tax-year 2017 returns for a study of who receives the
earned income tax credit (Davis and White 2019). It gave other researchers the returns of
all California filers for 2000 to 2015 for a study of how high earners responded to the 2012
tax increase (Rauh and Shyu 2024). In New Jersey, the Division of Taxation gave university
researchers the complete resident income tax returns for 2000 to 2007 for a study of whether
high earners moved after a tax increase (Young and Varner 2011).8 In Minnesota, state law
requires the revenue commissioner to prepare samples of income tax returns for estimating
revenue, modeling changes in the law and analyzing who bears a tax, for use by the revenue
and budget departments and the legislature.9 Access of this kind would not link returns to
other agencies’ records, which is a separate step described earlier in this chapter.
7 Tax returns are confidential under state law; see HRS §§231-18 and 235-116.
8 Davis and White (2019), p. 6; Rauh and Shyu (2024), pp. 48–49; Young and Varner (2011), p. 264.
9 Minn. Stat. §270C.12.

Technical appendices
A. Data, current law and revenue reconciliation
Each of the seven combinations in section 9.7 pairs a tax increase with a tax cut and sets one
rate so that state revenue in 2027, counted after people respond, is unchanged. We find that
rate by interpolating between two model runs. Two more combinations, which are balanced
by extrapolating beyond the runs, are in Appendix I. County revenue is not held fixed, and
section 9.7 reports it where it moves: the grocery exemption, for example, reduces county
surcharge revenue by about $33 million before responses.1
All model estimates are for calendar 2027 and average three versions of the household data
that differ in how missing income is filled in. The mechanical change is what taxpayers would
pay before anyone responds, and it includes what visitors and nonresidents pay. Net revenue
is the change after responses, counted for the government that levies the tax: the state for the
income tax, GET and TAT, and the counties for the property tax. Changes in federal revenue
are not counted. The cost per net dollar raised is the mechanical change divided by net revenue.
In Table 1.1, DOTAX’s FY2025 total of $11.46 billion includes $565.3 million of county GET
surcharges and $87.8 million of county fuel tax that the state collects for the counties.2 County
property taxes are the amounts the four counties levied for FY2025: $1,733.4 million in Hon-
olulu, $585.4 million in Maui, $507.5 million in Hawaiʻi County and $235.1 million in Kauaʻi,
or $3,061.4 million in all.3 These are amounts levied, not collections. County TATs for FY2025
are $104.9 million in Honolulu and $72.2 million in Maui, from their annual comprehensive
financial reports,4 and $40.0 million in Hawaiʻi County, from the county’s proposed FY2027
budget.5 Kauaʻi had not published audited statements for FY2025, so we use its FY2024 figure,
$30.2 million.6 The Hawaiʻi County figure is unaudited and Kauaʻi’s is for the earlier year, so
the two are not strictly comparable with the others. The four add to $247.3 million. The state
and county rows add the county surcharges to the state GET and use tax ($4,630.4 million),
and the county TATs to the state TAT ($816.2 million).7

B. Evidence standards and methods
How people and businesses will respond to a tax change is not known exactly. We give esti-
mates based on different plausible assumptions, and choose a preferred estimate only when
there is much more evidence for it than for the others. Evidence from past reforms may be less
informative about very different reforms.8 Whatever the method, the effect of a large policy
change is estimated with substantial uncertainty. For a 1 percent rise in the top income-tax
rate, for example, the cost per net dollar raised is 1.10 or 1.18 with a constant income response
of 0.3 or 0.6, and 1.42 with the income-varying response we use as the main case, when no
household leaves the state. Allowing high-income households to leave the state gives 1.47
with a one-year response, 2.02 with our main long-run value and 4.46 with a larger long-run
value (section 9.2).9
B.1 Learning from Hawaiʻi’s own tax changes
Most of the responses this report relies on were estimated in other places and periods (Ap-
pendix D). Hawaiʻi’s own tax changes offer a way to estimate some of them here. When a
change in the law changes taxes for some households and not for others, or by more for some
than for others, we can compare how reported income, work, credit claims and residence
change for the two groups after the change. This difference is an estimate of the response to
the tax if anything else that changed at the same time affected both groups in the same way.
Three sets of recent changes could be used in this way. The first is Act 46 of 2024, which raises
the standard deduction and widens the brackets in steps through TY2031, so that the size of
the tax cut differs by income and by year. The second is the expansions of the earned income
tax credit, the food/excise tax credit and the dependent care credit made in 2023, which
end after TY2027. The expansions change the credits for some low- and moderate-income
families and not for others, so the claims and earnings of these families before and after the
expansions would show how credit receipt and work respond. The third is the top income tax
rates. Brackets with rates up to 11 percent applied from TY2009 to TY2015, lapsed in TY2016
and TY2017, and returned from TY2018, and a 13 percent top rate takes effect in TY2027.10
Comparing filers with incomes above and below the thresholds, before and after each change,
would show how reported top incomes respond in Hawaiʻi, and following the filers who leave
or arrive would show how moves respond.
Each of these comparisons has limits. The 2009 rates began during a recession, which may
have changed top incomes for other reasons. A change that affects few filers gives imprecise
estimates, which may not rule out a response of zero. Tax records also show a move out of the
state only as a return that stops being filed or becomes a nonresident return. To estimate how
Hawaiʻi households respond, we would need tax returns for the same filers over time and, for
credits and work, returns linked with wage and benefit records (chapter 10).
8 Responses can vary with what is taxed, how the tax is enforced and how visible a change is (Saez et al.
2012). In a grocery-store experiment, Chetty et al. (2009) find that posting prices that included the
sales tax reduced demand for the affected products by about 8 percent, and that alcohol consumption
fell more when a tax was included in posted prices than when it was added at the register.
9 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model.
10 Act 60 (SLH 2009); Act 107 (SLH 2017); Act 24 (SLH 2026), §2; Act 46 (SLH 2024), §§1–2; Act
163 (SLH 2023), §5. Chapter 2 describes each change.

C. The Hawaiʻi household model
The household model is a representative sample of Hawaiʻi’s resident households, weighted
so that together they match Hawaiʻi totals. We start with Hawaiʻi households from the Census
Bureau’s Current Population Survey (US Census Bureau n.d.-b) and add income and spend-
ing that the survey does not collect, such as capital gains.11 Wages are those that respondents
report to the survey. The survey has too few respondents with wages above $200,000 to de-
scribe them well, so for these respondents we take wages and other income together from
IRS tax data. We calculate each household’s federal and state income taxes, payroll taxes,
GET on spending and benefits such as SNAP, both under current law and with the proposed
change. The TAT, the county property tax and the income tax paid by nonresidents fall mostly
outside these households, so we estimate them from aggregate data and add them (section
9.1). For the property tax we assume that the tax on land and part of the tax on buildings are
reflected in lower property values and that occupants bear the rest. Full capitalization is the
upper bound. Chapter 9 treats all of the state TAT as borne by visitors, because there are no
data on residents’ share of the base.12
The weights make the sample match several hundred statewide totals, each to within its own
tolerance. These include the number of people and households, income, deductions and taxes
on federal returns, the Hawaiʻi income tax residents report at each income level, state tax
credits, SNAP cases and benefits, and Medicaid enrollment. For these totals we used aggregate
statistics from DOTAX reports and the federal IRS Statistics of Income (SOI) tables, among
other sources.13 The weighted sample has about 700,000 wage earners, compared with about
717,000 in the American Community Survey, and its wages at the 90th and 95th percentiles,
about $125,000 and $164,000, are close to the survey’s $122,000 and $162,000. Its median
wage, about $44,000, is below the survey’s $51,000, because the weights match the wages
reported on tax returns, which total $42.1 billion, compared with $46.2 billion in the survey.
The GET figures assign the whole tax to the people and institutions that finally pay it. They
assume that tax collected from businesses is passed forward in prices. A household therefore
bears the GET on its own taxable spending and the tax embedded in the prices it pays. About
37 percent of state GET liability is collected on sales between businesses. We allocate this
embedded tax to final buyers using input-output tables, which trace purchases along supply
chains. About half of the tax goes to resident households and a little under a quarter to visitors.
The rest goes to governments, health payers, nonprofits and out-of-state buyers. This split
changes little whether we use a national input-output table or one for Hawaiʻi. Spending levels
come from federal and state expenditure statistics and are reconciled with DOTAX data on the
11 The added income comes from IRS tax data (IRS Statistics of Income n.d.-d), and spending from
the Bureau of Labor Statistics’ Consumer Expenditure Survey (Bureau of Labor Statistics n.d.-c),
adjusted to published Honolulu averages (Bureau of Labor Statistics 2026). PolicyEngine US provides
the foundation for the report’s household calculations (PolicyEngine n.d.).
12 For comparison, DOTAX staff estimated that residents pay about 4 percent of the TAT directly and
that, after part of the tax is passed back to Hawaiʻi incomes, about 64 percent is borne outside
Hawaiʻi, mainly by visitors, not counting the part shifted to the federal government (DOTAX 2006b,
p. 8 and Appendix D, Table 2; PFM Group Consulting 2017, p. 52).

on the earned income tax credit, and data from the US Department of Agriculture (n.d.) and the
Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (n.d.) on SNAP and Medicaid.

tax base. Where a survey measure exceeded the reported tax base, as for visitor lodging, we
used the tax data. The responses to a GET change were estimated before this allocation was
made, and we scale them to the tax it assigns to households and visitors. The GET that the
state gains or loses when an income-tax change alters what households spend is counted
on the same allocation. Three inputs are approximate: government purchases from private
vendors, the level of exports, and the share of visitor food spending that a grocery exemption
would cover. Rent that tenants pay is counted as taxable spending in the same way, so renters
bear the GET on it. In 2027 the model charges the GET on about 60 percent of what residents
spend on consumption.
Exempting groceries from the GET would lower state GET revenue in 2027 by about $263
million before residents change their spending, and the state would lose about $255 million
after responses, which include residents spending part of the savings within the year. The
state recovers about $3 million more in the long run, as saved income is spent. The county
surcharge would fall by about $33 million before responses.14 In our main case, stores pass the
full exemption on to shoppers. In the lower case they pass on only 70 percent, and the other
30 percent stays with the owners of stores and their suppliers. The model treats these owners
as Hawaiʻi residents and divides the 30 percent among them in proportion to business income.
Some of the savings go to visitors. We assign residents’ savings to households according to
their taxable grocery purchases, and then estimate how much additional GET residents pay
when they spend what they save (chapter 3 and section 9.7). When we compare savings with
spending, spending means everything a household pays out in the year, including gifts and
retirement contributions.
When the income tax changes, households in the model can change the income they report,
when they sell assets and whether they work. For reported income we follow Kumar and Liang
(2020). In their estimates, the response of taxable income to the share of each additional dollar
the filer keeps rises with income, from about 0.3 for taxable income near $26,000 to about
1.3 at $450,000 and above, in 2027 dollars.15 Realized capital gains respond to the tax rate
on gains with an elasticity of −0.4 (Agersnap and Zidar 2021). For the decision whether to
work, we rely on studies of the earned income tax credit and assume that it responds most at
low earnings, and more for parents than for other workers. High-income households can also
leave the state. For the top income-tax rate, this raises the cost per net dollar raised from 1.42
to 2.02 (section 9.2).16 The income-tax, GET and property-tax estimates in chapter 9, and
the revenue-neutral combinations in section 9.7, all include this response. A tax cut works the

other way and keeps some households that would otherwise have left. Part of the tax on the
owners of partnerships and S corporations falls on employees: 14.5 cents of each dollar, the
midpoint of the 11 to 18 cents that Risch (2024) estimates, with 11 cents as the alternative.
All of it falls on the wages of private-sector employees in the highest-paid tenth of earners, 45
percent on the top 1 percent and 55 percent on the rest of that tenth, the division in Kennedy
et al. (2026). The model lowers these wages and makes no separate adjustment to owners’
income, because the response of reported income to tax rates already reflects what owners bear.
Only the TAT estimates allow visitor spending to respond, using our estimate from Hawaiʻi’s
past TAT increases (section 9.4 and Appendix F). For the GET and the other taxes, visitor
spending is held fixed. Each estimate includes the GET on the change in residents’ spending
when their taxes change: a 0.5-point increase in the GET rate, for example, loses $6.4 million
this way. The main estimates are for the short run, in which households change their spending
by about 57 cents for each dollar of tax, according to survey evidence. We report separately
the long run, as saved income is spent, in which spending changes by the full dollar. Other
purchases are held fixed.17
The model has two main limits. Income that the survey does not collect, such as capital gains,
is imputed from national tax data, so the estimates for reforms aimed at top incomes are the
least certain. In addition, the model is weighted to match Hawaiʻi totals but has not been
checked against Hawaiʻi data that were not used in the weighting (chapter 10).
D. Response estimates from the literature
A higher rate in one of the lower brackets collects more revenue from all filers with income
above that bracket, but it changes the incentive to earn an additional dollar only for filers
whose income falls within it. A schedule of rates that increase with income therefore allows the
state to collect more from those with higher incomes without raising the rate on an additional
dollar of income for all filers. This lowers the cost of raising revenue and also places more of
the tax on higher incomes (section 2.3).
In the top bracket there is no such distinction, because all filers who pay the higher rate also
face it on an additional dollar of income. Raising the top rate raises more revenue the further
top incomes lie above the threshold, and loses more revenue the more responsive these filers
are to the rate (Saez 2001). The top rate that maximizes state income-tax revenue is

296

===== page 299 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission Technical appendices
The formula rests on four assumptions. First, we assume that an additional dollar of state in-
come tax does not increase the federal deduction for state and local taxes, which is the case
for most filers in the top bracket because their state and local taxes already exceed the federal
cap on this deduction.19 Second, 𝑒 is the response of top incomes reported in Hawaiʻi, which
includes people moving to or from the state, and so it is larger than the national estimates
(section 1.6 reviews the evidence on moves). Third, the formula counts only income-tax rev-
enue, and the rate would be lower if it also included the GET and other revenue that top
earners pay. Fourth, the formula gives the rate above which revenue falls. The responses of
top earners impose costs at rates well below it, so it is the highest rate at which an increase
still raises revenue, not a rate at which revenue is cheap to raise.20
IRS data on returns filed from Hawaiʻi give a first value of 𝑎. In TY2023, the 7,140 returns
with adjusted gross income (AGI) of $500,000 or more reported an average of about $1.17
million, so 𝑎 was about 1.74. Put another way, about 57 cents of each dollar on these returns
was income above $500,000, the part that a higher top rate reaches. Over TY2018–2023 it
ranged from 1.51 to 1.78. The same calculation for all US returns gives about 1.5 in TY2023,
and Hawaiʻi’s value was the higher of the two in each of the six years.21 A lower 𝑎 means a
fatter top tail, with more of top income far above the threshold. A higher 𝑎 means that less of
top income lies above the threshold, so for a given 𝑒 the revenue-maximizing rate is lower.
There is no estimate of 𝑒 from Hawaiʻi data. The household model uses the response of taxable
income in Kumar and Liang (2020), which rises with income to about 1.3 at the top (Appendix
C). This response does not include moves to or from the state, so the value of 𝑒 that the formula
calls for would be larger. An estimate of 𝑒 for Hawaiʻi could be obtained from the state’s own
changes in its top rates (section B.1).
In our household model, a 1 percent rise in the 13 percent top rate loses about 29 cents of
each mechanical dollar to lower reported income and spending, or about 50 cents once some
high-income households leave (section 9.2).22 The rate is below the point where revenue falls,
but each net dollar it raises costs taxpayers well over a dollar.
The formula can be applied to the household model’s own responses, which include the house-

Hawaiʻi income tax at the entity level, which the entity deducts (HRS §235-51.5).

21 UHERO calculations from IRS Statistics of Income (n.d.-b), returns and AGI by size of AGI for Hawaiʻi
and the United States, TY2018–2023. The US values range from 1.39 to 1.53. These data differ from
what the formula calls for in four ways. The income is federal AGI, where the formula calls for Hawaiʻi
taxable income, and it includes long-term capital gains, which Hawaiʻi taxes at no more than 7.25
percent. The returns are federal returns filed from Hawaiʻi addresses, not Hawaiʻi returns. The IRS
reports classes of AGI for all filing statuses together, while from TY2027 the top bracket begins at
$500,000 of taxable income for single filers and $1 million for joint filers (section 2.1). At $1 million,
𝑎 was 1.66 in TY2023 and ranged from 1.40 to 1.73. Only about 2,000 Hawaiʻi returns reported
$1 million or more in each of TY2022 and TY2023, so a few very large returns can move the result:
their average AGI was $3.50 million in TY2022 and $2.51 million in TY2023.
22 UHERO calculations using the Hawaiʻi household model.

holds that leave the state, the response of pass-through businesses and the wages they pay,
and the GET on what top earners would have spent. If each response keeps the same elasticity
as the rate rises, the share of an added dollar that is lost grows in proportion to 𝜏/(1−𝜏 −𝜏),
𝑓
and revenue is highest where that share reaches one. With the top federal rate of 37 percent,
the 50 cents lost at 13 percent then implies a revenue-maximizing top rate of about 22 percent.
If no household leaves, 29 cents are lost and the rate is about 30 percent.23 We do not run the
household model itself at rates this high. It scales up the response to a small change in the
rate, so its estimates apply only to changes of a point or two.
The main response estimates that we take from other studies are in Table D.1, which also
gives what each one measures and the place, period and population it comes from. Only one
of them, Bonham and Gangnes (1996), was estimated in Hawaiʻi. The estimates marked with
a dagger enter our own calculations, and the others are context for them. Our own estimates
of how TAT collections respond to the TAT rate are in Appendix F.

Figure E.1. Upstream excise tax costs by industry as a percentage of final sale, not including tax on final sale
Note: Upstream excise tax as a percent of each industry’s final sales, 2022, with the county surcharge. Estimates
give totals broadly in line with the excise tax collected (within 3 percent) and the exemptions claimed (within 6
percent) in 2022. Lines show a plausible range around each estimate. * Output that is not sold in a market. Source:
DBEDT (2025b), 2022 benchmark input-output table (detailed tables); Hawaiʻi Department of Taxation, General
Excise and Use Tax Collections, calendar year 2022, and General Excise Tax Exemptions report, tax year 2022;
state law for the rates;24 UHERO calculations.
The industry estimates in Figure E.1 rest on the 2022 benchmark input-output table that
DBEDT published in October 2025, which has 62 producing sectors. DBEDT did not build the
table from a survey of what Hawaiʻi businesses buy from one another. It started from its 2017
table, in which many purchases between industries were estimated from the national table,
24 Act 94 (SLH 2015) (exemptions report); HRS chapters 237 and 238 (rates).
307

===== page 310 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission Technical appendices
and adjusted that table to match 2022 totals.25 This limits our estimates in two ways. First,
the table does not show how much of each industry’s inputs is bought from Hawaiʻi sellers,
and pyramiding depends on this share. It estimates intermediate imports as a residual. Sec-
ond, the GET that the table reports for each industry is an allocation at the rates implicit in
the 2017 table, not GET that the industry was observed to pay. Total GET in the table is 98
percent of the collections that DOTAX allocated to activities in calendar year 2022. The totals
are therefore sound, while the split across industries is less certain.26
The household model in chapter 9 assigns the GET on business purchases to final buyers, on
the assumption that it is passed forward in prices. Pyramiding also distorts the inputs that
businesses choose to buy, and the model does not count this cost. The true cost per net dollar
raised is therefore probably higher than the 1.03 in Table 9.1.
F. Evidence on the effects of the TAT
This appendix discusses the data, method and further results behind section 4.5. It covers the
TAT itself (F.1–F.3), the GET (F.4), payroll (F.5), how we turn the estimates into dollars and
visitor-days (F.6), room rates (F.7) and the 2018 increase (F.8).
F.1 Data
Hawaiʻi’s TAT. Our measure for Hawaiʻi is the state TAT on transient accommodations, ex-
cluding the tax on timeshares, by quarter. DOTAX (n.d.-d) reports collections by the month
the money arrives. Returns for one month’s rentals were due by the last day of the following
month through 2010, and by the 20th of the following month from 2011. We use those due
dates to convert collections to the month of the rentals.27 DOTAX’s TAT collections include the
timeshare occupancy tax, which began in January 1999, so we remove timeshare collections
using a share for each fiscal year. DOTAX published this share for FY2006 to FY2013, and its
monthly reports give it from FY2018. For FY1999 to FY2005 we use the earliest published
share, 1.74 percent in FY2006; it reached 5.2 percent by FY2009. Calendar-year collections
from DOTAX records put the share at 0.93 percent in 1999, rising to 1.88 percent in 2004 and
1.86 percent in 2005 (Kwak and Mak 2008, Table 2), so the 1.74 percent share removes some-
what too much timeshare tax before FY2004 and slightly too little in FY2004 and FY2005. For
25 DBEDT (2025b), pp. 39–42. DBEDT describes regional tables as typically built by “non-survey or
partial-survey methods”. Where the 2017 table had no direct data, DBEDT filled cells from the
national table or from its 2007 Hawaiʻi table, and it balanced the 2022 table with a procedure meant
to keep the earlier structure. Evidence from elsewhere suggests that such methods can overstate or
understate multipliers: Bonfiglio and Chelli (2008) find this in a simulation, and Lahr (1993) reviews
the hybrid methods that arose because survey tables are costly and non-survey tables inaccurate.

FY2014 to FY2017 we interpolate the size of the timeshare tax base relative to the hotel tax
base between FY2013 and FY2018, and apply each year’s tax rates. In the quarters for which
DOTAX reports TAT liability directly, our rebuilt series is between 0.97 and 1.05 times the
reported figure. The taxable base is our liability series divided by the state rate in force.
Comparison places. For each comparison state we built a quarterly series of lodging tax col-
lections or taxable lodging sales, by county where available, from its revenue department’s
records, together with a history of every state and local tax rate that applies to a hotel room,
taken from statutes and published rate tables. The states are Arizona, Florida, Montana, Ne-
braska, New Hampshire, New York, Oregon, Utah, Virginia and Washington; Guam enters as
a single place. The comparison places are these states’ counties and Guam. Some places are
left out entirely: Idaho, because online platforms began collecting its lodging tax in 2018; Vir-
ginia in 2009–10, because of a change in industry classification; Virginia’s localities in 1999,
because their rates are not observed before mid-1999; two New York counties, one with a
large casino resort that opened in 2018 and one with a possible lapse in its occupancy tax;
and any locality whose rate history is disputed. We could not verify any usable places in Texas
or New Mexico.
Tourism screen. A county enters the comparison group only if accommodation (NAICS 721)
is a large part of its economy: its share of jobs in accommodation must be at least 1.5 times the
national share (a location quotient of 1.5), and it must have at least 250 accommodation jobs,
both averaged over the comparison period before the increase. The data are from the Quarterly
Census of Employment and Wages (QCEW; Bureau of Labor Statistics n.d.-b). Guam is not in
the QCEW, so we use its own accommodation employment, which passes every version of the
test.
The GET. To compare Hawaiʻi with other states, GET collections must be measured the same
way in Hawaiʻi and in every comparison state. The only source that does this is the Census
Bureau’s Quarterly Summary of State and Local Tax Revenue (US Census Bureau n.d.-a), item
T09 (general sales and gross receipts taxes), which applies one set of definitions to every state.
It is the only quarterly sales tax series we have for the comparison states, so we use it for
Hawaiʻi as well, rather than DOTAX’s own GET and use tax collections. The two Hawaiʻi series
agree in most quarters but not all: from 2008Q3 to 2009Q3, around the 2009 increase, the
Census figures are $5 million to $14 million a quarter higher. The DOTAX series also begins
in 1997Q3, too late for the 1999 comparison. From 2018Q1 the Census figures for Hawaiʻi
include the Honolulu county surcharge, which we remove. We also estimate the models using
the DOTAX series for Hawaiʻi and find that it does not meaningfully change the estimated GET
response. Specifically, the 2009–10 GET response is 0.03 rather than −0.03 (F.4).
Other data. Payroll is QCEW total wages in restaurants and bars (NAICS 722) and in amuse-
ment, gambling and recreation (NAICS 713), statewide for Hawaiʻi and by county for the
comparison places; the recreation series are often withheld for small counties. Visitor days
and visitor spending are from the Hawaiʻi Tourism Authority (HTA n.d.) and DBEDT (n.d.), as
compiled in UHERO (n.d.); the spending series begins in 2001. Prices are the consumer price
index for all urban consumers (CPI-U), annual averages (Bureau of Labor Statistics n.d.-a).

Which places count. A place is left out of the comparison group if it changed its corresponding
lodging tax rate during the comparison period. Places are also left out if a broad change in
what is taxed, such as online platforms beginning to collect the tax, reaches its series in the
year before the increase or after it.
Many specifications of the statistical model. Estimating statistical models involves many
choices (some of which are fairly arbitrary) that a researcher must make. These choices may
affect the results of the model. Table F.1 outlines a (likely non-exhaustive) set of researcher-
specified choices in our TAT setting which could be considered reasonable. For example, we
identify “tourism heavy” comparison counties by considering their location quotient. Our pri-
mary specification restricted the comparison group to counties with hotel jobs as a share of all
jobs in the county being at least 1.5× the national average. However, perhaps this value should
be 2× the national average or just anything above the national average. We estimated models
for every combination of arbitrary choices stated in Table F.1, which yields 144 specifications
for 1999 and 192 for 2009–10. We report their median, following Clemens et al. (2026). The
rules for which versions count were set before any of the versions were estimated.

F.4 The GET
We estimate the change in Hawaiʻi’s GET collections from TAT rate increases with the same
statistical method as with the TAT collections. The comparison states are those with a general
sales tax that changed neither their sales tax rate nor their sales tax base during the analysis
window (i.e. the 12 quarters before Hawaiʻi raised its TAT or the 8 quarters after). The main
specification considers 33 comparison states (counting the District of Columbia) for 1999 and
16 for 2009–10. The response is the change in log GET collections divided by the log change
in the TAT rate, so −0.1 means a 10 percent higher TAT rate caused GET collections to fall by
about 1 percent.

F.5 Payroll
In chapter 4, we discussed that GET responses are likely to create differential impacts across
Hawaiʻi business sectors. Estimating the effects on individual industries requires sales data
from that industry for both Hawaiʻi and the comparison states, and it requires understanding
if Hawaiʻi or if any given comparison state simultaneously enacted policy which would affect
sales in the respective states. We were not able to collect such data or information at such
a granular level. However, for illustrative purposes, we collected payroll data from Hawaiʻi’s
statewide QCEW for two tourism-heavy sectors: restaurants and recreation. We compare the
total wages in Hawaiʻi for those in a given sector with those of the comparison counties and
states, using the same rules, tourism screen, and statistical models as for the TAT. We also
leave out any county that changed a tax on restaurant meals during the period. Table F.4
reports the main specification and the median across versions. Because the 2009–10 recreation
comparison in the main version has fewer than five counties, the main text uses medians across
versions for both industries. In the main version, restaurant payroll’s response is −0.05 for
1999 and 0.02 for 2009–10.

Visitor-days and spending. The taxable room base changes by 𝑟𝜀−1 − 1: −3.2 percent for
1999 and −2.2 percent for 2009–10. Applying that change to all visitor days in the four
quarters before the increase (57.4 million and 60.2 million) gives 1.83 million and 1.31 million
visitor-days lost a year. Multiplying by visitor spending per day other than lodging gives the
spending lost: $347.0 million (1999) and $210.1 million (2009–10) a year in 2025 dollars.
Spending per day is DBEDT’s visitor expenditure divided by DBEDT and HTA visitor days over
the same quarters: $280 and $254 in 2025 dollars. Because lodging is already counted in
the lost room revenue, we subtract it using the lodging share of visitor spending (32 percent
in 2001, DBEDT 2002, Table 52; 37 percent in mid-2008 to mid-2009, Table 59 of DBEDT
2009 and HTA 2010). That leaves $189 (1999) and $160 (2009–10) a day for meals, tours,
shopping and other non-lodging spending. For 2026, the same steps with 2025 data (lodging
46 percent; DBEDT 2026a, Table 71) give $142 a day and, with 0.76 million visitor-days lost
(0.53 to 1.00 million), $108.1 million a year ($74.9 million to $141.3 million). These figures
understate the loss in that they ignore visitors who still came but spent less, and overstate it
to the extent that they treat the whole decline in taxable room revenue as coming from fewer
stays rather than lower pre-tax hotel room rates (see F.7).
The 2026 projection. We apply the 1999 and 2009–10 responses to CY2025 TAT collections
(at 10.25 percent) and Census GET collections, with 𝑟 = 11/10.25. The central projection av-
erages the two episodes’ responses (0.869 for the TAT, −0.063 for the GET) before computing
dollars. We assume there is no growth or inflation in 2026. The TAT base excludes timeshares,
so the increase in the tax on timeshares to 11 percent is not included.32
F.7 Room rates
The decline in the taxable room base could come from fewer or shorter stays or from lower
pre-tax room rates. If hotels decided to absorb the entire TAT increase, so that the price guests
paid with tax included stayed the same, their pre-tax rates would have had to fall by about
1.1 percent in 1999 and 0.9 percent at each of the 2009 and 2010 increases.
We searched for such a drop in two ways. First, for each TAT increase we fit a time-series
model to Hawaiʻi’s monthly average daily room rate, statewide and excluding taxes, over the
24 months before and after the increase. The model measures how much room rates rose or
fell, beyond their usual trend and seasonal pattern, in the month the new TAT rate took effect.
We did this with and without seasonal adjustment. We leave out 1987 and 1994 because we
could not confirm that the room rates recorded then exclude taxes.
Room rates rose after some TAT increases and fell after others. But room rates also rise and fall
in months when the TAT does not change. When we ran the same model for about 210 months
with no TAT change, we found that room rates typically rise or fall by 1.3 percent (seasonally
adjusted) every month in absence of tax changes. The TAT increases we study could have
lowered pre-tax room rates by at most about 1 percent, and only if hotels absorbed the whole
increase. Because room rates normally move by more than that from month to month, the
changes after the TAT increases cannot be told apart from ordinary movements in room rates.
We therefore cannot tell whether hotels absorbed none, some, or all of the TAT increases.
32 Act 96 (SLH 2025), §5.
316

===== page 319 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission Technical appendices
Second, we compared Hawaiʻi’s room rates with those in Puerto Rico and Guam, which publish
monthly room rates for these years. The gap between room rates in Hawaiʻi and in these islands
normally moves by 3 to 8 percent, again more than the TAT increases could have lowered
Hawaiʻi’s room rates (at most about 1 percent for each increase, or 2 percent for the 2009 and
2010 increases combined). Hawaiʻi’s room rates did fall relative to both islands in 2009–10,
but the fall began in 2008, a year before the July 2009 TAT increase, when the recession and
the shutdown of Aloha and ATA airlines hit Hawaiʻi.
Public room-rate data therefore cannot tell us how the burden of these TAT increases was split
between hotels and guests. Separating the effect of the tax from ordinary movements in room
rates would require a much larger TAT increase, or licensed hotel-level data on room rates
in many comparable destinations, which would give us much more statistical power than we
currently have.
F.8 The 2018 increase
In the main analysis, we omit the 2018 TAT increase from the set of tax increases we study. This
policy increased the TAT from 9.25 to 10.25 percent, but does not give a usable estimate of the
visitor response. The main specification estimates a TAT response of 1.26 (95 percent range
−0.10 to 2.62; Table F.2), and the median across 240 specifications is 1.44. A response above
1 would mean the taxable base grew faster in Hawaiʻi than in the comparison places because
of the tax increase, which is implausible. There are two issues: one in the period before 2018,
and one in the post period in 2019. First, from August 2016, after DOTAX moved the TAT to a
new processing system, Hawaiʻi’s TAT collections became more volatile and rose relative to the
comparison places, with no matching change in hotel payroll or in room revenue.33 Second,
Hawaiʻi also changed the TAT base in 2019, applying it to online travel companies’ margins
and to resort fees, which is why the 2018 period ends in 2018Q4.34 Limiting the post-TAT
period to just one year might be problematic because travel plans are “sticky” in the short run,
and it may take another year for behavioral responses to reveal themselves.
G. Film credit: summary statistics
The motion picture, digital media, and film production income tax credit is a refundable credit
on qualified production costs: 22 percent on Oʻahu and 27 percent on the other islands for
costs incurred from 2023, 20 and 25 percent before, and five percentage points more from
2026 for productions that meet Act 185’s local-hire test.35 This appendix reports summary
statistics on the credit from two public sources. Table G.1 gives the credit claimed on tax
returns in each tax year, as reported by DOTAX, together with the annual limit on total credits.
Table G.2 gives figures by production year from DBEDT’s annual reports on the credit (Bai
33 DOTAX’s Tax System Modernization moved the GET and TAT to the new system in August 2016: li-
censes with new Hawaiʻi Tax ID numbers were mailed to taxpayers with active GET and TAT accounts
that month (DOTAX n.d.-b). The cause of the rise in collections is not known. These diagnostics are
exploratory.
34 Act 211 (SLH 2018) (online travel companies, from January 1, 2019); Act 20 (SLH 2019) (resort
fees, from July 1, 2019).
35 HRS §235-17(a), (c). Act 185 (SLH 2026) adds 5 percentage points for qualified productions whose

H. Glossary
These are the terms used most often in the report, with the meaning we give them.
Behavioral response. A change in what people do when a tax changes, such as reporting less
income, selling fewer assets, working less, booking fewer hotel nights or moving away
(section 9.1).
Burden change. Under a revenue-neutral combination, the change in what taxpayers pay
before they adjust their behavior. When it is negative, taxpayers pay less and the state
still collects the same revenue (section 9.7).
Capitalization. When a tax change moves the price of an asset, such as a home, we say that
it is capitalized. Whoever owns the asset at the time of the change then bears it (chapter
5).
Conveyance tax. The state tax on transfers of real estate. It is collected on the full amount
paid for the property (chapter 5).
Cost per net dollar raised. The mechanical change in what taxpayers pay, divided by net
revenue. If it is above 1, taxpayers give up more than the government receives (Appendix
A; section 9.1).
County surcharge. Each of the four counties adds 0.5 percent to the GET on activities that
the state taxes at 4 percent (chapter 3).
Elasticity. By how many percent one quantity changes when another changes by 1 percent
(Appendix D).
Elasticity of taxable income. By how many percent reported taxable income changes when
the share of each additional dollar that the filer keeps after tax changes by 1 percent
(section 2.3).
Fiscal year (FY). The State’s budget year. It runs from July 1 to June 30 and takes the name
of the year in which it ends.
General excise tax (GET). Hawaiʻi’s tax on the gross receipts of businesses. Most sales to
final customers are taxed at 4 percent. Wholesale, manufacturing and some other sales
between businesses are taxed at 0.5 percent (chapter 3).
Household model. UHERO’s model of a representative sample of Hawaiʻi households. We
use it to estimate how a tax change affects revenue and which households gain or lose
(Appendix C).
43 TIR 2026-03, pp. 1–2: a local hire is an individual who is a Hawaiʻi resident under HRS §235-1
during the calendar year for which the credit is claimed.
320

===== page 323 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission Technical appendices
Incidence. Who bears the burden of a tax, through lower income or through higher prices.
This need not be the person who remits the tax to the government.
Income tenth (decile). We order households from lowest to highest income and divide them
into ten groups of equal size. Each group is an income tenth.
Input-output table. A table of how much each industry buys from every other industry, which
we use to estimate pyramiding (Appendix E).
Mechanical change. What taxpayers would pay, and so what the government would collect,
if a tax changed and nobody did anything differently (Appendix A).
Net revenue. The change in revenue once people have responded. We count it for the govern-
ment that levies the tax (Appendix A).
Pass-through. How much of a tax change shows up in the prices that buyers pay, as a share
of the change (chapter 3; Appendix C).
Pass-through entity. A partnership or S corporation. Its income is taxed to its owners and
not to the business itself (chapter 6).
Pyramiding. The GET paid on a sale between businesses is built into the prices of later sales,
which are taxed again. In this way the tax accumulates along the supply chain (chapter
3).
Refundable credit. A tax credit that the filer receives in full even when it is larger than the
tax owed (chapter 8).
Revenue-maximizing rate. The tax rate at which revenue is highest. If the rate goes any
higher, taxpayers’ responses take away more revenue than the higher rate adds (Ap-
pendix D).
Revenue-neutral combination. Two changes, one that raises a tax and one that cuts a tax,
chosen so that state revenue in 2027 is the same after behavioral responses (section
9.7).
Tax year (TY). The year of income covered by a tax return.
Transient accommodations tax (TAT). The tax on what is charged for a hotel room or other
short-term rental. From January 1, 2026 the state rate is 11 percent, and each county
adds 3 percent (chapter 4).
Use tax. The tax on goods brought into Hawaiʻi, which complements the GET on business
activity within the state (chapter 3).
I. Two combinations that change how capital gains are taxed
This appendix reports two revenue-neutral combinations whose results are more uncertain
than those in section 9.7. Each is balanced by extrapolating beyond the model runs: the first
needs a top rate of about 10.1 percent, and the runs were at 11 to 11.5 percent; the second
needs about 25.7 percent, and the runs were at 19 to 20 percent. Both also make large changes
to the top rate or to the taxation of capital gains, where the model’s calculation of responses
is least accurate.
321

===== page 324 =====
Hawaiʻi Tax Review Commission Technical appendices
I.1 Capital gains taxed as ordinary income, with a lower top rate
Hawaiʻi caps the tax on long-term capital gains at 7.25 percent, while the top rate on other
income is 13 percent from 2027 (chapter 2). This combination removes the cap, so that gains
are taxed at the same rates as wages, and lowers the top rate until state revenue is unchanged.
The top rate that balances is 10.08 percent, found by extrapolating from two model runs.44
Taxpayers as a group would pay about $65.0 million a year less than they do now for the same
state revenue (Figure I.1).
Taxed as ordinary income and with no other change, gains would add to tax bills, and the
cut in the top rate takes more off them. The difference is the $65.0 million. The state can
return more than it took, since people report more income when the top rate is lower and
fewer of them leave. Because a dollar of revenue from the top rate costs taxpayers $2.02 and
a dollar from the capital-gains rate only $1.35 (section 9.2), trading the first for the second
saves money.45
Almost all of the change occurs in the top tenth. In each of the other nine tenths the average
change is $7 a year or less. Within the top tenth, it is those with large capital gains who lose,
and those with high wages or business income who gain.
The average household in the top tenth is $2,614 a year better off. This is more than the cut in
its state tax. At a lower top rate these households earn and report more income, and the chart
counts that income as theirs. They also pay federal tax on it, and in this simulation residents
pay about $129.5 million more to the federal government. The $65.0 million figure measures
the state tax system only.
44 We change only the rate in the top bracket, which begins at $500,000 of taxable income for a single
filer in 2027. This leaves the balancing rate of 10.08 percent below the 11 percent rate of the bracket
beneath it, and a real reform would probably adjust several of the upper rates together. The result
shows the size of the rate cut that the revenue could pay for. County revenue changes by less than
$0.3 million.
45 The two costs are for small changes. Both count high-income households that leave the state, but
neither counts households that a lower top rate might attract to Hawaiʻi. If it does attract them, the
gain from this combination would be larger (section 9.8).

I.2 No tax on long-term capital gains, with a higher top rate
This is the reverse of the previous combination. The rate on long-term capital gains falls from
7.25 percent to zero and the top rate on other income rises to pay for it. The top rate would
have to reach about 25.7 percent, up from 13. We find that rate by extending the line through
two model runs at lower rates.46 Taxpayers as a group would pay about $402 million a year
more than they do now, a larger loss than in any combination in chapter 9 (Figure I.2).
Exempting gains takes less off tax bills than the higher top rate adds to them, and the dif-
ference is the $397.8 million. The higher rate brings in much less than it adds to tax bills,
because those who pay it report less income or leave the state. So the state would give up
revenue from capital gains, the tax base that responds less, and collect it from the base that
responds more.
The changes are concentrated in the top tenth, where the average household is $13,882 a
year worse off. The eighth tenth loses $20 on average and the ninth gains $47. The loss at the
top is larger than the added tax, since the chart also counts the income that these households
no longer earn or report at the higher rate. For the same reason residents pay about $519.9
million less in federal income tax in this simulation.
46 A top rate of 25.7 percent is above the rate at which revenue is highest by the formula in Appendix
D, about 22 percent. The model scales up the response to a small change in the rate, so it overstates
what so high a rate would collect. County revenue falls by $1.0 million.
